01 Kernel vs OS vs Distro

The vocabulary you need before anything else makes sense.

What is it?

The kernel is the core program that runs with full hardware access (CPU, RAM, disk, network cards) and mediates every other program's access to that hardware. Linux (lowercase l for the kernel) is just this program — originally written by Linus Torvalds in 1991. A distro (Ubuntu, Debian, Amazon Linux, RHEL) is the kernel + a package manager + a standard set of userland tools (bash, coreutils, systemd) + a default config, packaged for humans to actually install and use.

Why was it needed?

Before an OS abstraction layer, every program had to talk directly to hardware — meaning every app needed its own disk driver, its own way of talking to the network card, and no two programs could safely run at once without stepping on each other's memory. That's unworkable at any scale.

What problem does it solve?

  • Resource arbitration — many processes want CPU time and RAM; the kernel schedules and allocates.
  • Isolation — one process crashing shouldn't take down another (or the machine).
  • Abstraction — a program writes to a file descriptor; it doesn't care if that's an SSD, a network socket, or a pipe to another process.

How does it work internally?

The CPU supports privilege levels ("rings"). The kernel runs in kernel mode (ring 0, unrestricted). Your applications run in user mode (ring 3, restricted — can't touch hardware directly). When your app needs something privileged (read a file, open a socket, fork a process), it makes a system call (syscall) — a controlled, trapped jump into kernel mode. The kernel does the privileged work and hands control back.

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ USER SPACE (ring 3 — unprivileged) │ │ your app, bash, java, python, nginx... │ │ │ read()/write()/fork()/socket() │ │ ▼ (syscall — controlled trap) │ ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │ KERNEL SPACE (ring 0 — privileged) │ │ process scheduler | memory manager │ │ filesystem (VFS) | network stack │ │ device drivers │ ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │ HARDWARE: CPU, RAM, Disk, NIC │ └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Components

ComponentJob
Process schedulerDecides which process gets CPU time next
Memory managerVirtual memory, paging, per-process address space isolation
VFS (Virtual File System)Uniform interface over ext4, xfs, nfs, tmpfs etc.
Network stackTCP/IP implementation, sockets
Device driversTalk to actual hardware

Production implementation

You almost never touch the kernel directly. In production you pick a distro tuned for your workload: Amazon Linux 2023 / Ubuntu LTS for general EC2 workloads, Alpine for minimal Docker images (uses musl libc, not glibc — occasionally breaks native binaries), Bottlerocket for container-only EKS nodes (immutable, minimal attack surface).

Trade-offs

ChoiceProCon
Alpine~5MB base image, fast pullsmusl libc incompatibilities, harder debugging (no bash by default)
Ubuntu LTSglibc compat, huge community, 5yr supportBigger image size
Amazon LinuxOptimized for EC2, free extended support on AWSLess useful outside AWS

Common mistakes

  • Confusing "Linux" (kernel) with "Linux distro" in interviews — say the precise word.
  • Assuming a static binary built on Ubuntu (glibc) will run unmodified on Alpine (musl) — it often segfaults or fails to find shared libs.

Interview Qs

Q: What's the difference between a process running in user mode vs kernel mode?
A: User mode = restricted, can't directly access hardware/memory of other processes; must use syscalls. Kernel mode = full privilege, executes the actual syscall logic.
Q: Why does Alpine's small size sometimes cause production bugs?
A: musl libc has different behavior/quirks vs glibc (DNS resolution, locale handling), so binaries built/tested against glibc can misbehave.

02 Boot Process

What happens between "power on" and "you have a shell" — relevant for EC2 troubleshooting (instance stuck, user-data debugging).

What is it / why needed

The sequence that takes a machine from zero (no OS in memory) to a running, login-ready system. You need this mental model to debug an EC2 instance that "won't come up" — is it stuck at BIOS, bootloader, kernel init, or systemd?

1. BIOS/UEFI → POST (hardware self-test), finds boot device 2. Bootloader → GRUB reads kernel image + initramfs from disk 3. Kernel init → kernel decompresses, mounts initramfs, starts PID 1 4. PID 1 (systemd) → mounts real root fs, starts targets/services 5. Login prompt / SSH daemon ready

Components

  • BIOS/UEFI — firmware, does hardware POST (power-on self-test).
  • GRUB — bootloader; picks kernel version, passes kernel params.
  • initramfs — tiny temp root filesystem in RAM, has just enough drivers to mount the real disk.
  • PID 1 — first userspace process (systemd on modern distros), parent of all other processes.

Production relevance

On AWS: EC2 user-data scripts run via cloud-init which itself is a systemd service started late in boot. If your user-data script silently fails, check /var/log/cloud-init-output.log — the #1 place people forget to look when "the instance came up but my app isn't running."

Common mistakes

  • Debugging app-level issues on a fresh EC2 instance without checking cloud-init logs first.
  • Not knowing PID 1 can't be killed with normal SIGKILL semantics — relevant to Docker container root-process handling (zombie reaping).

Interview Q

Q: Your EC2 instance passes status checks but your app never started. First place to check?
A: /var/log/cloud-init-output.log for user-data script errors, then systemctl status your-service, then journalctl -u your-service.

03 Filesystem Hierarchy

"Everything is a file" — and where things actually live.

What is it?

Linux exposes a single unified tree rooted at / — no drive letters like Windows' C:\. Devices, sockets, processes, and configs are all represented as paths in that same tree.

Why needed / problem solved

A predictable, standardized layout (FHS — Filesystem Hierarchy Standard) means any sysadmin or script can find logs, configs, and binaries on any distro without guessing.

/ ├── bin, sbin → essential binaries (ls, mount) — often symlinked to /usr/bin now ├── boot → kernel image, GRUB config ├── dev → device files (/dev/sda, /dev/null, /dev/tty) ├── etc → system-wide config files (nginx.conf, fstab, passwd) ├── home → user home directories (/home/asmit) ├── lib, lib64 → shared libraries needed by /bin, /sbin ├── mnt, media → mount points for external/temp filesystems ├── opt → optional third-party software ├── proc → VIRTUAL fs — live kernel/process info (not real files on disk) ├── root → home directory of root user ├── run → runtime data since last boot (PIDs, sockets) ├── srv → data for services (web server data etc.) ├── sys → VIRTUAL fs — kernel/device info, tunables ├── tmp → temp files, often tmpfs (RAM-backed, wiped on reboot) ├── usr → user-installed programs, libraries, docs (bulk of the OS) └── var → variable data: /var/log, /var/lib, /var/www

How it works internally

/proc and /sys are not real disk files — they're a live window into kernel data structures. cat /proc/1234/status shows real-time memory/state of process 1234. This is how tools like ps, top, and free actually get their data — they read /proc.

Production usage

# Where you'll actually go in production:
/etc/nginx/nginx.conf        # nginx config
/var/log/nginx/access.log    # logs
/var/log/syslog | /var/log/messages   # system-wide log
/etc/systemd/system/         # your custom service unit files
/opt/myapp/                  # a hand-deployed app
/proc/loadavg                # instant CPU load without top
/proc/meminfo                # memory stats

Trade-offs / considerations

  • /tmp as tmpfs = fast but volatile and RAM-limited — don't dump large build artifacts there in CI.
  • Mixing app data into /opt vs a proper package under /usr — /opt is fine for hand-rolled deploys, but breaks convention for anything meant to be packaged (.deb/.rpm).

Common mistakes

  • Writing app logs to a location no one expects (breaks log-shipping agents' default configs).
  • Not knowing /proc is virtual — trying to cp it somewhere and being confused when file sizes show as 0.

Interview Q

Q: How does `top` get live CPU/memory numbers without asking the kernel via a special API each time?
A: It reads plain text files under /proc (e.g. /proc/stat, /proc/meminfo) which the kernel keeps live-updated — no special syscall needed, just read().

04 Users, Groups & Permissions

The single most-tested practical Linux topic in interviews.

What is it / why needed

Linux is a multi-user OS from birth (built for university mainframes shared by many students). Every file needs an owner and a set of rules for who can read/write/execute it — otherwise any process could read or corrupt any other user's data.

How it works internally

Every file has an owning user (UID) and owning group (GID), plus a permission bitmask for three actor classes: owner, group, others. Each class gets r (read=4), w (write=2), x (execute=1).

-rwxr-xr-- 1 asmit devs 1024 Jul 7 10:00 deploy.sh │└┬┘└┬┘└┬┘ │ │ │ └── others: r-- (4) = read only │ │ └───── group: r-x (5) = read + execute │ └──────── owner: rwx (7) = read+write+execute └────────── file type (- = regular file, d = directory, l = symlink) chmod 754 deploy.sh → same as rwxr-xr--

Components

ConceptMeaning
UID 0root — bypasses all permission checks
/etc/passwduser account records (name, UID, home dir, shell)
/etc/shadowhashed passwords, root-readable only
/etc/groupgroup definitions and membership
SUID bitrun binary as the file's owner, not the invoking user (e.g. /usr/bin/passwd runs as root)
sudocontrolled, logged, policy-based elevation — safer than giving out root password

Production implementation

# create a service user with no login shell (best practice for app processes)
sudo useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin appuser

# give a deploy script group execute rights only
chown appuser:deployers deploy.sh
chmod 750 deploy.sh

# check who can sudo
sudo -l

# add user to docker group (so they don't need sudo for docker commands)
sudo usermod -aG docker asmit
Security note: adding a user to the docker group is effectively root-equivalent access — the Docker daemon runs as root and a container can bind-mount the host filesystem. Never do this casually on shared/production boxes.

Trade-offs

  • chmod 777 "fixes" permission errors instantly but opens the file to any user/process on the box — a classic interview red flag if someone suggests it as a real fix.
  • SUID binaries are powerful but a common privilege-escalation vector if misconfigured.

Common mistakes

  • Running production app processes as root "because it's easier" — a container breakout or RCE then owns the whole host.
  • Using chmod -R 777 to silence a permission error instead of finding the actual owner/group mismatch.
  • Confusing chmod numeric mode order (owner-group-other) and computing it wrong under pressure.

Interview Qs

Q: What does chmod 640 mean?
A: Owner: rw- (6), Group: r-- (4), Others: --- (0). Common for config files with secrets — group can read, others get nothing.
Q: Why run app processes as a non-root user in production/Docker?
A: Principle of least privilege — if the process is compromised (RCE), the attacker only gets that user's limited permissions, not full host/root control.

05 Process Management

What actually happens when you run a program — critical for debugging "app is slow/hung/OOM-killed."

What is it / why needed

A process is a running instance of a program: its own memory space, file descriptors, and execution state. The kernel needs to track hundreds of these simultaneously on one CPU — that requires scheduling and state tracking.

How it works internally

fork() exec() Parent ─────► Child (copy) ─────► Child replaced with new program image PID 100 PID 101 (child) still PID 101, now running e.g. "nginx" Process states: R (running/runnable) → S (sleeping, waiting on I/O) → D (uninterruptible sleep, e.g. disk I/O) → T (stopped, e.g. Ctrl+Z) → Z (zombie: exited, parent hasn't read exit code yet)

Every new process is created via fork() (clone the calling process) then usually exec() (replace that clone's memory with a new program). This fork+exec pattern is why starting a process is relatively expensive — it's why things like pre-forked worker pools (Gunicorn, PHP-FPM) exist.

Components

TermMeaning
PIDProcess ID, unique per running process
PPIDParent process ID
SignalAsync notification to a process: SIGTERM (15, please exit gracefully), SIGKILL (9, force kill, un-ignorable), SIGHUP (1, reload config, historically "terminal hung up")
Zombie processExited but parent hasn't called wait() to reap its exit status — harmless individually but signals a bug if they accumulate
Orphan processParent died first; gets re-parented to PID 1 (init/systemd)
Nice valueScheduling priority hint, -20 (highest) to 19 (lowest)

Production commands

ps aux                     # snapshot of all processes
ps aux --sort=-%mem | head # top memory consumers
top / htop                 # live view
kill -15              # SIGTERM: ask nicely to shut down
kill -9                # SIGKILL: force, no cleanup allowed
pkill -f "java -jar app"    # kill by name match
nice -n 10 ./batch_job.sh   # start with lower priority
renice -n 5 -p         # change priority of running process
strace -p               # see live syscalls a process is making (debugging hangs)
lsof -p                  # what files/sockets a process has open

Failure scenario: OOM Killer

When the system runs out of RAM, the kernel's OOM killer picks a process (scored by memory usage + an adjustable oom_score_adj) and SIGKILLs it to save the system. In Kubernetes this shows up as a container being restarted with reason OOMKilled — meaning it hit its memory limit, not that the whole node was out of memory necessarily.

dmesg | grep -i "killed process"   # confirm OOM killer fired, and which process
journalctl -k | grep -i oom       # kernel log via systemd

Trade-offs

  • SIGKILL is instant but skips graceful shutdown (open DB connections, in-flight requests) — always try SIGTERM first, with a timeout before escalating to SIGKILL (this is exactly what Docker/K8s do: SIGTERM then SIGKILL after grace period).

Common mistakes

  • Always reaching for kill -9 — kills the process mid-write, can corrupt data or leave locks held.
  • Not understanding zombie vs orphan in interviews — they get asked about constantly and confused with each other.
  • Assuming a container "hung" when it's actually stuck in D-state waiting on slow disk I/O — that needs different debugging (iostat, not just kill+restart).

Interview Qs

Q: Difference between a zombie and an orphan process?
A: Zombie = child has exited but parent hasn't reaped its exit status yet (exists only as an entry in the process table). Orphan = parent died before child; child gets reparented to PID 1.
Q: Why does Kubernetes send SIGTERM before SIGKILL on pod termination?
A: To give the app a grace period (default 30s) to finish in-flight requests / close connections cleanly before being force-killed.

06 systemd & Services

How your app actually stays running across reboots and crashes on a real Linux box.

What is it / why needed

systemd is PID 1 on virtually every modern distro (replaced older SysV init scripts). Before it, starting services at boot meant sequential shell scripts in /etc/init.d/ — slow (serial startup) and inconsistent (every script reinvented dependency handling, logging, restart-on-crash).

Problem it solves

  • Parallel service startup (faster boot).
  • Declarative dependency graph ("start nginx after network is up").
  • Built-in restart policies, resource limits (cgroups), and unified logging (journald).

How it works internally

Services are defined as unit files (plain text, ini-style) under /etc/systemd/system/. systemd builds a dependency graph from these and starts units in parallel where possible.

# /etc/systemd/system/myapp.service
[Unit]
Description=My Spring Boot App
After=network.target

[Service]
User=appuser
WorkingDirectory=/opt/myapp
ExecStart=/usr/bin/java -jar /opt/myapp/app.jar
Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=5
Environment=SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod
LimitNOFILE=65536

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target

Components

TermMeaning
UnitAny manageable systemd resource: .service, .socket, .timer, .mount
TargetA grouping/synchronization point, like a runlevel (multi-user.target ≈ old runlevel 3)
journaldCentral logging daemon — collects stdout/stderr + kernel logs into a structured, queryable binary log
cgroupsKernel feature systemd uses to limit CPU/memory per service — same primitive Docker uses for containers

Production commands

sudo systemctl daemon-reload         # after editing a unit file
sudo systemctl enable myapp          # start on boot
sudo systemctl start myapp
sudo systemctl status myapp
journalctl -u myapp -f               # live tail logs for this service
journalctl -u myapp --since "10 min ago"
sudo systemctl restart myapp
systemctl list-units --failed        # what's broken right now

Trade-offs

  • systemd is powerful but heavier/more complex than plain init scripts — criticized for scope creep (it also manages logging, mounts, timers — "does too much").
  • In containers you typically don't run systemd (Docker's PID 1 is your app directly) — restart policy is handled by the container orchestrator instead (Docker's restart: always, K8s's pod restart policy).

Common mistakes

  • Forgetting daemon-reload after editing a unit file — old config keeps running.
  • Not setting Restart=on-failure, so a crashed app just stays down.
  • Running the service as root when a dedicated user was clearly warranted.

Interview Q

Q: Your systemd service keeps restarting in a crash loop. How do you debug it?
A: journalctl -u servicename -n 100 --no-pager to see recent logs/exit reason; systemctl status for last exit code; check RestartSec isn't masking a fast crash loop; verify ExecStart path/permissions/env vars.

07 Package Management

How software actually gets installed, versioned, and removed cleanly.

What is it / why needed

Before package managers, installing software meant manually downloading source, compiling it, resolving library dependencies by hand, and hoping nothing conflicted with something else on the box. A package manager tracks installed files, versions, and dependencies so install/upgrade/remove is atomic and reversible.

Distro familyPackage formatManager
Debian/Ubuntu.debapt / dpkg
RHEL/CentOS/Amazon Linux.rpmyum / dnf
Alpine.apkapk

Production commands

# Debian/Ubuntu
sudo apt update              # refresh package index (metadata, not upgrade)
sudo apt install nginx=1.18.0-0ubuntu1   # pin exact version
sudo apt list --installed | grep nginx
sudo apt remove --purge nginx           # remove + configs

# RHEL/Amazon Linux
sudo dnf install nginx
sudo dnf list installed | grep nginx

Trade-offs

  • apt upgrade vs apt full-upgrade: the latter can remove packages to resolve dependency changes — riskier on production, usually run in a controlled maintenance window with tested AMIs instead.
  • Pinning exact versions gives reproducibility but means you manually track security patches instead of getting them automatically.

Common mistakes

  • Running apt upgrade directly on a live production box instead of baking a new, tested AMI/image — classic outage cause.
  • Forgetting apt update before install and getting "package not found" on a fresh container.

Interview Q

Q: In a Dockerfile, why chain `apt update && apt install -y x && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*` into one RUN line?
A: Docker layers are cached per RUN command. A separate `apt update` layer gets cached and reused even when the package list is stale later, causing "package not found" bugs. Chaining into one RUN also lets you clean the apt cache in the same layer, keeping image size down.

08 Linux Networking Basics

Just enough to debug "can't connect" before we hit the dedicated Networking module.

Core commands

ip addr show                 # interfaces + IPs (replaces old ifconfig)
ip route                     # routing table
ss -tulnp                    # listening ports + owning process (replaces netstat)
curl -v http://localhost:8080/health
ping 8.8.8.8                 # basic reachability (ICMP)
dig example.com               # DNS resolution
traceroute example.com        # hop-by-hop path
nc -zv host 443                # is a specific TCP port open?
tcpdump -i eth0 port 443       # capture packets on that port

/etc files that matter

/etc/hostsstatic hostname→IP mapping, checked before DNS
/etc/resolv.confwhich DNS servers to query
/etc/nsswitch.conforder of lookup sources (files vs dns) for hostnames

Common mistakes

  • Assuming a "connection refused" and a "connection timed out" mean the same failure — refused = something IS listening and actively rejecting (or a firewall sent RST); timeout = nothing responded at all (likely security group/firewall silently dropping, or wrong IP).
  • Not checking ss -tulnp first when debugging "why can't my app be reached" — half the time the app bound to 127.0.0.1 instead of 0.0.0.0.

Interview Q

Q: curl to your app's port hangs forever with no response at all — vs. instantly says "connection refused." What's the difference in root cause?
A: Hang/timeout usually means a firewall/security group is silently dropping packets (no RST sent) or wrong network path. Instant "connection refused" means the packet reached the host but nothing is listening on that port (or it actively rejected).

09 Logging & Monitoring Basics

Where to look, in order, when something's wrong.

Debugging order of operations: 1. systemctl status → is it even running? last exit code? 2. journalctl -u -f → live application logs 3. dmesg / journalctl -k → kernel-level issues (OOM, disk errors) 4. /var/log/syslog or /var/log/messages → system-wide events 5. top / htop / free -h → resource pressure right now 6. df -h → disk full?

Production commands

tail -f /var/log/nginx/access.log
journalctl --since "1 hour ago" -p err   # only error-priority+ logs
df -h                       # disk space per mount
du -sh /var/log/*           # what's eating disk in /var/log
free -h                     # memory usage
vmstat 1 5                  # CPU/memory/IO snapshot every 1s, 5 times
iostat -x 1                 # disk I/O saturation

Common mistake

Letting logs grow unbounded until /var fills up and the whole box grinds to a halt (no space left for anything, including new log writes or temp files). Production boxes need logrotate configured — most distros ship this by default for syslog but not always for custom app logs.

Interview Q

Q: Server suddenly can't write any files and every command fails oddly. First thing to check?
A: df -h — disk full is one of the most common "everything is broken in weird ways" root causes, often from unrotated logs.

10 Storage & Disks

Enough to reason about EBS volumes and container volumes later.

Key concepts

Block deviceRaw storage device, e.g. /dev/xvda (an EBS volume on EC2)
PartitionA logical slice of a block device, e.g. /dev/xvda1
Filesystemext4, xfs — the actual format that organizes files/directories on a partition
MountAttaching a filesystem to a path in the tree, e.g. mounting an EBS volume at /data
inodeMetadata structure per file (permissions, owner, size, pointers to data blocks) — separate from the filename
lsblk                     # list block devices
mount /dev/xvdf /data     # mount a new volume
df -h                     # disk usage per mount, human readable
df -i                     # inode usage (can run out of inodes with disk space still free!)
fdisk -l                  # partition table info
Gotcha: "No space left on device" can happen with plenty of disk space free — if you've exhausted inodes (e.g. millions of tiny files). df -i, not df -h, diagnoses this.

Interview Q

Q: df -h shows 40% free but you still get "No space left on device." Why?
A: Inode exhaustion — the filesystem ran out of inode entries (common with millions of tiny files) even though raw byte capacity remains. Check with df -i.

★ Mini Project

Ties every concept above into one hands-on task.

Goal: Deploy a Java/Spring Boot jar as a properly managed Linux service, non-root, with logging, restart-on-crash, and a health-check debugging runbook.

# 1. create dedicated non-root service user
sudo useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin springapp

# 2. deploy the jar
sudo mkdir -p /opt/springapp
sudo cp app.jar /opt/springapp/
sudo chown -R springapp:springapp /opt/springapp

# 3. systemd unit
sudo tee /etc/systemd/system/springapp.service <<'EOF'
[Unit]
Description=Spring Boot App
After=network.target

[Service]
User=springapp
WorkingDirectory=/opt/springapp
ExecStart=/usr/bin/java -jar /opt/springapp/app.jar
Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=5
StandardOutput=journal
StandardError=journal

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
EOF

# 4. enable + start
sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl enable --now springapp

# 5. verify
systemctl status springapp
journalctl -u springapp -f
ss -tulnp | grep 8080
curl -s localhost:8080/actuator/health

Break it on purpose: kill -9 the java process and confirm systemd auto-restarts it within RestartSec. Fill up /opt with junk files and observe the app fail; diagnose via df -h before blindly restarting.

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Kernel = privileged core; syscalls are the only way user-space touches hardware.
  • Boot: BIOS → GRUB → kernel/initramfs → PID1(systemd) → services. Check /var/log/cloud-init-output.log for EC2 user-data issues.
  • Filesystem: single tree from /. /proc and /sys are virtual (live kernel data, not disk files).
  • Permissions: rwx per owner/group/others. chmod 750-style thinking. Never run app processes as root. docker group = root-equivalent.
  • Process: fork+exec model. SIGTERM (graceful) before SIGKILL (force). Zombie = unreaped exit status; orphan = reparented to PID1. OOM killer via dmesg.
  • systemd: unit files, daemon-reload after edits, journalctl -u for logs, Restart=on-failure for resilience.
  • Package mgmt: apt(Debian)/dnf(RHEL)/apk(Alpine). Never live-upgrade prod; bake new images instead.
  • Networking basics: ss -tulnp for listening ports, timeout vs refused distinction.
  • Debugging order: systemctl status → journalctl → dmesg → syslog → top/free → df -h.
  • Storage: inode exhaustion ≠ disk space exhaustion — check both.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What does chmod 644 grant?
Owner: rw-, Group: r--, Others: r-- (standard for readable config/text files, not executable, not writable by others)
2. Which signal cannot be caught or ignored by a process?
SIGKILL (9) — and SIGSTOP. SIGTERM (15) CAN be caught for graceful shutdown.
3. PID 1 dies unexpectedly on a Linux box. What happens?
Kernel panics / system typically halts or reboots — PID 1 is special and its death is fatal to the whole system (this is why container images should run the real app as PID1 carefully, or use an init like tini for signal handling).
4. Where does systemd store service logs by default?
journald (binary structured log), queried via journalctl -u <service>.
5. `/proc` on disk is:
Not on disk at all — it's a virtual filesystem reflecting live kernel/process state in memory.
6. Difference between hard link and symbolic link?
Hard link = another directory entry pointing to the same inode (same data, survives original deletion). Symlink = a separate file containing a path string, breaks if target moves ("dangling link").
7. `kill -9 ` on a process stuck in D state (uninterruptible sleep, e.g. waiting on NFS/disk) — what happens?
It typically WON'T die — D-state processes can't respond to any signal, including SIGKILL, until the I/O operation completes or times out.
8. Why prefer `ss` over the older `netstat`?
ss reads directly from kernel netlink sockets, faster and more detailed on modern kernels; netstat is largely deprecated/unmaintained on Linux.
9. `df -h` shows plenty of space but writes fail with ENOSPC. Likely cause?
Inode exhaustion — check with df -i.
10. What's the purpose of SUID bit on /usr/bin/passwd?
Lets any user run it with root's privileges temporarily, so they can edit /etc/shadow (root-only writable) to change their own password.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. Spring Boot app on EC2: systemctl status shows "active (running)" but curl to the port fails.
Check ss -tulnp — likely bound to 127.0.0.1 instead of 0.0.0.0; also check security group and any local iptables/firewalld rules.
2. App gets killed randomly every few hours, journalctl shows nothing app-side.
dmesg | grep -i "killed process" — probable OOM killer; check container/service memory limits vs actual usage.
3. Deploy script fails with "Permission denied" despite chmod +x being run.
Check the actual owner/group of the file vs the user running the script (ls -l), and whether the filesystem is mounted noexec (common on /tmp in hardened AMIs).
4. Box becomes completely unresponsive to new SSH sessions, existing sessions still work.
Likely resource exhaustion — check ulimit on max processes/file descriptors, or fork bomb; also check disk full (sshd may fail to write pid/lock files).
5. Log file grew to 40GB overnight and filled the disk, taking down the whole app.
No logrotate configured for the app's custom log path; fix immediately with logrotate config, and as immediate remediation truncate with `: > logfile` (not rm, since the process still holds the fd open on rm and won't free space until process restart).

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Write a bash one-liner to find the top 5 memory-consuming processes using only /proc (no ps/top allowed).
  2. Create a systemd unit for a Python Flask app that restarts on failure, runs as a non-root user, and logs to journald. Prove it survives a kill -9.
  3. Fill a small EBS/disk volume with tiny files until you hit inode exhaustion (not space exhaustion) — reproduce and diagnose with df -i vs df -h.
  4. Write a script that sends SIGTERM to a process, waits 10s, and force SIGKILLs only if it's still alive (mimic what Docker/K8s do on container stop).
  5. Set up logrotate for a custom app log at /opt/myapp/app.log — rotate daily, keep 7, compress old ones.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What is the difference between a process and a thread?
A process has its own isolated memory space; threads within the same process share memory/address space but have their own stack and register state. Context-switching between threads is cheaper than between processes.
Beginner-Intermediate: What does `chmod 750 file` do and when would you use it?
Owner rwx, group r-x, others none. Used for scripts/executables that a service group should be able to run/read but the wider world should have zero access to.
Intermediate: How would you find which process is using a specific port, and what would you do next?
`ss -tulnp | grep :8080` (or lsof -i :8080). Then decide: kill it if it's a stray dev process, or reconfigure your app to a free port / fix the actual conflict source.
Advanced: A production Java service intermittently freezes for a few seconds under load — how do you root-cause it at the OS level?
Check GC pauses first (app-level), but at OS level: vmstat/iostat for I/O wait spikes, check if the process is swapping (free -h, vmstat si/so columns), check /proc/<pid>/status for VmSwap, and strace -c -p <pid> briefly to see syscall time distribution (e.g. excessive futex calls indicating lock contention).
Advanced: Explain what happens at the OS level when Docker "isolates" a container — what Linux primitives make this possible?
Namespaces (PID, network, mount, UTS, IPC, user) provide isolated views of system resources per container; cgroups enforce resource limits (CPU/memory/IO); union filesystems (overlayfs) layer the image filesystem. There's no separate "container kernel" — all containers on a host share one kernel, which is why kernel-level exploits can break container isolation (unlike full VMs).
Module 01 / 30 — Linux. Next up: Bash Scripting, building directly on process management, permissions, and the /proc commands covered here.

01 Shell, Shebang & Execution

Before writing a single line of logic — how does the OS know a text file is a program?

What is it?

A shell is a program that reads commands (interactively or from a file) and asks the kernel to execute them via fork+exec (Module 1). Bash (Bourne Again SHell) is the most common one on Linux. A shebang (#!/bin/bash) is the first line of a script telling the kernel which interpreter to hand the rest of the file to.

Why was it needed?

Without a shebang, the kernel has no way to know if a text file full of commands should be fed to bash, python, or perl. The kernel's execve() syscall reads the first two bytes; if they're #!, it reads the rest of that line as the interpreter path and re-execs that binary with your script file as an argument.

$ ./deploy.sh │ ▼ kernel execve() reads first line: "#!/bin/bash" │ ▼ kernel actually runs: /bin/bash ./deploy.sh

How it works internally

#!/bin/bash              # explicit path — fails if bash isn't at /bin/bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash       # PORTABLE — finds bash wherever it is on $PATH (preferred)

Two ways to run a script: ./script.sh (needs execute permission, uses the shebang) vs bash script.sh (doesn't need execute permission, shebang is ignored — you're explicitly choosing the interpreter).

Production implementation

chmod +x deploy.sh
./deploy.sh                  # uses shebang
bash deploy.sh                # explicit, ignores shebang, no +x needed
set -euo pipefail            # ALWAYS put this near the top — see Error Handling below

Common mistakes

  • Using #!/bin/sh but writing bash-only syntax (arrays, [[ ]]) — sh is often dash on Debian/Ubuntu, not bash, and silently behaves differently or errors.
  • Hardcoding #!/bin/bash on a system where bash lives elsewhere (rare, but env bash avoids the whole class of problem).
  • Forgetting chmod +x and getting "Permission denied" — confusing this with an actual logic error in the script.

Interview Q

Q: Why prefer `#!/usr/bin/env bash` over `#!/bin/bash`?
A: env bash searches $PATH for bash, making the script portable across systems where bash isn't at /bin/bash (e.g. some macOS/Nix setups) — more robust for shared CI scripts.

02 Variables & Quoting

The #1 source of subtle production bash bugs.

What is it / why needed

Bash variables are untyped strings by default. There's no compiler catching type errors — quoting discipline is how you avoid bash treating your data as more commands or splitting it unexpectedly.

How it works internally

Bash performs word splitting and globbing on unquoted variable expansions. $var without quotes gets split on whitespace and any */? gets expanded against filenames in the current directory. This is why "$var" (double-quoted) is the safe default.

name="Asmit Kumar"
echo $name        # BAD: word-splits → prints as two separate args to echo
echo "$name"       # GOOD: prints "Asmit Kumar" as one string

files="*.txt"
echo $files        # BAD: globs — expands to actual filenames in cwd
echo "$files"       # GOOD: prints literal "*.txt"

# Command substitution
today=$(date +%F)          # preferred, modern
today=`date +%F`           # legacy backticks, avoid — doesn't nest cleanly

# Default values
: "${PORT:=8080}"           # set PORT to 8080 only if unset/empty
echo "${NAME:-anonymous}"    # use "anonymous" if NAME unset, without setting it

# Arrays
services=("nginx" "docker" "sshd")
echo "${services[@]}"        # all elements
echo "${#services[@]}"        # count
SyntaxMeaning
'single'Literal — no expansion of variables or $() at all
"double"Expands variables/command substitution, but prevents word-splitting/globbing
$(...)Command substitution — run command, capture stdout
${var:-default}Use default if var unset (doesn't modify var)
${var:=default}Use default AND assign it to var

Common mistakes

  • Not quoting variables holding file paths — breaks instantly on any path with a space (e.g. "Program Files"-style directories on shared/Windows-mounted volumes).
  • Using backticks instead of $() — harder to read and doesn't nest without escaping.
  • Assuming bash variables have types — comparing a "number" string with -eq when it's actually empty, causing a cryptic syntax error at runtime.

Interview Q

Q: What breaks if you write `rm $FILE` instead of `rm "$FILE"` where FILE="my report.txt"?
A: Word splitting turns it into `rm my report.txt` — two separate arguments — so rm tries to delete two files "my" and "report.txt", neither of which exist, instead of the one file you meant.

03 Conditionals & Loops

How it works

# Conditionals — [[ ]] is bash-specific and safer than [ ] (POSIX test)
if [[ -f "$file" ]]; then
  echo "file exists"
elif [[ -d "$file" ]]; then
  echo "it's a directory"
else
  echo "not found"
fi

# String/number comparisons
if [[ "$env" == "prod" ]]; then ...      # string equality
if [[ $count -gt 10 ]]; then ...          # numeric comparison (-gt -lt -eq -ne -ge -le)

# Loops
for service in nginx docker sshd; do
  systemctl status "$service"
done

for f in /var/log/*.log; do
  echo "processing $f"
done

i=0
while [[ $i -lt 5 ]]; do
  echo "$i"
  ((i++))
done

until curl -sf http://localhost:8080/health; do
  echo "waiting for app..."
  sleep 2
done
TestMeaning
-fregular file exists
-ddirectory exists
-xfile is executable
-zstring is empty
-nstring is non-empty

Common mistakes

  • Using [ ] (old POSIX test) and forgetting spaces around brackets — [$x -eq 1] fails silently/cryptically vs [ $x -eq 1 ].
  • Using == for numeric comparison instead of -eq — works by accident sometimes, breaks on negative numbers or leading zeros.

Interview Q

Q: Difference between [ ] and [[ ]] in bash?
A: [ ] is the POSIX test builtin — word-splitting and globbing still apply to its arguments, so unquoted variables can break it. [[ ]] is a bash keyword — safer, supports pattern matching (==, =~ for regex), and doesn't word-split its arguments.

04 Exit Codes & Error Handling

The difference between a script that "usually works" and one you can trust in a CI pipeline.

What is it / why needed

Every command returns an exit code (0–255): 0 = success, anything else = failure (specific non-zero meanings vary by tool). By default, bash keeps running even after a command fails — which silently masks real problems in automation scripts. This is exactly the kind of bug that passes locally and blows up in a pipeline.

How it works internally

ls /nonexistent
echo $?              # prints the exit code of the PREVIOUS command (2, here)

set -e     # exit immediately if any command exits non-zero
set -u     # error on use of an undefined variable (catches typos)
set -o pipefail   # a pipeline's exit code = the LAST non-zero command in it, not just the last command

set -euo pipefail   # the standard production-safe header for almost every script
Why pipefail matters: without it, false | true exits 0 (success!) because bash only looks at the last command's (true's) exit code by default — hiding the fact that `false` failed upstream in the pipe.

Production pattern

#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

deploy() {
  echo "Deploying..."
  if ! curl -sf http://localhost:8080/health; then
    echo "Health check failed" >&2
    exit 1
  fi
}

deploy || { echo "Deploy failed, rolling back" >&2; ./rollback.sh; exit 1; }

Trade-offs

  • set -e has known gotchas: it does NOT trigger inside a condition (if mycmd; then), inside &&/|| chains, or inside a function called as part of a condition. Interviewers love probing this exact nuance.

Common mistakes

  • Not using set -euo pipefail at all — script "works" until a step silently fails and later steps run on bad state (classic CI/CD deploy-script bug).
  • Checking $? after a compound command instead of immediately after the command whose result you care about — some other command overwrote it.

Interview Q

Q: You have `set -e` at the top of your script, but a command that fails still doesn't stop the script. Why?
A: set -e doesn't apply inside if/while conditions, inside && / || chains, or to commands whose exit status is otherwise being checked/consumed — bash assumes you're intentionally handling the failure in those contexts.

05 Functions & Arguments

#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

deploy_service() {
  local service_name="$1"     # local = scoped to function, don't leak into global namespace
  local env="${2:-staging}"    # default arg if not passed

  echo "Deploying $service_name to $env"
}

deploy_service "auth-service" "prod"

echo "Script name: $0"
echo "All args: $@"
echo "Arg count: $#"

# Positional args in a script itself
# ./deploy.sh myservice prod
service="$1"
environment="${2:-staging}"

Common mistakes

  • Forgetting local — a variable inside a function silently overwrites a global with the same name, causing bugs that only show up when functions are combined.
  • Confusing $@ and $* — with "$@" each argument stays a separate quoted word; "$*" joins them all into a single string. Matters when args contain spaces.

Interview Q

Q: What's the difference between "$@" and "$*" inside a function?
A: "$@" expands to each positional argument as a separate quoted string (preserves argument boundaries). "$*" expands to all arguments joined into ONE string. For loops that need to preserve args with spaces intact, always use "$@".

06 Text Processing — grep, sed, awk

The actual daily-driver tools for log parsing, config templating, and CI scripts.

grep — search

grep "ERROR" app.log                  # lines containing ERROR
grep -i "error" app.log                # case-insensitive
grep -v "DEBUG" app.log                # invert match — exclude DEBUG lines
grep -c "ERROR" app.log                # count matches
grep -r "TODO" ./src                    # recursive through directory
grep -A 3 -B 1 "Exception" app.log       # 3 lines after, 1 before each match
grep -E "error|fail|exception" app.log   # extended regex, multiple patterns

sed — stream editor (find & replace)

sed 's/staging/production/' config.yml           # replace first match per line
sed 's/staging/production/g' config.yml           # replace ALL matches per line
sed -i 's/PORT=8080/PORT=9090/' .env               # -i = edit file IN PLACE
sed -n '10,20p' app.log                             # print only lines 10-20
sed '/^#/d' config.yml                               # delete comment lines

awk — column-based processing

awk '{print $1}' access.log                 # print first column (field)
awk -F',' '{print $2}' data.csv               # custom delimiter
awk '{sum += $3} END {print sum}' data.txt     # sum a column
awk '$9 == 500 {print $0}' access.log          # print full lines where col 9 == 500
ps aux | awk '{print $4, $11}' | sort -rn | head   # mem% + command, sorted
Real production one-liner: top 5 IPs hitting your nginx access log awk '{print $1}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -5 │ │ │ │ │ │ get IP col sort for count sort by top 5 uniq to work dupes count desc

Common mistakes

  • Using sed -i without a backup suffix on production config and getting no way to diff/revert (sed -i.bak keeps the original).
  • Reaching for a Python script when a one-line awk pipeline would do it faster — not wrong, but slower to write and worse for a live incident where seconds matter.
  • Forgetting grep's regex is BRE by default (basic) — + and | need -E or escaping, a frequent "why doesn't my pattern match" bug.

Interview Q

Q: Write a one-liner to find the 5 most frequent error messages in a log file.
A: grep "ERROR" app.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -5

07 Redirection, Pipes & Streams

What is it / how it works

Every process starts with 3 open file descriptors: stdin (0), stdout (1), stderr (2). Redirection and pipes just rewire these to files or other processes' descriptors — this is a kernel-level concept (file descriptor table per process), not something magic to bash.

command > out.txt          # stdout → file (overwrite)
command >> out.txt          # stdout → file (append)
command 2> err.txt           # stderr → file
command > out.txt 2>&1        # BOTH stdout and stderr → same file (order matters! 2>&1 AFTER redirecting stdout)
command &> all.txt             # bash shortcut for the above
command < input.txt             # stdin from a file
command1 | command2              # stdout of cmd1 becomes stdin of cmd2

# Common real pattern: log everything, keep it running in background
nohup ./long_task.sh > task.log 2>&1 &
echo $!    # PID of the backgrounded process, saved for later kill/monitoring
Gotcha: command 2>&1 > out.txt is WRONG order — stderr gets duplicated to the terminal (wherever stdout was pointing at that moment, before the redirect), not the file. Always redirect stdout first, then duplicate stderr onto it: > out.txt 2>&1.

Common mistakes

  • Getting the 2>&1 ordering wrong (above) and losing error logs in production incident debugging.
  • Using & to background a process without nohup, then having it die the moment the SSH session closes (SIGHUP).

Interview Q

Q: Why does `cmd > file 2>&1` work but `cmd 2>&1 > file` doesn't send stderr to the file?
A: Redirections are processed left to right. In the working version, stdout is pointed at file FIRST, then stderr is duplicated to wherever stdout currently points (the file). In the broken version, stderr is duplicated to wherever stdout points AT THAT MOMENT (the terminal), and only afterward is stdout redirected to file — stderr keeps going to the terminal.

08 Traps & Signal Handling

Directly builds on Module 1's SIGTERM/SIGKILL — now from the script author's side.

What is it / why needed

A script can catch signals (like SIGTERM, SIGINT from Ctrl+C, or the script's own EXIT) and run cleanup code — critical for deploy scripts that create temp files, hold locks, or need to clean up a partially-completed deployment if interrupted.

#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

LOCKFILE="/tmp/deploy.lock"

cleanup() {
  echo "Cleaning up..."
  rm -f "$LOCKFILE"
}
trap cleanup EXIT              # runs on ANY script exit — normal, error, or signal
trap 'echo "Interrupted!"; exit 130' INT   # Ctrl+C specifically

touch "$LOCKFILE"
echo "Doing risky deploy work..."
sleep 30

Common mistakes

  • Not using trap ... EXIT for cleanup — a script killed mid-way leaves lockfiles/temp files behind, breaking the next run.
  • Assuming trap handlers run for SIGKILL — they don't; SIGKILL bypasses all userspace handling entirely (kernel-enforced, per Module 1).

Interview Q

Q: Why won't a `trap` handler run if the process receives SIGKILL?
A: SIGKILL is handled entirely by the kernel and cannot be caught, blocked, or ignored by the process — there's no opportunity for any userspace trap/signal handler to run.

★ Mini Project

A production-style deploy script tying every concept above together.

#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

APP_DIR="/opt/springapp"
SERVICE="springapp"
HEALTH_URL="http://localhost:8080/actuator/health"
LOCKFILE="/tmp/${SERVICE}.lock"
LOG="/var/log/${SERVICE}-deploy.log"

cleanup() { rm -f "$LOCKFILE"; }
trap cleanup EXIT

log() { echo "[$(date +'%F %T')] $*" | tee -a "$LOG"; }

if [[ -f "$LOCKFILE" ]]; then
  log "Another deploy already in progress. Exiting."
  exit 1
fi
touch "$LOCKFILE"

deploy() {
  local jar_path="$1"
  log "Deploying $jar_path"
  sudo cp "$jar_path" "${APP_DIR}/app.jar"
  sudo systemctl restart "$SERVICE"
}

wait_for_health() {
  local retries=10
  until curl -sf "$HEALTH_URL" > /dev/null; do
    ((retries--)) || { log "Health check failed after retries. Rolling back."; return 1; }
    log "Waiting for app to become healthy... ($retries left)"
    sleep 3
  done
  log "App healthy."
}

main() {
  [[ $# -eq 1 ]] || { log "Usage: $0 "; exit 1; }
  deploy "$1"
  if ! wait_for_health; then
    sudo systemctl restart "$SERVICE"   # naive rollback trigger
    exit 1
  fi
  log "Deploy successful."
}

main "$@"

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Shebang: #!/usr/bin/env bash preferred over hardcoded path.
  • Quoting: always "$var". Unquoted = word-splitting + globbing risk.
  • set -euo pipefail: standard header. Know its exceptions (if/while conditions, && / ||).
  • [[ ]] over [ ]: safer, no word-splitting, supports regex (=~).
  • local in every function var — avoid leaking into global scope.
  • grep/sed/awk: search / find-replace / column-processing. sort | uniq -c | sort -rn = frequency count pattern.
  • Redirection order matters: > file 2>&1, not the reverse.
  • trap EXIT for guaranteed cleanup (lockfiles, temp files) — but SIGKILL bypasses traps entirely.
  • nohup cmd & + $! to background and track a long-running process safely across SSH disconnects.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What does `set -u` protect against?
Using an undefined/unset variable — script errors out immediately instead of silently treating it as empty string.
2. `cmd1 | cmd2` — what's actually connected?
cmd1's stdout file descriptor is connected directly to cmd2's stdin file descriptor via a kernel pipe — no temp file involved.
3. What's wrong with `if [ $x == "yes" ]` if $x is unset?
Expands to `[ == "yes" ]` — malformed syntax, throws an error. Should quote: `[ "$x" == "yes" ]`.
4. Difference between `$(cmd)` and `` `cmd` ``?
Functionally similar (command substitution) but $() nests cleanly and is more readable; backticks require escaping to nest and are considered legacy.
5. What does `2>/dev/null` do?
Discards stderr output entirely — sends it to the null device, commonly used to suppress expected/noisy error messages.
6. In `awk -F',' '{print $2}'`, what does -F do?
Sets the field separator to comma instead of the default whitespace — needed for CSV-style data.
7. What does `local` do inside a bash function?
Scopes the variable to that function only, preventing it from overwriting or leaking into the global shell namespace.
8. Why is pipefail important with `set -e`?
Without pipefail, a pipeline's exit status is only that of the LAST command — an earlier failing command in the pipe is silently ignored, so set -e won't trigger on it.
9. What does `trap cleanup EXIT` guarantee?
cleanup() runs when the script exits for ANY reason — normal completion, error exit, or most signals — except SIGKILL.
10. What's the risk of `rm -rf $DIR` if $DIR is accidentally empty/unset?
Expands to `rm -rf` with no path in some shells' behavior, or worse depending on context — with set -u this is caught before execution since DIR would be flagged as unset.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. A deploy script "works" locally but silently does nothing in CI.
Almost certainly missing set -euo pipefail — an early command failed and the rest ran on bad assumptions without ever surfacing an error, or CI's shell isn't bash (dash/sh) and bash-only syntax silently misbehaves.
2. A log-parsing script crashes with "unbound variable" only in production, never in dev.
set -u is catching a variable that's set via an environment file in dev but never gets sourced/exported in the prod deploy path — check env var loading order.
3. `rm $TEMP_FILE` in a cleanup trap deletes way more than expected.
TEMP_FILE likely contains a glob pattern or unquoted path with spaces that got word-split/expanded — always quote and always use realpath/mktemp for temp files, never string-build paths.
4. A background health-check loop (`nohup ... &`) stops when you close your SSH session.
nohup alone doesn't fully detach from the session in all cases — check it's truly disowned (`disown`) or better, run it as a proper systemd service/timer instead of a manually backgrounded shell job.
5. A script that greps a log file for "ERROR" misses lines that clearly contain errors.
Check case sensitivity (add -i), check if it's actually "Error" or "ERR" in the real log format, and check if grep's default BRE regex is silently failing to match an extended-regex pattern (needs -E).

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Write a script that tails a log file and sends a desktop/console alert the moment it sees "ERROR" or "Exception" (using tail -f + grep --line-buffered).
  2. Write a health-check retry loop with exponential backoff (not fixed sleep) that gives up after 5 attempts and exits non-zero.
  3. Write a script using awk to parse an nginx access log and print requests-per-status-code counts.
  4. Write a deploy script with a trap-based lockfile so two instances can never run concurrently — test it by launching two at once.
  5. Write a function that takes a service name and environment as args, validates both against an allowed list, and errors clearly if invalid (practice arg validation + local scoping).

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What's the difference between running a script as `./script.sh` vs `bash script.sh`?
./script.sh requires execute permission and uses the shebang line to pick the interpreter. bash script.sh explicitly invokes bash regardless of permissions or shebang — the shebang is ignored.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why should you always quote variables in bash?
To prevent word-splitting (unquoted variables with spaces get split into multiple arguments) and globbing (unquoted values containing *, ? get expanded against real filenames) — both cause silent, hard-to-debug failures.
Intermediate: What does `set -euo pipefail` do and why is it considered best practice at the top of production scripts?
-e exits on any command failure, -u errors on unset variables (catches typos), -o pipefail makes a pipeline fail if ANY command in it fails, not just the last one. Together they turn "silent partial failures" into loud immediate ones — critical for scripts that run unattended in CI/CD.
Intermediate-Advanced: How would you make a long-running background script survive an SSH session disconnect, and why does it disconnect in the first place?
SSH disconnect sends SIGHUP to child processes by default; nohup ignores SIGHUP, and `disown` removes it from the shell's job table so it's not tied to that session at all. In production, prefer running it as a systemd service instead of a manually backgrounded job — proper logging, restart policy, and lifecycle management.
Advanced: Design a deploy script that's safe to re-run if it crashes halfway (idempotent) — what would you need?
A lockfile (trap-based cleanup) to prevent concurrent runs, health-check verification after each risky step before proceeding, state tracking (e.g. a status file marking which step last completed) so a re-run can resume or safely redo a step without side effects, and explicit rollback logic triggered on failure rather than assuming manual intervention.

Git & GitHub, from first principles to production

Not "commit, push, pull" — the actual object model underneath, so rebase/merge/reflog stop being magic incantations and become predictable operations on a graph.
