01 Public-Key Cryptography Basics

What is it / why needed

Before SSH, remote login (telnet, rsh) sent passwords in cleartext over the network — trivially sniffable. SSH needed a way to (a) encrypt the whole session and (b) authenticate without ever transmitting a secret that could be intercepted and reused.

How it works internally

A key pair = a private key (never leaves your machine, kept secret) and a public key (safe to share anywhere, even publicly). Data encrypted with the public key can only be decrypted with the matching private key — and critically, a signature made with the private key can be verified by anyone holding the public key, without ever exposing the private key itself.

Authentication proof, conceptually: Server: "Prove you hold the private key matching this public key I have on file." Server sends a challenge (random data) encrypted with your public key. Client: decrypts it with the private key (only possible if you actually have it), sends proof back. Server: verifies the proof matches → authenticated, WITHOUT the private key ever leaving your machine.

Common mistakes

  • Confusing "encrypt with public key" (anyone can encrypt, only private key holder can decrypt) with "sign with private key" (only the key holder can produce it, anyone can verify) — these are two different use patterns of the same key pair, both used within SSH/TLS.
  • Emailing or Slack-sharing a private key "just for convenience" — instantly compromises the identity that key represents; must be treated like a password, never transmitted.

Interview Q

Q: Why is public-key auth considered more secure than password auth for SSH?
A: The secret (private key) never travels over the network at all, even in encrypted form — only proof of possession is exchanged. Passwords, even sent over an encrypted channel, still require the actual secret to be transmitted and are vulnerable to brute-forcing, phishing, and reuse across services. Private keys are also typically much higher entropy than human-chosen passwords.

02 SSH Handshake & Host Keys

Client Server │── TCP handshake (Module 4) on port 22 ──────►│ │── protocol version negotiation ────────────────►│ │◄── Server's HOST KEY presented ─────────────────│ "I am this server, here's proof" │ (client checks against ~/.ssh/known_hosts) │ │── key exchange → symmetric session key derived ───►│ (same asymmetric-then-symmetric pattern as TLS) │◄══ encrypted channel established ══════════════►│ │── client authenticates (key pair or password) ────►│ │◄══════════ interactive shell session ═══════════►│

Host keys vs user keys — a critical distinction

Host keyUser key
Belongs toThe SERVER — proves server identity to the clientThe USER — proves client identity to the server
StoredServer: /etc/ssh/ssh_host_*_key. Client caches server's public host key in ~/.ssh/known_hostsClient: ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 (private, never shared). Server: ~/.ssh/authorized_keys (public keys allowed to log in)
"Host key verification failed" — never blindly bypass this. It means the server's presented host key doesn't match what's cached in your known_hosts — either the server was legitimately rebuilt/reinstalled (common after replacing an EC2 instance), OR you're being man-in-the-middled. Verify out-of-band (check the new instance's actual host key fingerprint via AWS console/docs) before removing the old entry and accepting the new one — don't reflexively `ssh-keygen -R` and reconnect without checking why it changed.

Common mistakes

  • Automatically accepting "yes" to unknown host key prompts in scripts (StrictHostKeyChecking=no) in production automation — defeats the entire point of host verification, opens door to MITM.
  • Not realizing an EC2 instance replacement (new instance, same IP via Elastic IP) will have a DIFFERENT host key than the old instance — expected, not a security incident, but still worth verifying.

Interview Q

Q: What's the difference between a host key and a user key in SSH?
A: A host key belongs to the SERVER and proves the server's identity to connecting clients (checked against the client's known_hosts). A user key belongs to the CLIENT/person and proves their identity to the server (the private key stays with the user, the public key is placed in the server's authorized_keys).

03 Key-Based Auth Setup

# Generate a key pair (ed25519 is modern default, faster & more secure than old RSA)
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "asmit@laptop" -f ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
# creates: ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 (PRIVATE, chmod 600, never share)
#          ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub (PUBLIC, safe to share/copy to servers)

# Copy public key to a server's authorized_keys (adds without overwriting existing entries)
ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub user@server-ip

# Manual equivalent (what ssh-copy-id does under the hood)
cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub | ssh user@server-ip "mkdir -p ~/.ssh && cat >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys"

# Connect
ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 user@server-ip

# Correct permissions (SSH refuses to use keys/dirs with overly permissive perms)
chmod 700 ~/.ssh
chmod 600 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
chmod 644 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub
chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
Permission strictness is a real feature, not pedantry: SSH refuses to use a private key file or authorized_keys if permissions are too open (e.g. world-readable) — this prevents other local users on a shared machine from reading your private key or tampering with your authorized_keys list.

Common mistakes

  • "Permission denied (publickey)" and immediately assuming the key is wrong — usually a permissions issue (600 on private key, 700 on .ssh dir) instead.
  • Reusing the same key pair across every server/service instead of generating per-purpose keys — makes revocation and audit trails much harder later.

Interview Q

Q: You get "Permission denied (publickey)" connecting to a server you're sure has your key in authorized_keys. First things to check?
A: File permissions — ~/.ssh must be 700, authorized_keys must be 600 on the server; also check the private key's permissions locally (600), verify you're specifying the correct key with -i if not using the default, and check sshd_config on the server actually allows PubkeyAuthentication.

04 SSH Config, Agent, Tunneling

~/.ssh/config — stop typing long ssh commands

Host prod-app
    HostName 203.0.113.5
    User ec2-user
    IdentityFile ~/.ssh/prod_key.pem
    Port 22

Host bastion
    HostName 198.51.100.10
    User ec2-user
    IdentityFile ~/.ssh/bastion_key.pem

Host private-app
    HostName 10.0.1.20
    User ec2-user
    ProxyJump bastion    # tunnel through bastion automatically — see jump host pattern below
ssh prod-app          # now this just works, no long flags needed

SSH agent — avoid re-entering passphrase constantly

eval "$(ssh-agent -s)"       # start agent for this session
ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_ed25519      # load key into agent (unlocks passphrase once)
ssh-add -l                       # list loaded keys

Bastion/jump host pattern — the production standard for private subnets

Your laptop ──SSH──► Bastion host (public subnet, only this has a public IP) │ └──SSH──► App server (private subnet, NO public IP, unreachable directly) ProxyJump makes this a single transparent hop from your perspective: ssh -J bastion-user@bastion-ip app-user@private-app-ip

This is exactly why production VPCs (covered in Module 15) put app servers in private subnets with no public IP at all — the bastion (or AWS Systems Manager Session Manager, the more modern replacement) is the only front door, drastically shrinking the attack surface.

Port forwarding / tunneling

# Local forward: access a remote-only service (e.g. a DB only reachable from inside the VPC) via your local port
ssh -L 5432:localhost:5432 user@bastion-ip
# now localhost:5432 on YOUR machine tunnels through the bastion to the DB

# Dynamic (SOCKS proxy) — route arbitrary traffic through the SSH tunnel
ssh -D 1080 user@bastion-ip

Common mistakes

  • Giving app servers public IPs "just for easy SSH access" instead of using a bastion/Session Manager — needlessly expands attack surface.
  • Not using ProxyJump/agent forwarding, instead copying private keys onto the bastion host itself — the bastion becoming compromised then exposes every downstream key.

Interview Q

Q: Why use a bastion host instead of giving every app server a public IP for SSH access?
A: Minimizes attack surface — only one hardened, tightly monitored host is internet-facing, while all actual app servers stay in private subnets, unreachable directly from the internet even if their SSH credentials were somehow compromised. It centralizes access logging/auditing too. (AWS Systems Manager Session Manager is the increasingly preferred modern alternative — no bastion, no open port 22 at all, IAM-based access instead.)

05 Production Hardening

# /etc/ssh/sshd_config — production hardening baseline
PermitRootLogin no                  # never allow direct root SSH login
PasswordAuthentication no             # keys only, no password brute-forcing surface
PubkeyAuthentication yes
Port 2222                               # security-through-obscurity, minor deterrent only, not real protection
AllowUsers deploy ec2-user                # explicit allowlist of who can even attempt login
ClientAliveInterval 300                     # kick idle/dead connections
MaxAuthTries 3                                # limit brute-force attempts per connection

# apply
sudo systemctl reload sshd

Trade-offs

  • Disabling password auth entirely is standard in production but means losing your only key = losing access (mitigated by having break-glass access via cloud console/Session Manager, not by keeping password auth enabled).
  • Changing the SSH port reduces automated scanner noise but is NOT real security — don't rely on it alone, and remember to update security groups/firewalls accordingly.

Common mistakes

  • Leaving PermitRootLogin enabled — every automated brute-force bot on the internet targets root first.
  • No fail2ban or equivalent — unauthenticated SSH ports get constant scanning/brute-force attempts; rate-limiting/banning repeat offenders is a real production necessity.

Interview Q

Q: What's the single most important sshd_config change for production hardening, and why?
A: Disabling password authentication (PasswordAuthentication no) in favor of key-only auth — eliminates the entire class of brute-force/credential-stuffing attacks against SSH, which is the most commonly automated attack vector against any internet-facing SSH port. PermitRootLogin no is a close second, removing the most commonly targeted account entirely.

★ Mini Project

Simulate a real bastion + private-subnet SSH setup (can be done with 2 local VMs/containers or 2 EC2 instances).

# 1. Generate keys
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -f ~/.ssh/bastion_key -C "bastion access"
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -f ~/.ssh/app_key -C "app server access"

# 2. Configure ~/.ssh/config for one-hop transparent access
cat >> ~/.ssh/config <<'EOF'
Host bastion
    HostName 
    User ec2-user
    IdentityFile ~/.ssh/bastion_key

Host app-server
    HostName 
    User ec2-user
    IdentityFile ~/.ssh/app_key
    ProxyJump bastion
EOF

# 3. Connect through the jump host transparently
ssh app-server

# 4. Harden the bastion
# edit /etc/ssh/sshd_config on bastion: PermitRootLogin no, PasswordAuthentication no
sudo systemctl reload sshd

# 5. Tunnel a "private" database port through the bastion to your laptop
ssh -L 5432:app-private-ip:5432 bastion
psql -h localhost -p 5432 -U appuser mydb   # connects as if DB were local

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Key pair: private (secret, never shared) + public (shareable). Signature-based proof of possession, private key never travels the network.
  • Host key = proves SERVER identity (client checks known_hosts). User key = proves USER identity (server checks authorized_keys).
  • "Host key verification failed" — investigate before bypassing; could be legit rebuild or a real MITM.
  • Permission strictness is enforced by SSH itself: .ssh=700, private key=600, authorized_keys=600.
  • ProxyJump / bastion pattern: private subnet servers have no public IP; bastion is the sole front door (or use AWS Session Manager instead entirely).
  • Local port forwarding (-L) tunnels a remote-only service to your local machine.
  • Hardening baseline: PermitRootLogin no, PasswordAuthentication no, AllowUsers allowlist, MaxAuthTries limit.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What file on the server holds the public keys allowed to authenticate as a given user?
~/.ssh/authorized_keys
2. What does the client check the server's host key against?
~/.ssh/known_hosts
3. Correct permission mode for a private key file?
600 (owner read/write only).
4. What flag creates a local port forward through an SSH tunnel?
-L (e.g. ssh -L 5432:remotehost:5432 user@jumphost)
5. What does ProxyJump (-J) accomplish?
Transparently tunnels an SSH connection through an intermediate jump/bastion host to reach a private-network destination.
6. Should PermitRootLogin be enabled in production?
No — should be disabled to remove the most commonly brute-forced account entirely.
7. Modern recommended key type over older RSA?
ed25519 — smaller, faster, and considered at least as secure.
8. What's the AWS-native alternative to a bastion host for private-subnet access?
AWS Systems Manager Session Manager — IAM-based access, no open port 22, no bastion needed.
9. Should you disable PasswordAuthentication if you rely only on keys?
Yes — eliminates brute-force/credential-stuffing attack surface against SSH entirely.
10. Does changing SSH's default port (2222 instead of 22) provide real security?
Only minor obscurity/reduced scanner noise — not real security; must be combined with actual hardening (key-only auth, fail2ban, etc).

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. "Permission denied (publickey)" despite the correct key being specified with -i.
Check local key/dir permissions (600/700), check the public key is actually present in the server's authorized_keys, and check sshd_config allows PubkeyAuthentication and hasn't restricted the user via AllowUsers.
2. "REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED" warning appears.
Server's host key doesn't match your cached known_hosts entry — verify out-of-band whether the server was legitimately rebuilt/replaced before removing the old entry (ssh-keygen -R) and reconnecting; don't blindly accept.
3. SSH connection hangs indefinitely instead of connecting or refusing.
Per Module 4's timeout-vs-refused distinction — likely a security group/firewall silently dropping port 22 traffic, or wrong IP/route; check with nc -zv host 22.
4. ProxyJump through a bastion fails even though direct SSH to the bastion works fine.
Check that the target's key is actually loaded (ssh-add -l, or agent forwarding enabled with -A / ForwardAgent yes) since the bastion needs to relay auth to the final destination, and confirm the bastion has network access (security group) to reach the private target on port 22.
5. SSH works fine but is extremely slow to even show a password/key prompt.
Very commonly a reverse DNS lookup delay on the server (sshd trying to resolve your client's IP) — check/disable UseDNS in sshd_config, a well-known and often-forgotten slow-SSH cause.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Generate an ed25519 key pair, deploy it to a test VM/container, and connect with password auth fully disabled.
  2. Set up a 2-hop bastion + private-server topology and connect transparently via ~/.ssh/config with ProxyJump.
  3. Set up local port forwarding to reach a database that's only listening on a private-subnet server, from your laptop.
  4. Harden an sshd_config from scratch (root login off, password auth off, MaxAuthTries, AllowUsers) and verify each change actually takes effect.
  5. Simulate and correctly resolve a "host key changed" warning by rebuilding a test VM and reconnecting deliberately.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What's the difference between a public key and a private key in SSH?
The private key is secret and stays only on your machine, used to prove your identity. The public key can be freely shared and is placed on servers you want to access — it lets the server verify proofs made with the matching private key, without ever needing the private key itself.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why does SSH warn you the first time you connect to a new server, and what does "yes" actually mean when you accept it?
SSH has no prior record of that server's host key, so it can't yet verify it's really who it claims to be — accepting means "I trust this is the right server" and caches its host key in known_hosts for future verification. Accepting blindly (especially in production) opens the door to a man-in-the-middle attack if you can't otherwise verify the fingerprint.
Intermediate: Design a secure SSH access pattern for a fleet of EC2 instances in private subnets, for a small team.
No public IPs on app instances at all. Use a bastion host (hardened, key-only auth, minimal software, ideally auto-scaled/ephemeral) OR preferably AWS Systems Manager Session Manager (IAM-based, fully audited, no open port 22 anywhere, no bastion to maintain/patch). Per-user SSH keys (not shared team keys) for individual accountability and easy revocation. CloudTrail/audit logging of all access.
Intermediate-Advanced: Why is agent forwarding (`ssh -A`) considered risky, and what's the safer alternative for multi-hop access?
Agent forwarding exposes your loaded private key's signing capability to whatever you connect through — if that intermediate host (e.g. bastion) is compromised, an attacker there can potentially use your forwarded agent to authenticate elsewhere as you, for the duration of your session. ProxyJump (-J) is safer for straightforward jump-host scenarios since it tunnels the connection through the intermediate host without exposing your agent to it at all — the intermediate host just relays encrypted bytes, never touches your keys.
Advanced: A production incident: an engineer's laptop with SSH keys to prod was stolen. Walk through full incident response from an SSH perspective.
Immediately remove that engineer's public key from every server's authorized_keys (or, if using centralized auth like an IAM-integrated SSH CA/Session Manager, revoke their access centrally in one place instead of per-server). Rotate any shared/team keys if they had access to those too. Check auth logs (journalctl / /var/log/auth.log) across the fleet for any successful logins using that key since the theft. Rebuild/re-image any host where suspicious activity is found rather than trusting it's clean. Long-term: this incident is a strong argument for moving off static per-host keys entirely toward short-lived, centrally-issued credentials (Session Manager, SSH certificates with short TTL) precisely so a stolen laptop's access expires quickly and doesn't require per-server manual revocation.
Module 07 / 30 — SSH. Next up: Reverse Proxy (Nginx) — routing, load balancing, and TLS termination in front of your app servers.
DevOps / SDE-1 Interview Track — Module 08 / 30

Reverse Proxy (Nginx), from first principles to production

The traffic cop sitting in front of your app servers. Everything here maps almost 1:1 onto AWS ALB later — understanding Nginx by hand makes ALB feel like "the same thing, managed."

01 Forward vs Reverse Proxy

What is it / why needed

A proxy sits between a client and a server, forwarding requests. The direction of "who it's hiding" determines the type.

FORWARD PROXY (hides the CLIENT from the server): Many clients ──► Forward proxy ──► Internet/server (server sees the proxy's IP, not the real client's — e.g. corporate proxy, VPN) REVERSE PROXY (hides the SERVER(s) from the client): Client ──► Reverse proxy (nginx) ──► one of many backend servers (client only ever sees the proxy's address, never knows/cares which real backend served it)

Reverse proxies solve: load distribution across multiple backend instances, hiding internal topology/IPs, centralizing TLS termination, and providing a single point for caching/rate-limiting/compression before requests ever reach app servers.

Common mistakes

  • Confusing which direction is which in interviews — the trick: forward proxy protects/represents the CLIENT side, reverse proxy protects/represents the SERVER side.

Interview Q

Q: Is a corporate web filter a forward or reverse proxy? What about Nginx sitting in front of your API servers?
A: Corporate web filter = forward proxy (represents/controls the client side accessing the internet). Nginx in front of API servers = reverse proxy (represents/protects the server side from direct client exposure).

02 Nginx Architecture

How it works internally

Master process (root, reads config, manages workers, doesn't handle traffic itself) │ ├── Worker process 1 (event-driven, non-blocking, handles thousands of connections) ├── Worker process 2 └── Worker process N (typically = number of CPU cores) Each worker uses an event loop (epoll on Linux) to handle many connections on ONE thread — this is WHY nginx scales to tens of thousands of concurrent connections with low memory, unlike a thread-per-connection model (e.g. old Apache prefork) which gets expensive fast.

This event-driven, non-blocking architecture is the single biggest reason Nginx replaced Apache as the default choice for high-concurrency reverse proxying — a thread-per-connection server pays real OS overhead (memory + context switching) per connection; Nginx's workers multiplex many connections cheaply via the OS's epoll/kqueue mechanism.

Common mistakes

  • Setting worker_processes far higher than actual CPU cores — doesn't help throughput and adds unnecessary context-switch overhead; auto (matches core count) is usually correct.

Interview Q

Q: Why can Nginx handle far more concurrent connections per unit of memory than a naive thread-per-connection server?
A: Nginx workers use an event-driven, non-blocking model (epoll on Linux) — one worker thread can manage thousands of connections simultaneously by reacting to I/O readiness events, rather than dedicating a full OS thread (with its own stack/memory overhead and context-switching cost) to each individual connection.

03 Config: Server Blocks & Locations

# /etc/nginx/nginx.conf structure
http {
    include /etc/nginx/conf.d/*.conf;   # typically one server block per site/domain
}

# /etc/nginx/conf.d/myapp.conf
server {
    listen 80;
    server_name api.example.com;

    location /health {
        return 200 'OK';                # simple static health check, doesn't hit backend
        add_header Content-Type text/plain;
    }

    location /static/ {
        root /var/www/myapp;              # serve files directly, no backend involved — fast
        expires 30d;
    }

    location / {
        proxy_pass http://backend_pool;      # forward everything else to app servers
        proxy_set_header Host $host;            # preserve original Host header
        proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;  # pass real client IP to backend (Module 5's X-Forwarded-For pattern)
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
    }
}
Why proxy_set_header matters so much: without it, your backend app sees every request as coming from Nginx's IP (127.0.0.1 or the proxy's internal IP), breaking IP-based rate limiting, geo features, and audit logs at the application layer. This is the exact same X-Forwarded-For concern from Module 5, now from the config side that sets it.

Common mistakes

  • Forgetting proxy_set_header entirely — backend logs show every request "from" the Nginx box, useless for debugging or abuse detection.
  • Serving static files through the app server (Spring Boot/Node) instead of directly via Nginx's location block — wastes app server resources on something Nginx does far more efficiently.

Interview Q

Q: Why do you configure Nginx to serve static files directly instead of proxying them to your app server?
A: Nginx serving files directly from disk is dramatically more efficient (no app-layer overhead, no framework routing, no business logic execution) than routing every image/CSS/JS request through your application process — freeing app server capacity for actual dynamic request handling.

04 Load Balancing

upstream backend_pool {
    least_conn;                       # algorithm: send to backend with fewest active connections
    server 10.0.1.10:8080 weight=3;      # gets 3x traffic relative to others
    server 10.0.1.11:8080;
    server 10.0.1.12:8080 backup;         # only used if all primary servers are down
}
AlgorithmBehavior
round_robin (default)Cycles through servers in order, evenly
least_connSends to whichever backend currently has fewest active connections — better for uneven request durations
ip_hashSame client IP always routed to same backend — a simple form of session affinity/"sticky sessions"

Health checks (passive, in open-source Nginx)

upstream backend_pool {
    server 10.0.1.10:8080 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=30s;  # mark down after 3 failures, retry after 30s
    server 10.0.1.11:8080 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=30s;
}
Open-source vs Plus: open-source Nginx only does passive health checks (marks a backend down after real request failures). Active health checks (proactively polling a /health endpoint on a schedule, independent of real traffic) require Nginx Plus or an external tool — this exact gap is one reason cloud load balancers like ALB (active health checks built in) are often preferred in AWS-native architectures.

Common mistakes

  • Using ip_hash for session affinity, then wondering why load becomes uneven when a small number of client IPs (e.g. behind a corporate NAT) generate disproportionate traffic — sticky routing by IP isn't a great affinity strategy at scale.
  • Not setting max_fails/fail_timeout — a dead backend keeps receiving traffic and erroring until manually removed.

Interview Q

Q: What's the practical difference between passive and active health checks, and which does open-source Nginx support?
A: Passive: the proxy only learns a backend is unhealthy after real client requests to it start failing (reactive). Active: the proxy independently and proactively polls a health endpoint on a schedule, catching problems before real traffic hits a dead backend (proactive). Open-source Nginx only supports passive; active checks require Nginx Plus or an external health-checking layer (or switching to a cloud LB like ALB which has active checks natively).

05 TLS Termination

server {
    listen 443 ssl;
    server_name api.example.com;

    ssl_certificate     /etc/nginx/ssl/fullchain.pem;
    ssl_certificate_key /etc/nginx/ssl/privkey.pem;
    ssl_protocols TLSv1.2 TLSv1.3;          # disable old/insecure versions explicitly

    location / {
        proxy_pass http://backend_pool;       # plain HTTP to backend — TLS already terminated here
    }
}

server {
    listen 80;
    server_name api.example.com;
    return 301 https://$host$request_uri;      # force redirect all HTTP to HTTPS
}

This is the exact "TLS terminated at the edge, plain HTTP internally within the trusted network" pattern flagged in Module 5 — Nginx here plays the same role an ALB plays in AWS.

Common mistakes

  • Forgetting to explicitly restrict ssl_protocols — leaving old TLS 1.0/1.1 enabled is a real, commonly-flagged security audit finding.
  • No HTTP→HTTPS redirect — silently allows insecure access alongside secure, when only HTTPS should ever be reachable in production.

Interview Q

Q: Why is it acceptable for traffic between Nginx and backend app servers to be plain HTTP in many production setups?
A: If that traffic stays entirely within a trusted private network boundary (e.g. same VPC, protected by security groups/firewalls preventing any external access to that internal segment), the risk of interception there is very low, and the performance/complexity cost of internal TLS (cert management, CPU overhead) often isn't justified — though stricter compliance regimes (PCI-DSS, HIPAA) or zero-trust architectures may still require it end-to-end regardless.

★ Mini Project

# Full working reverse proxy + load balancer + TLS setup, from scratch
sudo apt install nginx -y

sudo tee /etc/nginx/conf.d/myapp.conf <<'EOF'
upstream backend_pool {
    least_conn;
    server 127.0.0.1:8081;
    server 127.0.0.1:8082;
}

server {
    listen 80;
    server_name localhost;

    location /health {
        return 200 'nginx OK';
    }

    location / {
        proxy_pass http://backend_pool;
        proxy_set_header Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
    }
}
EOF

# spin up two dummy backends to prove load balancing
python3 -m http.server 8081 &
python3 -m http.server 8082 &

sudo nginx -t                # ALWAYS test config before reload
sudo systemctl reload nginx

# hammer it and watch requests distribute across both backends
for i in {1..10}; do curl -s localhost/ > /dev/null; done
tail -f /var/log/nginx/access.log

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Forward proxy hides the client; reverse proxy hides the servers.
  • Nginx: master process manages config/workers; workers use event-driven epoll for massive concurrency on few threads.
  • Always set proxy_set_header X-Real-IP / X-Forwarded-For / X-Forwarded-Proto or backend loses real client context.
  • Serve static assets directly via Nginx location blocks — never proxy them through the app.
  • Load balancing algorithms: round_robin (default), least_conn, ip_hash (sticky by IP).
  • Open-source Nginx: passive health checks only (reactive). Active checks need Nginx Plus or ALB.
  • TLS termination at the proxy is standard; internal traffic often plain HTTP within a trusted network.
  • Always `nginx -t` before reload — catches config syntax errors before they take down the whole proxy.

Q 10 MCQs

1. Reverse proxy hides which side from clients?
The backend servers.
2. What Linux mechanism lets one Nginx worker handle thousands of connections?
epoll — event-driven, non-blocking I/O.
3. Header used to pass the real client IP to a backend behind Nginx?
X-Real-IP or X-Forwarded-For.
4. Which load balancing algorithm provides simple sticky sessions?
ip_hash.
5. Does open-source Nginx support active health checks natively?
No — only passive (reactive to real request failures); active checks need Nginx Plus or an external tool.
6. Command to validate Nginx config syntax before reloading?
nginx -t
7. Why serve static files via Nginx location blocks instead of the app server?
Far more efficient — no app-layer/framework overhead for something that's just reading a file from disk.
8. What does `least_conn` optimize for that round_robin doesn't?
Uneven request durations — sends new requests to whichever backend currently has the fewest active connections rather than blindly cycling.
9. What directive forces HTTP to HTTPS redirect in Nginx?
A server block on port 80 with `return 301 https://$host$request_uri;`
10. What does max_fails/fail_timeout control in an upstream block?
How many consecutive failures mark a backend as down, and how long before Nginx retries sending it traffic again.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. After editing nginx.conf and reloading, the site goes down entirely.
Should have run `nginx -t` before reload — a config syntax error causes reload to fail (old config either stays running or, in a worse case, nginx fails to restart). Fix the syntax, test again, then reload.
2. Backend logs show every request coming from 127.0.0.1.
Missing proxy_set_header X-Real-IP/X-Forwarded-For — backend needs to be configured to read those headers instead of the raw connecting peer IP, but they must actually be SET by Nginx first.
3. One backend server keeps getting traffic even though it's crashed.
max_fails/fail_timeout isn't configured (or thresholds too lenient) — Nginx needs explicit failure-detection config to stop routing to a dead upstream.
4. Load isn't evenly distributed despite round_robin being the default.
Check for keep-alive connection reuse concentrating many requests on the same already-established backend connection, or check if ip_hash was accidentally left configured from earlier testing.
5. Users get a browser TLS warning despite a valid-looking cert being configured.
Check ssl_certificate is pointing at the FULL CHAIN (fullchain.pem including intermediate CA certs), not just the leaf cert — a common misconfiguration that breaks validation for clients that don't already have the intermediate cached.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Set up Nginx as a reverse proxy in front of two simple backend servers and prove round_robin distribution with access logs.
  2. Configure and test least_conn vs ip_hash against a load test where one backend deliberately responds slower — observe the distribution difference.
  3. Set up self-signed TLS termination in Nginx and verify the HTTP→HTTPS redirect works.
  4. Break the config on purpose (typo a directive) and practice the nginx -t → fix → reload workflow.
  5. Configure a location block serving static files directly, and measure/compare response time vs proxying the same file through a backend app.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What problem does a reverse proxy solve that a single app server can't?
A single app server has a hard capacity ceiling and is a single point of failure. A reverse proxy can distribute traffic across multiple backend instances (load balancing), hide internal server topology, and centralize cross-cutting concerns like TLS termination — none of which a lone app server does on its own.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why does Nginx's architecture (event-driven workers) scale better than a thread-per-connection model for high-concurrency workloads?
Thread-per-connection servers pay real OS overhead (memory for each thread's stack, context-switching cost) per simultaneous connection, which becomes expensive at high concurrency. Nginx's workers use non-blocking I/O (epoll) to manage thousands of connections on very few OS threads, since most connection time is spent idle waiting on I/O rather than doing CPU work — a much more efficient use of resources for typical web traffic patterns.
Intermediate: You need to add a third backend server to an Nginx load-balanced pool with zero downtime. What's the process?
Add the new server line to the upstream block, run nginx -t to validate syntax, then nginx -s reload (or systemctl reload nginx) — reload gracefully starts new worker processes with the updated config while letting old workers finish their in-flight requests before exiting, so no connections are dropped during the change.
Intermediate-Advanced: Explain the security and performance implications of terminating TLS at Nginx versus passing it through to the backend untouched.
Terminating at Nginx centralizes cert management (one place to renew/rotate), offloads the CPU cost of TLS handshakes from app servers, and allows Nginx to inspect/route based on the decrypted request (headers, paths) which passthrough mode can't do. The trade-off is that traffic between Nginx and the backend is unencrypted unless a second TLS layer is added — acceptable within a trusted private network boundary for most architectures, but insufficient for zero-trust or strict compliance requirements (PCI-DSS, HIPAA) which may mandate end-to-end encryption even within internal segments.
Advanced: Design a zero-downtime blue-green deployment using only Nginx upstream configuration (no orchestrator).
Maintain two upstream pools (blue: currently live instances, green: newly deployed instances). Deploy the new version to the green pool entirely, health-check it independently (hit its instances directly, bypassing Nginx, to confirm readiness). Update the active server block's proxy_pass (or use a variable-driven upstream selection) to point at the green pool, validate with nginx -t, then reload — existing in-flight requests to blue finish naturally while new requests immediately go to green. Keep blue running briefly as an instant rollback target (just flip the config back and reload) before decommissioning it once green is confirmed stable under real traffic.

Docker, from first principles to production

Not "another VM" — containers reuse the SAME host kernel via namespaces and cgroups (Module 1 concepts you already know). This module builds the mental model that makes Kubernetes (Module 11) click immediately.

01 Why Containers, VM Comparison

What is it / why needed

Before containers, "works on my machine" was a constant plague — different OS versions, different installed library versions, different configs between dev/staging/prod. VMs solved isolation but at a heavy cost: each VM runs a FULL separate OS kernel, meaning gigabytes of overhead and slow boot times just to isolate one app.

VIRTUAL MACHINES CONTAINERS ┌───────┐┌───────┐┌───────┐ ┌───────┐┌───────┐┌───────┐ │ App A ││ App B ││ App C │ │ App A ││ App B ││ App C │ │ Bins/ ││ Bins/ ││ Bins/ │ │ Bins/ ││ Bins/ ││ Bins/ │ │ Libs ││ Libs ││ Libs │ │ Libs ││ Libs ││ Libs │ │ GUEST ││ GUEST ││ GUEST │ └───────┘└───────┘└───────┘ │ KERNEL ││ KERNEL ││ KERNEL │ Container Runtime (Docker Engine) └───────┘└───────┘└───────┘ ┌─────────────────────────────┐ Hypervisor │ HOST OS — ONE SHARED KERNEL │ ┌─────────────────────────────┐ └─────────────────────────────┘ │ HOST OS │ Hardware └─────────────────────────────┘ Hardware
VMContainer
KernelEach VM has its OWN full guest kernelALL containers SHARE the host's single kernel
Boot timeMinutes (booting a whole OS)Milliseconds-seconds (just starting a process)
SizeGBs (full OS image)MBs typically (just app + its deps)
Isolation strengthVery strong (separate kernel = hardware-level isolation via hypervisor)Weaker (shared kernel — a kernel exploit can theoretically escape container isolation)
DensityTens per hostHundreds-thousands per host
Key interview insight: containers are NOT lightweight VMs. There is no separate kernel involved at all — a container is just a normal Linux process with restricted views of the system (namespaces) and resource limits (cgroups), both enforced by the SAME kernel running everything else on that host.

Common mistakes

  • Calling a container "a lightweight VM" in an interview — technically wrong and reveals a shallow understanding; it's a process with kernel-enforced isolation, no hypervisor or guest kernel involved at all.
  • Assuming container isolation is equally strong as VM isolation for genuinely untrusted/hostile multi-tenant workloads — it's weaker precisely because the kernel is shared (this is why things like gVisor/Firecracker microVMs exist for stronger isolation when needed, e.g. AWS Fargate/Lambda use Firecracker).

Interview Q

Q: Is a Docker container a lightweight virtual machine?
A: No. There's no hypervisor and no separate guest kernel involved at all — a container is a normal Linux process on the host, given an isolated view of the filesystem/network/process tree via kernel namespaces, and resource limits via cgroups. All containers on a host share literally the same kernel that's running the host OS itself.

02 Namespaces & Cgroups — The Actual Mechanism

This is the single most-asked "prove you actually understand Docker" interview topic.

Namespaces — isolation (what a process can SEE)

NamespaceIsolates
PIDProcess IDs — container's process 1 is a totally different PID in the host's process table
NETNetwork stack — own interfaces, IP addresses, routing table, port space
MNT (mount)Filesystem mount points — container sees its own root filesystem
UTSHostname — container can have its own hostname independent of the host
IPCInter-process communication — shared memory/semaphores isolated from host
USERUser/group IDs — root inside the container can map to an unprivileged user on the host

Cgroups (control groups) — resource limits (what a process can USE)

Cgroups cap and account for CPU, memory, disk I/O, and network bandwidth per process group. This is the exact mechanism Docker uses to enforce --memory and --cpus flags, and the same one systemd uses under the hood (Module 1) — Docker didn't invent this, it just orchestrates existing kernel features.

docker run --memory=512m --cpus=1.5 myapp
# under the hood: creates a cgroup limiting this process to 512MB RAM, 1.5 CPU cores worth of time

See it yourself

# Find a container's PID on the HOST (proving it's just a regular host process)
docker inspect --format '{{.State.Pid}}' 
ps aux | grep           # you'll see it right there in the HOST's process list

# See its cgroup limits directly
cat /sys/fs/cgroup/memory/docker//memory.limit_in_bytes   # (cgroup v1 path, varies by system)

# Confirm PID namespace isolation: inside the container, this process looks like PID 1
docker exec -it  ps aux    # shows PID 1 = your app, isolated view
Why this matters for security thinking: since containers share the host kernel, a kernel-level vulnerability (rare, but real) can potentially let a process "escape" its namespace/cgroup confinement — this is precisely why running containers as non-root (Module 1's least-privilege principle) and keeping the host kernel patched both matter enormously more for containers than people initially assume.

Common mistakes

  • Not being able to explain namespaces/cgroups by name in an interview — this is the #1 differentiator between "has used Docker" and "understands Docker."
  • Assuming resource limits are optional/cosmetic — without explicit --memory limits, a runaway container can consume all host RAM and take down every OTHER container on the same host (the Module 1 OOM killer then picks a victim, possibly not even the offending container).

Interview Q

Q: What Linux kernel features does Docker actually build on to provide container isolation and resource limits?
A: Namespaces (PID, NET, MNT, UTS, IPC, USER) provide isolated VIEWS of system resources — what a process can see. Cgroups provide resource ACCOUNTING and LIMITS — how much CPU/memory/IO a process (or group of processes) can actually use. Docker is an orchestration/UX layer on top of both; it didn't invent either mechanism.

03 Images & Layers

What is it / how it works internally

A Docker image is a stack of read-only layers, each representing a filesystem diff from the layer below (implemented via a union filesystem, typically overlayfs on Linux). Each Dockerfile instruction that changes the filesystem (RUN, COPY, ADD) creates a NEW layer. Layers are content-addressed (hashed) and cached/shared across images — this is directly analogous to Git's blob-sharing model from Module 3.

Image = stack of read-only layers: ┌─────────────────────────┐ │ Layer 4: COPY app.jar │ ← changes most often, put LAST for best cache reuse ├─────────────────────────┤ │ Layer 3: RUN mvn install │ ← expensive, changes when deps change ├─────────────────────────┤ │ Layer 2: COPY pom.xml │ ← changes less often ├─────────────────────────┤ │ Layer 1: FROM eclipse-temurin:17 │ ← base image, changes rarely └─────────────────────────┘ + a thin writable layer on top when a CONTAINER is actually running from this image

When you run a container, Docker adds ONE thin writable layer on top of the image's read-only stack (copy-on-write) — any file changes during the container's life happen there, and vanish when the container is removed unless persisted via a volume (covered later).

Production commands

docker build -t myapp:1.0 .
docker history myapp:1.0            # see every layer and its size — great for finding bloat
docker inspect myapp:1.0              # full image metadata
docker images                            # list local images
docker image prune -a                      # remove unused images, reclaim disk

Common mistakes

  • Ordering Dockerfile instructions so frequently-changing content (app code) comes BEFORE rarely-changing content (dependencies) — busts the cache for every layer after it on every single build, dramatically slowing CI.
  • Not understanding that a "deleted" file in a later layer isn't actually gone from the image — it's marked deleted in the overlay, but the earlier layer's data (and its size) is still baked into the image, a real cause of unexpectedly large images.

Interview Q

Q: You deleted a large file in a later RUN layer of your Dockerfile, but the image size didn't shrink. Why?
A: Each layer is an immutable diff — a later layer can only mark a file as deleted (a "whiteout") in the union filesystem view, it can't retroactively remove the actual bytes from an EARLIER layer. Those bytes are still physically present in the image. The fix is to avoid creating the large file in the first place within the same logical step, or use multi-stage builds so the large file never makes it into the final image's layers at all.

04 Dockerfile & Multi-Stage Builds

Production Dockerfile for a Spring Boot app

# --- Stage 1: build (has the full JDK + Maven, but none of this ships in the final image) ---
FROM maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-17 AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY pom.xml .
RUN mvn dependency:go-offline -B          # cache deps in their OWN layer — huge build speedup
COPY src ./src
RUN mvn clean package -DskipTests

# --- Stage 2: runtime (only the JRE + the built jar — tiny final image) ---
FROM eclipse-temurin:17-jre-alpine
RUN addgroup -S appgroup && adduser -S appuser -G appgroup     # non-root user (Module 1 principle)
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=build /app/target/*.jar app.jar
USER appuser
EXPOSE 8080
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s CMD wget -qO- http://localhost:8080/actuator/health || exit 1
ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "app.jar"]

Why multi-stage builds matter

The build stage needs Maven, the full JDK, and source code — none of that should exist in the production image (bigger attack surface, bigger image, slower pulls, and leaks source code into what ships). Multi-stage builds let you use a heavy "build" image, then copy ONLY the final artifact into a minimal "runtime" image — the intermediate stage's layers never appear in the final image at all.

InstructionPurpose
FROM ... AS nameNames a build stage for later reference
COPY --from=nameCopies files from a NAMED EARLIER STAGE, not the host — this is the multi-stage magic
ENTRYPOINT vs CMDENTRYPOINT = the fixed executable; CMD = default args, overridable at `docker run` time
HEALTHCHECKDocker-native liveness probe — `docker ps` shows (healthy)/(unhealthy) status

Common mistakes

  • Shipping the JDK (not JRE) to production — JDK includes compiler tools unnecessary at runtime, needlessly larger image and attack surface.
  • Running as root inside the container (no USER directive) — a container escape or RCE then has root-equivalent reach, same Module 1 principle applied to containers.
  • Not using .dockerignore — copying .git, node_modules, target/ into the build context bloats build time and can leak files unintentionally.
  • COPY . . before dependency installation — busts the Docker layer cache on every single code change, re-downloading all dependencies every build.

Interview Q

Q: Why use a multi-stage Dockerfile instead of just installing build tools and cleaning them up in a single stage?
A: Even if you `apt remove` build tools afterward within the same stage, their layer already contains those bytes in the image's history (per the previous section's layer-immutability point) — cleanup in a later RUN doesn't shrink earlier layers. Multi-stage builds physically exclude the entire build-stage layer set from the final image, since only explicitly COPY --from='d files cross over — a fundamentally cleaner and smaller result, not just a "hidden" one.

05 Container Lifecycle & Runtime

docker run → Created → Running → (Paused) → Stopped → Removed │ docker exec (run a command inside a running container) │ docker logs (view stdout/stderr — this is WHY apps should log to stdout, not files, in containers) 
docker run -d --name myapp -p 8080:8080 --memory=512m --restart=unless-stopped myapp:1.0
docker ps                        # running containers
docker ps -a                       # all containers, including stopped
docker logs -f myapp                  # follow logs (stdout/stderr)
docker exec -it myapp sh                # shell into a running container
docker stop myapp                         # SIGTERM, then SIGKILL after grace period (Module 1's pattern again)
docker rm myapp                              # remove a stopped container
docker stats                                    # live CPU/memory usage per container
Why containers should log to stdout, not a file: `docker logs` only captures stdout/stderr of PID 1 in the container. An app writing logs to a file INSIDE the container writes them to that thin writable layer — invisible to `docker logs`, and gone forever when the container is removed, unless a volume is mounted specifically for it. Production convention: always log to stdout/stderr and let the container runtime/orchestrator handle log shipping (12-factor app principle).

Common mistakes

  • Not setting --restart policy — a crashed container just stays dead instead of coming back (the container-world equivalent of Module 1's systemd Restart=on-failure).
  • Logging to a file inside the container instead of stdout — logs silently vanish on container removal/restart.
  • No --memory limit — one runaway container can starve every other container on the host of RAM.

Interview Q

Q: Your app writes logs to /var/log/app.log inside the container. You restart the container and all logs are gone. Why, and what's the fix?
A: Container filesystem changes live in the ephemeral writable layer on top of the image, which is discarded when the container is removed/recreated (restart typically keeps the same container/writable layer, but recreate/redeploy does not). The fix: log to stdout/stderr (12-factor convention) so `docker logs` and any log-shipping agent captures it externally, or explicitly mount a volume for the log directory if file-based logging is unavoidable.

06 Docker Networking

Network driverBehavior
bridge (default)Each container gets its own virtual network interface + private IP on a virtual bridge; NAT to reach outside (Module 4's NAT concept again)
hostContainer shares the host's network namespace directly — no isolation, no port mapping needed, but no per-container port conflicts protection either
noneNo networking at all — fully isolated, used for pure compute tasks needing zero network access
overlayMulti-host networking (Docker Swarm) — containers on different physical hosts communicate as if on one network
docker network create mynet
docker run -d --network mynet --name db postgres
docker run -d --network mynet --name app myapp
# 'app' can reach 'db' by NAME (docker's built-in DNS resolves container names to IPs within a user-defined network)

docker run -p 8080:80 myapp     # host port 8080 → container port 80 (this is Docker doing port-mapping/NAT)
docker port myapp                  # see current port mappings
Default bridge vs user-defined bridge: containers on the DEFAULT bridge network can only reach each other by IP, not by name (no built-in DNS there) — a very common source of "why can't my containers see each other by hostname" confusion. Always create a user-defined bridge network for multi-container setups; Docker Compose does this automatically (Module 10).

Common mistakes

  • Assuming container name resolution works on the default bridge network — it doesn't; only user-defined networks get Docker's embedded DNS.
  • Using --network host in production "for simplicity" — removes network isolation and can cause port conflicts across containers sharing the host's network stack directly.

Interview Q

Q: Two containers on the same DEFAULT bridge network can't reach each other by container name, but it works fine when you put them on a custom network. Why?
A: Docker's embedded DNS server (which resolves container names to their internal IPs) is only enabled on user-defined networks, not the default bridge network — a deliberate legacy behavior. Creating a custom bridge network (or using Docker Compose, which creates one automatically per project) enables name-based service discovery between containers.

07 Volumes & Persistence

What is it / why needed

Container filesystems are ephemeral by design (previous sections) — but databases, uploaded files, and other real state need to survive container restarts/recreation. Volumes are Docker-managed storage that lives OUTSIDE the container's writable layer, on the host, persisting independently of container lifecycle.

TypeManaged byUse case
Named volumeDocker (stored under /var/lib/docker/volumes/)Database data, anything Docker should manage and you don't need direct host access to
Bind mountYou — maps a specific HOST path directly into the containerLocal dev (live-mounting source code), config files
tmpfs mountRAM only, never touches diskSensitive temp data that must never persist to disk
docker volume create pgdata
docker run -d --name db -v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data postgres
docker volume ls
docker volume inspect pgdata
docker run -v $(pwd)/src:/app/src myapp    # bind mount — live dev code reload pattern
docker run --tmpfs /app/tmp myapp             # RAM-only scratch space
Common real incident: `docker rm` on a container does NOT remove its volumes by default — but `docker rm -v` DOES, and `docker system prune` with certain flags can also nuke volumes. Confirm exactly what you're removing before running cleanup commands against anything with a database volume attached.

Common mistakes

  • Storing database data directly in the container's writable layer with no volume at all — a routine container recreation (redeploy) silently wipes the database.
  • Confusing bind mounts and named volumes — bind mounts are great for local dev, but production usually prefers named volumes (Docker manages permissions/location; more portable across hosts, works better with orchestrators).

Interview Q

Q: You redeploy a container running Postgres and all your data is gone. What went wrong and what's the fix?
A: The database's data directory was living in the container's own ephemeral writable layer with no volume attached — recreating the container discards that layer entirely. Fix: run Postgres with a named volume mounted at its data directory (-v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data), so data persists independently of the container's own lifecycle.

08 Security

PracticeWhy
Run as non-root (USER directive)Limits blast radius of a container escape/RCE — same Module 1 least-privilege principle
Minimal base images (alpine/distroless)Smaller attack surface — fewer installed packages/binaries an attacker could leverage
Don't mount the Docker socket into containersA container with access to /var/run/docker.sock can control the ENTIRE host's Docker daemon — root-equivalent
Scan images for CVEs`docker scan` / Trivy / Snyk — catch known vulnerable dependencies before they ship
Read-only root filesystem where possible`--read-only` flag — prevents malware/tampering from persisting writes to the container's own filesystem
Drop unnecessary Linux capabilities`--cap-drop=ALL --cap-add=` — narrows what even a root-in-container process could do
Docker group = root-equivalent, revisited from Module 1: any user in the `docker` group (or with docker socket access) can trivially get full root on the host: `docker run -v /:/host -it alpine chroot /host` mounts the ENTIRE host filesystem into a container and chroots into it — instant root. Treat docker group membership exactly like granting root/sudo.

Common mistakes

  • Adding CI/CD service accounts to the docker group casually — equivalent to giving that account root on every host it touches.
  • Never scanning images — shipping known CVEs (e.g. an outdated base image with a public RCE) straight to production.

Interview Q

Q: Why is mounting the Docker socket (/var/run/docker.sock) into a container considered a serious security risk?
A: It gives that container's process full control over the host's Docker daemon — able to start new privileged containers, mount arbitrary host paths, and effectively achieve root access on the host itself. It's functionally equivalent to giving that container's process root on the host, regardless of what user it runs as INSIDE the container.

★ Mini Project

# Multi-stage, non-root, healthchecked Spring Boot container — production pattern end to end
mkdir myapp && cd myapp

cat > Dockerfile <<'EOF'
FROM maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-17 AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY pom.xml .
RUN mvn dependency:go-offline -B
COPY src ./src
RUN mvn clean package -DskipTests

FROM eclipse-temurin:17-jre-alpine
RUN addgroup -S appgroup && adduser -S appuser -G appgroup
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=build /app/target/*.jar app.jar
USER appuser
EXPOSE 8080
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s CMD wget -qO- http://localhost:8080/actuator/health || exit 1
ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "app.jar"]
EOF

cat > .dockerignore <<'EOF'
target/
.git/
*.md
EOF

docker build -t myapp:1.0 .
docker history myapp:1.0                          # inspect layer sizes

docker network create appnet
docker run -d --name db --network appnet -v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=pw postgres:16-alpine
docker run -d --name app --network appnet -p 8080:8080 --memory=512m --restart=unless-stopped \
  -e DB_HOST=db myapp:1.0

docker ps                                              # confirm both healthy/running
docker logs -f app
docker exec -it app sh -c "wget -qO- http://localhost:8080/actuator/health"

# prove PID namespace isolation
docker exec app ps aux    # your app shows as PID 1 inside
docker inspect --format '{{.State.Pid}}' app   # different PID from the HOST's perspective
ps aux | grep $(docker inspect --format '{{.State.Pid}}' app)   # same process, visible on host too

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Containers ≠ lightweight VMs. Same shared host kernel, isolated via namespaces (PID/NET/MNT/UTS/IPC/USER) + limited via cgroups.
  • Images = stacked read-only layers (overlayfs); running container adds one thin writable layer on top.
  • Layer order matters for cache efficiency: rarely-changing stuff (deps) first, frequently-changing stuff (app code) last.
  • Deleting a file in a later layer doesn't shrink the image — earlier layer bytes persist regardless.
  • Multi-stage builds: build stage's tools/layers never enter the final image — only explicit COPY --from='d artifacts do.
  • Always run as non-root (USER directive) in production images.
  • Log to stdout/stderr, never to a file inside the container — `docker logs` only sees stdout/stderr, and file writes vanish with the writable layer.
  • Default bridge network has no name-based DNS between containers; user-defined networks (and Compose) do.
  • Volumes persist data outside the container lifecycle — named volumes (Docker-managed) vs bind mounts (host path, dev-friendly) vs tmpfs (RAM only).
  • Docker socket access = root-equivalent host access. Treat docker group membership like sudo.
  • Always set --restart policy and --memory limits in production.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What kernel feature gives a container its isolated process ID view?
PID namespace.
2. What kernel feature enforces a container's memory limit?
Cgroups.
3. Does a container run its own separate kernel?
No — all containers share the single host kernel.
4. Why put COPY of app source AFTER dependency installation in a Dockerfile?
Preserves Docker's layer cache for the (usually much slower) dependency-install layer across builds where only app code changed.
5. What does a multi-stage build let you exclude from the final image?
Build-time tools/dependencies (compilers, build systems, intermediate artifacts) that aren't needed at runtime.
6. Where does docker logs pull its output from?
The container's stdout/stderr streams — not any file written inside the container's filesystem.
7. Do containers on Docker's DEFAULT bridge network resolve each other by container name?
No — only user-defined bridge networks get Docker's embedded DNS-based name resolution.
8. What Docker volume type is best for a production database's data directory?
A named volume (Docker-managed, persists independently of container lifecycle).
9. Why is mounting the Docker socket into a container dangerous?
Grants that container root-equivalent control over the entire host via the Docker daemon.
10. What flag limits a container to at most 1.5 CPU cores?
--cpus=1.5

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. A container keeps getting killed unexpectedly with exit code 137.
137 = 128+9 = SIGKILL, almost always the OOM killer enforcing the container's cgroup memory limit (Module 1's OOM killer concept, scoped to a cgroup this time). Check docker stats / dmesg, then raise the memory limit or fix the app's actual memory usage.
2. Two containers on the same custom network can't reach each other by name.
Confirm they're actually on the SAME user-defined network (docker network inspect), not accidentally each on their own default network; also verify the target's actual listening port with docker exec + ss -tulnp inside it.
3. Image builds are extremely slow every single time, even for tiny code changes.
Dockerfile likely COPYs all source before installing dependencies, busting the cache on every build — reorder so dependency manifests (pom.xml/package.json) are copied and installed BEFORE the rest of the source.
4. Database container restarted and all data is gone.
No volume was mounted at the database's data directory — data lived in the ephemeral writable layer, discarded when the container was recreated. Requires a named volume going forward; the lost data itself is not recoverable from Docker.
5. `docker build` produces a huge final image despite a "clean" Dockerfile with only a few RUN commands.
Check docker history for oversized layers — likely a single RUN downloaded/installed something and didn't clean up its own cache/temp files WITHIN that same RUN instruction (cleanup in a later separate RUN doesn't help, per the layer-immutability rule); combine install+cleanup into one RUN, or move to a multi-stage build.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Build a multi-stage Dockerfile for any small app, run docker history, and identify exactly which layer contributes the most size.
  2. Prove PID namespace isolation: run a container, find its host-visible PID via docker inspect, then confirm `ps aux` inside the container shows it as PID 1.
  3. Deliberately trigger an OOM kill by running a container with --memory=50m and a script that allocates more; observe exit code 137 and confirm via dmesg.
  4. Set up a named volume for a Postgres container, insert data, remove and recreate the container (not the volume), and verify data survived.
  5. Create a user-defined bridge network, run two containers on it, and demonstrate name-based resolution working — then repeat on the default bridge network and show it failing.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What's the difference between a Docker image and a Docker container?
An image is a read-only, layered template/blueprint (built once, stored, reusable). A container is a running (or stopped) INSTANCE of that image — the image's layers plus one thin writable layer on top, actually executing as a process.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why are Docker images typically much smaller and faster to start than virtual machines?
A VM must boot an entire separate guest operating system (its own kernel, init system, drivers) inside a hypervisor — genuinely gigabytes and minutes of overhead. A container is just a process on the HOST's existing running kernel, isolated via namespaces/cgroups — starting one is as fast as starting any other process, and the image only needs to contain the app and its direct dependencies, not a whole OS.
Intermediate: Explain exactly what happens, layer by layer, when you run `docker build` on a Dockerfile with 5 instructions, and why changing line 4 doesn't force a rebuild of lines 1-3.
Docker builds each instruction into its own layer, hashing the instruction plus its inputs (for COPY, the actual file contents) to produce a cache key. If a layer's cache key hasn't changed (same instruction, same input files), Docker reuses the previously-built layer from cache instead of re-executing it. Changing line 4's content changes ITS cache key (and every layer after it, since each layer's hash also depends on its parent layer's hash) — but layers 1-3, whose inputs are unchanged, are still cache hits and reused directly.
Intermediate-Advanced: A security review flags that your production containers run as root. Walk through the fix and any complications it might introduce.
Add a dedicated non-root user in the Dockerfile (RUN addgroup/adduser) and a USER directive before the entrypoint. Complications: the app may try to bind to a privileged port (<1024) which non-root can't do inside the container either — fix by using a higher port (e.g. 8080) and relying on the container runtime/orchestrator's port mapping to expose it externally as 80/443 instead. File permission issues can also arise if the app writes to paths owned by root in the image — ensure COPY/RUN steps set correct ownership (--chown flag on COPY, or explicit chown in a RUN) for any directories the non-root user needs to write to.
Advanced: Design a container security posture for a multi-tenant CI/CD platform where untrusted user-submitted code runs inside containers. What Docker-level protections are and aren't sufficient?
Standard Docker isolation (namespaces + cgroups) shares the host kernel — insufficient alone for genuinely untrusted/hostile code, since a kernel exploit could escape container boundaries entirely. A real design would layer: non-root execution, dropped Linux capabilities (--cap-drop=ALL, add back only the few needed), read-only root filesystem, strict resource limits (cgroups) to prevent noisy-neighbor DoS, seccomp profiles restricting available syscalls, AND critically, a stronger isolation boundary than plain containers for truly untrusted code — e.g. gVisor (userspace kernel intercepting syscalls) or Firecracker microVMs (what AWS Lambda/Fargate actually use), which reintroduce VM-level kernel isolation while keeping container-like density/speed. Relying on Docker's default isolation alone for genuinely adversarial code is a known-insufficient architecture.
Module 09 / 30 — Docker. Next up: Docker Compose — orchestrating multi-container applications declaratively, directly building on the networking/volumes concepts just covered.
DevOps / SDE-1 Interview Track — Module 10 / 30

Docker Compose, from first principles to production

Managing 4 separate `docker run` commands by hand doesn't scale past a weekend project. Compose declares your whole multi-container app in one file — and is the conceptual on-ramp to Kubernetes' declarative model in the next module.
