01 Why Compose / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

A real app is rarely one container — it's an app server + a database + a cache + maybe a message queue. Running each with separate long `docker run` commands is error-prone (easy to forget a flag), not version-controlled, and painful to reproduce identically on a teammate's machine. Compose declares the ENTIRE stack — services, networks, volumes, dependencies — in one YAML file, checked into git alongside the code.

What problem it solves specifically

  • Reproducibility — `docker compose up` gives every developer the identical stack, every time.
  • Networking — Compose auto-creates a dedicated user-defined bridge network (Module 9's insight: this is exactly why Compose containers CAN resolve each other by name, unlike the default bridge).
  • One command to bring the whole stack up/down, instead of remembering the run order and flags for each container.
Compose is explicitly a single-host tool — it's the right choice for local dev, small production deployments on one machine, and CI test environments. For multi-host, auto-healing, auto-scaling production orchestration, you need Kubernetes (Module 11) — Compose doesn't do rolling updates across a fleet, self-healing across node failures, or genuine horizontal auto-scaling.

Interview Q

Q: Why wouldn't you use Docker Compose to run a production system across multiple servers?
A: Compose is fundamentally single-host — it has no concept of scheduling containers across multiple machines, no built-in self-healing if a node dies, and no native horizontal auto-scaling. Those are exactly the problems Kubernetes was built to solve; Compose is best suited to local development, single-host deployments, and CI test environments.

02 Compose File Anatomy

# docker-compose.yml
version: "3.9"

services:
  app:
    build: .                          # build from local Dockerfile (Module 9)
    ports:
      - "8080:8080"
    environment:
      - SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod
      - DB_HOST=db
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy       # don't start app until db passes its healthcheck
    restart: unless-stopped
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          memory: 512M
          cpus: "1.0"

  db:
    image: postgres:16-alpine
    environment:
      - POSTGRES_PASSWORD=secretpw
      - POSTGRES_DB=myapp
    volumes:
      - pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U postgres"]
      interval: 5s
      timeout: 3s
      retries: 5

  redis:
    image: redis:7-alpine
    command: redis-server --maxmemory 256mb --maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru

volumes:
  pgdata:                              # named volume, same concept as Module 9

networks:
  default:
    name: myapp_network                  # explicit name instead of Compose's auto-generated one
KeyPurpose
build vs imagebuild = build from local Dockerfile; image = pull a pre-built image from a registry
depends_on (+condition)Controls START ORDER — plain depends_on only waits for the container to START, not be actually ready; condition: service_healthy waits for its healthcheck to pass (critical distinction, covered next)
environmentEnv vars injected into the container
volumes (top-level)Declares named volumes usable by any service

Common mistakes

  • Assuming plain depends_on: db means "db is ready to accept connections" — it only means "db's container has STARTED," which for something like Postgres can be seconds before it's actually accepting connections. This causes classic "connection refused" crash loops on cold start.
  • Hardcoding secrets directly in the compose file committed to git — use an env_file (gitignored) or secrets management instead.

Interview Q

Q: Your app container crashes on startup with "connection refused" to the database, even though depends_on: db is set. Why, and how do you fix it?
A: Plain depends_on only guarantees the database's CONTAINER has started, not that Postgres inside it has finished initializing and is actually accepting connections — there's a real gap between "container running" and "service ready." Fix: add a healthcheck to the db service and use `depends_on: db: condition: service_healthy` on the app service, so Compose actually waits for the database to report healthy before starting the app.

03 Networking & Service Discovery

Compose automatically creates a dedicated user-defined bridge network for the project (all services in one compose file share it by default) — directly building on Module 9's point that user-defined networks get Docker's embedded DNS. This is why DB_HOST=db in the example above just works: "db" resolves to that service's container IP automatically, no manual network setup required.

docker compose ps                       # see all services + their status
docker network ls | grep myapp             # confirm the auto-created network
docker compose exec app ping db              # prove name resolution works between services

Common mistakes

  • Hardcoding an IP address instead of the service name in app config — container IPs on the Compose network can change between restarts; service names are stable.

Interview Q

Q: In a Compose file, your app connects to the database using the hostname "db" — how does that actually resolve?
A: Compose creates a dedicated user-defined bridge network for the project and registers each service's container name (here "db") in Docker's embedded DNS on that network — so "db" resolves to that container's current internal IP automatically, the same mechanism covered in Module 9 for user-defined networks generally.

04 Dependencies & Health-Gated Startup

Already covered the core mechanism above (service_healthy) — worth calling out as its own topic since it's the single most commonly misunderstood/misused Compose feature in real projects.

services:
  app:
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy
      redis:
        condition: service_started    # weaker guarantee — just "container started," fine for less-critical deps

  db:
    image: postgres:16-alpine
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U postgres"]
      interval: 5s
      timeout: 3s
      retries: 5
      start_period: 10s                # grace period before failed checks count against retries

Common mistakes

  • Using service_started for a hard dependency like a database — should be service_healthy whenever the dependency has a meaningful readiness state.
  • No start_period on a slow-starting service — early failed health checks during normal startup time get counted against retries, causing false "unhealthy" status.

05 Environments & Overrides

Multiple environments from one base file

# docker-compose.yml (base — common to all environments)
services:
  app:
    build: .
    environment:
      - SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=default

# docker-compose.override.yml (auto-merged in local dev, no flag needed)
services:
  app:
    volumes:
      - ./src:/app/src          # live-mount source for hot reload locally
    environment:
      - SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=dev
    ports:
      - "5005:5005"               # expose debugger port locally only

# docker-compose.prod.yml (explicit, merged only when specified)
services:
  app:
    environment:
      - SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod
    deploy:
      replicas: 3
    restart: always
docker compose up                                  # base + override.yml automatically
docker compose -f docker-compose.yml -f docker-compose.prod.yml up -d    # base + prod, explicit
This pattern — one base file + environment-specific overlays — is the exact same philosophy Kubernetes' Kustomize and Helm (Module 12) use for managing dev/staging/prod differences without duplicating the whole manifest per environment.

Interview Q

Q: Why does `docker-compose.override.yml` get merged automatically but `docker-compose.prod.yml` doesn't?
A: Compose's convention is that override.yml represents the common local-development customization layer and is merged by default purely by filename convention — designed so `docker compose up` "just works" for local dev with zero extra flags. Any other named file (like prod.yml) must be explicitly specified with -f, preventing production-only config from accidentally leaking into a developer's local environment.

★ Mini Project

mkdir myapp-compose && cd myapp-compose

cat > docker-compose.yml <<'EOF'
services:
  app:
    build: .
    ports:
      - "8080:8080"
    environment:
      - DB_HOST=db
      - REDIS_HOST=redis
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy
      redis:
        condition: service_started
    restart: unless-stopped

  db:
    image: postgres:16-alpine
    environment:
      - POSTGRES_PASSWORD=devpassword
      - POSTGRES_DB=myapp
    volumes:
      - pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U postgres"]
      interval: 5s
      retries: 5
      start_period: 10s

  redis:
    image: redis:7-alpine

volumes:
  pgdata:
EOF

docker compose up -d
docker compose ps
docker compose logs -f app
docker compose exec app ping -c 2 db          # prove service discovery works
docker compose down                              # stop and remove containers, keeps volumes
docker compose down -v                             # ALSO removes volumes — be deliberate about this flag

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Compose = declarative multi-container definition for a SINGLE host — not a substitute for Kubernetes at scale.
  • Auto-creates a user-defined bridge network → services resolve each other by NAME (builds on Module 9's DNS point).
  • depends_on alone only waits for container START, not readiness — use condition: service_healthy with a real healthcheck for hard dependencies.
  • build (local Dockerfile) vs image (pull from registry).
  • docker-compose.override.yml auto-merges; any other named file needs explicit -f.
  • `docker compose down -v` removes volumes too — dangerous against anything with real data, be deliberate.

Q 10 MCQs

1. Is Docker Compose suitable for multi-host production orchestration?
No — it's single-host; use Kubernetes for multi-host orchestration with self-healing and auto-scaling.
2. What guarantees does plain `depends_on` (no condition) provide?
Only that the dependency's container has STARTED — not that the service inside it is ready to accept requests/connections.
3. How do services in the same Compose file resolve each other?
By service name, via Docker's embedded DNS on the auto-created user-defined bridge network.
4. Which compose override file merges automatically without any -f flag?
docker-compose.override.yml
5. What does `condition: service_healthy` require the dependency service to have defined?
A healthcheck block.
6. Does `docker compose down` remove named volumes by default?
No — only `docker compose down -v` removes volumes as well.
7. What does `build:` vs `image:` control in a service definition?
build builds a local Dockerfile into an image; image pulls (or references) an already-built image from a registry.
8. Why add `start_period` to a healthcheck?
Gives the service a grace period on startup before failed checks start counting toward the retries limit — avoids false "unhealthy" during normal slow initialization.
9. What real-world problem does one base compose file + environment overlay files solve?
Avoids duplicating the entire stack definition per environment — shared config lives once, only the differences (dev vs prod) are overlaid.
10. Why shouldn't secrets be hardcoded directly into a docker-compose.yml committed to git?
They'd be exposed in version control history to anyone with repo access — should use an env_file (gitignored) or a proper secrets manager instead.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. App container crash-loops on first `docker compose up`, but works fine on the second `up` a moment later.
Classic depends_on-without-healthcheck race — the database container started but wasn't ready to accept connections yet on the first pass; add a proper healthcheck and service_healthy condition.
2. Two services can't resolve each other by name despite being in the same compose file.
Check they're actually both defined in the same compose project/file (not accidentally split across separate `docker compose up` invocations creating separate networks), and confirm the correct service name (not container_name override, if one was set) is being used in config.
3. Local dev hot-reload isn't picking up code changes.
Check docker-compose.override.yml actually bind-mounts the source directory (volumes: ./src:/app/src) and that it's being auto-merged (verify with `docker compose config` to see the fully resolved merged config).
4. Ran `docker compose down -v` before a demo and lost all local database data.
The -v flag explicitly removes volumes along with containers — this was expected behavior given that flag, not a bug; going forward use plain `down` (no -v) when data should persist, reserving -v for genuine full resets.
5. Production compose file accidentally exposes a debug port to the internet.
The override.yml (with its debug-port mapping) was likely used directly in production instead of a proper prod.yml — reinforces why override.yml should be purely for local dev and production should use its own explicit, reviewed compose file.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Build a 3-service Compose stack (app + Postgres + Redis) with proper healthchecks gating startup order.
  2. Create a base docker-compose.yml plus an override.yml for local dev (bind-mounted source, debug port) and a separate prod.yml (resource limits, restart:always, replicas).
  3. Deliberately remove the healthcheck/condition and observe the crash-loop race condition on a fresh `docker compose up`; then fix it.
  4. Use `docker compose config` to print and inspect the fully merged configuration when combining base + override files.
  5. Add resource limits (deploy.resources.limits) to a service and verify with `docker stats` that they're actually enforced.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What problem does Docker Compose solve compared to running several `docker run` commands manually?
It declares the entire multi-container stack (services, networking, volumes, dependencies) in one version-controlled file, making the setup reproducible with a single command instead of manually remembering and running several long, error-prone docker run commands in the right order.
Beginner-Intermediate: How do containers in a Compose stack find and talk to each other?
Compose automatically creates a dedicated user-defined bridge network for the project, and Docker's embedded DNS on that network resolves each service's name to its container's IP — so a service can simply connect to "db" or "redis" by name rather than needing to know or hardcode an IP.
Intermediate: Why is `depends_on` alone often insufficient for real dependency ordering, and what's the correct fix?
depends_on only sequences container STARTUP order — it guarantees the dependency's container process has begun, not that the service inside is actually ready to serve requests (e.g. a database that takes a few seconds to finish initializing after its process starts). The fix is defining a proper healthcheck on the dependency and using `depends_on: : condition: service_healthy` so Compose actually waits for readiness, not just process start.
Intermediate-Advanced: How would you manage different configurations for local development, staging, and production using Compose, without duplicating the entire stack definition three times?
Keep a single base docker-compose.yml with everything common across environments. Use docker-compose.override.yml (auto-merged) purely for local-dev-specific concerns like bind-mounted source and debug ports. Create explicit, separately-named files (e.g. docker-compose.staging.yml, docker-compose.prod.yml) containing only the environment-specific deltas (replica counts, resource limits, restart policies, environment variables), merged explicitly via `-f base.yml -f prod.yml` — this avoids duplicating the whole stack while keeping environment differences clear and auditable.
Advanced: What are the concrete limitations of Docker Compose that would force a team to migrate to Kubernetes, even for a relatively small production workload?
Compose has no concept of scheduling across multiple physical hosts — it's fundamentally tied to a single Docker Engine instance, so it can't survive a whole host failing, and can't scale beyond that host's raw capacity. It has no built-in self-healing at the infrastructure level beyond simple container restart policies (no rescheduling onto a healthy node if the current node dies entirely). It has no native rolling-update strategy across a fleet with traffic-aware draining, no horizontal pod/container auto-scaling based on real-time metrics, and no built-in service mesh/ingress capabilities for complex routing. Any of these becoming a genuine business requirement (high availability across node failure, real auto-scaling, zero-downtime rolling deploys across many replicas) is a concrete forcing function toward Kubernetes.
Module 10 / 30 — Docker Compose. Next up: Kubernetes — everything Compose can't do (multi-host scheduling, self-healing, auto-scaling) at real production scale.
DevOps / SDE-1 Interview Track — Module 11 / 30

Kubernetes, from first principles to production

Everything Docker Compose couldn't do at scale: multi-host scheduling, self-healing, rolling updates, auto-scaling. This is the single heaviest-weighted module in most DevOps interviews — invest accordingly.

01 Why Kubernetes / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

Kubernetes (K8s) is a container orchestration platform: given a FLEET of machines (nodes) and a declarative description of what you want running, it schedules containers onto nodes, restarts/reschedules them on failure, load-balances traffic to them, and scales them — all without a human manually SSHing anywhere. It exists precisely because Compose's single-host model breaks down the moment you need real high availability across multiple physical machines.

The core philosophy: declarative, not imperative

You don't tell Kubernetes HOW to run 3 replicas of your app — you declare "I want 3 replicas running" (desired state) and a continuous reconciliation loop (the "controller pattern") constantly compares actual state to desired state and takes action to close any gap. This is fundamentally different from a script that imperatively runs commands once.

Reconciliation loop (the core mental model behind EVERYTHING in K8s): ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Desired state (what you declared in YAML) │ │ "3 replicas of myapp should be running" │ └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ compared continuously against ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Actual state (what's really running right now) │ └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ Gap found? → controller takes action to close it (e.g. a pod died → schedule a replacement immediately)

Common mistakes

  • Thinking of K8s commands as imperative scripts ("start this container") rather than declarations of desired state that a controller continuously enforces — this misunderstanding causes confusion about why a manually `kubectl delete`'d pod just reappears (the Deployment controller notices the gap and recreates it).

Interview Q

Q: You manually delete a pod that's managed by a Deployment. What happens, and why?
A: A new pod is immediately created to replace it. The Deployment's controller continuously reconciles actual state against the desired replica count declared in its spec — deleting a pod creates a gap (2 running instead of the desired 3), which the controller detects and closes by scheduling a new pod, entirely without human intervention.

02 Control Plane & Node Architecture

┌─────────────────────────── CONTROL PLANE ───────────────────────────┐ │ API Server → the ONLY component everything talks through │ │ (kubectl, controllers, kubelets — all via this) │ │ etcd → distributed key-value store, THE source of truth │ │ for all cluster state (every object's spec/status) │ │ Scheduler → decides WHICH node a new pod should run on │ │ (based on resource requests, affinity rules, taints) │ │ Controller Manager → runs the reconciliation loops (Deployment, ReplicaSet, │ │ Node controllers etc.) — the "brains" enforcing desired state│ └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ (all communication via API server) ┌──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┐ ▼ ▼ ▼ ┌─────────────┐ ┌─────────────┐ ┌─────────────┐ │ WORKER NODE │ │ WORKER NODE │ │ WORKER NODE │ │ kubelet │ │ kubelet │ │ kubelet │ ← agent that actually │ (talks to API, │ │ │ │ │ runs pods via the │ runs pods via │ │ │ │ │ container runtime │ container runtime)│ │ │ │ │ │ kube-proxy │ │ kube-proxy │ │ kube-proxy │ ← implements Service │ (networking rules) │ │ │ │ │ routing (below) │ [Pod] [Pod] [Pod] │ │ [Pod] [Pod] │ │ [Pod] [Pod] [Pod] │ └─────────────┘ └─────────────┘ └─────────────┘
ComponentRole
etcdThe actual database — if you lose etcd with no backup, you lose the entire cluster's state
API serverStateless REST front-door; validates and persists changes to etcd; every other component watches it for changes
SchedulerOnly decides PLACEMENT (which node) — doesn't actually start the container itself
kubeletPer-node agent that actually talks to the container runtime (containerd/CRI-O) to start/stop/monitor pods, and reports status back to the API server
kube-proxyPer-node component implementing Service networking rules (iptables/IPVS rules that route traffic to the right pod)
etcd is the single most critical piece to back up. It holds the entire cluster's desired-state configuration — a corrupted or lost etcd with no backup means total cluster state loss, not just "some pods are gone." Managed Kubernetes (EKS) handles etcd backup/HA for you — a major reason to prefer EKS over self-managed control planes in production, covered in Module 21.

Interview Q

Q: What's the difference in responsibility between the Scheduler and the kubelet?
A: The Scheduler only decides WHICH node a new pod should run on (a placement decision, based on resource requests/limits, affinity/anti-affinity rules, taints/tolerations) — it never actually starts anything. The kubelet, running on the chosen node, is what actually instructs the container runtime to pull the image and start the container, then continuously monitors and reports its health back to the API server.

03 Pods

What is it / why needed

A Pod is the smallest deployable unit in Kubernetes — NOT a container itself, but a wrapper around one or more containers that always get scheduled together on the same node, sharing the same network namespace (same IP, can reach each other via localhost) and optionally storage.

Why not just deploy containers directly?

Some workloads genuinely need multiple tightly-coupled containers sharing network/storage — e.g. a main app container plus a "sidecar" container doing log shipping or a service-mesh proxy. The Pod abstraction gives Kubernetes a stable unit that always co-schedules these together, rather than leaving that co-location up to chance.

apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: myapp-pod
  labels:
    app: myapp
spec:
  containers:
    - name: app
      image: myregistry/myapp:1.0
      ports:
        - containerPort: 8080
      resources:
        requests:                  # what the scheduler GUARANTEES this pod gets
          memory: "256Mi"
          cpu: "250m"
        limits:                    # hard ceiling — Module 9's cgroup limits again, K8s-managed
          memory: "512Mi"
          cpu: "500m"
      readinessProbe:                # covered in depth in Scheduling section
        httpGet:
          path: /actuator/health/readiness
          port: 8080
        initialDelaySeconds: 10
      livenessProbe:
        httpGet:
          path: /actuator/health/liveness
          port: 8080
        periodSeconds: 15
requests vs limits, asked constantly: requests is what the Scheduler uses to decide if a node has ENOUGH capacity to place this pod (a guarantee/reservation). limits is the hard ceiling the kubelet/cgroups enforce at runtime — exceed the memory limit and the pod gets OOMKilled (Module 9's cgroup mechanism again); exceed the CPU limit and it just gets throttled, not killed.

Common mistakes

  • Not setting requests/limits at all — the Scheduler can then overcommit a node, or one pod's runaway usage can starve every other pod on that node.
  • Setting requests == limits for CPU with no headroom — can cause unnecessary throttling under legitimate burst load; usually requests should reflect steady-state usage, with limits allowing some burst headroom.
  • Treating Pods as long-lived, precious entities to be manually managed — Pods are meant to be disposable/ephemeral; you manage them via a controller (Deployment) instead, never create bare Pods directly in production.

Interview Q

Q: What's the practical difference between a pod exceeding its memory limit vs exceeding its CPU limit?
A: Exceeding the memory limit gets the pod's container OOMKilled by the kernel's cgroup enforcement (Module 9's mechanism) — an abrupt, unrecoverable termination. Exceeding the CPU limit results in CPU throttling instead — the container isn't killed, it's just denied additional CPU time beyond its limit for that scheduling period, causing slowdowns rather than termination.

04 Deployments, ReplicaSets, StatefulSets

The layering, and why each layer exists

Deployment (you actually manage THIS — declares desired state + rollout strategy) │ creates/manages ▼ ReplicaSet (ensures exactly N pod replicas exist at any given moment — the reconciliation loop) │ creates/manages ▼ Pods (the actual running containers) Why the extra ReplicaSet layer? Deployments create a NEW ReplicaSet on every rollout (one per version), which is exactly how rolling updates and instant rollbacks work — the OLD ReplicaSet is scaled to 0 (not deleted) so rolling back is just re-scaling the previous ReplicaSet back up, near-instantly. 
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: myapp
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: myapp
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate:
      maxSurge: 1            # can create 1 EXTRA pod above desired count during rollout
      maxUnavailable: 0        # never go BELOW desired count — zero-downtime guarantee
  template:                    # this is the POD template — same spec structure as a standalone Pod
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: myapp
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: app
          image: myregistry/myapp:1.1
          ports:
            - containerPort: 8080

StatefulSets — for stateful workloads (databases, etc.)

Deployments assume pods are interchangeable/stateless — any replica can be replaced by any other. StatefulSets exist for workloads that need STABLE, unique identities: predictable pod names (myapp-0, myapp-1, myapp-2, not random suffixes), stable network identity, and stable per-pod storage that follows a specific pod even if it's rescheduled — essential for databases, Kafka, Elasticsearch, where "which specific instance holds this data" matters.

DeploymentStatefulSet
Pod identityInterchangeable, random suffix namesStable, ordinal names (pod-0, pod-1...)
StorageTypically shared or ephemeralEach pod gets its OWN persistent volume, tied to its identity permanently
Scaling orderAny order, parallelSequential (0, then 1, then 2...) — respects dependency ordering
Use caseStateless web apps, APIsDatabases, message queues, anything needing stable identity/storage

Common mistakes

  • Running a database as a plain Deployment — losing the stable identity/storage guarantees StatefulSet provides, risking data consistency issues on rescheduling.
  • Setting maxUnavailable > 0 for a critical low-replica-count service — during rollout, this can genuinely reduce serving capacity below what's needed, causing real user-facing errors.

Interview Q

Q: Why does a Deployment create a new ReplicaSet on every rollout instead of just updating the existing pods in place?
A: Keeping the OLD ReplicaSet around (scaled to 0 rather than deleted) is exactly what makes instant rollback possible — rolling back a bad deployment is just scaling the previous ReplicaSet back up and the new one down, which is near-instantaneous, rather than needing to somehow reconstruct the previous pod spec from scratch.

05 Services & Networking

What is it / why needed

Pods are ephemeral — they get new IPs every time they're recreated (a redeploy, a reschedule after node failure). Nothing should ever hardcode a pod's IP. A Service provides a STABLE virtual IP and DNS name that load-balances across whichever pods currently match its label selector — abstracting away pod churn entirely.

Service "myapp-svc" (stable virtual IP + DNS name "myapp-svc") │ selector: app=myapp (matches pods by LABEL, not by name/IP) │ ┌────┼────┬────────┐ ▼ ▼ ▼ [Pod A] [Pod B] [Pod C] ← pods come and go, get new IPs on reschedule; app=myapp app=myapp app=myapp Service traffic keeps routing correctly regardless, because it always re-evaluates the label selector
Service typeBehavior
ClusterIP (default)Internal-only virtual IP, reachable only from within the cluster
NodePortExposes the service on a static port on EVERY node's IP — a basic way to reach it from outside the cluster
LoadBalancerProvisions an actual cloud load balancer (e.g. an AWS NLB/ALB via the cloud provider's integration) pointing at the service
ExternalNamePure DNS-level alias to an external service outside the cluster — no proxying, just a CNAME-like redirect
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: myapp-svc
spec:
  selector:
    app: myapp            # matches pods with this label — this is the ONLY link, no direct pod references
  ports:
    - port: 80              # the Service's own port
      targetPort: 8080        # the port on the actual pod containers
  type: ClusterIP

How this actually gets implemented (kube-proxy)

kube-proxy on each node watches the API server for Service/Endpoint changes and programs the node's iptables (or IPVS) rules accordingly — so traffic to a Service's virtual IP gets transparently DNAT'd to one of the actual healthy backing pod IPs, entirely at the kernel networking level, no extra hop through a userspace proxy process for most modes.

Common mistakes

  • Selector label mismatch between Service and Deployment's pod template — a classic "Service has zero endpoints" bug (`kubectl get endpoints` shows empty), traffic just goes nowhere.
  • Confusing ClusterIP (internal only) with needing external exposure — using the wrong Service type for the actual requirement.

Interview Q

Q: Your Service has zero traffic reaching any pod, despite the Deployment showing 3/3 pods Running. First thing to check?
A: `kubectl get endpoints ` — if it shows no addresses, the Service's label selector doesn't match the pods' actual labels (a very common typo/mismatch). Fix by aligning the Service's selector with the Deployment template's pod labels exactly.

06 ConfigMaps, Secrets, Volumes

ObjectPurpose
ConfigMapNon-sensitive config (env vars, config files) decoupled from the container image — same image can run in dev/staging/prod with different ConfigMaps
SecretSensitive data (passwords, API keys, certs) — base64-ENCODED (not encrypted!) at rest by default unless encryption-at-rest is explicitly configured on the cluster
PersistentVolume (PV) / PersistentVolumeClaim (PVC)Kubernetes' abstraction over Module 9's Docker volumes — a PVC is a pod's REQUEST for storage; a PV is the actual provisioned storage (often dynamically provisioned via a StorageClass backed by, e.g., an AWS EBS volume)
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: myapp-config
data:
  SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE: "prod"
  LOG_LEVEL: "INFO"
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: myapp-secret
type: Opaque
data:
  DB_PASSWORD: c2VjcmV0cGFzcw==      # base64 of "secretpass" — NOT encryption, trivially decoded

# consuming both in a pod spec:
spec:
  containers:
    - name: app
      envFrom:
        - configMapRef:
            name: myapp-config
        - secretRef:
            name: myapp-secret
Secrets are base64, NOT encrypted, by default. Anyone with API access to read the Secret object (or etcd directly, if unencrypted at rest) can trivially decode it — base64 is an ENCODING, not encryption. Real production security requires: encryption-at-rest enabled for etcd, tight RBAC restricting who can read Secret objects, and often an external secrets manager (AWS Secrets Manager / HashiCorp Vault) with a K8s integration (e.g. External Secrets Operator) rather than relying on native Secrets alone.

Common mistakes

  • Assuming Kubernetes Secrets are inherently secure/encrypted just because they're a distinct object type from ConfigMap — the ONLY practical difference by default is base64 encoding and slightly more restrictive RBAC defaults, not actual encryption.
  • Baking environment-specific config into the container image itself instead of externalizing via ConfigMap — defeats "build once, deploy anywhere" and forces separate image builds per environment.

Interview Q

Q: Is storing a database password in a Kubernetes Secret sufficient security on its own?
A: Not by default — Secrets are base64-encoded, not encrypted, and trivially decodable by anyone who can read the object via the API or access etcd directly if it's not encrypted at rest. Real security requires enabling etcd encryption-at-rest, strict RBAC limiting who/what can read Secret objects, and often integrating an external, purpose-built secrets manager (AWS Secrets Manager, HashiCorp Vault) rather than relying on the native Secret object as the sole protection.

07 Scheduling, Probes & Self-Healing

Liveness vs Readiness vs Startup probes — asked in nearly every K8s interview

ProbeQuestion it answersFailure action
Liveness"Is this container still alive/functional, or stuck/deadlocked?"Kubelet KILLS and restarts the container
Readiness"Is this container ready to receive traffic RIGHT NOW?"Pod is removed from the Service's endpoints (no traffic sent) but NOT restarted — can recover and rejoin automatically
Startup"Has this slow-starting container finished its initial startup yet?"Disables liveness/readiness checks until startup succeeds — prevents a slow-starting app from being killed by an impatient liveness probe before it's even fully up
The classic mistake: using the SAME endpoint/logic for both liveness and readiness. If a pod is temporarily overloaded and slow to respond (but not actually broken), a shared check might fail readiness (correctly — stop sending it traffic) AND liveness (incorrectly — now it gets KILLED for being merely slow, potentially causing a cascading restart storm under load, the exact opposite of what you want during a traffic spike).

Self-healing in practice

  • Pod crashes → kubelet restarts it locally first (respecting a restart backoff), and if the whole NODE dies, the Node controller notices (missed heartbeats) and the Deployment's ReplicaSet controller reschedules its pods onto healthy nodes.
  • Node failure → after a configurable timeout (default ~5 min) with no heartbeat, the node is marked NotReady/Unreachable, and pods on it are evicted and rescheduled elsewhere.

Common mistakes

  • Sharing one endpoint for liveness and readiness — causes exactly the cascading-restart-under-load scenario above.
  • No startup probe (or too-aggressive liveness initialDelaySeconds) for a genuinely slow-starting JVM app — gets killed mid-startup in a loop, never actually reaching a running state.

Interview Q

Q: Why should liveness and readiness probes usually check different things, even for the same app?
A: Readiness should reflect "can I currently serve traffic well" — which might legitimately be false during a heavy load spike or while a downstream dependency is temporarily degraded, without meaning the app itself is broken. Liveness should reflect "is the process itself fundamentally stuck/deadlocked and needs a hard restart to recover." Using the same check for both means a temporary, recoverable slowdown (which should just pause traffic via readiness) instead triggers unnecessary container KILLS via liveness — potentially causing a pile of simultaneous restarts that make an already-struggling system worse.

08 Autoscaling (HPA)

What is it / how it works

The Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) automatically adjusts a Deployment's replica count based on observed metrics (typically CPU/memory utilization, or custom metrics like requests-per-second via an adapter). This is a reconciliation loop just like everything else in K8s — it periodically checks current metric values against a target and adjusts replicas to close the gap.

apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata:
  name: myapp-hpa
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: myapp
  minReplicas: 2
  maxReplicas: 10
  metrics:
    - type: Resource
      resource:
        name: cpu
        target:
          type: Utilization
          averageUtilization: 70    # scale up if average CPU usage across pods exceeds 70% of REQUESTED cpu
Requires the metrics-server (or a custom/external metrics adapter for non-CPU/memory metrics) to actually be running in the cluster — HPA has nothing to act on without it. This is a real, commonly forgotten prerequisite.

Trade-offs / considerations

  • Scaling based on CPU alone can be a poor proxy for actual load on I/O-bound or memory-bound apps — custom metrics (request queue depth, requests/sec) are often more accurate for real-world traffic patterns.
  • Scale-up is naturally faster to react than scale-down (which has stabilization windows to avoid flapping) — expected/tunable behavior, not a bug.

Interview Q

Q: You've configured an HPA targeting 70% CPU utilization, but it never scales up despite the app clearly struggling under load. What's likely missing?
A: The metrics-server (or appropriate metrics adapter) may not be installed/running in the cluster — the HPA has no metric source to evaluate against without it, so it silently has nothing to act on. Also verify resource REQUESTS are actually set on the pod spec, since utilization percentage is calculated relative to requested CPU, not an absolute value.

09 Rolling Updates & Rollbacks

kubectl set image deployment/myapp app=myregistry/myapp:1.2
kubectl rollout status deployment/myapp             # watch progress live
kubectl rollout history deployment/myapp               # see past revisions
kubectl rollout undo deployment/myapp                     # instant rollback to previous revision
kubectl rollout undo deployment/myapp --to-revision=3        # rollback to a SPECIFIC revision

Directly building on the ReplicaSet mechanics from earlier: a rollout creates a new ReplicaSet and gradually shifts pods from old to new, respecting maxSurge/maxUnavailable. A rollback is simply reversing that shift back to the previous (still-existing, scaled-to-0) ReplicaSet — which is why it's near-instant, not a fresh deploy from scratch.

Common mistakes

  • Not watching `rollout status` during a deploy in CI/CD — a broken rollout can silently hang partway (some new pods crash-looping) without the pipeline noticing unless it explicitly waits on this command's exit code.
  • Deleting old ReplicaSets manually "to clean up" — removes the instant-rollback safety net entirely.

Interview Q

Q: Why is `kubectl rollout undo` so much faster than redeploying the previous image version from scratch?
A: The previous version's ReplicaSet was never deleted during the rollout — just scaled down to 0. Rolling back is simply re-scaling that already-existing ReplicaSet back up (and the current one down), which requires no image pull or fresh scheduling decisions if those pods' specs are unchanged — a matter of seconds versus a full fresh deployment cycle.

★ Mini Project

Requires a local cluster: minikube, kind, or k3d.

minikube start

kubectl create configmap myapp-config --from-literal=LOG_LEVEL=INFO
kubectl create secret generic myapp-secret --from-literal=DB_PASSWORD=devpass

cat > deployment.yaml <<'EOF'
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: myapp
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels: {app: myapp}
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate: {maxSurge: 1, maxUnavailable: 0}
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: {app: myapp}
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: app
          image: myregistry/myapp:1.0
          ports: [{containerPort: 8080}]
          envFrom:
            - configMapRef: {name: myapp-config}
            - secretRef: {name: myapp-secret}
          resources:
            requests: {cpu: "100m", memory: "128Mi"}
            limits: {cpu: "250m", memory: "256Mi"}
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /actuator/health/readiness, port: 8080}
            initialDelaySeconds: 5
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /actuator/health/liveness, port: 8080}
            periodSeconds: 15
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: myapp-svc
spec:
  selector: {app: myapp}
  ports: [{port: 80, targetPort: 8080}]
EOF

kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
kubectl get pods -w
kubectl get endpoints myapp-svc

# self-healing proof
kubectl delete pod 
kubectl get pods -w         # watch it get replaced automatically

# rolling update + rollback proof
kubectl set image deployment/myapp app=myregistry/myapp:1.1
kubectl rollout status deployment/myapp
kubectl rollout undo deployment/myapp

# HPA (needs metrics-server enabled: minikube addons enable metrics-server)
kubectl autoscale deployment myapp --cpu-percent=70 --min=2 --max=10
kubectl get hpa -w

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Declarative model: you declare desired state, controllers continuously reconcile actual state toward it.
  • Control plane: API server (front door), etcd (source of truth, back it up!), Scheduler (placement only), Controller Manager (reconciliation loops).
  • Node: kubelet (runs pods via container runtime), kube-proxy (implements Service networking).
  • Pod = smallest unit, wraps 1+ containers sharing network/storage. requests = scheduler guarantee, limits = hard ceiling (OOMKill on memory, throttle on CPU).
  • Deployment → ReplicaSet (per-version) → Pods. Old ReplicaSets kept scaled-to-0 for instant rollback.
  • StatefulSet for stable identity + per-pod persistent storage (databases); Deployment for stateless/interchangeable pods.
  • Service = stable virtual IP/DNS, routes by label selector, NOT by pod name/IP. ClusterIP (internal) / NodePort / LoadBalancer / ExternalName.
  • ConfigMap = non-sensitive externalized config. Secret = base64 ONLY by default, not encrypted — needs etcd encryption + RBAC + often an external secrets manager for real security.
  • Liveness = restart if stuck. Readiness = pause traffic if temporarily not ready (no restart). Startup = grace period before liveness/readiness kick in.
  • HPA needs metrics-server (or adapter) running; scales based on requests-relative utilization.
  • Rollback = re-scaling the previous (still-existing) ReplicaSet — near-instant, not a fresh deploy.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What component actually starts a container on a chosen node?
The kubelet (via the container runtime).
2. What is the single source of truth for all cluster state?
etcd.
3. What happens when a pod exceeds its memory LIMIT?
It gets OOMKilled.
4. What happens when a pod exceeds its CPU LIMIT?
It gets throttled, not killed.
5. Why does a Deployment keep old ReplicaSets around (scaled to 0) instead of deleting them?
To enable near-instant rollback by simply re-scaling the old one back up.
6. How does a Service know which pods to route traffic to?
By matching its label selector against pod labels — not by pod name or IP.
7. Are Kubernetes Secrets encrypted by default?
No — base64 encoded only, unless etcd encryption-at-rest is explicitly configured.
8. What's the difference between a failing liveness probe and a failing readiness probe?
Failing liveness → container is killed and restarted. Failing readiness → pod is removed from Service endpoints (no traffic) but NOT restarted, can recover on its own.
9. What must be running in the cluster for HPA to function at all?
metrics-server (or an appropriate custom/external metrics adapter).
10. Which controller type is correct for a database needing stable identity and per-pod storage?
StatefulSet, not Deployment.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. A Service shows 3/3 pods Running but no traffic reaches any of them.
`kubectl get endpoints` likely shows zero — check Service selector matches pod labels exactly (common typo/mismatch).
2. A pod is stuck in CrashLoopBackOff.
`kubectl logs --previous` to see the crash reason from the last attempt; `kubectl describe pod` for events (OOMKilled, failed probes, image pull errors); check if a liveness probe is killing it before the app even finishes starting up (needs a startup probe or longer initialDelaySeconds).
3. A rollout hangs partway, some pods Running, some stuck Pending.
`kubectl describe pod` on a Pending one — commonly insufficient node resources for the requested CPU/memory, or an unsatisfied node affinity/taint rule; check `kubectl get nodes` capacity and `kubectl describe node` allocatable resources.
4. HPA shows target CPU metrics as "unknown" and never scales.
metrics-server isn't installed/running, or pods don't have resource requests set (utilization is calculated relative to requests) — check `kubectl top pods` works at all first as a sanity check.
5. After a node failure, pods that were on it don't get rescheduled for several minutes.
Expected — the Node controller waits for a configurable grace period (~5 min default, node-monitor-grace-period) before marking a node NotReady and evicting its pods, to avoid overreacting to a brief network blip; this is tunable but the default delay is by design, not a bug.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Deploy a 3-replica Deployment with proper requests/limits and both liveness+readiness probes; kill a pod and observe self-healing.
  2. Deliberately misconfigure a Service's selector, observe zero endpoints, then fix it and confirm traffic flows.
  3. Perform a rolling update with maxSurge=1/maxUnavailable=0, then deliberately roll out a broken image and roll back with `kubectl rollout undo`.
  4. Set up an HPA with metrics-server enabled and load-test the app (e.g. with `hey` or `ab`) to observe it scale up, then watch it scale back down.
  5. Create a ConfigMap and Secret, consume both in a pod via envFrom, and verify with `kubectl exec` that the values are actually present as env vars inside the container.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What is a Pod and why isn't it just "a container"?
A Pod is the smallest deployable unit in Kubernetes — a wrapper that can hold one or more containers that are always scheduled together on the same node, sharing the same network namespace (same IP) and optionally storage. It's distinct from "a container" because some workloads genuinely need multiple tightly-coupled containers (main app + sidecar) that must always run together, and Kubernetes needed an abstraction guaranteeing that co-location.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why do Services exist instead of apps just talking to pods directly by IP?
Pod IPs are not stable — pods get new IPs every time they're recreated (redeploy, reschedule after node failure, scaling). A Service provides a stable virtual IP and DNS name that continuously routes to whichever pods currently match its label selector, so consumers never need to track individual pod IPs at all.
Intermediate: Walk through exactly what happens when you run `kubectl apply` on a Deployment manifest with an updated image tag.
The API server persists the updated Deployment spec to etcd. The Deployment controller notices the pod template changed and creates a NEW ReplicaSet with the updated image (the old ReplicaSet is kept, scaled down gradually rather than deleted). Following the RollingUpdate strategy's maxSurge/maxUnavailable settings, new pods are created and old ones terminated incrementally — new pods must pass their readiness probe before being added to the Service's endpoints and receiving traffic, ensuring no user-facing downtime during the transition if configured correctly (maxUnavailable: 0).
Intermediate-Advanced: Design the probe configuration for a Spring Boot app with a 30-second JVM warm-up time and occasional 5-10 second GC pauses under load.
Use a startupProbe hitting a lightweight endpoint with enough failureThreshold × periodSeconds to comfortably cover the 30s warm-up (e.g. periodSeconds: 5, failureThreshold: 10 = 50s budget) — this prevents liveness from killing the app mid-startup. Once startup succeeds, liveness should check something minimal (is the JVM thread responsive at all) with a generous enough timeout/period to tolerate the known 5-10s GC pauses without false-triggering a restart (e.g. periodSeconds: 15, timeoutSeconds: 8, failureThreshold: 3, so only SUSTAINED unresponsiveness across ~45s triggers a kill). Readiness should be stricter and faster-reacting (shorter period) since briefly pulling a GC-paused pod out of rotation is cheap and safe, unlike killing it outright.
Advanced: A production incident — etcd for a self-managed Kubernetes cluster becomes corrupted with no recent backup. What's actually lost, and how would you have prevented this?
Everything that exists ONLY as declarative state in etcd is lost: all object specs (Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps, Secrets, current replica counts, rollout history) — effectively the cluster's entire configuration and desired-state history. Actually-running pods on healthy nodes may keep running in the short term (kubelets continue operating on their last-known state), but any reconciliation, scaling, or rescheduling stops working correctly, and a control-plane restart would leave the cluster with no memory of what should be running. Prevention: regular automated etcd snapshots/backups to durable external storage, ideally a multi-node etcd cluster for its own HA (odd number of members, e.g. 3 or 5, for quorum), and practicing restore drills — or, the pragmatic industry-standard answer for most teams: use a managed control plane (EKS) where the cloud provider handles etcd's HA and backup entirely, removing this failure mode from your team's operational responsibility.
Module 11 / 30 — Kubernetes. Next up: Helm — templating and packaging these YAML manifests so they're reusable across environments instead of hand-edited per deploy.
DevOps / SDE-1 Interview Track — Module 12 / 30

Helm, from first principles to production

Kubernetes' package manager — templating away the pain of hand-editing dozens of near-identical YAML files across dev/staging/prod, and versioning entire releases as a single unit.

01 Why Helm / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

A real app's Kubernetes manifests (Module 11) usually span a Deployment, Service, ConfigMap, Secret, HPA, Ingress — and you need SLIGHTLY different versions of all of them per environment (different replica counts, different resource limits, different image tags). Copy-pasting and hand-editing raw YAML across dev/staging/prod is exactly the kind of repetitive, error-prone task Module 10's Compose-overlay pattern was also trying to solve — Helm is the same idea, formalized and scaled up for Kubernetes specifically.

What problem it solves specifically

  • Templating — one parameterized set of YAML templates, values injected per environment.
  • Packaging — a whole application's manifests bundled as one versioned "chart," installable/upgradable/removable as a single unit.
  • Release management — Helm tracks EVERY install/upgrade as a numbered revision, enabling one-command rollback of an entire multi-resource release, not just a single Deployment (which is all `kubectl rollout undo` covers).
  • Reuse — public charts exist for virtually every common piece of infrastructure (Postgres, Redis, Prometheus, Nginx Ingress) — install a battle-tested, configurable chart instead of writing all that YAML from scratch.

Common mistakes

  • Thinking Helm is "just templating" — it's templating PLUS a full release/versioning system (Helm keeps its own revision history independent of what a bare `kubectl apply` gives you).

Interview Q

Q: What can Helm's rollback do that `kubectl rollout undo` can't?
A: `kubectl rollout undo` only rolls back a SINGLE Deployment (or other single rollout-capable resource) to its previous ReplicaSet. Helm tracks an entire RELEASE — potentially dozens of resources (Deployment, Service, ConfigMap, Secret, HPA, Ingress) — as one versioned unit, so `helm rollback` reverts ALL of them together to a previous consistent revision in one command, which plain kubectl has no native concept of.

02 Chart Anatomy

mychart/ ├── Chart.yaml ← metadata: name, version, description ├── values.yaml ← DEFAULT configuration values (overridable per install) ├── templates/ ← Kubernetes manifest TEMPLATES (Go templating + values injected) │ ├── deployment.yaml │ ├── service.yaml │ ├── configmap.yaml │ ├── hpa.yaml │ ├── _helpers.tpl ← reusable named template snippets (like functions) │ └── NOTES.txt ← printed to user after install/upgrade (usage hints) └── charts/ ← subcharts (dependencies), if any 
# Chart.yaml
apiVersion: v2
name: myapp
description: My Spring Boot app
version: 1.2.0            # CHART version — increments with template changes
appVersion: "1.1.0"          # APP version — the actual app/image version being deployed

# values.yaml (defaults)
replicaCount: 3
image:
  repository: myregistry/myapp
  tag: "1.1.0"
resources:
  requests: {cpu: "250m", memory: "256Mi"}
  limits: {cpu: "500m", memory: "512Mi"}
service:
  port: 80
autoscaling:
  enabled: true
  minReplicas: 2
  maxReplicas: 10
Chart version vs App version, a real distinction interviewers probe: Chart version tracks changes to the TEMPLATES themselves (e.g. you added a new ConfigMap to the chart). App version tracks the actual application/image being deployed. You can bump the app version (new image release) without any template changes at all, and vice versa (refactor templates without touching the app).

Common mistakes

  • Conflating chart version and app version, or forgetting to bump either when relevant — makes it unclear later exactly what changed between two chart versions.

Interview Q

Q: What's the difference between a chart's `version` and `appVersion` fields?
A: `version` tracks the Helm chart's OWN version — bumped whenever the templates/structure change. `appVersion` tracks the actual application/container image version being deployed by the chart — these can and do change independently of each other.

03 Templating Engine

# templates/deployment.yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: {{ .Release.Name }}-myapp
spec:
  replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: {{ .Release.Name }}-myapp
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: {{ .Release.Name }}-myapp
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: app
          image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"
          resources:
            requests: {{ toYaml .Values.resources.requests | nindent 12 }}
            limits: {{ toYaml .Values.resources.limits | nindent 12 }}
          {{- if .Values.autoscaling.enabled }}
          # (autoscaling handled by separate hpa.yaml template, conditionally rendered)
          {{- end }}
ObjectMeaning
.ValuesEverything from values.yaml (or overridden via --set / -f custom-values.yaml)
.ReleaseMetadata about THIS install (Release.Name, Release.Namespace, Release.IsUpgrade)
.ChartMetadata from Chart.yaml itself
{{- if }} / {{- end }}Conditional rendering — entire blocks can be included/excluded based on values
{{ toYaml ... | nindent N }}Common pattern: dump a values.yaml map/object as properly-indented YAML inline
helm template mychart/ -f values-prod.yaml    # RENDER locally without installing — always do this before deploying
helm install myapp mychart/ -f values-prod.yaml --set replicaCount=5
helm lint mychart/                                # catch template/schema issues before deploying
Always `helm template` before `helm install`/`upgrade` in any serious workflow. It renders the final YAML locally so you can review EXACTLY what will be applied to the cluster — catches templating logic errors, wrong indentation, and unexpected value substitutions before they ever touch a live cluster.

Common mistakes

  • Never running `helm template`/`helm lint` before deploying — templating bugs (bad indentation from a missing nindent, wrong conditional logic) only get caught live in the cluster, sometimes mid-outage.
  • Hardcoding environment-specific values directly in templates instead of parameterizing via values.yaml — defeats the entire point of using Helm.

Interview Q

Q: Why should you run `helm template` before every `helm upgrade` in a CI/CD pipeline?
A: It renders the fully substituted YAML manifests locally (no cluster interaction) so you can review, diff, or validate the EXACT output that would be applied — catching templating bugs, bad value substitutions, or unexpected conditional logic before they're ever sent to a live cluster, rather than discovering a broken manifest only after `helm upgrade` has already started rolling it out.

04 Releases & Lifecycle

helm install myapp mychart/                 # first install → creates release "myapp", revision 1
helm upgrade myapp mychart/ --set image.tag=1.2.0   # revision 2
helm history myapp                                    # see all revisions
helm rollback myapp 1                                   # revert ENTIRE release back to revision 1
helm uninstall myapp                                      # remove everything the release created
helm list                                                   # all releases in current namespace
helm status myapp                                             # current state of the release

Every install/upgrade is stored as a Secret (by default) in the cluster containing the fully rendered manifest for that revision — this is literally how `helm rollback` works: it re-applies the exact previously-rendered manifest from that stored revision, not a re-templating from scratch.

Common mistakes

  • Manually `kubectl edit`-ing resources that Helm manages — Helm has no visibility into out-of-band changes, so the next `helm upgrade` can silently overwrite/revert your manual edit, or worse, drift detection becomes impossible.
  • Not pinning a specific chart version when installing from a public repo — an upstream chart update could silently change behavior on your next `helm upgrade` if you're tracking "latest" implicitly.

Interview Q

Q: If someone manually edits a Deployment that Helm manages (via kubectl edit), what happens on the next `helm upgrade`?
A: Helm has no awareness of that out-of-band change — it will apply its own rendered manifest based on the chart's templates and values, which can silently overwrite the manual edit (since Helm doesn't merge external drift, it reconciles toward its own known state). This is why manual kubectl edits on Helm-managed resources are a real anti-pattern — all changes should go through the chart/values.

★ Mini Project

helm create myapp                     # scaffolds a starter chart with sensible defaults
cd myapp

# edit values.yaml to point at your actual image
# edit templates/deployment.yaml, service.yaml as needed (or use the scaffolded defaults)

helm lint .
helm template . -f values.yaml           # review rendered output BEFORE installing

helm install myapp-dev . -f values.yaml --namespace dev --create-namespace
helm status myapp-dev
kubectl get all -n dev

# simulate a version bump
helm upgrade myapp-dev . --set image.tag=1.1.0 -n dev
helm history myapp-dev -n dev

# simulate a bad deploy and roll back the WHOLE release
helm upgrade myapp-dev . --set image.tag=broken-tag -n dev
helm rollback myapp-dev 1 -n dev
helm history myapp-dev -n dev

helm uninstall myapp-dev -n dev

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Helm = templating + packaging + release/revision management for Kubernetes manifests.
  • Chart structure: Chart.yaml (metadata), values.yaml (defaults), templates/ (Go-templated manifests).
  • Chart version (template changes) vs appVersion (deployed app/image version) — independent.
  • Always `helm lint` + `helm template` before install/upgrade — review rendered output first.
  • `helm rollback` reverts an ENTIRE multi-resource release to a previous revision, unlike `kubectl rollout undo` (single Deployment only).
  • Every revision is stored (as a Secret by default) with the fully rendered manifest — rollback reapplies that stored manifest exactly.
  • Never manually kubectl-edit Helm-managed resources — causes silent drift/overwrite on next upgrade.
  • Pin specific chart versions from public repos to avoid silent upstream behavior changes.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What file holds a chart's default configuration values?
values.yaml
2. What command renders templates locally without touching the cluster?
helm template
3. Difference between chart `version` and `appVersion`?
version = the chart/template's own version. appVersion = the actual application/image version being deployed — independent of each other.
4. Does `helm rollback` re-template from the chart source, or reapply a stored manifest?
Reapplies the exact previously-rendered manifest stored for that revision.
5. What can `helm rollback` revert that `kubectl rollout undo` can't?
An entire multi-resource release (Deployment + Service + ConfigMap + etc.) as one unit, not just a single Deployment.
6. Where does Helm store release revision history by default?
As Secret objects in the cluster.
7. What's a real risk of manually `kubectl edit`-ing a Helm-managed resource?
The next helm upgrade can silently overwrite the manual change, since Helm has no visibility into out-of-band drift.
8. What does `helm lint` check?
Chart structure and template/schema issues before actually deploying.
9. What Helm templating syntax injects a values.yaml map as indented YAML?
{{ toYaml .Values.someMap | nindent N }}
10. What's the risk of installing a public Helm chart without pinning a version?
An upstream chart update could silently change behavior the next time you upgrade/reinstall, since you're implicitly tracking "latest."

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. `helm install` fails with a YAML indentation error deep in a template.
Run `helm template` locally first to see the fully rendered output and pinpoint exactly where the indentation breaks — usually a missing/incorrect `nindent` value on a toYaml call.
2. After `helm upgrade`, a manually-added label on a Service disappeared.
Someone kubectl-edited that resource outside of Helm; the upgrade reconciled it back to exactly what the chart/values define, silently discarding the manual change — the label needs to be added properly via values/templates instead.
3. `helm rollback` succeeds but the app is still running the broken version.
Check whether the underlying Deployment's rollout actually completed (`kubectl rollout status`) — Helm rollback reapplies the manifest, but the actual pod-level rolling update still needs to finish; also verify you rolled back to the correct revision number via `helm history`.
4. Two different environments (staging/prod) are silently running different chart behavior despite using "the same chart."
Likely tracking an unpinned/floating chart version from a repo, and each environment happened to install/upgrade at different times against different upstream chart versions — pin an explicit chart version going forward.
5. A values override passed via --set isn't taking effect at all.
Check the exact dot-path matches the values.yaml structure precisely (--set is easy to typo on nested keys, e.g. resources.requests.cpu vs resources.request.cpu), and confirm no -f values file listed AFTER the --set is overriding it back (flag order/precedence matters).

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Scaffold a chart with `helm create`, strip it down to a minimal Deployment+Service+ConfigMap, and parameterize replica count and image tag via values.yaml.
  2. Create separate values-dev.yaml and values-prod.yaml (different replica counts/resource limits) and deploy both, confirming via `helm template` that the diffs are exactly as expected.
  3. Practice the full upgrade → history → rollback cycle, verifying with `kubectl get pods` that the actual running version matches what `helm history` claims at each step.
  4. Add a conditional block (an optional HPA template that only renders if `.Values.autoscaling.enabled` is true) and test both states.
  5. Install a real public chart (e.g. bitnami/postgresql) with a pinned version, customize key values, and inspect the rendered output with `helm template` before actually installing.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What problem does Helm solve that plain `kubectl apply` on YAML files doesn't?
Plain YAML files are static — every environment difference (replica count, resource limits, image tag) requires hand-editing or maintaining fully duplicated files. Helm templates parameterize these differences via one set of values files, and additionally tracks every install/upgrade as a versioned release, enabling whole-release rollback that plain kubectl apply has no concept of.
Beginner-Intermediate: Explain the relationship between a Helm chart, a release, and a revision.
A chart is the packaged template (reusable, like a class/blueprint). A release is a specific INSTALLATION of that chart into a cluster/namespace with a given set of values (an instance of that blueprint, given a name). A revision is a numbered snapshot of that release's state at a point in time — every install/upgrade creates a new revision, enabling rollback to any previous one.
Intermediate: Your team wants to deploy the same application to dev, staging, and prod with different resource limits and replica counts, but avoid maintaining three near-duplicate sets of raw Kubernetes YAML. How would Helm solve this?
Maintain a single chart with templates parameterized against values.yaml for everything that varies (replicas, resource requests/limits, image tag). Create separate values-dev.yaml, values-staging.yaml, values-prod.yaml files containing only the environment-specific overrides layered on top of the chart's defaults. Deploy each environment with `helm install/upgrade -f values-.yaml` — one source of template truth, environment differences isolated to small values files rather than duplicated manifests.
Intermediate-Advanced: What are the risks of relying purely on Helm's default templating without any additional validation in a CI/CD pipeline, and how would you mitigate them?
Risks: a templating logic error (bad conditional, missing indentation) could render invalid or unintended YAML that only fails at apply-time against the live cluster — potentially mid-deployment. A values override typo could silently apply wrong resource limits or the wrong image tag without any error at all, since Helm doesn't validate values against a schema by default unless you define one. Mitigation: run `helm lint` and `helm template` in CI BEFORE any `helm upgrade` step, diff the rendered output against the previous revision's manifest for unexpected changes, define a values.schema.json for the chart to catch invalid/missing values at template time, and gate the actual upgrade behind that validation passing.
Advanced: Design a GitOps-friendly Helm workflow for a team practicing continuous deployment, and explain what Helm alone still doesn't solve.
Store chart + per-environment values files in git as the single source of truth; CI validates (lint/template/schema-check) on every PR; on merge, a deployment pipeline (or GitOps tool like ArgoCD, covered in Module 29) runs `helm upgrade` targeting the appropriate cluster/namespace, with the actual `git` commit as the auditable trigger for every change. What Helm alone doesn't solve: it has no built-in drift detection (if someone manually changes a live resource, Helm won't proactively notice or alert — it just silently overwrites on the next upgrade), no continuous reconciliation loop of its own (it's push-based, only acts when you explicitly run helm commands), and no native progressive-delivery capabilities (canary/blue-green traffic shifting) — these gaps are exactly why GitOps tools like ArgoCD are often layered ON TOP of Helm charts in mature production setups, adding continuous drift detection and reconciliation that Helm itself doesn't provide.
Module 12 / 30 — Helm. Next up: AWS IAM — leaving the Kubernetes/container world to build the AWS foundation everything from EC2 through EKS sits on.
DevOps / SDE-1 Interview Track — Module 13 / 30

AWS IAM, from first principles to production

Leaving the container world for AWS. IAM is the foundation every other AWS service (EC2, S3, EKS, everything) sits on top of for access control — get this right first or nothing else in the AWS block makes sense.
