01 Why IAM / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

IAM (Identity and Access Management) is AWS's system for controlling WHO (identity) can do WHAT (actions) on WHICH resources, and under WHAT conditions. It exists because AWS accounts hold enormously destructive power by default (spin up/delete any resource, read any data) — without fine-grained access control, every person/service touching your AWS account would need full account access, an unacceptable blast radius for any mistake or compromise.

The core principle: least privilege

Grant EXACTLY the permissions needed for a task, no more. This is the AWS-scale version of Module 1's "don't run processes as root" principle — applied to human users, applications, and AWS services themselves.

Common mistakes

  • Using the AWS account's root user for daily work — root has unrestrictable full access (including billing, account closure); should be locked away with MFA and used only for a handful of account-level tasks.
  • Granting AdministratorAccess to everyone "to unblock work faster" — defeats the entire purpose of IAM and turns any single compromised credential into a full account compromise.

Interview Q

Q: Why shouldn't you use the AWS root account for everyday operations?
A: The root user has unrestrictable, unremovable full access to everything in the account, including billing and the ability to close the account entirely — no IAM policy can limit it. Using it daily massively increases blast radius if credentials leak, and prevents the granular access control/audit trail that IAM users/roles provide. Best practice: lock root away with MFA, use it only for the few tasks that genuinely require it (like changing account settings), and do all daily work through IAM users/roles with least-privilege policies.

02 Users, Groups, Roles, Policies

ConceptWhat it is
IAM UserA permanent identity representing a person or a specific application — has its own long-term credentials (password and/or access keys)
IAM GroupA collection of users — attach a policy to the group, every member inherits it. Pure organizational convenience, not itself an identity that can be assumed
IAM RoleAn identity with NO long-term credentials of its own — must be ASSUMED (temporarily) by a user, an AWS service (EC2, Lambda), or another account, receiving short-lived temporary credentials
PolicyA JSON document defining exactly which actions are Allowed/Denied on which resources — attached to users, groups, or roles
Users vs Roles, the single most important distinction: a User has permanent credentials that exist until explicitly rotated/deleted — a real, standing liability if leaked. A Role has NO standing credentials at all; anyone/anything that assumes it gets temporary credentials (expire automatically, typically within an hour) via AWS STS (Security Token Service). This is why production AWS architecture strongly prefers roles over long-lived user access keys wherever possible — an EC2 instance, a Lambda function, or an EKS pod should assume a ROLE, never carry a user's static access keys.

Common mistakes

  • Creating an IAM user with access keys for an application to use, instead of an IAM role the application's compute (EC2/Lambda/EKS) assumes — static keys can leak (committed to git, per Module 3's incident scenario) and don't expire on their own.
  • Attaching policies directly to individual users instead of to a group — becomes unmanageable as team size grows; every permission change needs repeating per user.

Interview Q

Q: Why is an IAM Role generally preferred over an IAM User with access keys for an application running on EC2?
A: A Role has no long-lived credentials at all — the EC2 instance assumes it and receives short-lived, automatically-rotating temporary credentials via STS, with no static secret ever needing to be stored on the instance or in application config. A User's access keys are permanent until manually rotated, creating a standing secret that could leak (accidentally committed to git, exposed in logs) and remain valid indefinitely if not caught.

03 Policy Anatomy & Evaluation Logic

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "AllowS3ReadOnMyBucket",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:ListBucket"],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:s3:::my-app-bucket",
        "arn:aws:s3:::my-app-bucket/*"
      ],
      "Condition": {
        "IpAddress": {"aws:SourceIp": "203.0.113.0/24"}
      }
    }
  ]
}
FieldMeaning
EffectAllow or Deny
ActionWhich API calls this applies to (e.g. s3:GetObject)
ResourceWhich specific ARNs (Amazon Resource Names) this applies to — never use "*" in production unless truly intended for all resources
ConditionExtra constraints — IP range, time of day, MFA presence, tags, etc.

Policy evaluation logic — asked in nearly every IAM interview

Evaluation order for any single request: 1. Default: implicit DENY (nothing is allowed unless explicitly granted) 2. Check all applicable policies (identity-based + resource-based + SCPs + permission boundaries) 3. Is there an EXPLICIT DENY anywhere? → DENY wins, full stop, no matter how many Allows exist elsewhere 4. Is there an explicit ALLOW? → ALLOWED (assuming no explicit deny found in step 3) 5. Otherwise → implicit DENY (nothing matched) RULE OF THUMB: explicit DENY always wins. Otherwise, need at least one explicit ALLOW.

Common mistakes

  • Using wildcard Resource ("*") or Action ("*") in production policies "to get things working faster" — massively over-permissions, a top security audit finding.
  • Not realizing an explicit Deny in ANY attached policy overrides every Allow elsewhere — debugging "why am I denied despite having an Allow policy" requires checking ALL attached policies for a hidden Deny, not just the obvious one.

Interview Q

Q: A user has an IAM policy explicitly allowing s3:GetObject on a bucket, but a separate policy attached to the same user has an explicit Deny for the same action. What happens?
A: The explicit Deny wins, unconditionally — AWS's evaluation logic always prioritizes any explicit Deny over any Allow, regardless of how many other policies grant access. The user is denied.

04 Roles & Trust Policies

What is it / how it works internally

A Role has TWO policy types attached: a trust policy (who/what is ALLOWED to assume this role) and one or more permission policies (what the role can actually DO once assumed). This is a frequently confused distinction — the trust policy controls assumption, the permission policy controls capability.

# Trust policy — WHO can assume this role (attached to the role itself)
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": {"Service": "ec2.amazonaws.com"},   # only EC2 instances can assume this
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRole"
  }]
}

# Permission policy — WHAT the role can do once assumed (attached separately)
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": ["s3:GetObject"],
    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-bucket/*"
  }]
}
EC2 instance ──assumes──► IAM Role ──via STS──► temporary credentials (auto-rotated, ~1hr) │ └── permission policy defines what those credentials can actually DO For EC2: this happens via an "instance profile" — a wrapper connecting the role to the instance, so the app running on it can just call AWS SDK methods with NO credentials configured at all; the SDK automatically fetches temporary creds from the instance metadata service.

Cross-account and service-to-service roles

The Principal in a trust policy can be another AWS account, a specific AWS service (Lambda, EC2, EKS), or a federated identity provider (e.g. for EKS's IRSA — IAM Roles for Service Accounts, letting individual Kubernetes pods assume distinct IAM roles, covered in Module 21).

Common mistakes

  • Confusing "who can assume this role" (trust policy) with "what this role can do" (permission policy) — a common point of confusion when debugging AssumeRole failures.
  • Overly broad trust policies (e.g. Principal: "*") — allows ANY AWS account/entity to attempt assuming the role, a serious security gap if the permission policy is at all powerful.

Interview Q

Q: Your EC2 instance's application gets "Access Denied" calling an AWS API, even though the attached IAM role's permission policy clearly allows that action. What else should you check?
A: Verify the role's TRUST POLICY actually allows EC2 (Principal: {"Service": "ec2.amazonaws.com"}) to assume it in the first place, and confirm the instance actually has that role attached via an instance profile — a permission policy granting an action is irrelevant if the role was never successfully assumed to begin with.

05 Production Best Practices

PracticeWhy
MFA on root and privileged usersEven a leaked password alone isn't enough to authenticate
Roles over long-lived access keysNo standing secret to leak; auto-expiring credentials
Least privilege, scoped policiesLimits blast radius of any single compromised identity
IAM Access AnalyzerAWS-native tool that flags overly permissive policies and unintended external access
Permission boundariesA hard ceiling on the MAXIMUM permissions a role/user can ever have, even if its attached policies are broader — useful for letting teams self-manage roles within a safe bound
Service Control Policies (SCPs)Org-wide guardrails applied across multiple AWS accounts (via AWS Organizations) — even account admins can't exceed an SCP's limits
Regular access key rotation + unused credential cleanupIAM Credential Reports flag keys unused for 90+ days — a real, commonly-audited finding

Production commands (AWS CLI)

aws sts get-caller-identity                 # who am I, right now, as far as AWS is concerned
aws iam list-attached-user-policies --user-name asmit
aws iam simulate-principal-policy --policy-source-arn  --action-names s3:GetObject   # test BEFORE granting
aws iam generate-credential-report && aws iam get-credential-report    # audit stale/unused keys
aws iam get-account-authorization-details      # full dump of everything for auditing

Common mistakes

  • Never rotating long-lived access keys — an old leaked key from years ago can still be silently valid and exploitable.
  • Not using `simulate-principal-policy` before granting broad access "just to test" — leads to over-permissioning that never gets walked back.

Interview Q

Q: What's the difference between a permission boundary and a regular IAM policy?
A: A regular policy GRANTS permissions when attached. A permission boundary sets a MAXIMUM ceiling — even if a user/role's attached policies grant broader access, the permission boundary caps what's actually effective, intersecting with (never exceeding) the boundary. It's used to safely let teams create/manage their own roles within an org-defined limit, without risking privilege escalation beyond that ceiling.

★ Mini Project

# Create a least-privilege role for an EC2 instance that only needs to read from one S3 bucket

# 1. Trust policy (who can assume it)
cat > trust-policy.json <<'EOF'
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": {"Service": "ec2.amazonaws.com"},
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRole"
  }]
}
EOF

aws iam create-role --role-name EC2-S3ReadOnly --assume-role-policy-document file://trust-policy.json

# 2. Permission policy (what it can do)
cat > s3-read-policy.json <<'EOF'
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:ListBucket"],
    "Resource": [
      "arn:aws:s3:::my-app-bucket",
      "arn:aws:s3:::my-app-bucket/*"
    ]
  }]
}
EOF

aws iam put-role-policy --role-name EC2-S3ReadOnly --policy-name S3ReadOnly --policy-document file://s3-read-policy.json

# 3. Create an instance profile and attach the role
aws iam create-instance-profile --instance-profile-name EC2-S3ReadOnly-Profile
aws iam add-role-to-instance-profile --instance-profile-name EC2-S3ReadOnly-Profile --role-name EC2-S3ReadOnly

# 4. Launch (or attach to an existing) EC2 instance with this instance profile,
#    then from INSIDE the instance, prove no credentials are configured yet it still works:
aws s3 ls s3://my-app-bucket/       # works — SDK auto-fetches temp creds from instance metadata
aws s3 cp somefile.txt s3://my-app-bucket/   # should FAIL — no s3:PutObject in the policy, proving least privilege

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Least privilege = grant exactly what's needed, nothing more. Root account locked away with MFA, never used daily.
  • User = permanent identity/credentials. Group = policy-attachment convenience for users. Role = temporary, assumed identity, no standing credentials.
  • Prefer Roles over Users-with-access-keys for any application/compute — no static secret to leak, auto-expiring via STS.
  • Policy evaluation: default implicit deny → explicit Deny ALWAYS wins → else need explicit Allow → else implicit deny.
  • Role has TWO policy types: trust policy (who can assume it) + permission policy (what it can do once assumed) — different concerns, both required.
  • Permission boundary = hard ceiling on max possible permissions, even if attached policies are broader.
  • SCPs = org-wide guardrails across multiple AWS accounts, even admins can't exceed them.
  • Use `simulate-principal-policy` to test permissions before granting broadly; rotate/audit access keys regularly.

Q 10 MCQs

1. Does an IAM Role have long-lived credentials?
No — it has no standing credentials; assuming it produces short-lived temporary credentials via STS.
2. What wins if one policy has Allow and another has explicit Deny for the same action?
The explicit Deny always wins.
3. What's the default access level if no policy explicitly allows an action?
Implicit deny.
4. What does a trust policy control?
Who/what is allowed to assume a given role.
5. What does a permission policy control?
What actions/resources the role can actually access once assumed.
6. What connects an IAM role to an EC2 instance?
An instance profile.
7. What AWS service issues temporary credentials when a role is assumed?
STS (Security Token Service).
8. What does a permission boundary do that a regular policy doesn't?
Sets a maximum ceiling on effective permissions, regardless of how broad attached policies are.
9. What tool lets you test whether a policy would allow a specific action before granting it?
aws iam simulate-principal-policy.
10. Should the AWS root account be used for daily development work?
No — it has unrestrictable full access; should be locked with MFA and reserved for a few account-level tasks only.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. An EC2 app gets "Access Denied" calling S3 despite the attached role's policy allowing it.
Check the role's trust policy actually permits ec2.amazonaws.com to assume it, confirm the instance profile is genuinely attached to the instance, and check for a Deny in ANY other attached policy (SCP, permission boundary) that might be overriding the Allow.
2. A user can perform an action in one AWS account but the same role fails in another account.
Check for account-level SCPs (via AWS Organizations) that may restrict actions in the second account regardless of the role's own permission policy — SCPs apply an additional ceiling on top of IAM.
3. A newly created IAM user can't do anything at all, even basic actions.
Confirm at least one policy (directly attached or via group membership) actually grants an explicit Allow — a user with zero attached policies has only the default implicit deny for everything.
4. `aws sts assume-role` fails with an access denied error.
The calling identity likely isn't listed as an allowed Principal in the target role's trust policy — check the trust policy's Principal field matches the actual caller's identity/account/service.
5. A security audit flags several IAM users with access keys unused for over a year.
Generate and review an IAM credential report (aws iam generate-credential-report / get-credential-report) to identify and deactivate/delete genuinely unused keys — standing unused credentials are pure unnecessary risk with zero business benefit.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Create an IAM role with a trust policy scoped to EC2 and a permission policy allowing only read access to one specific S3 bucket; verify write access is correctly denied.
  2. Write two conflicting policies (one Allow, one explicit Deny for the same action) and attach both to a test user; verify the Deny wins.
  3. Use `aws iam simulate-principal-policy` to test a proposed policy's effect BEFORE actually attaching it to a real user.
  4. Create a permission boundary that caps a role to read-only S3 access, then attach a broader (e.g. full S3) permission policy to that role and verify the boundary still restricts it to read-only.
  5. Generate an IAM credential report for a test account and identify any access keys older than 90 days.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What's the difference between an IAM User and an IAM Role?
A User is a permanent identity with its own long-term credentials (password/access keys), typically representing a person or a specific application needing standing access. A Role has no standing credentials at all — it must be assumed (by a user, AWS service, or another account) to receive short-lived, auto-expiring temporary credentials via STS.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why is granting a wildcard ("*") Resource or Action in an IAM policy considered risky?
It grants far more access than almost any real task requires, violating least privilege — if that identity's credentials are ever compromised, or the policy is misapplied elsewhere, the blast radius is the entire account/service scope rather than a narrow, intended set of resources.
Intermediate: Explain how an EC2 instance can call AWS APIs securely without ever having credentials stored on it.
An IAM role is created with a trust policy allowing the EC2 service to assume it, and attached to the instance via an instance profile. The AWS SDK running on the instance automatically fetches short-lived temporary credentials from the instance metadata service (which internally calls STS on the role's behalf) — no access keys are ever manually configured or stored on the instance, and the credentials rotate automatically roughly every hour.
Intermediate-Advanced: A team wants developers to be able to create and manage their own IAM roles for their projects, but the security team is worried about privilege escalation. How would you design this safely?
Attach a permission boundary to any role/user that developers are allowed to create — this sets a hard ceiling on the MAXIMUM effective permissions any role they create can ever have, regardless of how permissive the actual attached policy is. Developers get self-service flexibility to iterate quickly, while the security team's boundary guarantees no role can ever escalate beyond an org-approved limit (e.g. never able to touch IAM itself, never able to access resources outside their project's tagged scope) — combined with SCPs at the AWS Organizations level for an even broader account-wide guardrail that developers can't touch at all.
Advanced: Design the IAM strategy for a multi-account AWS Organization (separate accounts for dev/staging/prod) with a central CI/CD pipeline that needs to deploy to all three.
Each environment lives in its own AWS account for blast-radius isolation (a mistake in dev can never touch prod resources at all, since they're different accounts entirely). The CI/CD pipeline (running in its own dedicated tooling account) uses cross-account IAM roles: a deploy role exists in EACH target account (dev/staging/prod) with a trust policy allowing ONLY the CI/CD account's specific role to assume it, and each has a permission policy scoped tightly to exactly what deployment requires in that environment (e.g. can update ECS/EKS resources and read from ECR, but can't touch IAM or billing). The CI/CD pipeline itself never holds long-lived credentials for any target account — it assumes the appropriate cross-account role per deployment target via STS, and Organization-level SCPs enforce hard guardrails (e.g. prod account can never be modified outside specific approved actions) that apply even if a role's own policy were somehow misconfigured to be broader.

01 What is EC2 / Instance Types

What is it / why needed

EC2 (Elastic Compute Cloud) rents virtual machines by the second/hour, backed by AWS's own hypervisor (Nitro). It exists because before cloud compute, running a server meant buying physical hardware, racking it, and over-provisioning for peak load you might rarely hit — EC2 lets you provision exactly the compute you need, scale it up/down, and pay only for what you use.

Instance familyOptimized forExample use
T (t3, t4g)Burstable, general purpose, cheap baseline with burst creditsDev/test, low-traffic web apps
M (m5, m6i)Balanced compute/memory, general purposeTypical application servers, Spring Boot APIs
C (c5, c6i)Compute-optimized (high CPU:memory ratio)CPU-heavy batch processing, video encoding
R (r5, r6i)Memory-optimizedIn-memory caches, large databases
I (i3, i4i)Storage-optimized, high IOPS local NVMeDatabases needing fast local disk
T-family "burstable" gotcha, commonly asked: T-instances earn CPU credits during idle time and spend them during bursts. Sustained high CPU usage exhausts credits, after which performance is throttled hard (or billed extra in "unlimited" mode) — a real production surprise for teams that pick a cheap t3.micro for a workload that turns out to be consistently CPU-heavy.

Common mistakes

  • Choosing a T-family instance for a consistently CPU-intensive workload "because it's cheap" — credit exhaustion causes severe, confusing throttling under sustained load.
  • Over-provisioning "just in case" instead of right-sizing based on actual CloudWatch metrics (Module 22) — wastes budget at scale.

Interview Q

Q: Your t3.micro-based API was fine in testing but starts timing out intermittently under sustained real production load. Why?
A: T-family instances are burstable — they accumulate CPU credits during low-usage periods and spend them during bursts, but SUSTAINED high CPU usage exhausts those credits, after which the instance is throttled to its baseline performance level (far below burst capacity), causing exactly this kind of "fine in short tests, degrades under real sustained load" pattern. Fix: switch to a non-burstable family (M/C) sized for the actual sustained load, or enable "unlimited" mode (at extra cost) if bursts are only occasional.

02 EBS vs Instance Store

EBS (Elastic Block Store)Instance Store
PersistencePersists independently of the instance lifecycle (survives stop/start, can be detached/reattached)Ephemeral — physically attached to the host; DATA IS LOST on stop or termination (not just reboot)
PerformanceNetwork-attached (slight latency), but supports snapshotting, resizing, various IOPS tiersDirectly attached NVMe — much lower latency, higher IOPS
Use caseRoot volumes, databases, anything needing persistenceScratch space, cache, temporary processing data where loss on restart is acceptable
Real production incident pattern: storing a database's data directory on instance store because "it's faster," then a routine instance replacement (auto-scaling, or an AWS-initiated instance retirement) wipes all data instantly and irrecoverably — this is the exact Module 9 Docker-volumes lesson, replayed at the EC2 level.

EBS volume types

TypeBest for
gp3 (general purpose SSD)Default choice for most workloads — balanced cost/performance
io2 (provisioned IOPS SSD)High-performance databases needing guaranteed, consistent IOPS
st1 (throughput HDD)Big sequential workloads (data warehousing, log processing), cheaper per GB

Interview Q

Q: Why is it dangerous to run a production database's data directory on EC2 instance store instead of EBS?
A: Instance store is physically tied to the underlying host hardware and is wiped whenever the instance is stopped or terminated (not just rebooted) — a routine event like an Auto Scaling replacement or AWS instance retirement would silently and irrecoverably destroy all database data. EBS persists independently of the instance lifecycle and can be detached/reattached/snapshotted, making it the correct choice for anything requiring durability.

03 AMIs & User Data

What is it / why needed

An AMI (Amazon Machine Image) is a snapshot template — OS, installed packages, and configuration — used to launch new instances identically. Building a custom "golden AMI" bakes your app/dependencies in ahead of time, making instance launches fast and consistent (directly parallel to Module 9's point about pre-built container images vs installing everything at runtime).

User data — first-boot automation (revisits Module 1's boot process)

#!/bin/bash
# EC2 user-data script — runs once via cloud-init on first boot (Module 1 concept, applied)
yum update -y
yum install -y docker
systemctl enable --now docker
docker run -d -p 8080:8080 myregistry/myapp:1.0

As covered in Module 1: if user-data fails silently, check /var/log/cloud-init-output.log first — the most commonly forgotten debugging step for "instance came up but my app didn't start."

Golden AMI vs user-data-only approach

Golden AMI (pre-baked)User-data only (configure at boot)
Boot speedFast — everything already installedSlower — installs/configures on every launch
ConsistencyVery high — exact same image every timeLower — depends on package repos being reachable/unchanged at boot time
Update processRebuild and roll out a new AMI version (often via Packer)Just edit the user-data script

Common mistakes

  • Relying purely on user-data for a production fleet — if a package repo is briefly down during a scale-out event, new instances fail to configure correctly at the worst possible time (under load, needing MORE capacity).
  • Not checking cloud-init logs when an instance "looks fine" (passes status checks) but the app never actually started — status checks only confirm the OS/networking layer is healthy, not that your user-data script succeeded.

Interview Q

Q: Why might a production team prefer a pre-baked "golden AMI" over configuring everything via user-data at boot time?
A: Golden AMIs boot faster (nothing to install at launch) and are more consistent/reliable (no dependency on external package repos being reachable at that exact moment) — critical during an Auto Scaling event where new capacity is needed URGENTLY under load; a flaky package repo at that moment with a user-data-only approach could mean new instances fail to become healthy exactly when they're needed most.

04 Security Groups

What is it / how it works

A Security Group is a virtual, STATEFUL firewall attached to an instance's network interface. "Stateful" means if you allow inbound traffic on a port, the corresponding RETURN traffic is automatically allowed outbound without needing a separate explicit rule — unlike NACLs (Module 15), which are stateless.

PropertyBehavior
Default denyAll inbound traffic denied unless explicitly allowed; all outbound allowed by default (can be restricted)
Only Allow rulesSecurity Groups cannot have explicit Deny rules — unlike IAM policies, there's no way to deny a specific source while allowing a broader range
StatefulReturn traffic for an allowed connection is automatically permitted, no matching outbound rule needed
Reference by SG IDA rule's source can be another Security Group itself (not just an IP/CIDR) — lets you say "allow traffic FROM anything with the app-tier SG" without hardcoding IPs
Security Groups have NO explicit Deny — a real gotcha: unlike IAM (Module 13), you cannot write "deny this specific IP but allow everything else." If you need to explicitly block a specific source while allowing a broader range, you need a NACL (Module 15) instead, since NACLs support both Allow and Deny rules and are evaluated at the subnet level.

Common mistakes

  • Opening SSH (port 22) to 0.0.0.0/0 "temporarily" and forgetting to restrict it — a constant target for automated scanners/brute-force bots.
  • Not using SG-to-SG references for internal traffic, instead hardcoding private IP ranges that break the moment instances are replaced/rescaled with new IPs.

Interview Q

Q: Why can't you use a Security Group alone to block a specific malicious IP while still allowing the rest of the internet?
A: Security Groups only support Allow rules, with an implicit deny-everything-else — there's no explicit Deny rule type available at the Security Group level. To selectively block a specific IP while allowing broader access, you need a Network ACL (Module 15), which operates at the subnet level and DOES support explicit Deny rules, evaluated before Security Group rules in the traffic path.

05 Pricing Models

ModelHow it worksBest for
On-DemandPay per second/hour, no commitmentUnpredictable/spiky workloads, short-term needs
Reserved Instances1-3 year commitment for a significant discount (up to ~72%)Stable, predictable baseline workloads
Savings PlansCommit to a $/hour spend for 1-3 years, more flexible across instance families than RIsPredictable spend with some flexibility needed on instance type
Spot InstancesBid on spare AWS capacity at up to ~90% discount — can be RECLAIMED by AWS with only a 2-minute warningFault-tolerant, interruptible workloads: batch jobs, CI runners, stateless workers
Spot instances can vanish with 2 minutes' notice. Only appropriate for workloads that can handle sudden termination gracefully — never for anything stateful without proper checkpointing, and never as the ONLY capacity for a critical service without a fallback to On-Demand.

Interview Q

Q: When would you use Spot instances in production, and what design consideration is mandatory?
A: Spot instances suit fault-tolerant, interruptible workloads — CI/CD build runners, batch data processing, stateless horizontally-scaled web tiers where losing one instance briefly is a non-event. Mandatory design consideration: the workload must gracefully handle sudden termination with only a 2-minute warning (via the Spot interruption notice) — checkpointing progress, quickly rescheduling work elsewhere, or (for a web tier) simply relying on the load balancer/ASG to route around the lost capacity without any single point of failure depending on that specific instance.

★ Mini Project

# Launch an EC2 instance with an IAM role (Module 13), a security group, and user-data automation

aws ec2 create-security-group --group-name web-sg --description "web tier"
aws ec2 authorize-security-group-ingress --group-name web-sg --protocol tcp --port 22 --cidr /32
aws ec2 authorize-security-group-ingress --group-name web-sg --protocol tcp --port 8080 --cidr 0.0.0.0/0

cat > user-data.sh <<'EOF'
#!/bin/bash
yum update -y
yum install -y docker
systemctl enable --now docker
docker run -d -p 8080:8080 --restart unless-stopped myregistry/myapp:1.0
EOF

aws ec2 run-instances \
  --image-id ami-0abcdef1234567890 \
  --instance-type t3.micro \
  --key-name my-key \
  --security-groups web-sg \
  --iam-instance-profile Name=EC2-S3ReadOnly-Profile \
  --user-data file://user-data.sh \
  --block-device-mappings '[{"DeviceName":"/dev/xvda","Ebs":{"VolumeSize":20,"VolumeType":"gp3"}}]'

# once running:
ssh -i my-key.pem ec2-user@
cat /var/log/cloud-init-output.log     # verify user-data ran successfully
docker ps                                 # confirm app container is running
curl localhost:8080/health

✓ Revision Sheet

  • EC2 = rented VMs. T-family = burstable (credit exhaustion risk under sustained load); M/C/R for balanced/compute/memory-optimized.
  • EBS persists independently of instance lifecycle; instance store is wiped on stop/terminate — never use for real data.
  • AMI = launch template; golden AMIs boot faster/more reliably than pure user-data configuration at scale-out time.
  • User-data runs once via cloud-init on first boot; debug failures via /var/log/cloud-init-output.log.
  • Security Groups: stateful, Allow-only, attached per-instance, can reference other SGs by ID.
  • Pricing: On-Demand (flexible, priciest), Reserved/Savings Plans (committed discount), Spot (cheapest, reclaimable with 2min notice — fault-tolerant workloads only).

Q 10 MCQs

1. What happens to instance store data when an instance is stopped?
It's lost entirely — instance store is physically tied to the host and wiped on stop/terminate.
2. Why might a t3.micro throttle under sustained load?
T-family burstable instances exhaust their accumulated CPU credits under sustained high usage, then throttle to baseline performance.
3. Where do you check if an EC2 user-data script failed silently?
/var/log/cloud-init-output.log
4. Are Security Groups stateful or stateless?
Stateful — return traffic for an allowed connection is automatically permitted.
5. Can a Security Group have an explicit Deny rule?
No — only Allow rules; everything else is implicitly denied.
6. What EBS volume type suits a high-performance database needing guaranteed IOPS?
io2 (provisioned IOPS SSD).
7. How much warning does AWS give before reclaiming a Spot instance?
2 minutes.
8. What pricing model suits a stable, predictable long-term workload?
Reserved Instances or Savings Plans.
9. What can a Security Group rule's source be, besides an IP/CIDR?
Another Security Group's ID.
10. Why prefer a golden AMI over pure user-data configuration for an Auto Scaling fleet?
Faster, more reliable boot — not dependent on external package repos being reachable at the exact moment new capacity is urgently needed.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. An EC2 instance passes status checks but the app never starts.
Check /var/log/cloud-init-output.log for a failed user-data script (Module 1's boot debugging order), then journalctl -u <service> if it's systemd-managed.
2. curl to the app's port hangs/times out from outside, but works locally on the instance.
Check the Security Group's inbound rules allow the port from the client's source, and confirm the app is bound to 0.0.0.0 not 127.0.0.1 (Module 4's networking distinction).
3. A database on instance-store-backed storage lost all data after routine maintenance.
Instance store doesn't survive stop/terminate — data needed to be on an EBS volume; the loss is not recoverable, only preventable going forward.
4. A batch-processing fleet running on Spot instances keeps losing in-progress work.
No checkpointing/graceful-interruption handling was implemented for the 2-minute Spot reclaim warning — jobs need to periodically save progress and handle the interruption notice to resume elsewhere.
5. CPU-bound workload runs fine in short load tests but degrades badly in sustained production traffic.
Likely a T-family instance exhausting CPU credits under real sustained load (short tests never ran long enough to deplete them) — switch to a non-burstable family sized for actual sustained usage.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Launch an EC2 instance with a user-data script installing Docker and running a container; verify via cloud-init logs it succeeded.
  2. Create a Security Group allowing SSH only from your own IP and app traffic from anywhere; verify both rules work as expected.
  3. Attach an IAM role (from Module 13's mini project) to an instance and prove the app can call AWS APIs with zero configured credentials.
  4. Create a custom AMI from a configured instance ("golden AMI"), then launch a new instance from it and time the boot-to-ready duration vs a fresh user-data-only launch.
  5. Launch a Spot instance running a simple long-running script, then manually terminate it to observe how quickly/gracefully (or not) the workload handles interruption.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What's the difference between EBS and instance store?
EBS is network-attached, persistent storage that survives instance stop/start and can be detached/reattached/snapshotted. Instance store is physically attached to the host hardware and is wiped whenever the instance is stopped or terminated — appropriate only for ephemeral/scratch data.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why are Security Groups described as "stateful," and why does that matter practically?
If inbound traffic on a port is allowed, the corresponding outbound RETURN traffic for that same connection is automatically permitted without needing a separate explicit rule. Practically, this means you only need to write inbound rules for a typical request/response service — you don't have to separately manage matching outbound rules for every allowed inbound connection, unlike stateless NACLs which require explicit rules in both directions.
Intermediate: Design the compute strategy (instance types + pricing models) for a system with a stable baseline traffic level plus occasional CPU-heavy nightly batch jobs.
Baseline steady-state web/API traffic: Reserved Instances or a Savings Plan on right-sized M-family instances for the committed discount, since the load is predictable. Nightly CPU-heavy batch jobs: Spot instances on C-family (compute-optimized), since batch jobs can typically checkpoint/retry and tolerate the 2-minute interruption notice, capturing significant savings on a workload that isn't continuously running anyway. Avoid T-family for either — baseline traffic is sustained (would exhaust burst credits) and batch jobs are explicitly CPU-heavy (need genuine sustained CPU, not burstable credits).
Intermediate-Advanced: A security review finds SSH open to 0.0.0.0/0 on several production instances. Walk through the fix, considering Module 7's SSH module.
Immediately restrict the Security Group's SSH ingress rule to a specific known range (office IP, VPN CIDR) or, better, remove direct SSH access entirely in favor of a bastion host (Module 7) or AWS Systems Manager Session Manager, which requires no open inbound port 22 at all — access is brokered via IAM permissions instead. If SSH must remain for specific need, combine with Module 7's hardening baseline (key-only auth, no root login) and Module 13's least-privilege IAM roles for anyone permitted to initiate that access.
Advanced: Design a cost-optimized, fault-tolerant EC2 fleet architecture for a web application with unpredictable traffic spikes, balancing cost against reliability.
Use a mixed-instance Auto Scaling Group (Module 17) spanning multiple Availability Zones for fault tolerance, combining a baseline of Reserved/Savings-Plan-covered On-Demand instances (sized for typical steady traffic, guaranteeing availability) with Spot instances layered on top to absorb burst capacity cost-effectively during traffic spikes — since a stateless web tier behind a load balancer (Module 16) tolerates individual instance loss gracefully, Spot's interruption risk is an acceptable trade for the cost savings on the "extra capacity for spikes" portion specifically, while the guaranteed baseline capacity remains on stable On-Demand/Reserved instances that won't be reclaimed. Golden AMIs (pre-baked with the app) ensure new capacity — Spot or On-Demand — boots and becomes healthy as fast as possible during a genuine spike, when slow scale-out defeats the entire point.
Module 14 / 30 — AWS EC2. Next up: AWS VPC — the networking foundation (subnets, route tables, NAT) that every EC2 instance and every other AWS service actually lives inside.
DevOps / SDE-1 Interview Track — Module 15 / 30

AWS VPC, from first principles to production

The networking sandbox every AWS resource lives inside — directly applying Module 4's IP/CIDR/NAT fundamentals and Module 14's Security Groups to real, production network topology.

01 What is a VPC / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

A VPC (Virtual Private Cloud) is your own logically isolated slice of the AWS network — your own private IP address space (Module 4's CIDR concept), completely separate from every other AWS customer's network, even though it's all running on shared physical infrastructure. It exists because without this isolation, every AWS customer's resources would need to coexist on one flat shared network — a security and addressing nightmare at AWS's scale.

AWS Account └── VPC (e.g. 10.0.0.0/16) ← your own private IP space, isolated from other customers ├── Availability Zone A │ ├── Public Subnet (10.0.1.0/24) ← has a route to the internet │ └── Private Subnet (10.0.2.0/24) ← no direct route to the internet └── Availability Zone B ├── Public Subnet (10.0.3.0/24) └── Private Subnet (10.0.4.0/24)

Common mistakes

  • Choosing a VPC CIDR too small (Module 4's point revisited) — running out of IPs when adding more subnets or scaling out EKS pods, which each consume an IP in some CNI configurations.
  • Putting everything in one Availability Zone — a single AZ failure takes down the entire application; production VPCs always span at least 2 AZs for high availability.

Interview Q

Q: Why does a production VPC typically span multiple Availability Zones?
A: An Availability Zone is a physically distinct data center (or cluster of them) within a region — spanning multiple AZs means a failure in one (power outage, hardware failure, network issue) doesn't take down the entire application, since healthy capacity in other AZs continues serving traffic. Single-AZ deployments have a real, avoidable single point of failure.

02 Subnets: Public vs Private

What is it / why needed

A subnet (Module 4's subnetting concept, applied) is a subdivision of the VPC's CIDR block, tied to a SPECIFIC Availability Zone. Whether a subnet is "public" or "private" is entirely determined by its ROUTE TABLE (next section) — not by any inherent property of the subnet itself, a common point of confusion.

Public subnetPrivate subnet
Route to internetYes — route table has a route to an Internet GatewayNo direct route — outbound-only via NAT Gateway (if needed at all)
Typical residentsLoad balancers, bastion hosts, NAT GatewaysApp servers, databases — anything that shouldn't be directly internet-reachable
Public IPInstances CAN have a public IP (if subnet's auto-assign is on)Instances should have NO public IP at all
Production principle, directly extending Module 7's SSH bastion pattern: app servers and databases belong in PRIVATE subnets, with NO public IP, reachable only via a load balancer (for HTTP/S traffic) or a bastion/Session Manager (for admin access) sitting in the public subnet. This is the concrete AWS implementation of "minimize attack surface."

Common mistakes

  • Assuming a subnet is "public" or "private" as some fixed AWS-configured property — it's purely determined by whatever route table is associated with it; you could accidentally make a "private" subnet public by attaching the wrong route table.
  • Giving app servers public IPs directly "for convenience" instead of routing all inbound traffic through a load balancer in the public subnet.

Interview Q

Q: What actually determines whether a subnet is "public" or "private" in AWS?
A: Its associated ROUTE TABLE — specifically, whether that route table has a route sending 0.0.0.0/0 traffic to an Internet Gateway. There's no separate "public/private" flag on the subnet itself; it's entirely a function of routing configuration, meaning a misconfigured route table association could inadvertently make an intended-private subnet public.

03 Route Tables & Internet Gateway

What is it / how it works

A route table is a set of rules determining where network traffic from a subnet gets directed, based on destination IP. An Internet Gateway (IGW) is a horizontally-scaled, AWS-managed gateway attached to the VPC that allows communication between instances and the internet.

# Public subnet's route table
Destination         Target
10.0.0.0/16          local              # traffic within the VPC stays within the VPC
0.0.0.0/0            igw-0abc123        # everything else goes to the Internet Gateway

# Private subnet's route table
Destination         Target
10.0.0.0/16          local
0.0.0.0/0            nat-0xyz789        # everything else goes through a NAT Gateway instead (next section)

Common mistakes

  • Forgetting the IGW needs to be BOTH created AND attached to the VPC — a route pointing to an unattached IGW simply doesn't work.
  • Associating the wrong route table with a subnet — the exact mechanism behind the "public/private is just routing" point above.

Interview Q

Q: An EC2 instance in what should be a public subnet, with a public IP assigned, still can't be reached from the internet. What's the most likely networking-layer cause?
A: The subnet's route table likely doesn't actually have a 0.0.0.0/0 route to an Internet Gateway (or the IGW isn't attached to the VPC at all) — having a public IP alone is insufficient; the ROUTE must also exist for that IP to actually be reachable. Also check the Security Group and NACL rules (next sections) aren't blocking the traffic.

04 NAT Gateway

What is it / why needed

Directly implementing Module 4's NAT concept: a NAT Gateway sits in a PUBLIC subnet and lets instances in PRIVATE subnets initiate outbound internet connections (for package updates, calling external APIs) WITHOUT being directly reachable from the internet themselves — exactly the private-subnet-reaches-internet pattern flagged back in Module 4.

Private subnet instance (10.0.2.5, no public IP) │ wants to reach the internet (e.g. pull a package update) ▼ Route table sends 0.0.0.0/0 → NAT Gateway (sitting in the PUBLIC subnet, has its own public/Elastic IP) │ ▼ NAT Gateway rewrites source IP to its own public IP, forwards to the internet │ Response comes back to the NAT Gateway's public IP, which maps it back to 10.0.2.5 internally Key: the internet can never initiate a connection back to 10.0.2.5 directly — only return traffic for connections the PRIVATE instance itself initiated is allowed back through.
NAT GatewayNAT Instance (legacy/self-managed)
Managed byAWS — fully managed, highly available within an AZYou — a regular EC2 instance running NAT software, needs its own patching/scaling
CostHourly charge + data processing chargeJust the EC2 instance cost, generally cheaper for low-traffic use cases
AvailabilityNeeds one per AZ for full HA (a NAT Gateway is AZ-scoped)Single point of failure unless you build your own HA setup
Cost gotcha frequently tested: NAT Gateway charges apply per-GB of data processed through it — a private-subnet fleet pulling large downloads (container images, dependencies) through a NAT Gateway can accumulate meaningful cost. VPC Endpoints (below) can bypass the NAT Gateway entirely for AWS-service traffic (like pulling from ECR or S3), often the single biggest NAT cost-reduction lever.

Common mistakes

  • One NAT Gateway shared across multiple AZs "to save cost" — if that AZ has an issue, private instances in OTHER AZs lose internet access too, since NAT Gateways are AZ-scoped resources; production should have one NAT Gateway PER AZ.
  • Not using VPC Endpoints for high-volume AWS-service traffic (S3, ECR) — needlessly routing that traffic through a NAT Gateway incurs unnecessary per-GB charges.

Interview Q

Q: Why should a highly-available production VPC have one NAT Gateway per Availability Zone rather than just one for the whole VPC?
A: A NAT Gateway is itself an AZ-scoped resource. If you have a single NAT Gateway in one AZ and that AZ experiences an outage, private-subnet instances in OTHER (otherwise healthy) AZs would lose their route to the internet too, since their traffic depends on that one NAT Gateway — reintroducing exactly the single-AZ single point of failure the multi-AZ design was meant to eliminate.

05 Security Groups vs NACLs

Directly extends Module 14's Security Group coverage — now contrasted with the subnet-level firewall.

Security GroupNetwork ACL (NACL)
ScopePer instance/ENI (network interface)Per subnet — applies to everything in it
StateStateful (return traffic auto-allowed)Stateless (must explicitly allow BOTH directions)
Rule typesAllow onlyAllow AND explicit Deny
EvaluationAll rules evaluated, any match allowsRules evaluated IN NUMBER ORDER, first match wins (like a firewall rule list)
DefaultDeny all inbound by defaultDefault NACL allows all; custom NACLs deny all by default
Traffic path for an inbound request to a private-subnet instance: Internet → IGW → Route Table → NACL (subnet-level, stateless, ordered rules) │ passes? ▼ Security Group (instance-level, stateful, allow-only) │ passes? ▼ Instance
NACL's ordered evaluation is a real gotcha: rules are evaluated by rule NUMBER, lowest first, and the FIRST matching rule wins — a broad Allow at rule #100 will win over a more specific Deny at rule #200 if the Allow matches first. Order rules deliberately, most specific/important first.

Common mistakes

  • Forgetting NACLs are stateless — allowing inbound port 8080 without also allowing the corresponding EPHEMERAL PORT RANGE outbound (for the response) causes connections to mysteriously hang, unlike Security Groups which don't need this.
  • Not understanding NACL rule ORDER matters — assuming all rules are evaluated together like a Security Group, when actually the first numerically-matching rule wins and stops evaluation.

Interview Q

Q: You've configured a NACL to allow inbound traffic on port 443, but responses never make it back to the client. What's missing?
A: NACLs are stateless — allowing inbound traffic does NOT automatically allow the corresponding outbound return traffic (unlike stateful Security Groups). You need an explicit outbound rule allowing traffic on the EPHEMERAL PORT RANGE (typically 1024-65535, per Module 4) back to the client, since that's the port range the client's OS assigned for its side of the connection.

06 VPC Peering & Endpoints

ConceptPurpose
VPC PeeringDirect private network connection between two VPCs (same or different accounts) — traffic never traverses the public internet. NOT transitive: if A peers with B and B peers with C, A cannot reach C through B.
Transit GatewayA hub-and-spoke alternative to peering for connecting MANY VPCs — solves peering's non-transitivity problem at scale (peering N VPCs pairwise needs N×(N-1)/2 connections; Transit Gateway needs just N).
VPC Endpoint (Gateway type)Private route to S3/DynamoDB from within the VPC, without needing a NAT Gateway or internet access at all — free, and removes that traffic from NAT Gateway cost entirely.
VPC Endpoint (Interface type, PrivateLink)Private connectivity to most other AWS services (and some third-party SaaS) via an ENI in your subnet — traffic never leaves the AWS network.
Real cost/security optimization, frequently discussed: a private-subnet EKS cluster pulling images from ECR and reading/writing S3 constantly can eliminate a huge amount of NAT Gateway data-processing cost (and improve security posture, since traffic never needs internet routing at all) by using VPC Endpoints for both S3 and ECR instead of routing that traffic through the NAT Gateway.

Common mistakes

  • Assuming VPC Peering is transitive — a very common design mistake when connecting more than 2 VPCs; needs Transit Gateway or explicit peering between every pair instead.
  • Not using VPC Endpoints for high-traffic AWS-service calls from private subnets — leaves easy NAT Gateway cost savings on the table.

Interview Q

Q: VPC A peers with VPC B, and VPC B peers with VPC C. Can an instance in VPC A reach an instance in VPC C?
A: No — VPC Peering is not transitive. A connection must be established directly between A and C for that traffic to work; peering with a common third VPC doesn't create an implicit path through it. For connecting many VPCs where this becomes impractical, AWS Transit Gateway is the standard solution instead.

★ Mini Project

# Build a standard 2-AZ, public+private subnet VPC from scratch via CLI

aws ec2 create-vpc --cidr-block 10.0.0.0/16 --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=vpc,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=myapp-vpc}]'
VPC_ID=

# Subnets across 2 AZs
aws ec2 create-subnet --vpc-id $VPC_ID --cidr-block 10.0.1.0/24 --availability-zone ap-south-1a  # public-a
aws ec2 create-subnet --vpc-id $VPC_ID --cidr-block 10.0.2.0/24 --availability-zone ap-south-1a  # private-a
aws ec2 create-subnet --vpc-id $VPC_ID --cidr-block 10.0.3.0/24 --availability-zone ap-south-1b  # public-b
aws ec2 create-subnet --vpc-id $VPC_ID --cidr-block 10.0.4.0/24 --availability-zone ap-south-1b  # private-b

# Internet Gateway
aws ec2 create-internet-gateway
aws ec2 attach-internet-gateway --vpc-id $VPC_ID --internet-gateway-id 

# Public route table
aws ec2 create-route-table --vpc-id $VPC_ID   # public-rt
aws ec2 create-route --route-table-id  --destination-cidr-block 0.0.0.0/0 --gateway-id 
aws ec2 associate-route-table --subnet-id  --route-table-id 
aws ec2 associate-route-table --subnet-id  --route-table-id 

# NAT Gateway (one per AZ for real HA — here just one for the demo)
aws ec2 allocate-address --domain vpc     # Elastic IP for the NAT Gateway
aws ec2 create-nat-gateway --subnet-id  --allocation-id 

# Private route table
aws ec2 create-route-table --vpc-id $VPC_ID   # private-rt
aws ec2 create-route --route-table-id  --destination-cidr-block 0.0.0.0/0 --nat-gateway-id 
aws ec2 associate-route-table --subnet-id  --route-table-id 

# S3 Gateway Endpoint — bypass NAT for S3 traffic entirely
aws ec2 create-vpc-endpoint --vpc-id $VPC_ID --service-name com.amazonaws.ap-south-1.s3 \
  --route-table-ids  --vpc-endpoint-type Gateway

# Verify: launch an instance in the private subnet, confirm it CAN reach the internet (via NAT)
# but is NOT reachable directly from the internet (no public IP, no IGW route)

✓ Revision Sheet

  • VPC = your own isolated private IP space in AWS. Span multiple AZs for high availability.
  • Public vs private subnet is determined ENTIRELY by its route table (IGW route present or not) — not an inherent subnet property.
  • Internet Gateway: needs creation AND attachment to the VPC; enables public subnet internet routing.
  • NAT Gateway: lets private subnets initiate OUTBOUND internet connections without being directly reachable inbound. AZ-scoped — need one per AZ for real HA.
  • Security Group: instance-level, stateful, Allow-only. NACL: subnet-level, stateless (need explicit rules both directions), supports Deny, evaluated in rule-number order (first match wins).
  • VPC Peering: not transitive. Transit Gateway: hub-and-spoke for connecting many VPCs.
  • VPC Endpoints (Gateway for S3/DynamoDB, Interface/PrivateLink for most others): private AWS-service access, bypasses NAT Gateway entirely — real cost + security win.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What determines whether a subnet is public or private?
Its associated route table (specifically, whether it has a route to an Internet Gateway).
2. Does a NAT Gateway allow inbound connections FROM the internet to a private instance?
No — only outbound connections initiated BY the private instance, with return traffic allowed back.
3. Is a NAT Gateway scoped to the whole VPC or to a single AZ?
A single AZ — need one per AZ for full high availability.
4. Are NACLs stateful or stateless?
Stateless — must explicitly allow both inbound and outbound directions.
5. How are NACL rules evaluated?
In numeric rule order, lowest first — the first matching rule wins.
6. Can a Security Group have a Deny rule?
No — Security Groups are Allow-only; use a NACL for explicit Deny.
7. Is VPC Peering transitive?
No — A peered with B and B peered with C does not let A reach C.
8. What AWS feature avoids pairwise peering complexity for many VPCs?
Transit Gateway.
9. What VPC Endpoint type gives free, private access to S3?
Gateway-type VPC Endpoint.
10. Why use a VPC Endpoint for S3 traffic from a private subnet instead of routing it through NAT?
Avoids NAT Gateway per-GB data processing costs and keeps traffic entirely within the AWS network rather than routing through a NAT Gateway.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. An instance with a public IP in a "public" subnet still isn't reachable from the internet.
Check the subnet's actual route table has a 0.0.0.0/0 route to an attached Internet Gateway, then check Security Group and NACL rules aren't blocking it.
2. A private-subnet instance can't reach the internet at all (e.g. can't run yum update).
Check the private subnet's route table has a 0.0.0.0/0 route to a NAT Gateway, and that the NAT Gateway itself is healthy and in a public subnet with its own working route to an IGW.
3. After configuring a NACL rule allowing inbound HTTPS, connections still hang.
Missing the outbound ephemeral port range rule — NACLs are stateless, so the RETURN traffic needs its own explicit allow rule.
4. One AZ's outage takes down internet access for private instances in a DIFFERENT, otherwise-healthy AZ.
Only one NAT Gateway existed for the whole VPC, located in the affected AZ — need a NAT Gateway per AZ for true isolation of failures.
5. Two VPCs are both peered with a third "hub" VPC, but still can't communicate with each other.
Expected — VPC Peering isn't transitive; either establish a direct peering connection between the two, or migrate to a Transit Gateway architecture for multi-VPC connectivity.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Build a 2-AZ VPC from scratch with public and private subnets in each AZ, an IGW, and one NAT Gateway per AZ.
  2. Launch an instance in a private subnet and prove it can reach the internet (via NAT) but is NOT reachable from the internet directly.
  3. Configure a NACL with both an inbound allow AND the required outbound ephemeral-port allow, and verify a connection actually completes (vs only inbound configured, which hangs).
  4. Set up a Gateway VPC Endpoint for S3 and verify (via VPC Flow Logs or NAT Gateway metrics) that S3 traffic from a private instance no longer routes through the NAT Gateway.
  5. Create two VPCs, peer them, and verify connectivity; then add a third VPC peered only to one of the first two and confirm the non-transitivity limitation firsthand.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What is a VPC and why does every AWS account need one?
A VPC is your own logically isolated private network within AWS, with its own IP address range, completely separated from other customers' networks despite running on shared physical infrastructure. Every account effectively needs one (a default VPC exists automatically) because virtually all networked AWS resources — EC2, RDS, EKS — need to live inside some VPC's network space.
Beginner-Intermediate: Explain why a private subnet instance can reach the internet through a NAT Gateway but the internet can't reach back into it.
The NAT Gateway only forwards traffic that the private instance itself INITIATED, translating the source address and keeping track of that specific outbound connection so the response can find its way back. It has no listening/inbound capability for the internet to initiate a NEW connection toward the private instance — there's no route or mechanism for unsolicited inbound traffic to reach it at all, unlike Application traffic arriving via a Load Balancer, which is a deliberately different, explicitly-routed path.
Intermediate: Design a VPC subnet layout for a 3-tier application (web, app, database) with high availability.
Span at least 2 (ideally 3) Availability Zones. Public subnets per AZ hold only the load balancer (and optionally a bastion/NAT Gateway). Private "app" subnets per AZ hold the application servers, reachable only from the load balancer's security group, with outbound internet access via a NAT Gateway (one per AZ). Private "database" subnets per AZ hold the database tier, with security groups permitting inbound ONLY from the app tier's security group, and typically no NAT Gateway route needed at all if the database never needs outbound internet access.
Intermediate-Advanced: Your NAT Gateway costs have grown significantly as traffic scaled. Walk through how you'd investigate and reduce this cost.
Check VPC Flow Logs or NAT Gateway CloudWatch metrics (BytesOutToDestination/BytesInFromDestination) to identify what's actually generating the volume — commonly, high-frequency AWS API calls (S3, ECR, DynamoDB) from private-subnet workloads. For AWS-service traffic specifically, add VPC Endpoints (Gateway type for S3/DynamoDB — free; Interface/PrivateLink for other services) so that traffic routes privately within AWS instead of through the NAT Gateway entirely, directly eliminating its associated data-processing charges for that traffic. For genuine third-party internet traffic that must go through NAT, evaluate whether consolidating/caching reduces the actual volume, since that portion has no VPC Endpoint alternative.
Advanced: Design network connectivity for an organization with 15 VPCs across different teams that all need to reach a shared services VPC (logging, monitoring, shared databases), while also occasionally needing to reach each other.
Pairwise VPC Peering across 15+ VPCs would require up to 15×14/2 ≈ 105 peering connections in the worst case (full mesh) — completely unmanageable, and Peering's non-transitivity means even a hub-and-spoke pattern via peering alone wouldn't let spoke VPCs reach each other through the hub. AWS Transit Gateway is the correct architecture here: each of the 15 VPCs attaches to the Transit Gateway once (15 attachments total, not 105), Transit Gateway route tables control which VPCs can reach which others (including permitting all-to-shared-services while selectively allowing or restricting spoke-to-spoke traffic based on actual business need), and it scales cleanly as more VPCs are added without the connection-count explosion peering would cause.
Module 15 / 30 — AWS VPC. Next up: AWS ALB — the Application Load Balancer sitting in your public subnets, doing what Nginx (Module 8) did, now as a managed AWS service.
DevOps / SDE-1 Interview Track — Module 16 / 30

AWS ALB, from first principles to production

Everything Module 8 did with Nginx as a reverse proxy — now as a fully managed, self-healing AWS service, sitting across your VPC's public subnets with active health checks Nginx open-source couldn't natively do.
