01 Why ALB / vs NLB vs Classic

What is it / why needed

An Application Load Balancer (ALB) is AWS's managed Layer 7 (application-layer, HTTP/HTTPS-aware — Module 5) load balancer. It exists to remove the operational burden of running and scaling your own Nginx/HAProxy reverse proxy fleet (Module 8) — AWS handles the scaling, patching, and high availability of the load balancer itself.

ALBNLB (Network LB)Classic LB (legacy)
OSI Layer7 (application — HTTP/HTTPS aware)4 (transport — TCP/UDP, no HTTP awareness)Both, poorly — legacy, avoid for new designs
RoutingPath/host/header-based rulesNone — pure connection-level forwardingBasic
PerformanceSlightly higher latency (HTTP parsing overhead)Extremely low latency, very high throughput—
Use caseWeb APIs, microservices routing by path/hostExtreme performance needs, non-HTTP TCP/UDP traffic, static IP requirementLegacy only
This maps directly onto the OSI model from Module 4: ALB operates where HTTP (Module 5) lives, understanding paths/headers/hosts. NLB operates purely at the TCP/UDP level, blind to anything HTTP-specific — chosen when you need raw performance or need to load-balance non-HTTP protocols entirely.

Common mistakes

  • Using NLB when you actually need path-based routing — NLB has no concept of HTTP paths/headers at all, since it operates below that layer.
  • Defaulting to Classic Load Balancer for new projects — legacy, lacks modern features (target groups, advanced routing rules), should only be encountered in old/legacy systems.

Interview Q

Q: Why can't an NLB do path-based routing the way an ALB can?
A: NLB operates at Layer 4 (TCP/UDP) — it forwards connections based purely on IP/port, with no visibility into the HTTP request itself at all (no parsing of paths, headers, or hosts, which are Layer 7 concepts). ALB operates at Layer 7, actually parsing the HTTP request to make routing decisions based on its content — a fundamentally different level of the stack, per Module 4's OSI layering.

02 Architecture: Listeners, Target Groups, Rules

Client request │ ▼ ALB (spans multiple AZs, AWS-managed, auto-scales itself) │ ├── Listener (port 443, HTTPS) │ │ │ ├── Rule: path = /api/* → Target Group "api-tg" → [EC2/ECS/Lambda targets] │ ├── Rule: path = /admin/* → Target Group "admin-tg" → [different targets] │ └── Rule: default (no match) → Target Group "web-tg" → [default targets] │ └── Listener (port 80, HTTP) └── Rule: default → REDIRECT to HTTPS (443)
ComponentRole
ListenerChecks for connection requests on a specific port/protocol (e.g. 443/HTTPS)
RuleConditions (path, host, header, query string) matched against incoming requests, determining which Target Group handles it
Target GroupA set of registered targets (EC2 instances, IP addresses, Lambda functions, or ECS tasks) that requests get forwarded to, plus its own health check configuration

Common mistakes

  • Forgetting a default rule/catch-all — requests not matching any specific rule need somewhere to go, or they get an error.
  • Registering targets directly by instance without understanding Target Groups can point at IPs (useful for on-prem/hybrid or non-EC2 targets) or Lambda functions too, not just EC2.

Interview Q

Q: Can a single ALB route /api/* to one fleet of servers and /admin/* to a completely different fleet?
A: Yes — this is exactly what Listener Rules and Target Groups are for. One ALB can have multiple rules under a single listener, each matching different path patterns (or hosts/headers) and forwarding to entirely different Target Groups, each with its own set of registered targets and health check configuration.

03 Health Checks & Deregistration

What is it / how it's different from Nginx (Module 8 callback)

Unlike open-source Nginx (which Module 8 noted only supports PASSIVE health checks), ALB performs genuine ACTIVE health checks — proactively polling each target's configured health check path on a schedule, entirely independent of real traffic.

Target Group health check config:
  Path: /actuator/health
  Protocol: HTTP
  Healthy threshold: 3        # 3 consecutive successes → mark healthy
  Unhealthy threshold: 2        # 2 consecutive failures → mark unhealthy
  Timeout: 5s
  Interval: 15s
  Success codes: 200
Target lifecycle: Initial → registering → healthy ⇄ unhealthy → draining (on deregistration) → deregistered "Draining" (connection draining / deregistration delay): When a target is deregistered (e.g. during a deploy or scale-in), the ALB stops sending NEW requests to it immediately, but allows IN-FLIGHT requests up to a configurable deregistration delay (default 300s) to complete gracefully before fully removing it — directly parallel to Module 1's SIGTERM-then-grace-period-then-SIGKILL pattern.

Common mistakes

  • Deregistration delay set too short for a target with genuinely long-running requests — in-flight requests get cut off mid-response during a deploy.
  • Health check path checking something too shallow (e.g. just returns 200 always) instead of actually verifying downstream dependencies (DB connectivity) — ALB keeps sending traffic to a target that's "up" but functionally broken.

Interview Q

Q: During a rolling deployment, some users experience dropped connections despite the ALB's health checks seemingly working correctly. What's a likely cause?
A: The deregistration delay (connection draining period) may be set too short for the actual time some in-flight requests take to complete — when a target is deregistered, the ALB stops NEW traffic to it immediately, but only waits up to the configured delay before forcibly cutting off anything still in progress. Increasing the deregistration delay to comfortably exceed the app's slowest realistic request duration resolves this.

04 Path/Host-Based Routing

# AWS CLI: create a rule routing /api/* to a specific target group
aws elbv2 create-rule \
  --listener-arn  \
  --priority 10 \
  --conditions Field=path-pattern,Values='/api/*' \
  --actions Type=forward,TargetGroupArn=

# Host-based routing example — same ALB serving multiple domains
aws elbv2 create-rule \
  --listener-arn  \
  --priority 20 \
  --conditions Field=host-header,Values='admin.example.com' \
  --actions Type=forward,TargetGroupArn=
Real production pattern: one ALB can front an entire microservices architecture, with each service getting its own Target Group and a path-based rule (/users/* → users-service, /orders/* → orders-service) — avoiding the cost and complexity of provisioning a separate load balancer per microservice.

Common mistakes

  • Rule priority conflicts — lower priority number is evaluated first (similar to NACL's ordering from Module 15); overlapping path patterns with wrong priority ordering send traffic to the wrong target group.

Interview Q

Q: How can a single ALB serve multiple independent microservices cost-effectively, instead of provisioning one load balancer per service?
A: Using path-based (or host-based) Listener Rules, each pointing to a different microservice's own Target Group — e.g. /users/* routes to the users-service target group, /orders/* to the orders-service target group — all fronted by the same ALB. This avoids the fixed hourly cost of a separate load balancer per microservice while still keeping each service's traffic and health checks fully independent.

05 TLS Termination & SSL Policies

Directly the same pattern as Module 8's Nginx TLS termination and Module 5's TLS-terminated-at-edge concept — ALB terminates TLS using a certificate from ACM (AWS Certificate Manager, which handles automatic renewal, removing the Module 5 cert-expiry incident risk entirely), then typically forwards plain HTTP to targets within the trusted VPC.

aws elbv2 create-listener \
  --load-balancer-arn  \
  --protocol HTTPS --port 443 \
  --certificates CertificateArn= \
  --ssl-policy ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-2021-06 \
  --default-actions Type=forward,TargetGroupArn=
SSL Policy choice matters: AWS provides named SSL policies controlling which TLS versions/cipher suites are accepted — an outdated policy allowing TLS 1.0/1.1 is exactly the security audit finding flagged in Module 8's Nginx section, now at the ALB level. Always select a modern policy (TLS 1.2+ minimum, ideally requiring 1.3 where client compatibility allows).

Common mistakes

  • Using ACM certs only for the ALB but manually managing (and forgetting to renew) certs elsewhere in the same architecture — ACM's automatic renewal only covers what's actually using ACM-issued/managed certs.

Interview Q

Q: Why is using ACM (AWS Certificate Manager) with an ALB preferred over manually uploading and managing your own certificates?
A: ACM handles automatic certificate renewal for certificates it issues and that are used with integrated AWS services like ALB — eliminating the real production risk (flagged back in Module 5) of a cert silently expiring because a manual renewal process was forgotten. Manually managed certs require you to track expiry and rotate them yourself.

★ Mini Project

# Full ALB setup: HTTPS listener, HTTP-to-HTTPS redirect, path-based routing, health checks

aws elbv2 create-load-balancer --name myapp-alb --subnets   \
  --security-groups  --scheme internet-facing --type application

aws elbv2 create-target-group --name api-tg --protocol HTTP --port 8080 --vpc-id  \
  --health-check-path /actuator/health --health-check-interval-seconds 15 \
  --healthy-threshold-count 3 --unhealthy-threshold-count 2

aws elbv2 register-targets --target-group-arn  --targets Id= Id=

aws elbv2 create-listener --load-balancer-arn  --protocol HTTPS --port 443 \
  --certificates CertificateArn= --ssl-policy ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-2021-06 \
  --default-actions Type=forward,TargetGroupArn=

aws elbv2 create-listener --load-balancer-arn  --protocol HTTP --port 80 \
  --default-actions Type=redirect,RedirectConfig='{Protocol=HTTPS,Port=443,StatusCode=HTTP_301}'

# verify
aws elbv2 describe-target-health --target-group-arn 
curl -v https:///actuator/health

# deregister one target and observe connection draining in action
aws elbv2 deregister-targets --target-group-arn  --targets Id=
aws elbv2 describe-target-health --target-group-arn    # watch state: draining → deregistered

✓ Revision Sheet

  • ALB = Layer 7 (HTTP-aware, path/host routing). NLB = Layer 4 (raw TCP/UDP, extreme performance, no HTTP awareness). Classic = legacy, avoid.
  • Listener (port/protocol) → Rules (match conditions: path/host/header) → Target Group (registered targets + health check config).
  • ALB does genuine ACTIVE health checks (unlike open-source Nginx's passive-only, per Module 8).
  • Deregistration delay/connection draining: in-flight requests get a grace period before a deregistering target is fully removed — same SIGTERM-grace-SIGKILL pattern as Module 1.
  • One ALB can front many microservices via path/host-based rules — avoids per-service load balancer cost.
  • Rule priority: lower number evaluated first.
  • ACM certs auto-renew when used with ALB — removes manual cert-expiry risk from Module 5.
  • Always use a modern SSL policy (TLS 1.2+ minimum) — outdated policies are a real audit finding.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What OSI layer does ALB operate at?
Layer 7 (Application).
2. What OSI layer does NLB operate at?
Layer 4 (Transport).
3. What determines which Target Group handles a given request?
Listener Rules matching conditions like path, host, or header against the request.
4. Does ALB support active health checks natively?
Yes — unlike open-source Nginx, which only supports passive checks.
5. What happens to in-flight requests when a target is deregistered?
They're allowed to complete within the configured deregistration delay (connection draining) before the target is fully removed.
6. Can one ALB route different paths to entirely different backend fleets?
Yes — via path-based Listener Rules pointing to different Target Groups.
7. What AWS service issues and auto-renews TLS certs for use with ALB?
ACM (AWS Certificate Manager).
8. What determines evaluation order among multiple ALB rules?
Rule priority — lower number evaluated first.
9. Which load balancer type would you choose for a non-HTTP TCP protocol needing extreme low latency?
NLB.
10. What ALB feature avoids a security audit finding around outdated TLS versions?
Selecting a modern SSL Policy (e.g. requiring TLS 1.2 or 1.3 minimum).

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. ALB returns 502 Bad Gateway for all requests.
Per Module 5's status code breakdown: backend targets are likely unhealthy, refusing connections, or returning malformed responses — check target health status and target-side app logs.
2. ALB returns 504 Gateway Timeout intermittently under load.
Backend targets are taking longer than the ALB's idle timeout to respond — check target response times and consider whether the idle timeout needs raising or the actual backend slowness needs fixing.
3. A newly registered target never becomes healthy.
Check the health check path actually exists and returns the expected success code on the target, check Security Group allows the ALB to reach that port, and confirm the app is actually listening on the correct port/interface (0.0.0.0, not 127.0.0.1, per Module 4).
4. Some users get dropped mid-request during a deployment despite health checks passing before deregistration.
Deregistration delay is likely too short for the actual in-flight request duration — increase it to safely exceed the slowest realistic request time.
5. Path-based routing sends traffic to the wrong target group despite rules looking correct individually.
Check rule PRIORITY ordering — an earlier (lower-numbered) rule with an overlapping/broader pattern may be matching first and intercepting traffic meant for a later, more specific rule.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Set up an ALB with two target groups and path-based routing (/api/* and /admin/*), verifying each path reaches the correct fleet.
  2. Configure an HTTP listener that redirects all traffic to HTTPS, and verify with curl -v that the 301 redirect actually occurs.
  3. Deliberately misconfigure a health check path to a nonexistent endpoint and observe targets never becoming healthy; fix it and watch them recover.
  4. Deregister a target mid-request (simulate with a slow endpoint) and observe connection draining behavior with different deregistration delay settings.
  5. Create overlapping path-based rules with different priorities and demonstrate how priority ordering determines which one actually wins.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What's the main difference between an ALB and an NLB?
ALB operates at Layer 7, understanding and routing based on HTTP-specific content (paths, hosts, headers). NLB operates at Layer 4, forwarding raw TCP/UDP connections with no awareness of HTTP content at all — chosen for extreme performance needs or non-HTTP protocols.
Beginner-Intermediate: How does an ALB determine a target is unhealthy and stop sending it traffic?
Via active health checks — the ALB independently polls each target's configured health check path on a schedule (interval), and after a configured number of consecutive failures (unhealthy threshold), marks the target unhealthy and stops routing new traffic to it, continuing to monitor for recovery via the healthy threshold.
Intermediate: Design an ALB setup for a system with a public API and a separate internal admin panel, both needing to be reachable but the admin panel only from internal IPs.
Use path or host-based routing rules (e.g. api.example.com vs admin.example.com, or /api/* vs /admin/*) pointing to separate target groups. For the admin panel's added restriction, layer a condition using the ALB's source-IP conditional rule matching (or, more robustly, restrict it at the Security Group level on the ALB itself for that specific listener/rule context, or use a separate internal-facing ALB entirely for admin traffic that isn't internet-facing at all) — the key design point is not relying on "security through obscurity" of an unlisted path alone.
Intermediate-Advanced: A deployment strategy calls for zero dropped connections during rolling updates behind an ALB. What configuration elements need to align to guarantee this?
The ALB's deregistration delay must exceed the application's maximum realistic in-flight request duration, so draining targets get enough time to finish existing work. The target's health check must accurately reflect true readiness (not a shallow "always 200" check) so the ALB never sends new traffic to a target that isn't genuinely ready. The deployment orchestration (ASG instance refresh, ECS/EKS rolling update) must deregister/mark targets unhealthy BEFORE actually terminating the underlying compute, giving the ALB's health-check-driven removal process time to react rather than the target vanishing abruptly mid-request.
Advanced: You're migrating from Nginx-based reverse proxying (Module 8) on self-managed EC2 to ALB. What operational capabilities do you gain, and what do you need to explicitly replicate that Nginx was doing implicitly?
Gained: fully managed scaling/patching/HA of the load balancer itself (no more capacity planning or patching your own proxy fleet), genuine active health checks (vs Nginx open-source's passive-only), and native integration with ACM for automatic cert renewal. Need to explicitly replicate: any custom Nginx logic beyond basic routing — rate limiting, custom header manipulation/rewriting, response caching, or complex regex-based routing rules that Nginx's config language supports natively but ALB's simpler rule conditions may not fully cover (potentially requiring AWS WAF for rate-limiting/security rules, or a Lambda@Edge/CloudFront layer for more complex request manipulation) — a straight lift-and-shift assuming feature parity without auditing the existing Nginx config first risks silently dropping functionality.

AWS Auto Scaling, from first principles to production

The EC2-level equivalent of Module 11's HPA — automatically adjusting fleet size behind the ALB based on real demand, with the same self-healing reconciliation philosophy applied to whole instances instead of pods.

01 Why ASG / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

An Auto Scaling Group (ASG) maintains a desired number of EC2 instances automatically — launching replacements for unhealthy/terminated instances and scaling the fleet size up/down based on demand. It exists because manually watching traffic and launching/terminating EC2 instances doesn't scale operationally (the exact problem Module 11's Kubernetes reconciliation loop solves at the pod level, now applied to whole VMs).

Same reconciliation philosophy as Kubernetes (Module 11), just at the EC2 level: Desired capacity: 4 instances │ continuously compared against ▼ Actual running instances: currently 3 (one failed health check and was terminated) │ └── ASG detects the gap → launches 1 new instance from the Launch Template to restore desired capacity — no human intervention needed

Common mistakes

  • Manually launching/terminating instances that are part of an ASG — the ASG's own reconciliation will fight against manual changes, relaunching terminated instances or terminating manually-added ones that don't match its launch template.

Interview Q

Q: You manually terminate an instance that's part of an Auto Scaling Group. What happens?
A: The ASG detects the gap between desired capacity and actual running instances, and automatically launches a replacement instance from its Launch Template to restore the desired count — the same reconciliation-loop philosophy as Kubernetes' Deployment controller (Module 11), just operating on EC2 instances instead of pods.

02 Launch Templates & ASG Config

ComponentRole
Launch TemplateDefines WHAT to launch — AMI, instance type, security groups, IAM instance profile, user-data script (all of Module 14's concepts, templated)
ASGDefines HOW MANY and WHERE — min/max/desired capacity, which subnets/AZs, which Target Group(s) to register new instances with
aws autoscaling create-auto-scaling-group \
  --auto-scaling-group-name myapp-asg \
  --launch-template LaunchTemplateName=myapp-lt,Version='$Latest' \
  --min-size 2 --max-size 10 --desired-capacity 3 \
  --vpc-zone-identifier "subnet-priv-a,subnet-priv-b" \
  --target-group-arns  \
  --health-check-type ELB \
  --health-check-grace-period 60
health-check-type ELB vs EC2, a real gotcha: the default EC2 health check only checks basic instance-level status (is the VM itself running) — it has NO idea if your actual application is healthy. Setting health-check-type to ELB makes the ASG defer to the ALB Target Group's application-level health check (Module 16) instead — without this, an ASG can consider an instance "healthy" even though the app inside it has crashed and the ALB has already marked it unhealthy.

Common mistakes

  • Leaving health-check-type as EC2 (default) instead of ELB — the ASG won't replace an instance whose actual application has crashed, since basic instance status still reports healthy.
  • health-check-grace-period too short for a slow-starting app — the ASG can prematurely consider a legitimately-still-starting instance unhealthy and cycle it before it ever gets a chance to pass.

Interview Q

Q: Why would you set an ASG's health-check-type to ELB instead of leaving the default EC2?
A: The default EC2 health check only reflects basic instance-level status (is the VM itself running/reachable) — it has no visibility into whether the actual APPLICATION on that instance is healthy. Setting health-check-type to ELB makes the ASG use the ALB Target Group's application-level health check result instead, so a crashed app (even on an otherwise fine-looking instance) correctly triggers replacement.

03 Scaling Policies

Policy typeBehavior
Target TrackingSet a target metric value (e.g. 60% avg CPU) — ASG automatically calculates and applies the scaling adjustments needed to maintain it. Simplest, most commonly used.
Step ScalingDefine specific scaling actions triggered at different CloudWatch alarm thresholds (e.g. +2 instances if CPU > 70%, +5 if CPU > 90%) — more granular control
Scheduled ScalingScale based on a known time-based pattern (e.g. scale up every weekday at 9am ahead of predictable traffic)
Predictive ScalingUses ML on historical CloudWatch data to forecast and pre-scale ahead of recurring demand patterns
aws autoscaling put-scaling-policy \
  --auto-scaling-group-name myapp-asg \
  --policy-name cpu-target-tracking \
  --policy-type TargetTrackingScaling \
  --target-tracking-configuration '{
    "PredefinedMetricSpecification": {"PredefinedMetricType": "ASGAverageCPUUtilization"},
    "TargetValue": 60.0
  }'
Same trade-off as Module 11's HPA: CPU-based target tracking can be a poor proxy for actual load on I/O-bound applications — a custom CloudWatch metric (request count per target, queue depth) is often more accurate, mirroring the exact same discussion from Kubernetes' HPA.

Common mistakes

  • Scaling purely on CPU for a workload that's actually memory or I/O bound — the metric never reflects real strain, so scaling reacts too late or not at all.
  • No scheduled scaling for genuinely predictable traffic patterns (e.g. daily 9am spike) — pure reactive scaling always lags slightly behind a sudden real spike, when a scheduled pre-scale would avoid that lag entirely.

Interview Q

Q: Your e-commerce site has a predictable traffic spike every day at 9am when a promotional email goes out. What scaling approach minimizes the risk of being under-provisioned right at 9am?
A: Scheduled Scaling — proactively increase the minimum/desired capacity a few minutes before the known 9am spike, rather than relying purely on reactive target-tracking scaling, which necessarily lags behind the actual metric crossing its threshold (new instances still take time to launch and pass health checks even once scaling triggers). Reactive scaling can supplement this as a safety net for any traffic beyond the predicted pattern.

04 Lifecycle Hooks & Self-Healing

What is it / why needed

Lifecycle hooks let you PAUSE an instance in a "Pending" or "Terminating" state to run custom actions before it's fully placed into service or fully removed — e.g. running configuration/registration steps before it starts receiving traffic, or draining connections/uploading final logs before termination completes.

Scale-out lifecycle: Pending → [Pending:Wait — lifecycle hook pauses here] → Pending:Proceed → InService Scale-in lifecycle: InService → Terminating → [Terminating:Wait — lifecycle hook pauses here] → Terminating:Proceed → Terminated

Common mistakes

  • Not using a Terminating lifecycle hook when instances hold in-flight work (e.g. processing a queue) — the instance can be terminated mid-task with no chance to finish or checkpoint.
  • Forgetting to call `complete-lifecycle-action` after the hook's custom logic finishes — the instance stays stuck in the Wait state until the hook's timeout expires (default 1 hour), needlessly slowing scaling operations.

Interview Q

Q: How would you ensure a scaling-in instance finishes processing its current queue messages before being terminated?
A: Attach a Terminating lifecycle hook to the ASG — when scale-in selects that instance, it enters Terminating:Wait, giving you a window (via a custom script/Lambda watching for the lifecycle event) to signal the instance to stop pulling new work and finish its current in-flight messages, then explicitly call complete-lifecycle-action once done so the instance actually proceeds to termination — rather than being abruptly killed mid-task.

05 Instance Refresh

What is it / why needed

Instance Refresh replaces ALL instances in an ASG with new ones based on an updated Launch Template (e.g. a new AMI/golden image), in a controlled, gradual rolling fashion — directly the ASG-level equivalent of Module 11's Kubernetes rolling update, with the same maxSurge-like configurability.

aws autoscaling start-instance-refresh \
  --auto-scaling-group-name myapp-asg \
  --preferences '{"MinHealthyPercentage": 90, "InstanceWarmup": 120}'

MinHealthyPercentage mirrors Kubernetes' maxUnavailable concept — ensuring capacity never drops below a safe threshold during the rollout. InstanceWarmup gives new instances time to fully initialize/pass health checks before being counted as healthy for refresh-progression purposes.

Common mistakes

  • Setting MinHealthyPercentage too low for a critical low-instance-count ASG — during refresh, actual serving capacity could dip enough to cause real user-facing degradation.
  • Not accounting for InstanceWarmup on slow-starting apps — the refresh process may consider a not-yet-actually-ready instance as done too early.

Interview Q

Q: How does ASG Instance Refresh's MinHealthyPercentage setting parallel a concept from Kubernetes rolling updates?
A: It's directly analogous to Kubernetes Deployment's maxUnavailable setting (Module 11) — both guarantee a minimum safe level of available capacity throughout a rolling replacement process, preventing the update itself from causing a capacity-driven outage, just applied to whole EC2 instances instead of pods.

★ Mini Project

# Full ASG setup: launch template, target-tracking scaling, ELB health checks, instance refresh

aws ec2 create-launch-template --launch-template-name myapp-lt \
  --launch-template-data '{
    "ImageId": "ami-0abcdef1234567890",
    "InstanceType": "t3.small",
    "IamInstanceProfile": {"Name": "EC2-S3ReadOnly-Profile"},
    "SecurityGroupIds": [""],
    "UserData": ""
  }'

aws autoscaling create-auto-scaling-group \
  --auto-scaling-group-name myapp-asg \
  --launch-template LaunchTemplateName=myapp-lt,Version='$Latest' \
  --min-size 2 --max-size 10 --desired-capacity 3 \
  --vpc-zone-identifier "subnet-priv-a,subnet-priv-b" \
  --target-group-arns  \
  --health-check-type ELB --health-check-grace-period 90

aws autoscaling put-scaling-policy --auto-scaling-group-name myapp-asg \
  --policy-name cpu-tracking --policy-type TargetTrackingScaling \
  --target-tracking-configuration '{"PredefinedMetricSpecification":{"PredefinedMetricType":"ASGAverageCPUUtilization"},"TargetValue":60.0}'

# simulate a deploy: create a new launch template version with an updated AMI, then instance-refresh
aws ec2 create-launch-template-version --launch-template-name myapp-lt \
  --source-version 1 --launch-template-data '{"ImageId":"ami-0newimageid"}'

aws autoscaling start-instance-refresh --auto-scaling-group-name myapp-asg \
  --preferences '{"MinHealthyPercentage":90,"InstanceWarmup":120}'

aws autoscaling describe-instance-refreshes --auto-scaling-group-name myapp-asg   # watch progress

# terminate an instance manually and watch the ASG replace it automatically
aws autoscaling terminate-instance-in-auto-scaling-group --instance-id  --no-should-decrement-desired-capacity

✓ Revision Sheet

  • ASG = EC2-level reconciliation loop, same philosophy as Kubernetes (Module 11): maintain desired count, self-heal on failure.
  • Launch Template = what to launch (AMI, instance type, IAM role, user-data). ASG = how many/where (min/max/desired, subnets, target groups).
  • health-check-type ELB (not default EC2) is essential for real application-level health awareness.
  • Scaling policies: Target Tracking (simplest), Step Scaling (granular thresholds), Scheduled (known patterns), Predictive (ML-based forecast).
  • Lifecycle hooks pause instances in Pending/Terminating states for custom pre-service or pre-termination logic — must call complete-lifecycle-action or it times out (default 1hr).
  • Instance Refresh = rolling replacement on Launch Template change, with MinHealthyPercentage (≈K8s maxUnavailable) and InstanceWarmup controls.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What defines WHAT to launch in an ASG?
The Launch Template (AMI, instance type, IAM role, user-data, etc).
2. What's the risk of leaving health-check-type as the default EC2 instead of ELB?
The ASG won't detect an app-level crash — only basic instance status, which can still report healthy while the actual application is down.
3. What scaling policy type is simplest and most commonly used?
Target Tracking.
4. What scaling approach suits a known, predictable daily traffic spike?
Scheduled Scaling.
5. What must you call after a lifecycle hook's custom logic completes?
complete-lifecycle-action, or the instance stays stuck in Wait state until the hook's timeout expires.
6. What ASG feature is directly analogous to a Kubernetes rolling update?
Instance Refresh.
7. What setting in Instance Refresh parallels Kubernetes' maxUnavailable?
MinHealthyPercentage.
8. What happens if you manually terminate an instance in an ASG?
The ASG detects the capacity gap and automatically launches a replacement.
9. What ASG feature would you use to finish in-flight queue processing before an instance is terminated during scale-in?
A Terminating lifecycle hook.
10. What's a known weakness of pure CPU-based target tracking for an I/O-bound app?
CPU usage may not reflect actual load/strain, causing scaling to react too late or not at all — a custom metric is often more accurate.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. An instance whose app has clearly crashed is never replaced by the ASG.
health-check-type is likely still the default EC2 instead of ELB — the ASG isn't aware of application-level health, only basic instance status.
2. Scaling reacts too slowly during a known daily traffic spike, causing brief user-facing errors right at spike time.
Pure reactive target-tracking scaling inherently lags — add Scheduled Scaling to pre-provision capacity just ahead of the known pattern.
3. An Instance Refresh gets stuck partway with new instances never becoming "healthy" in the refresh's tracking.
Check InstanceWarmup is long enough for the app's actual startup time, and check the Target Group health check (Module 16) is passing on the new instances at all — an underlying app startup failure would also manifest here.
4. An instance stays stuck in "Terminating:Wait" for a very long time during scale-in.
A Terminating lifecycle hook's custom logic likely never called complete-lifecycle-action — check whatever Lambda/script was supposed to handle the hook actually completed and made that call, or it will sit until the hook's timeout (default 1 hour) expires.
5. CPU-based target tracking never triggers scale-out despite the app clearly struggling under load.
The workload is likely I/O or memory bound rather than CPU bound — CPU utilization simply isn't a good proxy for its actual strain; switch to a custom CloudWatch metric (e.g. request count per target, queue depth) that actually reflects real load.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Create a Launch Template and ASG with ELB health checks, register it with an ALB Target Group (Module 16), and verify unhealthy instances get replaced automatically.
  2. Set up target-tracking scaling on CPU and load-test the fleet to observe scale-out and scale-in behavior firsthand.
  3. Add Scheduled Scaling for a specific time and verify capacity actually increases at that time regardless of current load.
  4. Attach a Terminating lifecycle hook with a simple Lambda that logs the event, and observe the instance pausing in Terminating:Wait until you call complete-lifecycle-action.
  5. Perform an Instance Refresh after updating the Launch Template's AMI, tuning MinHealthyPercentage and InstanceWarmup, and observe the gradual rollout.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What problem does an Auto Scaling Group solve?
It automatically maintains a desired number of healthy EC2 instances, replacing failed ones and adjusting fleet size based on demand, removing the need for manual monitoring and manual instance launch/termination.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why is setting health-check-type to ELB important for an ASG fronted by an Application Load Balancer?
Without it, the ASG only checks basic EC2 instance-level status, which stays "healthy" even if the actual application inside has crashed. Setting it to ELB makes the ASG defer to the ALB Target Group's application-level health check, so a genuinely broken application correctly triggers instance replacement, not just a genuinely broken VM.
Intermediate: How would you safely roll out a new application version to an ASG-managed fleet with zero capacity dips below a safe threshold?
Update the Launch Template with the new AMI/version, then use Instance Refresh with an appropriately conservative MinHealthyPercentage (e.g. 90%, ensuring at most 10% of capacity is ever replaced simultaneously) and sufficient InstanceWarmup to let new instances genuinely finish starting up and pass health checks before being counted, ensuring the ALB (Module 16) never routes traffic to a not-yet-ready instance during the transition.
Intermediate-Advanced: Design the scaling strategy for a workload with both a predictable daily pattern and genuinely unpredictable occasional spikes.
Combine Scheduled Scaling to pre-provision a baseline capacity increase ahead of the known predictable pattern (removing the lag inherent to purely reactive scaling for the anticipated load), layered with Target Tracking (or Step Scaling for more granular control) as a reactive safety net handling any load beyond what was predicted — ensuring the system responds gracefully to genuine surprises without over-provisioning a large "just in case" baseline for the entire day.
Advanced: A workload processes messages from a queue, and instances must never be terminated mid-message during scale-in, since messages aren't idempotent and a partial process would corrupt state. Design the ASG configuration to guarantee this.
Attach a Terminating lifecycle hook to the ASG. When scale-in selects an instance for termination, it enters Terminating:Wait — configure a CloudWatch Event/EventBridge rule reacting to this lifecycle transition that signals the specific instance (e.g. via SSM Run Command or a self-polling mechanism on the instance) to stop pulling NEW messages from the queue immediately but finish any message currently being processed. Only once that in-flight message is fully processed and safely acknowledged does the instance (or the automation acting for it) call complete-lifecycle-action, allowing termination to actually proceed — combined with a reasonably generous lifecycle hook timeout (beyond the default 1 hour if messages can genuinely take that long) to avoid a slow message being forcibly cut off anyway once the hook's own timeout is reached.
Module 17 / 30 — AWS Auto Scaling. Next up: AWS S3 — durable object storage, the backbone for static assets, backups, and data lakes.

01 Object Storage vs Block/File Storage

What is it / why needed

S3 (Simple Storage Service) stores arbitrary data as OBJECTS (a blob of bytes + metadata + a unique key) inside BUCKETS — fundamentally different from Module 14's EBS (block storage, mounted as a raw disk a single instance attaches to) or traditional file storage (hierarchical directory trees mounted by multiple clients). It exists because a huge amount of real-world data (images, backups, logs, static website files) doesn't need a filesystem's directory semantics or a block device's low-level access — it needs simple, massively durable, massively scalable key-based storage.

Block (EBS)File (EFS)Object (S3)
Access unitRaw disk blocksFiles in a directory hierarchyWhole objects, addressed by key
Attached toOne instance at a time (usually)Many instances simultaneously (network filesystem)Accessed over HTTP(S) API, not mounted like a disk at all
DurabilityTied to the volume, can fail with the AZ unless snapshottedHighly durable, multi-AZ11 nines of durability by design — data is redundantly stored across multiple AZs automatically
Use caseDatabase data directories, boot volumesShared config/data across many EC2 instancesStatic assets, backups, logs, data lakes, ML training data

Common mistakes

  • Trying to use S3 like a traditional filesystem (frequent small in-place edits) — S3 objects are immutable; "editing" means re-uploading the whole object, which is a poor fit for workloads needing true file-level random-write access (that's what EFS or EBS are for).

Interview Q

Q: Why wouldn't you use S3 as the backing storage for a running database's data directory?
A: S3 is object storage accessed over an HTTP API, not mounted as a low-level block device or POSIX filesystem — databases need direct block-level read/write access with strong consistency and low latency for individual page writes, which S3's object model (whole-object PUT/GET, no in-place partial writes) fundamentally doesn't support. That's exactly the use case EBS (Module 14) is built for instead.

02 Buckets, Keys, Objects

ConceptMeaning
BucketA globally-unique-named (across ALL of AWS, not just your account) top-level container for objects, created in a specific region
KeyThe full "path"-like string identifying an object within a bucket (e.g. images/2026/photo.jpg) — S3 has no REAL directory structure; keys just LOOK hierarchical via the "/" convention, but internally it's a flat key-value namespace
ObjectThe actual data (up to 5TB) plus its metadata (content-type, custom tags, etc.)
"Folders" in S3 are a UI illusion, a real interview gotcha: S3 has no true directory/folder concept internally — images/2026/photo.jpg is just one long key string. The AWS console/CLI display "folders" purely by parsing the "/" characters for convenience; there's no actual nested filesystem structure being traversed underneath.
aws s3 mb s3://my-unique-bucket-name-12345
aws s3 cp app.log s3://my-unique-bucket-name-12345/logs/2026/07/app.log
aws s3 ls s3://my-unique-bucket-name-12345/logs/2026/07/
aws s3 sync ./local-dir s3://my-unique-bucket-name-12345/backup/    # only uploads changed/new files

Common mistakes

  • Assuming "renaming a folder" in S3 is a cheap metadata operation — it's actually a full copy of every object under that key prefix to new keys, then deleting the originals; can be slow/expensive for large "folders."

Interview Q

Q: Why is "renaming a folder" in S3 potentially a slow, expensive operation compared to renaming a folder on a normal filesystem?
A: S3 has no real directory structure — a "folder" is just a shared key prefix among otherwise-flat object keys. Renaming it requires copying every object under that prefix to a new key (new full string) and then deleting the old ones — an O(number of objects) operation, unlike a real filesystem's rename which is typically an O(1) metadata pointer update.

03 Access Control: Policies, ACLs, Block Public Access

Layered access control, directly extending Module 13's IAM

MechanismScope
IAM policyAttached to a USER/ROLE (Module 13) — controls what that identity can do across S3 generally
Bucket policyAttached to the BUCKET itself — a resource-based policy controlling who can access THIS bucket, regardless of their own IAM permissions
ACL (Access Control List)Legacy, object/bucket-level permission grants — mostly superseded by bucket policies; AWS now recommends disabling ACLs entirely for new buckets
Block Public AccessAn account/bucket-level SETTING that can override and block public access even if a policy would otherwise allow it — a safety net against accidental public exposure
The single most common real-world AWS security incident: an accidentally public S3 bucket. Countless data breaches have traced back to a bucket policy (or a legacy ACL) mistakenly granting public read access to sensitive data. AWS's "Block Public Access" setting exists specifically as a blanket safety override — it's enabled by default on new buckets/accounts, and should almost never be disabled without a very deliberate, reviewed reason (e.g. genuinely hosting public static website assets).
# Bucket policy allowing public READ on a specific prefix (e.g. for static website assets)
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": "*",
    "Action": "s3:GetObject",
    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-static-site-bucket/public/*"
  }]
}
# Even with this policy, Block Public Access (if enabled) would STILL prevent public access —
# it must be deliberately disabled for this specific bucket for the policy above to actually take effect.

Common mistakes

  • Writing an overly broad bucket policy (Principal: "*", Resource: entire bucket) when only a specific prefix needed public access.
  • Disabling Block Public Access account-wide instead of scoping the change to just the one bucket that genuinely needs it.

Interview Q

Q: A bucket policy explicitly grants public s3:GetObject access, but objects still aren't publicly reachable. Why?
A: "Block Public Access" is likely still enabled on that bucket (or at the account level) — it's a safety override that can block public access even when a bucket policy would otherwise allow it, specifically to prevent accidental public exposure incidents. It must be deliberately disabled (ideally scoped to just this bucket) for the policy's public grant to actually take effect.

04 Storage Classes & Lifecycle Rules

Storage ClassUse caseRetrieval
S3 StandardFrequently accessed dataImmediate
S3 Intelligent-TieringUnknown/changing access patterns — automatically moves objects between tiers based on actual usageImmediate
S3 Standard-IA (Infrequent Access)Accessed occasionally but needs fast retrieval when neededImmediate, but higher per-GB retrieval cost
S3 Glacier Instant/Flexible/Deep ArchiveLong-term archival, compliance retentionMinutes to hours depending on tier (Deep Archive can take up to 12 hours)
# Lifecycle rule: move to IA after 30 days, Glacier after 90, delete after 365
aws s3api put-bucket-lifecycle-configuration --bucket my-bucket --lifecycle-configuration '{
  "Rules": [{
    "ID": "archive-old-logs",
    "Filter": {"Prefix": "logs/"},
    "Status": "Enabled",
    "Transitions": [
      {"Days": 30, "StorageClass": "STANDARD_IA"},
      {"Days": 90, "StorageClass": "GLACIER"}
    ],
    "Expiration": {"Days": 365}
  }]
}'
Real cost optimization pattern: logs/backups that are rarely accessed after the first month but must be retained for compliance are a textbook Lifecycle Rule use case — automatically tiering down storage cost over time without any manual intervention, often one of the single biggest easy wins in an AWS cost review.

Common mistakes

  • Storing everything in S3 Standard indefinitely without any lifecycle policy — needlessly paying premium storage rates for data that's rarely if ever accessed after a certain age.
  • Moving frequently-needed data to Glacier "to save cost" without accounting for retrieval time (minutes to hours) — breaks any workflow needing that data promptly.

Interview Q

Q: Your S3 storage costs have grown significantly, largely from years of accumulated application logs that are almost never accessed after the first month. What's the straightforward fix?
A: A Lifecycle Rule transitioning objects under the logs/ prefix to a cheaper storage class (Standard-IA after ~30 days, Glacier after ~90 days) and eventually expiring/deleting them after the required retention period — automating the cost-tiering entirely rather than manually managing storage class per object or leaving everything in expensive Standard storage indefinitely.

05 Versioning & Consistency

Versioning

When enabled, S3 keeps every version of an object ever written to a given key, rather than overwriting in place — a "delete" just adds a delete marker (the old version is still recoverable) unless explicitly permanently removed. This is a real production safety net against accidental overwrites/deletions.

Versioning cost gotcha: every version of every object still counts toward storage cost — a bucket with versioning enabled and frequent overwrites of large objects (without a lifecycle rule to expire old versions) can silently accumulate significant unexpected storage cost over time.

Consistency model

S3 now provides strong read-after-write consistency for all operations (a relatively recent AWS change — historically S3 only offered eventual consistency for overwrite/delete operations, an older but still sometimes-referenced interview point). A PUT immediately followed by a GET for that same key is guaranteed to return the latest version.

Common mistakes

  • Enabling versioning without a lifecycle rule to eventually clean up old versions — unbounded storage cost growth over time.
  • Assuming a "deleted" object with versioning enabled is truly gone — it's just marked with a delete marker; the actual data (and its storage cost) persists until explicitly permanently deleted.

Interview Q

Q: A bucket has versioning enabled. A teammate "deletes" an important file, but you're told it's actually recoverable. Why?
A: With versioning enabled, a normal delete operation just adds a "delete marker" as the new current version — the actual previous object version remains fully stored and can be recovered by removing the delete marker or explicitly restoring that prior version. True permanent removal requires explicitly deleting that specific version, not just a regular delete call.

★ Mini Project

# Production-style S3 setup: versioned bucket, lifecycle rules, scoped bucket policy, VPC endpoint access

aws s3api create-bucket --bucket myapp-artifacts-12345 --region ap-south-1 \
  --create-bucket-configuration LocationConstraint=ap-south-1

aws s3api put-bucket-versioning --bucket myapp-artifacts-12345 --versioning-configuration Status=Enabled

aws s3api put-public-access-block --bucket myapp-artifacts-12345 --public-access-block-configuration \
  BlockPublicAcls=true,IgnorePublicAcls=true,BlockPublicPolicy=true,RestrictPublicBuckets=true

aws s3api put-bucket-lifecycle-configuration --bucket myapp-artifacts-12345 --lifecycle-configuration '{
  "Rules": [{
    "ID": "cleanup-old-versions",
    "Status": "Enabled",
    "Filter": {},
    "NoncurrentVersionExpiration": {"NoncurrentDays": 30}
  }]
}'

# Upload and demonstrate versioning
aws s3 cp app-v1.jar s3://myapp-artifacts-12345/releases/app.jar
aws s3 cp app-v2.jar s3://myapp-artifacts-12345/releases/app.jar    # overwrites — but old version preserved
aws s3api list-object-versions --bucket myapp-artifacts-12345 --prefix releases/app.jar

# Delete and prove recoverability
aws s3 rm s3://myapp-artifacts-12345/releases/app.jar
aws s3api list-object-versions --bucket myapp-artifacts-12345 --prefix releases/app.jar   # shows delete marker + old versions still present

✓ Revision Sheet

  • S3 = object storage (key-value, flat namespace, HTTP API) — fundamentally different from EBS (block) or EFS (file).
  • "Folders" in S3 are a UI illusion over flat keys with "/" — renaming a "folder" is O(n) copy+delete, not O(1).
  • Access control layers: IAM policy (identity-based) + bucket policy (resource-based) + Block Public Access (safety override) + legacy ACLs (avoid for new buckets).
  • Block Public Access can override an otherwise-permissive bucket policy — must be deliberately disabled for intentional public access.
  • Storage classes: Standard → Intelligent-Tiering/Standard-IA → Glacier tiers, trading retrieval speed for cost. Lifecycle rules automate tiering/expiration.
  • Versioning: keeps all versions; delete = delete marker, not permanent removal, unless explicitly version-deleted. Real safety net, real potential cost if unmanaged.
  • S3 provides strong read-after-write consistency today.

Q 10 MCQs

1. Does S3 have real nested directories internally?
No — keys are flat strings; "/" is just a display convention.
2. What can override an otherwise-permissive bucket policy to prevent public access?
Block Public Access.
3. What storage class suits data with unpredictable/changing access patterns?
S3 Intelligent-Tiering.
4. What does a Lifecycle Rule automate?
Automatic transition between storage classes over time, and/or expiration/deletion of objects after a set period.
5. With versioning enabled, does a normal delete permanently remove an object?
No — it adds a delete marker; the prior version remains recoverable until explicitly version-deleted.
6. What's a risk of enabling versioning without a cleanup lifecycle rule?
Unbounded storage cost growth as old versions accumulate indefinitely.
7. What's the difference between an IAM policy and a bucket policy?
IAM policy is attached to a user/role, controlling what that identity can do. Bucket policy is attached to the bucket itself (resource-based), controlling who can access it regardless of their own IAM permissions.
8. Does AWS recommend using ACLs for new S3 buckets?
No — bucket policies are preferred; AWS recommends disabling ACLs for new buckets.
9. What's the retrieval time consideration for Glacier Deep Archive?
Can take up to about 12 hours — unsuitable for data needed promptly.
10. What consistency model does S3 provide today for reads after writes?
Strong read-after-write consistency.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. A bucket policy grants public read access, but objects still return Access Denied publicly.
Block Public Access is likely still enabled on the bucket or account — needs to be deliberately disabled for the intended public prefix/bucket.
2. S3 storage costs have grown far beyond expectations for a logs bucket.
Likely no lifecycle rule tiering/expiring old logs, and/or versioning enabled with no cleanup rule for old versions accumulating indefinitely.
3. An application expects immediate retrieval of an object just archived to Glacier, and it fails.
Glacier tiers require an explicit restore request and a retrieval delay (minutes to hours) before the object becomes accessible again — not suitable for data needed on-demand without that lead time.
4. A "renamed" S3 folder with millions of objects takes an unexpectedly long time and costs more than anticipated.
Expected — S3 has no real rename operation; it's a full copy of every object to new keys plus deletion of the originals, an O(n) operation scaling with object count.
5. A teammate accidentally overwrote a critical file in a versioned bucket and needs the previous content back.
List object versions for that key (aws s3api list-object-versions) and restore/copy the specific prior version back as the current version — versioning preserves the overwritten content as a prior version rather than losing it.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Create a versioned bucket, overwrite an object multiple times, and list/restore a specific prior version.
  2. Write a bucket policy granting public read on only a specific prefix, and demonstrate Block Public Access overriding it until deliberately disabled.
  3. Set up a lifecycle rule transitioning objects through Standard-IA and Glacier, and verify (or simulate) the transition timings.
  4. Enable versioning, delete an object, and prove via list-object-versions that the data is still present behind a delete marker; then permanently remove that specific version.
  5. Set up a VPC Gateway Endpoint for S3 (from Module 15) and verify via a private-subnet instance that S3 access works without any NAT Gateway route.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What is S3 and how is it different from a traditional filesystem?
S3 is object storage — a flat key-value namespace for arbitrary-sized blobs of data, accessed over an HTTP API. Unlike a traditional filesystem, it has no true directory hierarchy (just a "/" naming convention), no in-place partial-file edits (objects are replaced wholesale), and is designed for massive scale/durability rather than low-latency block-level access.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why is "Block Public Access" enabled by default, and when would you disable it?
It's a safety-net override preventing accidental public exposure of data even if a bucket policy or (legacy) ACL would otherwise grant public access — protecting against the single most common category of real-world S3 security incidents. You'd disable it (ideally scoped to one specific bucket, not account-wide) only when you deliberately intend to serve public content, such as static website assets, with a carefully scoped bucket policy limiting exactly what's public.
Intermediate: Design a cost-effective S3 storage strategy for an application generating large daily log files that must be retained for 7 years for compliance but are almost never accessed after 60 days.
Store new logs in S3 Standard for immediate access during the first ~30 days when they're most likely to be queried for active debugging. A Lifecycle Rule transitions them to Standard-IA around day 30 (still reasonably fast retrieval, lower cost, for the occasional lookup), then to Glacier (or Glacier Deep Archive, given the multi-year retention with near-zero access expectation) around day 90, minimizing storage cost for the vast majority of the 7-year retention period where retrieval speed genuinely doesn't matter. An Expiration rule at the 7-year mark automatically removes the data once compliance retention is satisfied, with no manual cleanup process needed.
Intermediate-Advanced: A security audit flags an S3 bucket containing sensitive customer data as publicly accessible. Walk through the full incident response.
Immediately enable Block Public Access on the bucket (and audit whether it should be default account-wide, if this wasn't an intentional exception) to stop further exposure right away. Review CloudTrail data-event logs for that bucket to determine what was actually accessed, by whom, and over what window — establishing actual breach scope, not just theoretical exposure. Identify and fix the root cause (an overly permissive bucket policy, a legacy public ACL, or a misconfigured Block Public Access setting) and add automated detection going forward (AWS Config rules or S3 Access Analyzer specifically flagging public buckets) so this class of misconfiguration is caught immediately rather than only during periodic audits. Depending on the sensitivity/regulatory context of the exposed data, this may also trigger mandatory breach disclosure obligations — a business/legal decision informed by, but beyond, the technical remediation.
Advanced: Design a multi-account AWS architecture where a central logging account needs to receive S3 access logs and CloudTrail logs from dozens of other accounts, with strict access control ensuring log integrity (logs can be written but never modified or deleted by the source accounts).
Each source account's services (S3 access logging, CloudTrail) are configured to deliver logs to a bucket in the CENTRAL logging account, using a bucket policy on that central bucket granting each source account's specific service principal (e.g. cloudtrail.amazonaws.com scoped via aws:SourceArn conditions to each specific source account) PutObject permission only — never GetObject, DeleteObject, or any modification action. Enable S3 Object Lock (WORM — write-once-read-many) in Compliance mode on the central logging bucket, making it impossible for even the bucket owner/root to delete or overwrite objects before a configured retention period expires, providing genuine tamper-evidence for audit/forensic purposes. Versioning is enabled as a baseline requirement for Object Lock to function. Cross-account access to READ those logs (for the security team) is granted via a separate, tightly-scoped IAM role in the central account, keeping write-access (source accounts) and read-access (security team) as entirely separate permission grants on the same bucket.

AWS RDS, from first principles to production

Managed relational databases — AWS handles patching, backups, and failover for Postgres/MySQL/etc, removing the operational burden of running a database on raw EC2 (Module 14) yourself.

01 Linux02 Bash03 Git04 Networking05 HTTP06 DNS07 SSH08 Nginx09 Docker10 Compose11 K8s12 Helm13 IAM14 EC215 VPC16 ALB17 ASG18 S319 RDS20 ECR21 EKS22 CloudWatch23 Actions24 Jenkins25 Prometheus26 Grafana27 Terraform28 Ansible29 ArgoCD30 SysDesign

01 Why RDS / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

RDS (Relational Database Service) is a managed database offering (Postgres, MySQL, MariaDB, SQL Server, Oracle, plus Aurora — AWS's own compatible high-performance engine). It exists because running a production database yourself on EC2 means YOU own patching, backups, failover, replication setup, and monitoring — a substantial, error-prone operational burden that RDS automates.

Self-managed on EC2RDS
You patch the OS and database engineAWS handles patching (with a configurable maintenance window)
You build your own backup/snapshot processAutomated daily backups + point-in-time recovery built in
You build your own replication/failoverMulti-AZ automatic failover is a checkbox
Full control over every configurationSome configuration abstracted away (no direct SSH to the DB host at all)

Common mistakes

  • Running a production database on self-managed EC2 "for full control" without a genuinely strong reason — reinventing what RDS already solves reliably, at real ongoing operational cost.

Interview Q

Q: What operational burdens does RDS remove compared to running Postgres yourself on an EC2 instance?
A: Automated patching (OS and database engine) on a maintenance window you control, automated backups with point-in-time recovery, and built-in high-availability failover (Multi-AZ) — all things you'd otherwise need to build, test, and maintain yourself if self-managing the database on raw EC2.

02 Multi-AZ vs Read Replicas

The single most confused RDS distinction in interviews — they solve DIFFERENT problems.

MULTI-AZ (high availability — for FAILOVER, not read scaling): Primary (AZ-a) ──synchronous replication──► Standby (AZ-b) │ │ serves ALL traffic NOT accessible for queries at all — (read + write) purely a hot standby for failover If primary fails: AWS automatically fails over to the standby (same endpoint, DNS re-points) — typically 60-120 seconds of downtime, no manual intervention. READ REPLICAS (read scaling — NOT primarily for HA): Primary ──asynchronous replication──► Read Replica 1 (can serve read queries) │ └───► Read Replica 2 (can serve read queries) All WRITES still go to the primary only. Reads can be distributed across replicas to reduce load on the primary. Replication lag exists (asynchronous) — a replica might briefly serve slightly stale data relative to the primary.
Multi-AZRead Replica
PurposeHigh availability / failoverRead scaling
ReplicationSynchronousAsynchronous (replication lag possible)
Standby queryable?No — standby is not accessible for queriesYes — replicas actively serve read queries
Automatic failoverYesNo (a replica CAN be manually promoted to standalone, but it's not automatic failover)
The common interview trap: Multi-AZ does NOT help with read scaling (the standby isn't queryable at all) and Read Replicas do NOT provide automatic failover (they're async, with lag, and require manual promotion). Production systems needing BOTH properties often deploy Multi-AZ (for HA) with Read Replicas built off the Multi-AZ primary (for read scaling) — the two features are complementary, not substitutes for each other.

Common mistakes

  • Enabling Multi-AZ and expecting it to reduce load on the primary by serving reads from the standby — it can't; the standby is failover-only.
  • Relying on Read Replicas alone for disaster recovery — no automatic failover, and promoting one to a standalone primary is a manual, one-way operation (can't easily revert).

Interview Q

Q: Your database is under heavy read load. Would enabling Multi-AZ help?
A: No — Multi-AZ's standby instance is not queryable at all; it exists purely as a synchronously-replicated hot standby for automatic failover in case the primary fails. To scale read load, you need Read Replicas instead, which ARE queryable (asynchronously replicated, with some possible lag) and can have read traffic distributed across them.

03 Backups & Point-in-Time Recovery

FeatureWhat it does
Automated backupsDaily snapshot + continuous transaction log backup, retained for a configurable period (up to 35 days)
Point-in-Time Recovery (PITR)Restore the database to ANY specific second within the retention window, not just a daily snapshot boundary — achieved by replaying transaction logs on top of the nearest snapshot
Manual snapshotsUser-triggered, retained indefinitely until explicitly deleted (unlike automated backups, which expire per the retention setting)
aws rds restore-db-instance-to-point-in-time \
  --source-db-instance-identifier myapp-db \
  --target-db-instance-identifier myapp-db-restored \
  --restore-time 2026-07-08T14:30:00Z
Real incident pattern this solves: a bad migration or an accidental DELETE without a WHERE clause happens at 2:15pm — PITR lets you restore to 2:14pm precisely, recovering nearly all data right up to just before the incident, rather than losing an entire day back to the last daily snapshot.

Common mistakes

  • Setting backup retention to the minimum, or disabling it entirely "to save cost" — removes the safety net for exactly the kind of incident PITR is meant to solve.
  • Restoring a PITR snapshot AS the same instance in place — restore always creates a NEW instance; you then need to redirect application connections to it (and often rename instances to swap the new restored one into the original's place).

Interview Q

Q: A bad deployment ran a destructive query at 2:15pm. How would RDS's Point-in-Time Recovery help, and what's the actual recovery process?
A: PITR can restore the database to its exact state at, say, 2:14pm (just before the destructive query), by replaying transaction logs on top of the nearest prior snapshot up to that specific second — recovering nearly all data rather than losing everything back to the last daily backup. The restore creates a brand NEW RDS instance (not an in-place rewind of the existing one) — you'd then need to validate the restored data and redirect the application (often via a DNS/endpoint swap or instance rename) to point at the newly restored instance.

04 Networking & Security

Directly applies Module 15's VPC concepts: RDS instances live in a DB Subnet Group (a set of private subnets across multiple AZs), with a Security Group (Module 14) permitting inbound access ONLY from the application tier's security group — never from the public internet directly.

Public subnet (ALB) ──► Private "app" subnet (Security Group: app-sg) │ DB Security Group allows inbound 5432 ONLY from app-sg ▼ Private "db" subnet (RDS instance, NO public accessibility)
SettingProduction recommendation
Publicly AccessibleNo — database should never be directly internet-reachable
Encryption at restEnabled — uses AWS KMS, essentially free peace of mind
Encryption in transitEnforce SSL/TLS connections from the application (same TLS concepts from Module 5)
IAM database authenticationOptional but recommended where supported — lets DB access be governed by IAM (Module 13) short-lived tokens instead of long-lived static DB passwords

Common mistakes

  • Setting "Publicly Accessible" to Yes for developer convenience — needlessly exposes the database directly to the internet, dependent entirely on Security Group rules as the only remaining protection layer.
  • Storing the DB password as a plain environment variable instead of in AWS Secrets Manager (with automatic rotation) or using IAM database authentication.

Interview Q

Q: Why should an RDS instance almost never have "Publicly Accessible" set to true in production?
A: It assigns the database a public endpoint reachable directly from the internet, relying entirely on Security Group rules as the sole protective layer — any misconfiguration there directly exposes the database. Keeping it private (accessible only from within the VPC, via app-tier security groups) adds a fundamental network-layer barrier in addition to security groups, following the same least-exposure principle as keeping app servers in private subnets (Module 15).

05 Parameter Groups & Maintenance

A Parameter Group holds engine configuration settings (equivalent to editing postgresql.conf directly) — since you can't SSH into an RDS instance, all engine-level tuning goes through parameter groups instead.

ConceptNote
Parameter GroupEngine config (connection limits, query timeouts, logging verbosity)
Option GroupEngine-specific add-on features (e.g. Oracle-specific options)
Maintenance WindowA configured time window when AWS applies pending patches/updates — choose off-peak hours deliberately
Static vs Dynamic parametersDynamic parameters apply immediately; static parameters require an instance REBOOT to take effect — a real operational planning consideration

Common mistakes

  • Not realizing some parameter changes require a reboot (static parameters) — assuming a change is live immediately when it actually needs a maintenance-window restart.
  • Leaving the default maintenance window unconfigured/random — a patch could apply and briefly restart the instance during actual peak traffic hours.

Interview Q

Q: You changed an RDS parameter group setting but the change doesn't seem to be in effect. What might explain this?
A: Some RDS parameters are "static" rather than "dynamic" — static parameter changes are recorded but only actually take effect after the instance is rebooted, unlike dynamic parameters which apply immediately. Check the parameter's type in the parameter group documentation/console and schedule a reboot during your maintenance window if it's static.

★ Mini Project

# Production-style RDS setup: Multi-AZ, private subnets, encrypted, with a read replica

aws rds create-db-subnet-group --db-subnet-group-name myapp-db-subnets \
  --subnet-ids subnet-priv-db-a subnet-priv-db-b \
  --db-subnet-group-description "private DB subnets across 2 AZs"

aws rds create-db-instance \
  --db-instance-identifier myapp-db \
  --db-instance-class db.t3.medium \
  --engine postgres \
  --master-username admin \
  --master-user-password  \
  --allocated-storage 50 \
  --db-subnet-group-name myapp-db-subnets \
  --vpc-security-group-ids  \
  --multi-az \
  --storage-encrypted \
  --backup-retention-period 14 \
  --no-publicly-accessible

# Create a read replica for read-scaling
aws rds create-db-instance-read-replica \
  --db-instance-identifier myapp-db-replica-1 \
  --source-db-instance-identifier myapp-db

# Simulate PITR: restore to 10 minutes ago
aws rds restore-db-instance-to-point-in-time \
  --source-db-instance-identifier myapp-db \
  --target-db-instance-identifier myapp-db-pitr-test \
  --restore-time $(date -u -d '10 minutes ago' +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S)

# Verify Multi-AZ failover behavior (in a test environment)
aws rds reboot-db-instance --db-instance-identifier myapp-db --force-failover

✓ Revision Sheet

  • RDS removes the operational burden of patching, backups, and failover vs self-managed database on EC2.
  • Multi-AZ = synchronous, HA/failover only, standby NOT queryable. Read Replica = asynchronous, read-scaling, queryable, no automatic failover. Different problems, often used together.
  • Automated backups + PITR: restore to any second within retention window, not just daily snapshot boundaries. Restore always creates a NEW instance.
  • Keep "Publicly Accessible" off; database lives in private subnets, Security Group allows only the app tier.
  • Enable encryption at rest (KMS) and enforce TLS in transit; consider IAM database authentication over static passwords.
  • Parameter Group = engine config (no SSH access to tune directly). Static parameters need a reboot to apply; dynamic apply immediately.
  • Configure the maintenance window deliberately for off-peak hours.

Q 10 MCQs

1. Is a Multi-AZ standby instance queryable?
No — it's purely a synchronously-replicated hot standby for failover.
2. Does a Read Replica provide automatic failover?
No — replication is asynchronous and promotion to standalone is manual.
3. What does Point-in-Time Recovery let you restore to?
Any specific second within the backup retention window, not just a daily snapshot boundary.
4. Does a PITR restore modify the existing instance in place?
No — it creates a brand new RDS instance.
5. Should an RDS instance be set to "Publicly Accessible" in production?
No — should remain private, reachable only from within the VPC via app-tier security groups.
6. What holds engine-level configuration settings for RDS, since you can't SSH in directly?
Parameter Groups.
7. What's the difference between a static and dynamic RDS parameter?
Static parameters require an instance reboot to take effect; dynamic parameters apply immediately.
8. What replication type does Multi-AZ use?
Synchronous.
9. What replication type do Read Replicas use?
Asynchronous (with possible lag).
10. What's the maximum typical automated backup retention period in RDS?
35 days.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. Enabling Multi-AZ didn't reduce load on the primary database.
Expected — Multi-AZ's standby is not queryable at all; use Read Replicas instead for actual read-scaling.
2. A read replica occasionally returns slightly outdated data compared to the primary.
Expected behavior — replication to read replicas is asynchronous, so some replication lag is normal; for reads requiring absolute freshness, query the primary directly instead.
3. A parameter group change was saved but the database's behavior hasn't changed.
The changed parameter is likely "static" and requires an instance reboot to take effect — check parameter type and schedule a reboot in the maintenance window.
4. After a PITR restore, the application is still writing to the old (pre-incident) instance.
PITR creates a NEW instance with a new endpoint — the application's connection string/config must be explicitly updated (or a DNS/endpoint swap performed) to point at the restored instance; it doesn't happen automatically.
5. A security review finds an RDS instance is publicly accessible with only a Security Group protecting it.
Change Publicly Accessible to false, moving the instance's actual network reachability to VPC-internal only, rather than relying solely on Security Group rules as the only protection against a directly internet-facing database endpoint.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Launch an RDS instance with Multi-AZ enabled, force a failover, and time how long the application experiences connectivity loss.
  2. Create a Read Replica, generate write load on the primary, and observe replication lag by comparing query results between primary and replica.
  3. Perform a Point-in-Time Recovery restore to a specific timestamp and validate the restored data matches expectations at that moment.
  4. Change a static parameter in a parameter group, confirm it's NOT yet in effect, reboot the instance, and confirm it now is.
  5. Configure a DB Security Group allowing access only from a specific app-tier Security Group (not any CIDR range), and verify direct access from elsewhere is denied.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What does RDS manage for you that you'd otherwise have to handle yourself?
OS and database engine patching, automated backups with point-in-time recovery, and (with Multi-AZ) automatic failover to a standby — all things that require significant manual setup and ongoing maintenance if running a database yourself on EC2.
Beginner-Intermediate: Explain the difference between Multi-AZ and Read Replicas, and why they solve different problems.
Multi-AZ maintains a synchronously-replicated standby purely for automatic failover if the primary fails — the standby isn't queryable and doesn't help with load. Read Replicas are asynchronously-replicated, independently queryable copies used to distribute read traffic away from the primary, but provide no automatic failover of their own. A system needing both HA and read-scaling typically uses both features together, since neither substitutes for the other.
Intermediate: Design a disaster recovery strategy for a critical production RDS database, considering both hardware failure and human error (accidental data deletion).
For hardware/AZ failure: Multi-AZ provides automatic failover with minimal downtime. For accidental data deletion or a bad migration: automated backups with Point-in-Time Recovery allow restoring to the exact second before the incident, since Multi-AZ's synchronous replication would have ALSO replicated the bad delete to the standby (Multi-AZ protects against infrastructure failure, not logical/application-level mistakes) — the two mechanisms cover genuinely different failure modes and both matter for a complete DR strategy.
Intermediate-Advanced: A read-heavy application is experiencing high latency on the primary RDS instance from read queries, but writes still need strong consistency. Design a solution.
Add one or more Read Replicas and route read-only queries to them (via application-level read/write splitting logic, or a proxy like RDS Proxy / a database connection router), keeping all writes directed at the primary. Accept that reads from replicas may reflect a small replication lag — for any specific reads that genuinely require the absolute latest write (e.g. reading back data immediately after writing it in the same user flow), route those specific queries to the primary rather than a replica, rather than assuming ALL reads can safely move to replicas.
Advanced: Design the full networking and security posture for an RDS instance supporting a compliance-sensitive application (e.g. handling financial data).
Instance lives in private subnets only (Module 15), spanning multiple AZs via a DB Subnet Group, with Publicly Accessible explicitly disabled. Security Group allows inbound access ONLY from the application tier's specific security group on the database port — no CIDR-based rules at all. Encryption at rest enabled via KMS (ideally a customer-managed key for additional control/audit over key rotation and access policies, rather than the default AWS-managed key). Encryption in transit enforced by requiring SSL/TLS for all connections (rejecting non-TLS connection attempts at the parameter group level where the engine supports it). Credentials stored in AWS Secrets Manager with automatic rotation configured, rather than static passwords in application config or environment variables — or IAM database authentication where the engine/access pattern supports it, avoiding long-lived database credentials entirely. CloudTrail and RDS-specific logging (e.g. Postgres's log_statement) enabled and shipped to a centralized, tamper-evident logging account (per Module 18's design) for audit trail purposes, satisfying typical compliance requirements around data access logging.
Module 19 / 30 — AWS RDS. Next up: AWS ECR — the container registry for images built in Module 9, feeding into EKS deployments in Module 21.

AWS ECR, from first principles to production

A private, IAM-integrated home for the Docker images built in Module 9 — the missing link between "docker build" on your laptop and "kubectl apply" pulling that exact image in EKS (Module 21).
