01 Why ECR / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

ECR (Elastic Container Registry) is AWS's private Docker registry — storing the images built in Module 9's Dockerfile workflow, integrated directly with IAM (Module 13) for access control instead of a separate credential system. It exists because pushing production images to a public registry (Docker Hub) is often unacceptable (proprietary code baked into layers, rate limits, no fine-grained access control), and self-hosting a private registry is unnecessary undifferentiated operational work.

Docker Hub (public)ECR (private, AWS-native)
Access controlDocker Hub's own account/org systemIAM policies (Module 13) — same identity system as everything else in AWS
Network pathPublic internetCan stay entirely within AWS's network (via VPC Endpoints, Module 15) — no NAT Gateway cost, no internet dependency
Rate limitsYes, for anonymous/free-tier pullsNone (within your own account)

Common mistakes

  • Pulling base images from Docker Hub directly in a CI/CD pipeline running at scale — hitting Docker Hub's rate limits; ECR Public (a separate ECR feature) or pull-through cache can mitigate this.

Interview Q

Q: Why would a company use ECR instead of just pushing images to Docker Hub?
A: ECR integrates natively with IAM, so image push/pull access is controlled with the exact same identity and policy system (Module 13) as every other AWS resource, rather than a separate credential system. It also allows traffic to stay entirely within AWS's private network via VPC Endpoints (avoiding NAT Gateway costs and internet dependency for pulls from EKS/ECS), and avoids Docker Hub's rate limits entirely for private, high-volume internal use.

02 Authentication & Push/Pull Flow

How it works internally

ECR authentication uses temporary tokens obtained via the AWS CLI/SDK (leveraging whatever IAM identity — user or role, per Module 13 — is currently authenticated), NOT a long-lived static Docker Hub-style password. The Docker CLI is then given this token to authenticate against the registry, valid for 12 hours.

# 1. Authenticate Docker CLI against ECR using current IAM identity
aws ecr get-login-password --region ap-south-1 | \
  docker login --username AWS --password-stdin .dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com

# 2. Tag your locally-built image (from Module 9) for ECR
docker tag myapp:1.0 .dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/myapp:1.0

# 3. Push
docker push .dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/myapp:1.0

# 4. Pull (from an EC2 instance/EKS node with an appropriate IAM role, Module 13)
docker pull .dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/myapp:1.0
This is exactly the same "role over static credentials" principle from Module 13 applied to registry access — an EKS node's IAM role (or IRSA-scoped pod role, covered in Module 21) can be granted ecr:GetDownloadUrlForLayer / ecr:BatchGetImage permissions, letting it pull images with zero static registry credentials configured anywhere.

Common mistakes

  • Hardcoding a docker login token/credential in a script or CI config instead of dynamically fetching a fresh one each time — tokens expire (12 hours), so a stale hardcoded one silently breaks pulls later.
  • Forgetting the repository must exist BEFORE the first push — unlike Docker Hub's implicit repo creation on push, ECR (by default) requires the repository to be explicitly created first (though `--create-repository` automation flags exist in some tooling).

Interview Q

Q: How does ECR authentication differ fundamentally from a typical Docker Hub username/password login?
A: ECR uses short-lived (12-hour) tokens dynamically obtained via the AWS CLI/SDK based on whatever IAM identity (user or role) is currently authenticated — there's no long-lived static registry password to manage or leak. This lets EC2/EKS compute pull images using their attached IAM role's permissions (Module 13) with zero static credentials configured anywhere, unlike a typical registry login requiring a persistent stored credential.

03 Image Scanning

What is it / why needed

ECR can automatically scan pushed images for known CVEs (Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures) in OS packages and (with enhanced scanning) application dependencies — directly addressing Module 9's security section point about needing to scan images before they ship.

aws ecr put-image-scanning-configuration \
  --repository-name myapp --image-scanning-configuration scanOnPush=true

aws ecr describe-image-scan-findings --repository-name myapp --image-id imageTag=1.0
Scan-on-push alone doesn't block a bad deploy — a real gotcha. By default, ECR scanning just reports findings; it doesn't automatically prevent a vulnerable image from being pulled and deployed. A real production pipeline needs an explicit gate (a CI/CD step checking scan results and failing the pipeline above a severity threshold) to actually enforce anything based on scan findings.

Common mistakes

  • Enabling scan-on-push and assuming that alone prevents vulnerable images from reaching production — it only reports; enforcement requires an explicit pipeline gate.
  • Not scanning base images at all, only your own application layer — a vulnerable base image (Module 9's layer discussion) is just as real a risk.

Interview Q

Q: Does enabling ECR's scan-on-push feature automatically stop a critically vulnerable image from being deployed?
A: No — by default, scanning only reports findings; it doesn't block anything on its own. A real production pipeline needs to explicitly query the scan results (aws ecr describe-image-scan-findings) as a CI/CD gate and fail the deployment if findings exceed an acceptable severity threshold — the scan is a data source, not an enforcement mechanism by itself.

04 Lifecycle Policies

What is it / why needed

Directly parallel to Module 18's S3 lifecycle rules: an ECR repository accumulates images with every build, and without cleanup, storage cost grows indefinitely holding old, unused image versions. A lifecycle policy automatically expires old images based on rules.

{
  "rules": [{
    "rulePriority": 1,
    "description": "Keep only the last 10 tagged images",
    "selection": {
      "tagStatus": "tagged",
      "tagPrefixList": ["v"],
      "countType": "imageCountMoreThan",
      "countNumber": 10
    },
    "action": {"type": "expire"}
  }, {
    "rulePriority": 2,
    "description": "Expire untagged images after 7 days",
    "selection": {
      "tagStatus": "untagged",
      "countType": "sinceImagePushed",
      "countUnit": "days",
      "countNumber": 7
    },
    "action": {"type": "expire"}
  }]
}

Common mistakes

  • No lifecycle policy at all — repositories accumulate years of build artifacts, most never referenced again, purely wasting storage cost.
  • An overly aggressive policy that expires a tagged image still referenced by a running production deployment — always confirm currently-deployed tags are excluded/protected from expiry rules.

Interview Q

Q: Why is it particularly important to have a lifecycle policy expiring UNTAGGED images specifically, separate from tagged ones?
A: Untagged images typically result from CI/CD pipeline builds where a new push to the same tag (e.g. "latest") orphans the previous image (its old tag reference is gone, but the underlying image layers remain, now untagged) — these accumulate rapidly with every single build and are essentially never intentionally referenced again, making them the highest-value, lowest-risk cleanup target compared to explicitly version-tagged images that might still be actively referenced by a deployment.

★ Mini Project

# Full build → scan → push → lifecycle-managed ECR workflow, building on Module 9's Dockerfile

aws ecr create-repository --repository-name myapp --image-scanning-configuration scanOnPush=true

# Build using Module 9's multi-stage Dockerfile
docker build -t myapp:1.0 .

# Authenticate and push
aws ecr get-login-password --region ap-south-1 | \
  docker login --username AWS --password-stdin .dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com
docker tag myapp:1.0 .dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/myapp:1.0
docker push .dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/myapp:1.0

# Check scan results — and GATE on them, not just log them
aws ecr wait image-scan-complete --repository-name myapp --image-id imageTag=1.0
FINDINGS=$(aws ecr describe-image-scan-findings --repository-name myapp --image-id imageTag=1.0 \
  --query 'imageScanFindings.findingSeverityCounts.CRITICAL' --output text)
if [ "$FINDINGS" != "None" ] && [ "$FINDINGS" -gt 0 ]; then
  echo "CRITICAL vulnerabilities found — failing pipeline"
  exit 1
fi

# Attach a lifecycle policy
aws ecr put-lifecycle-policy --repository-name myapp --lifecycle-policy-text file://lifecycle-policy.json

# Verify an EC2/EKS node with the right IAM role (Module 13) can pull with zero static creds
# (from the instance itself, having only an attached IAM role with ecr:GetDownloadUrlForLayer etc.)
docker pull .dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/myapp:1.0

✓ Revision Sheet

  • ECR = private, IAM-integrated Docker registry — same identity/policy system as the rest of AWS (Module 13), unlike Docker Hub's separate credentials.
  • Auth uses short-lived (12hr) tokens fetched dynamically via AWS CLI/SDK, not static passwords — compute can pull images via its attached IAM role, zero static registry credentials needed.
  • Repository must generally exist before first push (unlike Docker Hub's implicit creation).
  • Scan-on-push reports CVE findings but does NOT block deployment by itself — needs an explicit CI/CD gate checking findings against a severity threshold.
  • Lifecycle policies auto-expire old images (mirrors S3's lifecycle rules, Module 18) — untagged images from repeated pushes to the same tag are the highest-value, safest cleanup target.
  • Can stay entirely within AWS's private network via VPC Endpoints (Module 15), avoiding NAT Gateway cost/dependency for pulls.

Q 10 MCQs

1. How long is an ECR authentication token typically valid?
12 hours.
2. Does ECR use the same access-control system as the rest of AWS?
Yes — IAM policies (Module 13), not a separate credential system.
3. Does scan-on-push automatically block a vulnerable image from being deployed?
No — it only reports findings; blocking requires an explicit pipeline gate.
4. What kind of images are the highest-value target for lifecycle expiry cleanup?
Untagged images, typically orphaned by repeated pushes to the same tag.
5. Can an EC2/EKS node pull ECR images with zero static credentials configured?
Yes — via its attached IAM role's permissions (Module 13's role-based access pattern).
6. What AWS networking feature lets ECR pulls stay entirely within AWS's private network?
VPC Endpoints (Module 15).
7. Does ECR generally require a repository to exist before the first image push?
Yes, by default — unlike Docker Hub's implicit repo creation on push.
8. What CLI command fetches an ECR login token?
aws ecr get-login-password
9. What tells you the actual CVE findings for a pushed image?
aws ecr describe-image-scan-findings
10. What's a real risk of hardcoding an ECR docker login token in a long-running script?
The token expires within 12 hours, so a stale hardcoded one will silently break pulls once expired — should be freshly fetched each time.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. `docker push` fails with an authentication error despite having successfully logged in earlier.
The ECR token has likely expired (12hr validity) — re-run `aws ecr get-login-password | docker login` to fetch a fresh token.
2. `docker push` fails with "repository does not exist."
The ECR repository must generally be explicitly created first (aws ecr create-repository) before the first push, unlike Docker Hub's implicit creation.
3. An EKS node fails to pull an image with an IAM/permission-related error.
The node's (or pod's, via IRSA) IAM role likely lacks the necessary ECR permissions (ecr:GetDownloadUrlForLayer, ecr:BatchGetImage, ecr:GetAuthorizationToken) — check the attached role's policy per Module 13.
4. A critical CVE was found in a production image weeks after deployment, and no one noticed.
Scan-on-push was likely enabled but never actually gated in the CI/CD pipeline — findings were generated but nothing failed the build or alerted on them; add an explicit check-and-fail step.
5. ECR storage costs have grown significantly over time.
No lifecycle policy is expiring old/untagged images — add one targeting untagged images first (safest, highest-value cleanup) and tune tagged-image retention as needed.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Push an image from Module 9's multi-stage Dockerfile to a new ECR repository with scan-on-push enabled, and review the findings.
  2. Write a CI-style script that fails (non-zero exit) if any CRITICAL severity findings are present in the scan results.
  3. Create a lifecycle policy expiring untagged images after 7 days and tagged images beyond the most recent 10, and verify it against a repository with test image history.
  4. Launch an EC2 instance with an IAM role granting only ECR pull permissions (no push), and verify pull succeeds while push correctly fails.
  5. Set up a VPC Gateway/Interface Endpoint for ECR and verify (via VPC Flow Logs or by removing NAT Gateway access) that pulls still succeed without any NAT Gateway route.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What is ECR and why might a company use it instead of Docker Hub?
ECR is AWS's private, IAM-integrated Docker registry. Companies use it instead of Docker Hub for private images needing fine-grained access control via the same IAM system as the rest of their AWS resources, avoiding rate limits, and enabling image pulls to stay entirely within AWS's private network for compute like EKS/EC2.
Beginner-Intermediate: How does an EC2 instance pull an image from ECR without ever having a stored password or credential?
The instance has an IAM role attached (via an instance profile, Module 13) granting it ECR pull permissions. The AWS CLI/SDK (or a credential helper Docker uses) automatically fetches short-lived temporary credentials via that role and uses them to authenticate the pull — no static secret is ever configured on the instance itself.
Intermediate: Design a CI/CD gate that prevents critically vulnerable images from ever reaching a production EKS cluster.
After building and pushing the image to ECR with scan-on-push enabled, the pipeline explicitly waits for the scan to complete (aws ecr wait image-scan-complete) and then queries the findings (describe-image-scan-findings), failing the pipeline outright if CRITICAL (or, depending on policy, HIGH) severity findings exceed an acceptable threshold — only allowing the subsequent deployment step (e.g. a Helm upgrade per Module 12, or triggering ArgoCD per Module 29) to proceed if the image passes this explicit gate, since scanning alone doesn't enforce anything without this additional pipeline logic.
Intermediate-Advanced: A team wants to reduce their monthly AWS bill and notices ECR storage costs are unexpectedly high. Walk through the investigation and fix.
Check repository image counts and total size via `aws ecr describe-images` — commonly, CI/CD pushes to a mutable tag (like "latest" or a branch name) generate a new image with every build, orphaning the PREVIOUS image as untagged (its layers remain stored, just no longer referenced by that tag) — these accumulate rapidly and are the highest-value cleanup target. Implement a lifecycle policy expiring untagged images after a short window (e.g. 7 days) and capping the retained count of tagged images to a reasonable number (e.g. last 10-20 releases), while explicitly protecting any tags currently referenced by a live deployment from expiry.
Advanced: Design a secure, cost-optimized container image supply chain from developer commit through to a running EKS pod, incorporating ECR, IAM, and VPC networking.
Developer pushes code; CI/CD (Module 23/24) builds the image using a multi-stage Dockerfile (Module 9), tags it with a specific immutable version (e.g. git commit SHA, never "latest" for anything deployed), and pushes to ECR using a CI/CD-specific IAM role (Module 13) scoped to only that repository's push permission. Scan-on-push runs automatically; the pipeline gates on findings before proceeding. EKS worker nodes (Module 21) sit in private subnets (Module 15) and pull images via a node IAM role (or IRSA-scoped role per specific workload) granting only ECR pull permissions — never push — with the pull traffic routed through a VPC Interface Endpoint for ECR (and the associated S3 Gateway Endpoint, since ECR image layers are actually stored in S3 under the hood) so that image pulls never traverse a NAT Gateway or the public internet at all, reducing both cost and attack surface. A lifecycle policy keeps the repository from accumulating unbounded untagged/superseded images indefinitely.

AWS EKS, from first principles to production

Managed Kubernetes — AWS runs and backs up the control plane (etcd, API server, scheduler from Module 11) so you never have to. Everything from Module 11 applies directly; this module covers what's specifically different in AWS's implementation.

  9. 5 Debugging Scenarios
  10. 5 Lab Exercises
  11. 5 Interview Qs

01 Why EKS / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

EKS (Elastic Kubernetes Service) is AWS's managed Kubernetes offering — AWS runs, patches, backs up, and scales the CONTROL PLANE (API server, etcd, scheduler, controller manager — all of Module 11's control plane components) across multiple AZs for you. It directly solves Module 11's flagged etcd-backup/HA concern: that operational burden and failure risk is now AWS's responsibility, not yours.

Self-managed Kubernetes (kubeadm on EC2)EKS
Control plane HA/backupYou build and maintain this yourselfAWS-managed, multi-AZ by default
Control plane upgradesYou orchestrate the upgrade yourselfLargely automated, still requires you to trigger/schedule it
Worker nodesYou manage regardlessYou still manage worker nodes (or use Fargate to avoid even that)
CostJust EC2 cost for control plane instancesEC2/Fargate cost for workers + a per-cluster hourly control plane fee

Common mistakes

  • Assuming EKS manages EVERYTHING — worker nodes (unless using Fargate) still need patching/scaling management, typically via Managed Node Groups or your own ASG (Module 17) integration.

Interview Q

Q: What specifically does EKS manage for you that you'd otherwise have to build yourself with self-managed Kubernetes?
A: The control plane — API server, etcd (with its HA and backup, directly addressing Module 11's flagged etcd risk), scheduler, and controller manager — all run across multiple AZs, patched and monitored by AWS. Worker nodes, however, are still your responsibility to provision and manage (unless using Fargate), just as they would be with self-managed Kubernetes.

02 Node Types: Managed, Self-Managed, Fargate

Node typeWho manages whatBest for
Self-Managed Node GroupYou manage the underlying ASG (Module 17), Launch Template (Module 14/17), and AMI entirely yourselfNeed full control over node configuration/AMI
Managed Node GroupAWS provisions and manages the underlying ASG, handles node draining during updates/terminationMost common choice — good default, less operational overhead
Fargate (for EKS)No EC2 nodes at all — AWS runs each POD in its own isolated, right-sized micro-VM (Firecracker, from Module 9's security discussion)Workloads wanting zero node management, or requiring strong per-pod isolation
Fargate directly resolves Module 9's flagged security concern: recall Module 9 noted that container isolation (shared kernel via namespaces/cgroups) is weaker than VM isolation for genuinely untrusted workloads, and mentioned Firecracker microVMs as the real fix. EKS Fargate is exactly that — each pod gets its own Firecracker microVM with a dedicated kernel, not sharing a kernel with other pods on a shared EC2 node at all.

Common mistakes

  • Choosing Fargate for cost-sensitive, high-density workloads without checking pricing — Fargate's per-pod billing can be more expensive than tightly-packed EC2 nodes at high utilization; it trades some cost efficiency for operational simplicity/isolation.
  • Not realizing Fargate doesn't support every Kubernetes feature (e.g. DaemonSets don't run on Fargate, since there's no persistent node to run them on) — a real architectural constraint to check before committing.

Interview Q

Q: How does EKS Fargate provide stronger isolation than running pods on regular EC2-backed nodes, and why might that matter?
A: Each Fargate pod runs in its own dedicated Firecracker microVM with its own kernel, rather than sharing a kernel with other pods via namespaces/cgroups on a shared EC2 node (Module 9's standard container isolation model). This matters for genuinely untrusted or highly sensitive multi-tenant workloads where a kernel-level container escape would otherwise be a real risk — Fargate removes that shared-kernel attack surface entirely, at some cost/feature trade-off (e.g. no DaemonSet support, since there's no persistent node).

03 IRSA — IAM Roles for Service Accounts

What is it / why needed

Recall Module 11's Kubernetes Secrets discussion flagged that storing AWS credentials as a K8s Secret is insufficient security (base64, not encrypted). IRSA solves the deeper problem: how does a POD (not a whole EC2 node) get scoped, temporary AWS permissions without any static credential at all?

Without IRSA (the bad old way): Every pod on a node inherits the NODE's IAM role — overly broad, every pod can do whatever the node can do, no per-workload scoping. With IRSA: Kubernetes ServiceAccount ──annotated with──► a specific IAM Role ARN │ └── pod using that ServiceAccount gets a projected token, exchanged via AWS STS (Module 13) for temporary credentials SCOPED TO THAT SPECIFIC ROLE — not the node's broader role at all Result: pod A (using service-account-A → role-A) and pod B (using service-account-B → role-B) on the SAME node get COMPLETELY DIFFERENT, independently-scoped AWS permissions. 
# IAM role's trust policy — trusts the EKS cluster's OIDC provider specifically
{
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Principal": {"Federated": "arn:aws:iam:::oidc-provider/"},
  "Action": "sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity",
  "Condition": {
    "StringEquals": {
      ":sub": "system:serviceaccount:default:myapp-sa"
    }
  }
}

# ServiceAccount annotated to use that role
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: myapp-sa
  annotations:
    eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn: arn:aws:iam:::role/myapp-s3-read-role
---
# Pod spec references the ServiceAccount
spec:
  serviceAccountName: myapp-sa
  containers:
    - name: app
      image: myapp:1.0

Common mistakes

  • Granting the NODE's IAM role broad permissions instead of using IRSA for per-workload scoping — every pod on that node inherits that broad access, violating least privilege (Module 13) at the pod level.
  • Forgetting to actually set `serviceAccountName` in the pod spec, defaulting to the "default" ServiceAccount which has no IRSA role attached — the pod falls back to only the node's role instead.

Interview Q

Q: Two pods run on the same EKS worker node. How can they have completely different, independently-scoped AWS permissions?
A: Via IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) — each pod uses a different Kubernetes ServiceAccount, each annotated with a DIFFERENT IAM role ARN. When a pod authenticates to AWS, it exchanges a projected, ServiceAccount-specific token via STS for temporary credentials scoped to ITS OWN role, entirely independent of the underlying node's own IAM role or any other pod's ServiceAccount/role — giving true per-workload least-privilege (Module 13) even when pods share physical node infrastructure.

04 Networking: VPC CNI & Load Balancer Controller

VPC CNI — how EKS pods get real VPC IPs

Unlike many Kubernetes networking setups using an overlay network (a separate virtual IP space layered on top of the real network), EKS's default AWS VPC CNI plugin assigns each pod an actual IP address from the VPC's own CIDR range (Module 15) — pods are first-class VPC citizens, directly reachable/routable within the VPC without any overlay/NAT translation layer.

Real capacity-planning gotcha: because each pod consumes a real VPC IP, and each EC2 instance type has a maximum number of ENI (network interface) slots and IPs-per-ENI it supports, an instance type can hit a hard MAX PODS limit well before its actual CPU/memory (Module 11's resource requests/limits) is exhausted — a genuinely different bottleneck than Kubernetes' usual compute-based scheduling constraints, and a real reason node CIDR/subnet sizing (Module 15's original point about generous VPC CIDR planning) matters even more for EKS.

AWS Load Balancer Controller

A Kubernetes controller (running as pods in the cluster) that watches for Kubernetes Service (type: LoadBalancer) or Ingress objects and automatically provisions/configures actual AWS ALBs/NLBs (Module 16) to match — the concrete implementation connecting Module 11's Service abstraction to Module 16's real ALB.

apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: myapp-ingress
  annotations:
    kubernetes.io/ingress.class: alb
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/scheme: internet-facing
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/target-type: ip     # route directly to pod IPs (via VPC CNI) rather than node ports
spec:
  rules:
    - http:
        paths:
          - path: /api
            pathType: Prefix
            backend:
              service:
                name: myapp-svc
                port:
                  number: 80

Common mistakes

  • Not accounting for the ENI/IP-per-instance-type limit when sizing an EKS node group — a smaller instance type can become "full" of pods far before CPU/memory limits are hit, an easy-to-miss capacity planning surprise.
  • Using target-type "instance" (routes via NodePort) when "ip" (routes directly to pod IPs via VPC CNI) is both more efficient and avoids an extra network hop.

Interview Q

Q: An EKS node group is hitting a "max pods" ceiling well before CPU or memory requests/limits (Module 11) are anywhere near exhausted. Why?
A: EKS's default VPC CNI assigns each pod a real IP address from the VPC's CIDR, and each EC2 instance type has a hard limit on the number of ENIs (network interfaces) and IPs-per-ENI it supports — this becomes an independent scheduling constraint separate from compute resource requests/limits. Fix by choosing a larger instance type (more ENI/IP capacity) or tuning the CNI's IP prefix delegation mode for higher pod density per node.

05 Cluster Access & aws-auth

What is it / how it works

EKS cluster access is controlled by mapping IAM identities (Module 13) to Kubernetes RBAC permissions — historically via a ConfigMap called aws-auth in the kube-system namespace (increasingly being replaced by newer native "EKS Access Entries" API, but the underlying IAM-to-RBAC mapping concept is the same either way).

# aws-auth ConfigMap (legacy but still widely seen) mapping an IAM role to a K8s group
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: aws-auth
  namespace: kube-system
data:
  mapRoles: |
    - rolearn: arn:aws:iam:::role/eks-node-role
      username: system:node:{{EC2PrivateDNSName}}
      groups: ["system:bootstrappers", "system:nodes"]
    - rolearn: arn:aws:iam:::role/developer-role
      username: developer
      groups: ["view-only-group"]                              # mapped to a K8s RBAC ClusterRoleBinding
A real, damaging mistake: misconfiguring or accidentally deleting the aws-auth ConfigMap (or the entry mapping node roles) can lock EVERYONE out of the cluster, including the person who created it — since even node bootstrap depends on this mapping. Treat it with the same caution as any IAM trust policy (Module 13), version-controlled and changed carefully, never edited casually.

Common mistakes

  • Granting overly broad Kubernetes RBAC permissions (e.g. cluster-admin) to a broad IAM group "for convenience" — same least-privilege violation as Module 13's IAM discussion, just at the K8s RBAC layer.
  • Not understanding that IAM alone doesn't grant K8s permissions — an IAM user/role must ALSO be mapped to K8s RBAC (via aws-auth or Access Entries) for kubectl access to actually work; the two systems are layered, not automatically unified.

Interview Q

Q: An IAM user has full AdministratorAccess in AWS, but `kubectl get pods` against an EKS cluster returns "Unauthorized." Why?
A: IAM permissions and Kubernetes RBAC permissions are two SEPARATE authorization layers — having broad IAM access doesn't automatically grant any Kubernetes-level permissions. The IAM identity must ALSO be explicitly mapped to a Kubernetes RBAC role/group via the aws-auth ConfigMap (or EKS Access Entries) before kubectl commands against that cluster will succeed, regardless of how permissive that identity's IAM policies are.

★ Mini Project

# Create an EKS cluster with a managed node group, IRSA for S3 access, and ALB ingress

eksctl create cluster --name myapp-cluster --region ap-south-1 \
  --nodegroup-name standard-workers --node-type t3.medium --nodes 3 \
  --vpc-private-subnets subnet-priv-a,subnet-priv-b --managed

# Enable IAM OIDC provider (needed for IRSA)
eksctl utils associate-iam-oidc-provider --cluster myapp-cluster --approve

# Create an IAM role + K8s ServiceAccount bound via IRSA
eksctl create iamserviceaccount \
  --cluster myapp-cluster --name myapp-sa --namespace default \
  --attach-policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/AmazonS3ReadOnlyAccess \
  --approve

# Deploy the app (from Module 9/11's Dockerfile + Deployment), referencing the IRSA service account
kubectl apply -f - <<'EOF'
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: myapp
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector: {matchLabels: {app: myapp}}
  template:
    metadata: {labels: {app: myapp}}
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: myapp-sa
      containers:
        - name: app
          image: .dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/myapp:1.0
          ports: [{containerPort: 8080}]
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata: {name: myapp-svc}
spec:
  selector: {app: myapp}
  ports: [{port: 80, targetPort: 8080}]
  type: ClusterIP
EOF

# Install the AWS Load Balancer Controller (via Helm, Module 12) then create an Ingress
kubectl apply -f - <<'EOF'
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: myapp-ingress
  annotations:
    kubernetes.io/ingress.class: alb
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/scheme: internet-facing
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/target-type: ip
spec:
  rules:
    - http:
        paths:
          - path: /
            pathType: Prefix
            backend: {service: {name: myapp-svc, port: {number: 80}}}
EOF

# Verify the pod can access S3 via IRSA with zero static credentials
kubectl exec -it  -- aws s3 ls

✓ Revision Sheet

  • EKS = AWS-managed Kubernetes control plane (multi-AZ, backed-up etcd) — solves Module 11's flagged etcd HA/backup risk. Worker nodes still your responsibility (unless Fargate).
  • Node types: Self-Managed (full control), Managed Node Group (AWS-assisted, common default), Fargate (per-pod Firecracker microVM, no nodes at all, stronger isolation, some feature/cost trade-offs).
  • IRSA: per-pod (not per-node) scoped IAM permissions via ServiceAccount → IAM role mapping through OIDC + STS — true least-privilege at the workload level.
  • VPC CNI: pods get REAL VPC IPs (not overlay) — creates an ENI/IP-based max-pods-per-node ceiling independent of compute resources.
  • AWS Load Balancer Controller: watches Ingress/Service objects, provisions real ALBs/NLBs (Module 16) automatically.
  • IAM permissions and Kubernetes RBAC are separate layers — an identity needs BOTH (mapped via aws-auth ConfigMap or Access Entries) for kubectl access to work.
  • aws-auth misconfiguration can lock everyone out of the cluster — treat with the same caution as an IAM trust policy.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What does EKS manage that Module 11's self-managed Kubernetes concerns flagged as risky?
The control plane, including etcd's HA and backup — across multiple AZs, AWS-managed.
2. What EKS node option gives each pod its own dedicated microVM with a separate kernel?
Fargate.
3. What does IRSA let a pod have that the node's own IAM role can't provide?
Independently scoped, per-workload AWS permissions rather than inheriting the broader node role.
4. Do EKS pods (with default VPC CNI) get real VPC IPs or overlay IPs?
Real VPC IPs.
5. What can create a "max pods" ceiling on a node independent of CPU/memory limits?
ENI/IP-per-instance-type limits, since each pod consumes a real VPC IP under the VPC CNI.
6. What controller provisions real AWS ALBs/NLBs from Kubernetes Ingress/Service objects?
The AWS Load Balancer Controller.
7. Does having full IAM AdministratorAccess automatically grant kubectl access to an EKS cluster?
No — IAM and Kubernetes RBAC are separate layers; the identity must also be mapped via aws-auth/Access Entries.
8. What Kubernetes object does IRSA annotate with an IAM role ARN?
A ServiceAccount.
9. Does Fargate support DaemonSets?
No — there's no persistent node for a DaemonSet to run on.
10. What's the risk of misconfiguring the aws-auth ConfigMap?
It can lock everyone (including the original creator) out of cluster access, since node bootstrap and user access both depend on it.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. A pod fails to access an S3 bucket despite its ServiceAccount being IRSA-annotated correctly.
Check the pod spec actually sets serviceAccountName to that specific ServiceAccount (easy to forget, silently falls back to "default"), and check the IAM role's trust policy condition matches the exact namespace/service-account-name string.
2. An EKS node group can't schedule new pods, reporting insufficient IP addresses, despite plenty of CPU/memory being available.
Hit the ENI/IP-per-instance max-pods ceiling from the VPC CNI — either choose a larger instance type or tune CNI IP prefix delegation for higher pod density.
3. kubectl commands fail with "Unauthorized" for a user with full AWS IAM AdministratorAccess.
That IAM identity isn't yet mapped to any Kubernetes RBAC group via aws-auth/Access Entries — IAM permissions alone don't grant Kubernetes API access.
4. An Ingress object is created but no ALB ever appears in the AWS console.
Check the AWS Load Balancer Controller is actually installed and running in the cluster, and check the Ingress has the correct ingress.class annotation (alb) and IAM permissions for the controller's own IRSA role to create ALB resources.
5. Everyone loses kubectl access to a cluster after an aws-auth ConfigMap edit.
The edit likely broke or removed a critical mapping (often the node role mapping itself) — recovery typically requires access via the AWS root/break-glass path or a backup of the previous aws-auth content; this is exactly why such changes should be version-controlled and reviewed carefully beforehand.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Create an EKS cluster with a managed node group and deploy a simple app, verifying pods get real VPC-routable IPs.
  2. Set up IRSA for a pod needing S3 read access, and verify via kubectl exec that the pod can call aws s3 ls with zero static credentials.
  3. Deploy the AWS Load Balancer Controller and create an Ingress, confirming a real ALB is provisioned and routes traffic correctly.
  4. Deliberately undersize a node group's instance type and load it with many low-resource-request pods until hitting the ENI/IP max-pods ceiling, observing the scheduling failure.
  5. Map a new IAM role to a read-only Kubernetes RBAC group via aws-auth, and verify that identity can list but not modify resources.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What's the main advantage of EKS over running Kubernetes yourself on EC2?
AWS manages the control plane (API server, etcd, scheduler) for you — including etcd's high availability and backup, a genuinely risky operational burden if self-managed (per Module 11) — letting your team focus on worker nodes and workloads instead of control-plane operations.
Beginner-Intermediate: What problem does IRSA solve that giving the whole EKS node an IAM role doesn't?
A node-level IAM role is shared by ALL pods scheduled on that node — any pod gets whatever access the node has, violating least privilege at the workload level. IRSA lets each individual pod (via its ServiceAccount) assume its OWN distinct, narrowly-scoped IAM role, so two pods on the same node can have completely different, independently-controlled AWS permissions.
Intermediate: Why might a team choose EKS Fargate over standard EC2-backed managed node groups for a specific workload, despite Fargate's typically higher per-unit cost?
Fargate removes node management entirely (no patching, no capacity planning, no ASG/Launch Template maintenance) AND provides genuinely stronger isolation — each pod runs in its own Firecracker microVM with a dedicated kernel, rather than sharing a kernel with other pods via namespaces/cgroups on a shared node (Module 9's isolation discussion). This is worth the cost premium for genuinely multi-tenant or highly sensitive workloads where kernel-level isolation matters, or for teams wanting zero operational node-management overhead even at some cost trade-off.
Intermediate-Advanced: An EKS cluster's worker nodes are running out of IP capacity for new pods well before hitting CPU/memory resource limits. Diagnose and propose fixes.
Root cause: EKS's default VPC CNI gives each pod a real IP from the VPC CIDR, and each EC2 instance type supports a maximum number of ENIs × IPs-per-ENI, creating a pod-count ceiling independent of compute resources. Fixes: choose larger instance types (more ENI/IP capacity per node, trading node count for pod density), enable the VPC CNI's IP prefix delegation mode (assigns whole /28 prefixes per ENI instead of individual IPs, dramatically increasing effective pod density per node), or reduce the number of very small, low-resource-request pods being densely packed if the workload pattern allows consolidating them. Also revisit Module 15's original VPC CIDR sizing — a too-small subnet CIDR compounds this problem by limiting the total IP pool available to the CNI in the first place.
Advanced: Design the full access-control model for a multi-team EKS cluster where each team should only be able to manage resources in their own namespace, using IAM-native identities (no separate Kubernetes-specific credential system).
Each team's engineers are members of a team-specific IAM group/role (Module 13). Each such IAM role is mapped (via aws-auth or EKS Access Entries) to a Kubernetes RBAC RoleBinding scoped to that team's specific namespace, granting appropriate verbs (get/list/create/update on relevant resource types) WITHIN that namespace only — never cluster-wide permissions like cluster-admin. Separately, workloads running IN each namespace use IRSA-scoped ServiceAccounts (per this module's core IRSA section) for their own AWS API access, keeping human RBAC access and workload AWS permissions as two distinct, independently-scoped concerns. Namespace-level resource quotas (a Kubernetes-native feature) additionally prevent one team's workloads from starving another team's namespace of cluster capacity, and network policies can restrict pod-to-pod traffic across namespace boundaries if genuine tenant isolation (not just access control) is required.
Module 21 / 30 — AWS EKS. Next up: CloudWatch — the monitoring/logging backbone tying together everything from EC2 to EKS to RDS covered so far.

AWS CloudWatch, from first principles to production

01 Why CloudWatch / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

CloudWatch is AWS's native monitoring, logging, and alerting service — collecting metrics and logs from virtually every AWS service (EC2, RDS, ALB, EKS, Lambda) into one place. It exists because Module 1's debugging order (systemctl status → journalctl → dmesg → top/free → df -h) all assumed SSH access to one box — at cloud scale with dozens/hundreds of ephemeral instances and containers, you need centralized, queryable observability instead of manually SSHing into each one.

Common mistakes

  • Relying purely on SSHing into instances to check logs/metrics in a fleet of any real size — doesn't scale past a handful of servers, and instances may be terminated/replaced (Module 17's ASG) before you ever get to them.

Interview Q

Q: Why does centralized observability like CloudWatch become essential once you're running an Auto Scaling Group instead of a single server?
A: Instances in an ASG (Module 17) are ephemeral — they can be terminated and replaced automatically, meaning any logs/state only on that specific instance could vanish before you ever investigate an issue. Centralized log/metric collection ensures that data survives independently of any individual instance's lifecycle, and lets you query across the whole fleet at once instead of SSHing into each box individually.

02 Metrics & Namespaces

What is it / how it works

A CloudWatch metric is a time-ordered set of data points (e.g. CPUUtilization for a specific EC2 instance), organized under a namespace (e.g. AWS/EC2, AWS/RDS, AWS/ApplicationELB) with dimensions further identifying exactly which resource the data point belongs to.

Metric typeSourceResolution
Standard/basic metricsAutomatically collected by AWS for most services (e.g. EC2 CPUUtilization)5-minute intervals by default
Detailed monitoringOpt-in, higher-resolution automatic metrics1-minute intervals
Custom metricsPublished explicitly by your application/agent (e.g. actual memory usage, which EC2 doesn't report by default — a real gotcha)As frequently as you publish them, down to 1-second resolution
EC2 doesn't report memory or disk usage by default — a very commonly-missed gotcha. Standard EC2 CloudWatch metrics only cover CPU, network, and basic disk I/O — NOT actual memory utilization or disk space used, since those require an agent running INSIDE the instance's OS to report (the CloudWatch Agent). Many teams are surprised their dashboards show healthy CPU while an instance is actually about to OOM (Module 1) or run out of disk (Module 1's df -h point) with zero CloudWatch visibility into either, until the CloudWatch Agent is installed.
# Publish a custom metric (e.g. from the CloudWatch Agent or your own app)
aws cloudwatch put-metric-data --namespace "MyApp" --metric-name "QueueDepth" \
  --value 42 --dimensions Service=OrderProcessor

# Query metric data
aws cloudwatch get-metric-statistics --namespace AWS/EC2 --metric-name CPUUtilization \
  --dimensions Name=InstanceId,Value= \
  --start-time 2026-07-08T00:00:00Z --end-time 2026-07-08T23:59:59Z \
  --period 300 --statistics Average

Common mistakes

  • Assuming CloudWatch automatically shows memory/disk usage for EC2 without installing the CloudWatch Agent — leads to real blind spots exactly matching Module 1's OOM-killer and disk-full failure modes.
  • Relying only on 5-minute standard resolution for latency-sensitive alarms — a spike lasting under 5 minutes can be entirely averaged out/invisible at that granularity.

Interview Q

Q: Your CloudWatch dashboard shows healthy CPU utilization for an EC2 instance, but the instance's application just got OOM-killed. Why didn't CloudWatch show a memory problem?
A: Standard EC2 CloudWatch metrics don't include actual memory utilization at all by default — only CPU, network, and basic disk I/O, since memory usage requires an in-OS agent (the CloudWatch Agent) to report it. Without that agent installed and configured, memory (and disk space usage) remain a complete blind spot in CloudWatch, regardless of how healthy other metrics look.

03 Logs & Log Insights

What is it / how it works

CloudWatch Logs centralizes log data from EC2 (via the CloudWatch Agent), Lambda (automatic), ECS/EKS (via Fluent Bit or the Container Insights agent), and other sources into Log Groups (typically per-application/service) containing Log Streams (typically per-instance/task/pod).

# CloudWatch Logs Insights — SQL-like query language over log data
fields @timestamp, @message
| filter @message like /ERROR/
| stats count() by bin(5m)
| sort @timestamp desc
| limit 50
Direct extension of Module 9's "log to stdout" principle: the CloudWatch Agent (on EC2) or Fluent Bit (in EKS, commonly as a DaemonSet) tails your application's stdout/stderr (or a specified log file) and ships it to CloudWatch Logs — the exact same "log to stdout, let the platform handle shipping" pattern from Module 9's Docker logging discussion, just with CloudWatch as the destination instead of `docker logs`.

Common mistakes

  • No log retention policy set on a Log Group (defaults to "never expire") — silently accumulating storage cost indefinitely, the CloudWatch-equivalent of Module 18's S3 lifecycle-policy lesson.
  • Writing structured, greppable logs but never actually using Logs Insights queries to leverage that — falling back to manually scrolling through raw log streams instead.

Interview Q

Q: Why should every CloudWatch Log Group have an explicit retention policy set?
A: By default, a Log Group retains logs FOREVER, accumulating storage cost indefinitely — the exact same problem Module 18 flagged for unmanaged S3 storage. Setting an explicit retention period (e.g. 30, 90, or 365 days depending on actual operational/compliance need) automatically expires old log data, keeping costs bounded without manual cleanup.

04 Alarms

What is it / how it works

A CloudWatch Alarm watches a metric against a threshold over a defined evaluation period, and triggers an action (SNS notification, Auto Scaling policy — Module 17's target-tracking policies are actually implemented as CloudWatch Alarms under the hood, Lambda invocation) when breached.

aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm \
  --alarm-name high-cpu-myapp \
  --namespace AWS/EC2 --metric-name CPUUtilization \
  --dimensions Name=AutoScalingGroupName,Value=myapp-asg \
  --statistic Average --period 300 --evaluation-periods 3 \
  --threshold 80 --comparison-operator GreaterThanThreshold \
  --alarm-actions
evaluation-periods matters — a real alarm-flapping gotcha: setting evaluation-periods too low (e.g. 1) means a single brief metric spike triggers the alarm, potentially causing unnecessary pages/scaling actions for noise rather than a genuine sustained problem. A higher evaluation-periods (e.g. 3 consecutive periods breaching the threshold) requires SUSTAINED breach before alarming — directly parallel to Module 11's liveness-probe tuning discussion (avoiding overreacting to brief, recoverable blips).

Common mistakes

  • Alarming on raw instance-level metrics instead of aggregate fleet-level metrics for an ASG — a single instance's brief spike (which the ASG/ALB would naturally route around) shouldn't necessarily page anyone.
  • Not routing alarms anywhere actionable (no SNS topic/on-call integration) — an alarm that fires into the void provides zero actual value.

Interview Q

Q: Why might setting a CloudWatch Alarm's evaluation-periods to 1 cause more harm than good in a production on-call setup?
A: With evaluation-periods=1, a single brief metric spike (that might resolve on its own within seconds, e.g. from a momentary garbage collection pause) immediately triggers the alarm and any associated action (paging on-call, scaling event) — causing noisy, low-value alerts for transient blips rather than genuinely sustained problems. Requiring several consecutive breaching periods filters out this noise, alerting only on real, sustained issues — the same underlying principle as tuning Kubernetes liveness probes (Module 11) to tolerate brief, recoverable slowness.

05 Dashboards & Container Insights

Dashboards

Custom CloudWatch Dashboards visualize metrics/alarms/log query results across multiple services in one view — e.g. a single dashboard combining ALB request count (Module 16), ASG instance count (Module 17), RDS CPU (Module 19), and application error rate (custom metric) for one holistic view of a system's health.

Container Insights (for EKS/ECS)

An enhanced observability layer specifically for containerized workloads — collecting pod/node/cluster-level metrics (CPU, memory per pod, not just per node) that standard CloudWatch metrics don't natively provide for Kubernetes workloads, since EKS's control plane and worker nodes need a purpose-built collector (typically deployed as a DaemonSet, directly building on Module 11's DaemonSet concept) to surface pod-level granularity.

Common mistakes

  • Relying on generic EC2-level metrics for an EKS node without Container Insights — invisible to actual per-pod resource consumption, making it hard to identify which specific workload is responsible for node-level pressure.

Interview Q

Q: Why do you need Container Insights specifically for EKS observability, rather than relying on standard EC2 CloudWatch metrics for the underlying worker nodes?
A: Standard EC2 metrics only report at the NODE level (whole-instance CPU/memory) — with many pods packed onto one node, there's no visibility into which SPECIFIC pod is consuming resources or experiencing issues. Container Insights deploys a collector (typically a DaemonSet, per Module 11) that gathers pod-level and cluster-level metrics, giving the granularity actually needed to diagnose containerized workload issues rather than just "the node overall is under pressure."

★ Mini Project

# Full observability setup: CloudWatch Agent for real memory/disk metrics, log shipping, alarm, dashboard

# 1. Install and configure CloudWatch Agent on an EC2 instance (fixes the memory/disk blind spot)
sudo yum install -y amazon-cloudwatch-agent
sudo tee /opt/aws/amazon-cloudwatch-agent/etc/config.json <<'EOF'
{
  "metrics": {
    "metrics_collected": {
      "mem": {"measurement": ["mem_used_percent"]},
      "disk": {"measurement": ["used_percent"], "resources": ["/"]}
    }
  },
  "logs": {
    "logs_collected": {
      "files": {
        "collect_list": [{
          "file_path": "/var/log/myapp/app.log",
          "log_group_name": "/myapp/production",
          "log_stream_name": "{instance_id}"
        }]
      }
    }
  }
}
EOF
sudo /opt/aws/amazon-cloudwatch-agent/bin/amazon-cloudwatch-agent-ctl \
  -a fetch-config -m ec2 -s -c file:/opt/aws/amazon-cloudwatch-agent/etc/config.json

# 2. Set log retention (avoid the "never expire" cost trap)
aws logs put-retention-policy --log-group-name /myapp/production --retention-in-days 90

# 3. Create an alarm on the NOW-VISIBLE memory metric
aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm --alarm-name high-memory-myapp \
  --namespace CWAgent --metric-name mem_used_percent \
  --dimensions Name=InstanceId,Value= \
  --statistic Average --period 300 --evaluation-periods 3 \
  --threshold 85 --comparison-operator GreaterThanThreshold \
  --alarm-actions 

# 4. Query logs with Logs Insights
aws logs start-query --log-group-name /myapp/production \
  --start-time $(date -d '1 hour ago' +%s) --end-time $(date +%s) \
  --query-string 'fields @timestamp, @message | filter @message like /ERROR/ | sort @timestamp desc | limit 20'

# 5. Build a dashboard combining ALB, ASG, RDS, and this new memory metric (via console or:)
aws cloudwatch put-dashboard --dashboard-name myapp-overview --dashboard-body file://dashboard.json

✓ Revision Sheet

  • CloudWatch centralizes metrics/logs/alarms across the whole AWS fleet — necessary once instances are ephemeral (Module 17's ASG) and too numerous to SSH into individually.
  • Metrics: namespace + dimensions identify a specific data series. Standard EC2 metrics do NOT include memory or disk usage by default — needs the CloudWatch Agent installed.
  • Logs: Log Groups (per app/service) contain Log Streams (per instance/pod). Always set an explicit retention policy — default is "never expire," same cost trap as unmanaged S3 (Module 18).
  • Alarms: threshold + evaluation-periods. Higher evaluation-periods avoids alerting on brief, recoverable blips — same philosophy as tuning K8s liveness probes (Module 11).
  • Container Insights: pod/cluster-level granularity for EKS/ECS, since standard EC2 metrics only see the whole node, not individual pods.
  • Dashboards combine metrics/alarms/logs across multiple services into one operational view.

Q 10 MCQs

1. Does standard EC2 monitoring include memory utilization by default?
No — requires the CloudWatch Agent installed inside the instance's OS.
2. What identifies a specific metric data series within a namespace?
Its dimensions (e.g. InstanceId, AutoScalingGroupName).
3. What's the default log retention for a new CloudWatch Log Group?
Never expire — must be explicitly set otherwise.
4. What does raising an alarm's evaluation-periods accomplish?
Requires sustained metric breach across multiple consecutive periods before triggering, reducing noise from brief spikes.
5. What gives pod-level (not just node-level) observability for EKS?
Container Insights.
6. What query language does CloudWatch Logs Insights use?
Its own SQL-like query syntax (fields, filter, stats, sort, limit).
7. What resolution do detailed monitoring metrics provide, vs standard?
1-minute intervals (detailed) vs 5-minute intervals (standard/basic).
8. What AWS mechanism actually implements ASG target-tracking scaling policies under the hood?
CloudWatch Alarms.
9. What component typically ships EKS pod logs to CloudWatch?
Fluent Bit, commonly deployed as a DaemonSet.
10. Can you publish your own application-specific metrics to CloudWatch?
Yes — via custom metrics, using put-metric-data.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. An EC2 instance's app crashed (OOM-killed) but the CloudWatch dashboard showed no warning at all.
Standard EC2 metrics don't include memory usage — install and configure the CloudWatch Agent to get real memory visibility, and set an alarm on the resulting custom metric.
2. CloudWatch storage costs for logs have grown unexpectedly large over time.
Log Groups likely have no retention policy set (defaulting to never-expire) — set an explicit, appropriate retention period per Log Group.
3. An alarm keeps firing (and paging on-call) for what turn out to be brief, self-resolving spikes.
evaluation-periods is likely too low (e.g. 1) — raise it to require sustained breach across multiple consecutive periods before triggering.
4. An EKS node shows high aggregate CPU usage, but it's unclear which specific pod is responsible.
Container Insights isn't installed/enabled — standard node-level EC2 metrics can't attribute usage to individual pods; deploy Container Insights for that granularity.
5. A Logs Insights query returns no results despite the application clearly logging errors during the queried time window.
Check the log group/stream actually received data for that period (agent/Fluent Bit shipping might be delayed or broken), and confirm the query's filter pattern actually matches the real log format (e.g. case sensitivity, exact field/message structure).

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Install the CloudWatch Agent on an EC2 instance and confirm memory/disk metrics now appear where they previously didn't.
  2. Set an explicit retention policy on an existing Log Group and verify old entries are pruned accordingly over time.
  3. Create an alarm with evaluation-periods=1 vs evaluation-periods=3 against a fluctuating test metric, and compare triggering behavior.
  4. Write a Logs Insights query that counts error occurrences per 5-minute bucket over the last hour.
  5. Build a single dashboard combining an ALB request-count metric, an ASG instance-count metric, and a custom application metric.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What's the difference between a CloudWatch metric and a CloudWatch log?
A metric is numeric time-series data (e.g. CPU utilization over time) used for monitoring and alarming. A log is unstructured or semi-structured text data (application output, error messages) used for detailed investigation and debugging — metrics tell you something's wrong at a glance, logs tell you why.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why might a team be surprised that their EC2 fleet's memory usage was never tracked despite "using CloudWatch"?
Standard EC2 CloudWatch metrics are automatically collected by AWS purely from the hypervisor level (CPU, network, basic disk I/O) — they have no visibility into what's happening INSIDE the instance's operating system, including actual memory usage. That requires the CloudWatch Agent running inside the instance to explicitly collect and publish it as a custom metric — an easy-to-miss setup step, not something enabled by default just by using an AWS account.
Intermediate: Design an alarm strategy for an Auto Scaling Group that avoids both under-alerting (missing real problems) and over-alerting (paging on-call for noise).
Alarm on aggregate, ASG-level metrics (not individual instance metrics, which naturally fluctuate and get replaced per Module 17) with a reasonable evaluation-periods (e.g. 3 consecutive 1-minute periods) requiring sustained breach before triggering — filtering brief GC-pause-style blips while still catching genuine sustained problems. Route different severities to different channels (e.g. a sustained high-error-rate alarm pages on-call immediately; a slowly climbing disk-usage trend alarm creates a ticket for business-hours investigation rather than an urgent page) — not every threshold breach warrants the same urgency of response.
Intermediate-Advanced: A production incident investigation needs to correlate an RDS CPU spike, an ALB 5xx error increase, and application-level error logs, all around the same few minutes. How would CloudWatch support this investigation?
A CloudWatch Dashboard combining the RDS CPUUtilization metric (Module 19), the ALB HTTPCode_Target_5XX_Count metric (Module 16), and a Logs Insights query counting ERROR-level log lines over the same time window — all displayed with synchronized time ranges — lets an engineer visually correlate exactly when each symptom began relative to the others, often revealing causal order (e.g. did the RDS CPU spike precede the 5xx errors, suggesting the database was the root cause, or did app-level errors appear first, pointing elsewhere) far faster than manually cross-referencing separate tools/logs.
Advanced: Design a comprehensive observability strategy for a microservices architecture running on EKS, incorporating everything from this module plus prior modules' concepts.
Application containers log structured JSON to stdout/stderr (Module 9's 12-factor principle), shipped via a Fluent Bit DaemonSet (Module 11's DaemonSet concept) to CloudWatch Logs, with per-service Log Groups and appropriate retention policies set explicitly (avoiding the never-expire cost trap). Container Insights is enabled for genuine pod/cluster-level metric granularity beyond what node-level EC2 metrics alone provide. Custom application metrics (business-relevant signals like queue depth, request latency percentiles, error rates per endpoint) are published alongside infrastructure metrics, since infrastructure health (CPU/memory) alone often doesn't reflect actual user-facing service health. Alarms are tiered by severity and routed appropriately (immediate page vs ticket), with evaluation-periods tuned per-metric to balance responsiveness against noise. A unified dashboard per service (or per team, spanning their owned services) combines ALB, application, and downstream dependency (RDS, other services) metrics into one operational view — and this CloudWatch-native approach is often complemented by (or in more mature setups, largely superseded by) Prometheus/Grafana (Modules 25-26) for more Kubernetes-native, higher-cardinality metric querying, with CloudWatch remaining the system of record for AWS-managed-service-level metrics and logs that Prometheus can't natively scrape.
Module 22 / 30 — AWS CloudWatch. This closes the AWS infrastructure block (13-22). Next up: GitHub Actions — the CI/CD block begins, automating everything built so far.

GitHub Actions, from first principles to production

CI/CD begins here — automating the build → test → scan → deploy pipeline that's been manual across every module so far, directly triggered by the Git events from Module 3.
