01 Why Terraform / Problem Solved

What is it / why needed

Every AWS CLI command across Modules 14-22 (create a VPC, launch an ASG, create an RDS instance) was a one-off, manual, imperative action — nothing recorded WHAT was created, in what order, or how to recreate it identically in another environment/account. Terraform is an Infrastructure as Code (IaC) tool: you declare the DESIRED infrastructure state in configuration files, and Terraform figures out what API calls are needed to make reality match that declaration — the exact same declarative philosophy as Module 11's Kubernetes, just applied to the cloud infrastructure itself rather than containers running on top of it.

Manual CLI/ConsoleTerraform
ReproducibilityDepends entirely on memory/documentation of steps takenExact same config reproduces identical infrastructure anywhere
Change trackingNo inherent audit trail of what changed and whyVersion-controlled in git (Module 3) — every change is a reviewable commit/PR
Drift detectionNo way to know if reality still matches intent`terraform plan` shows exactly what's changed vs the declared config

Common mistakes

  • Manually clicking changes in the AWS Console for infrastructure that's ALSO managed by Terraform — causes drift (covered later) that Terraform doesn't know about until the next plan/apply, potentially reverting the manual change unexpectedly.

Interview Q

Q: What fundamental problem does Terraform solve compared to provisioning AWS infrastructure via the console or CLI directly?
A: It makes infrastructure changes declarative, version-controlled, and reproducible — instead of one-off imperative commands with no audit trail, infrastructure is defined in code (reviewable via git, per Module 3), and Terraform continuously reconciles actual cloud state toward that declared configuration, with `terraform plan` showing exactly what would change before anything is actually applied.

02 State — The Core Concept

What is it / why needed

Terraform maintains a state file (typically terraform.tfstate) — a JSON record mapping your configuration's declared resources to their REAL, currently-existing counterparts in AWS (actual instance IDs, ARNs, etc). This is Terraform's equivalent of Module 11's etcd: the single source of truth Terraform uses to know what it's already created and what needs to change.

Your .tf config (desired state) │ ▼ terraform plan compares config against ──► state file (what Terraform believes exists) │ │ │ └── (state file is a CACHE of real AWS state — │ it can drift out of sync with actual reality │ if changes happen outside Terraform) ▼ shows a diff: what would be created/changed/destroyed
Critical production requirement: remote state, never local. By default, the state file lives locally on whoever's machine ran terraform apply — completely unworkable for a team (no shared source of truth, risk of losing the file entirely) and a serious security risk (state files often contain sensitive data like database passwords in plain text). Production Terraform ALWAYS uses remote state (commonly an S3 bucket, Module 18, with DynamoDB for state locking, discussed below) shared across the whole team.
# backend.tf — remote state configuration
terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "myorg-terraform-state"
    key            = "prod/vpc/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "ap-south-1"
    dynamodb_table = "terraform-locks"      # state locking, see later section
    encrypt        = true                     # state can contain secrets — encrypt at rest (Module 18's principle)
  }
}

Common mistakes

  • Committing the state file directly to git — it often contains sensitive data (passwords, keys) in plaintext, and git isn't designed for the kind of concurrent-write coordination proper remote state backends provide.
  • Each team member running Terraform with LOCAL state — everyone has a different, unsynchronized view of "what's already been created," leading to conflicting or duplicate resource creation.

Interview Q

Q: Why is Terraform's state file described as a critical, sensitive artifact rather than just an implementation detail?
A: It's Terraform's ONLY record of the mapping between your declared configuration and the actual real-world resources it created — losing it means Terraform no longer knows what it manages, potentially leading to orphaned resources or accidental duplicate creation. It often contains sensitive data (like database passwords set via resource attributes) in plaintext, making its storage location (always a properly access-controlled, encrypted remote backend — never local, never committed to git) a genuine security consideration, not just a convenience choice.

03 Core Workflow: init/plan/apply

terraform init        # downloads providers (AWS provider, etc), configures the backend
terraform plan          # shows what WOULD change — read-only, safe to run anytime
terraform apply           # actually makes the API calls to create/modify/destroy resources
terraform destroy           # tears down everything this configuration manages
# main.tf — a simple VPC + subnet, directly codifying Module 15's manual CLI steps
resource "aws_vpc" "main" {
  cidr_block = "10.0.0.0/16"
  tags = { Name = "myapp-vpc" }
}

resource "aws_subnet" "public_a" {
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id      # reference — Terraform builds a dependency graph from this
  cidr_block         = "10.0.1.0/24"
  availability_zone    = "ap-south-1a"
  map_public_ip_on_launch = true
}
How Terraform decides order — the dependency graph: notice `aws_subnet.public_a` references `aws_vpc.main.id` — Terraform automatically infers from this reference that the VPC must be created FIRST, building an internal dependency graph rather than requiring you to manually sequence resource creation (unlike the ordered, manual CLI steps in Module 15's mini project). Resources with no dependency relationship are created in PARALLEL for speed.

Common mistakes

  • Running `terraform apply` directly in production without reviewing the `plan` output first — skipping the single most important safety check Terraform provides before making real changes.
  • Not pinning provider versions — an unpinned AWS provider could introduce breaking changes on a routine `terraform init` in the future, applying unexpectedly different behavior.

Interview Q

Q: How does Terraform know to create a VPC before the subnet that references it, without you specifying explicit ordering?
A: Terraform parses resource attribute references (like `aws_vpc.main.id` used inside the subnet's configuration) to automatically build a dependency graph — any resource referencing another resource's attribute is understood to depend on it, and Terraform sequences creation accordingly, running genuinely independent resources in parallel for efficiency, without requiring manual ordering in the configuration.

04 Modules & Reuse

What is it / why needed

A Terraform module is a reusable, parameterized package of resources — directly the same "templating instead of duplicating" philosophy as Module 12's Helm charts, just for cloud infrastructure instead of Kubernetes manifests.

# modules/vpc/main.tf — a reusable VPC module
variable "cidr_block" { type = string }
variable "environment" { type = string }

resource "aws_vpc" "this" {
  cidr_block = var.cidr_block
  tags = { Name = "${var.environment}-vpc" }
}

output "vpc_id" { value = aws_vpc.this.id }   # exposes this for other modules/root config to reference

# root main.tf — reusing the module for different environments
module "dev_vpc" {
  source      = "./modules/vpc"
  cidr_block  = "10.0.0.0/16"
  environment = "dev"
}

module "prod_vpc" {
  source      = "./modules/vpc"
  cidr_block  = "10.1.0.0/16"
  environment = "prod"
}
Same environment-parameterization pattern seen across this entire course: Module 10's Compose overlay files, Module 12's Helm values-per-environment, and now Terraform modules with per-environment variables — one reusable definition, environment-specific inputs, instead of duplicating the whole configuration per environment.

Common mistakes

  • Copy-pasting nearly-identical .tf files per environment instead of extracting a parameterized module — the same anti-pattern flagged in Modules 10 and 12, now at the infrastructure layer.
  • Modules with too many tightly-coupled responsibilities (e.g. one giant module creating VPC + EKS + RDS all together) — reduces reusability and makes partial updates riskier; prefer focused, composable modules.

Interview Q

Q: Why use a Terraform module instead of duplicating the same VPC configuration for dev, staging, and prod?
A: A module lets you define the VPC's structure ONCE, parameterized by variables (CIDR block, environment name, etc), and instantiate it multiple times with different inputs per environment — avoiding the maintenance burden and drift risk of keeping several near-identical, hand-copied configuration files in sync, the same underlying reuse principle as Helm charts (Module 12) applied to raw cloud infrastructure.

05 State Locking & Drift

State locking

If two people run `terraform apply` simultaneously against the same state, both could read the same "current state," calculate conflicting changes, and corrupt the state file or create duplicate/conflicting resources. A state lock (commonly implemented via a DynamoDB table alongside an S3 backend) prevents concurrent applies — the second person's apply waits or fails until the first completes.

Drift

Drift is when real infrastructure no longer matches what Terraform's state file believes exists — typically from a manual change made outside Terraform (someone edited a security group rule directly in the AWS Console). The next `terraform plan` will show this as an unexpected diff, and `terraform apply` would REVERT the manual change back to match the declared configuration — often surprising/alarming if the team didn't expect it.

Real production incident pattern: an engineer makes an "emergency" manual fix directly in the AWS Console during an incident (e.g. widening a security group rule to unblock something urgently). Weeks later, an unrelated `terraform apply` silently reverts that fix back to the old, narrower rule — because Terraform's state/config never knew about the manual change, and the next apply just reconciles reality back to its own declared truth. This is why manual "temporary" changes to Terraform-managed resources are genuinely risky, even when well-intentioned.

Common mistakes

  • Making manual "just this once" changes to Terraform-managed resources during an incident without immediately also updating the Terraform config to match — the next apply silently reverts the fix.
  • Not running `terraform plan` regularly (even without applying) to proactively detect drift before it causes a surprising revert.

Interview Q

Q: An engineer manually widens a security group rule in the AWS Console during an incident, without updating the Terraform config. Weeks later, an unrelated Terraform apply reverts that change back to the original, narrower rule. Why did this happen, and how should it have been handled?
A: Terraform's state file still reflects the OLD rule (since it was never informed of the manual change), so the next apply reconciles reality back to match its declared configuration — silently undoing the manual fix. This should have been handled by either updating the Terraform configuration itself to reflect the new intended rule (making it the actual source of truth going forward) immediately after the manual change, or treating the manual change as genuinely temporary and explicitly tracking that it needs to be either formalized in Terraform or reverted deliberately — never left as untracked drift indefinitely.

★ Mini Project

# Codify Module 15's VPC + Module 14's EC2 setup as reusable Terraform, with remote state

# backend.tf
terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "myorg-terraform-state"
    key            = "myapp/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "ap-south-1"
    dynamodb_table = "terraform-locks"
    encrypt        = true
  }
  required_providers {
    aws = { source = "hashicorp/aws", version = "~> 5.0" }   # pinned version
  }
}

provider "aws" { region = "ap-south-1" }

# modules/vpc/main.tf (reusable, per this module's Modules section)
variable "cidr_block" {}
variable "environment" {}

resource "aws_vpc" "this" {
  cidr_block = var.cidr_block
  tags = { Name = "${var.environment}-vpc" }
}

resource "aws_subnet" "public" {
  vpc_id                  = aws_vpc.this.id
  cidr_block              = cidrsubnet(var.cidr_block, 8, 1)
  map_public_ip_on_launch = true
  tags = { Name = "${var.environment}-public" }
}

output "vpc_id" { value = aws_vpc.this.id }
output "public_subnet_id" { value = aws_subnet.public.id }

# root main.tf
module "prod_vpc" {
  source      = "./modules/vpc"
  cidr_block  = "10.0.0.0/16"
  environment = "prod"
}

resource "aws_instance" "app" {
  ami           = "ami-0abcdef1234567890"
  instance_type = "t3.micro"
  subnet_id     = module.prod_vpc.public_subnet_id    # cross-module reference
  tags = { Name = "prod-app-server" }
}

# workflow
terraform init
terraform plan       # review the diff BEFORE applying — always
terraform apply

# simulate drift: manually change a tag via AWS CLI, then:
terraform plan          # observe it detects and shows the drift
terraform apply           # observe it reverts the manual change back to the declared config

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Terraform = declarative Infrastructure as Code — same reconciliation philosophy as Kubernetes (Module 11), applied to cloud infrastructure itself.
  • State file = Terraform's source of truth mapping config to real resources — ALWAYS remote (S3 + DynamoDB locking), never local, never committed to git (often contains secrets).
  • Workflow: init (setup) → plan (preview, safe, read-only) → apply (execute) → destroy (teardown). Always review plan before apply.
  • Dependency graph inferred automatically from resource attribute references — independent resources created in parallel.
  • Modules = reusable, parameterized infrastructure definitions — same "template once, parameterize per environment" philosophy as Helm (Module 12) and Compose overlays (Module 10).
  • State locking (DynamoDB) prevents concurrent applies from corrupting state.
  • Drift = reality diverging from declared config (usually from manual out-of-band changes) — the next apply silently reverts it back to the declared state, a real incident risk if manual "temporary" fixes aren't also reflected in the Terraform config.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What does Terraform's state file store?
A mapping between declared configuration and the actual real-world resources it created.
2. Where should production Terraform state live?
A remote backend (e.g. S3 with DynamoDB locking) — never local, never committed to git.
3. Which command shows a preview of changes without applying them?
terraform plan
4. How does Terraform determine resource creation order?
By inferring a dependency graph from resource attribute references.
5. What Terraform feature enables reusable, parameterized infrastructure definitions?
Modules.
6. What prevents two people from corrupting state via simultaneous applies?
State locking (e.g. via a DynamoDB table).
7. What is "drift"?
Real infrastructure diverging from what the Terraform state/config believes exists, usually from manual out-of-band changes.
8. What happens to a manual out-of-band change on the next `terraform apply`?
It's typically reverted back to match the declared configuration, since Terraform reconciles reality toward its own source of truth.
9. Why is committing the state file to git discouraged?
It often contains sensitive data in plaintext, and git isn't designed for the concurrent-write coordination a proper state backend provides.
10. What's a real risk of not pinning provider versions?
A future terraform init could pull a newer provider version with breaking changes, applying unexpectedly different behavior.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. `terraform apply` unexpectedly wants to revert a manual emergency fix made during an incident.
Expected behavior — drift from an out-of-band manual change; the Terraform config needs to be updated to reflect the intended state going forward, or the change should be deliberately reverted instead of left as untracked drift.
2. Two team members' applies conflict, resulting in a corrupted-looking state.
State locking likely wasn't configured (no DynamoDB table alongside the S3 backend) — set it up to serialize concurrent applies going forward; state may need manual recovery/import depending on the damage.
3. A `terraform plan` shows resources will be destroyed and recreated unexpectedly after a minor config change.
Some resource attributes can't be updated in place (force new resource) — check the provider documentation for which specific attribute change triggered replacement rather than an in-place update.
4. Sensitive data (a database password) is found in the state file, accessible to more people than intended.
Ensure the remote state backend (S3 bucket) has properly restrictive access controls and encryption (Module 18's principles) — the state file inherently contains sensitive resource attributes and must be treated with the same access rigor as the secrets themselves.
5. A newly cloned copy of the Terraform config fails on `terraform init` with a provider version error.
Provider versions weren't pinned, and a newer, incompatible provider version was resolved — pin exact or constrained versions in the required_providers block to ensure consistent behavior across clones/time.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Set up a remote S3 + DynamoDB backend for Terraform state and migrate an existing local-state configuration to it.
  2. Write a VPC + subnet configuration, run plan and apply, then manually change a resource's tag via AWS CLI and observe drift on the next plan.
  3. Extract a reusable module from a VPC configuration and instantiate it twice with different CIDR blocks for two environments.
  4. Simulate a concurrent-apply race condition (or review DynamoDB lock table entries during a deliberately slow apply) to observe state locking in action.
  5. Deliberately reference an unpinned provider version, then pin it explicitly and observe the difference in terraform init behavior.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What is Infrastructure as Code and what problem does it solve?
IaC means defining infrastructure (servers, networks, databases) in version-controlled configuration files rather than manually clicking through a console or running one-off CLI commands. It solves reproducibility (the same config recreates identical infrastructure anywhere), auditability (changes go through git, reviewable like code), and consistency (no more "what exactly did I click last time" uncertainty).
Beginner-Intermediate: Why is Terraform's state file considered such a critical piece of the system, and what are the risks of mismanaging it?
It's Terraform's only record of what real infrastructure it's actually responsible for and how that maps to your declared configuration. Mismanaging it (storing locally instead of a shared remote backend, losing it, or exposing it insecurely) risks losing track of managed resources entirely (leading to orphaned or duplicated infrastructure), team members working with conflicting views of reality, and potential secret exposure since state files often contain sensitive resource attributes in plaintext.
Intermediate: A team wants to manage infrastructure for dev, staging, and prod environments without duplicating configuration three times. Design the approach.
Extract the common infrastructure pattern (VPC, subnets, EKS cluster, RDS instance) into reusable Terraform modules, each parameterized by variables for whatever differs per environment (CIDR blocks, instance sizes, replica counts). Maintain separate root configurations (or a single root using workspaces/variable files) per environment that instantiate these shared modules with environment-specific variable values and separate remote state files (e.g. distinct S3 keys per environment) — ensuring dev and prod infrastructure are managed independently (different state, different blast radius) while sharing the same underlying, tested module definitions.
Intermediate-Advanced: A production incident required an emergency manual change to a security group. Design a process ensuring this doesn't cause a surprising revert on the next scheduled Terraform apply, while still allowing the urgent fix to happen quickly.
Immediately after the incident is stabilized (even if the manual change itself happened under time pressure), update the corresponding Terraform configuration to reflect the new intended state as the FIRST follow-up action, then run terraform plan to confirm it now shows no diff for that resource (proving config and reality are back in sync) before any other apply touches that resource. If the emergency change was meant to be temporary rather than permanent, explicitly track and schedule its reversion (both the manual AWS-side change and confirming Terraform's plan reflects the reversion) rather than leaving it as untracked, indefinite drift that could be silently reverted by an unrelated future apply.
Advanced: Design a Terraform workflow for a large organization with dozens of engineers across multiple teams, ensuring safety, auditability, and preventing destructive mistakes.
Remote state per logical component (VPC, EKS, RDS, etc — not one giant monolithic state file, limiting blast radius of any single apply) with DynamoDB locking preventing concurrent modification. All Terraform changes go through pull requests, with CI running `terraform plan` automatically and posting the diff as a PR comment for review (never applying directly from a local machine in production) — genuine terraform apply only happens via a CI/CD pipeline (Modules 23/24) after PR approval, using OIDC-federated credentials (Module 23's principle) scoped to exactly the permissions that specific Terraform configuration needs (Module 13's least privilege), never broad admin access. Destructive changes (resource replacement/destruction) are specifically flagged in the plan output and may require additional manual approval gates before the apply proceeds. Modules are versioned and published to a private module registry, letting teams consume tested, reviewed infrastructure patterns rather than each team writing raw resource blocks independently, reducing both duplication and the risk of subtly inconsistent configurations across the organization.
Module 27 / 30 — Terraform. Next up: Ansible — configuration management, handling what happens INSIDE servers Terraform provisions (packages, configs, application deployment) rather than the infrastructure itself.

Ansible, from first principles to production

Configuration management — handling what happens INSIDE servers after Terraform (Module 27) provisions them: packages, configs, application deployment. Same declarative philosophy, different layer of the stack.

01 Why Ansible / Terraform vs Ansible

What is it / why needed

Ansible is a configuration management tool — it handles what happens AFTER a server exists: installing packages, writing config files, starting services, deploying application code. This is a genuinely different layer from Module 27's Terraform, which only handles WHETHER the server (and surrounding infrastructure) exists at all.

TerraformAnsible
LayerInfrastructure provisioning (does this VPC/EC2/RDS instance exist?)Configuration management (what's installed/configured ON an existing server?)
Typical useCreate the VM, network, databaseInstall Nginx, configure it, deploy the app onto an already-existing VM
State trackingExplicit state file (Module 27)No persistent state file — checks actual current condition on each run instead
A very common real production pattern, directly combining Modules 27 and 28: Terraform provisions the EC2 instance/VPC/security group (the infrastructure), then Ansible runs against that newly-created instance to install and configure the actual application stack — two different tools, cleanly separated by layer, often triggered as sequential stages in the same CI/CD pipeline (Module 23/24).

Common mistakes

  • Trying to use Terraform's provisioner blocks for complex application configuration instead of handing that off to a dedicated configuration management tool like Ansible — Terraform provisioners are explicitly documented as a last resort, since they don't fit Terraform's plan/apply/state model well.

Interview Q

Q: Your team uses Terraform to create EC2 instances. Should application configuration (installing dependencies, deploying code) also be done in Terraform?
A: Generally no — Terraform excels at infrastructure PROVISIONING (does the resource exist, with what properties) but is a poor fit for iterative, in-server configuration management (installing packages, managing config files, deploying code), which is Ansible's specific strength. The common, clean pattern is Terraform provisions the infrastructure, then Ansible configures what runs on it — two tools cleanly separated by the layer of the stack they operate on.

02 Agentless Architecture

What is it / why needed

Ansible is agentless — it requires NO persistent software installed on managed servers. It connects over plain SSH (Module 7's foundation) and executes Python-based modules remotely, then removes any temporary files afterward. This is a deliberate architectural contrast to tools like Puppet/Chef, which require a persistent agent daemon running on every managed node.

Ansible Control Node ──SSH (Module 7)──► Target Server │ Ansible copies a small Python module over, executes it, then cleans up — NOTHING persists on the target after the run completes
Agentless (Ansible)Agent-based (Puppet, Chef)
Installed on targetsNothing persistent — just needs Python + SSH accessA persistent daemon running continuously on every managed node
Attack surfaceSmaller — no standing service to exploit/patch on targetsLarger — the agent itself is additional software to secure/patch everywhere
Real-time enforcementOnly when explicitly run (push-based)Can continuously self-correct drift on its own schedule (pull-based, agent checks in periodically)

Common mistakes

  • Assuming Ansible continuously enforces configuration like an agent-based tool would — it only applies configuration when you explicitly RUN a playbook; drift between runs isn't automatically self-corrected the way a persistent agent-based system might.

Interview Q

Q: Why is Ansible's agentless architecture considered a security advantage over agent-based configuration management tools?
A: There's no persistent daemon/service running on managed servers that itself needs securing, patching, and monitoring as additional attack surface — Ansible only needs standard SSH access (Module 7) and Python present, using them transiently during a run and leaving nothing behind afterward. Agent-based tools add a permanently-running process on every single managed node, which is itself software that could have vulnerabilities and must be maintained.

03 Playbooks & Idempotency

# site.yml — an Ansible playbook
- name: Configure web servers
  hosts: webservers
  become: true                          # run tasks with sudo (Module 1's privilege escalation)

  tasks:
    - name: Install Nginx
      apt:
        name: nginx
        state: present                   # idempotent — does nothing if already installed

    - name: Deploy Nginx config
      template:
        src: nginx.conf.j2
        dest: /etc/nginx/nginx.conf
      notify: reload nginx                 # only triggers the handler if this task ACTUALLY changed something

    - name: Ensure Nginx is running
      systemd:
        name: nginx
        state: started
        enabled: true

  handlers:
    - name: reload nginx
      systemd:
        name: nginx
        state: reloaded

Idempotency — the core Ansible design principle

Every well-written Ansible task is idempotent — running the playbook once or a hundred times produces the SAME end state, and Ansible reports whether each task actually CHANGED anything ("changed" vs "ok"). This is the exact same idempotency concept flagged in Module 5's HTTP methods discussion, now applied to configuration management: re-running a playbook should never cause unwanted duplicate side effects.

Handlers only fire on actual change — a real gotcha: the `notify: reload nginx` handler in the example above ONLY runs if the "Deploy Nginx config" task actually changed the file — if the config was already identical (playbook re-run with no real changes), the handler is skipped entirely, since there's nothing to reload. This is intentional (avoiding unnecessary service restarts) but surprises people expecting handlers to always fire.

Common mistakes

  • Writing tasks using raw `shell`/`command` modules for things that have a proper idempotent Ansible module available (like `apt` for package installation) — shell/command tasks report "changed" every single run regardless of actual effect, breaking idempotency and making playbook runs harder to reason about.
  • Not understanding that handlers are deduplicated and run once at the END of a play (even if multiple tasks notify the same handler) — assuming they fire immediately inline after each notifying task.

Interview Q

Q: Why is idempotency such a core design principle for Ansible playbooks, and what's a common way beginners accidentally break it?
A: Idempotency means running a playbook repeatedly is always safe and produces the same end state, whether it's the first run or the hundredth — critical for configuration management since playbooks are typically re-run regularly (e.g. as part of every deployment) without wanting unintended side effects each time. Beginners commonly break this by using the generic `shell` or `command` modules for tasks that have a proper dedicated Ansible module available (like using `shell: apt-get install nginx` instead of the idempotent `apt` module) — shell/command tasks report "changed" on every single run and don't check actual current state first, unlike purpose-built modules.

04 Inventory & Variables

An inventory defines which hosts Ansible manages and how they're grouped — can be a static file or dynamically generated (e.g. querying AWS EC2 tags directly, so newly Terraform-provisioned instances automatically appear as Ansible targets without manual inventory updates).

# inventory.ini — static inventory
[webservers]
web1.example.com
web2.example.com

[dbservers]
db1.example.com

[webservers:vars]
http_port=8080

# Dynamic inventory (AWS EC2 plugin) — automatically discovers instances by tag,
# directly complementing Module 27's Terraform-provisioned infrastructure
plugin: aws_ec2
regions: [ap-south-1]
filters:
  tag:Role: webserver
Dynamic inventory is the real production pattern for cloud environments: rather than manually maintaining a static list of IPs (which breaks the moment Module 17's Auto Scaling Group replaces an instance), Ansible queries AWS directly at run time for currently-running instances matching a tag — always accurate, zero manual inventory maintenance.

Common mistakes

  • Maintaining a static inventory file for an environment with Auto Scaling (Module 17) — instances get replaced/rescaled and the static list goes stale almost immediately.

Interview Q

Q: Why is a static Ansible inventory file a poor fit for a fleet managed by an Auto Scaling Group?
A: ASG-managed instances (Module 17) are ephemeral — replaced automatically on health check failures or scaled up/down based on demand, meaning specific instance IPs constantly change. A static inventory file listing fixed IPs/hostnames goes stale almost immediately. Dynamic inventory (e.g. the AWS EC2 plugin, querying by tag at run time) always reflects the CURRENT actual fleet, with zero manual inventory maintenance required.

05 Roles & Reuse

An Ansible role is a structured, reusable bundle of tasks/templates/variables/handlers — the same "package once, reuse across projects" philosophy as Module 12's Helm charts and Module 27's Terraform modules, now for configuration management.

roles/nginx/ ├── tasks/main.yml ← the actual install/configure steps ├── templates/nginx.conf.j2 ← Jinja2 templates (variables injected) ├── handlers/main.yml ← service restart/reload logic ├── defaults/main.yml ← default variable values (overridable) └── vars/main.yml ← role-specific variables 
# site.yml using a role
- name: Configure web servers
  hosts: webservers
  become: true
  roles:
    - role: nginx
      vars:
        nginx_worker_processes: 4

Common mistakes

  • Writing one giant, monolithic playbook covering every server type instead of extracting focused, reusable roles per responsibility (nginx, docker, monitoring-agent) — mirrors the exact same over-coupling anti-pattern flagged for Terraform modules in Module 27.

Interview Q

Q: Why extract Ansible tasks into a reusable role instead of keeping everything in one large playbook?
A: A role packages related tasks, templates, handlers, and default variables into a self-contained, reusable unit — usable across multiple playbooks/projects without duplication, and independently testable/versionable. This is the same modular reuse principle as Terraform modules (Module 27) and Helm charts (Module 12), just applied at the configuration-management layer.

★ Mini Project

# Full pipeline: Terraform provisions EC2 → Ansible dynamic inventory discovers it → configures + deploys app

# (Terraform from Module 27 provisions an EC2 instance tagged Role=webserver)

# ansible.cfg
cat > ansible.cfg <<'EOF'
[defaults]
inventory = inventory_aws_ec2.yml
EOF

cat > inventory_aws_ec2.yml <<'EOF'
plugin: aws_ec2
regions: [ap-south-1]
filters:
  tag:Role: webserver
  instance-state-name: running
keyed_groups:
  - key: tags.Role
EOF

ansible-inventory --list         # confirm the Terraform-created instance is auto-discovered

# roles/webapp/tasks/main.yml
cat > roles/webapp/tasks/main.yml <<'EOF'
- name: Install Docker (Module 9)
  apt: {name: docker.io, state: present}

- name: Ensure Docker is running
  systemd: {name: docker, state: started, enabled: true}

- name: Pull application image from ECR (Module 20)
  docker_image:
    name: "{{ ecr_repo }}:{{ app_version }}"
    source: pull

- name: Run application container
  docker_container:
    name: myapp
    image: "{{ ecr_repo }}:{{ app_version }}"
    ports: ["8080:8080"]
    restart_policy: unless-stopped
EOF

# site.yml
cat > site.yml <<'EOF'
- name: Configure and deploy web servers
  hosts: tag_Role_webserver
  become: true
  roles:
    - role: webapp
      vars:
        ecr_repo: ".dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/myapp"
        app_version: "1.0"
EOF

ansible-playbook site.yml
# re-run immediately and confirm every task reports "ok" (unchanged), proving idempotency

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Terraform = does the infrastructure exist. Ansible = what's configured/running ON that existing infrastructure. Different layers, often used together sequentially.
  • Agentless: connects via SSH (Module 7), needs only Python on targets, nothing persistent left behind — smaller attack surface than agent-based tools (Puppet/Chef).
  • Idempotency is the core design principle — re-running a playbook should always be safe, same end state. Avoid raw shell/command for things with a proper idempotent module.
  • Handlers only fire on ACTUAL change (notified by a task reporting "changed"), and run once at the end of a play even if notified multiple times.
  • Dynamic inventory (e.g. AWS EC2 plugin, tag-based) keeps target lists accurate for ephemeral, Auto-Scaled fleets — static inventory goes stale immediately in that context.
  • Roles = reusable, structured configuration bundles — same modular reuse philosophy as Helm charts (Module 12) and Terraform modules (Module 27).

Q 10 MCQs

1. What layer does Terraform operate at versus Ansible?
Terraform: infrastructure provisioning. Ansible: configuration management on existing infrastructure.
2. Does Ansible require a persistent agent on managed servers?
No — it's agentless, using SSH and transient Python module execution.
3. What does "idempotent" mean in the context of an Ansible task?
Running it repeatedly produces the same end state without unwanted side effects each time.
4. When does an Ansible handler actually run?
Only if a task that notifies it reports "changed," and typically once at the end of the play.
5. Why is dynamic inventory preferred over static inventory for Auto Scaling Group-managed fleets?
ASG instances are ephemeral and constantly replaced — a static list goes stale almost immediately, while dynamic inventory always reflects current reality.
6. What Ansible construct packages reusable tasks/templates/variables together?
A role.
7. What module type commonly breaks idempotency if used unnecessarily?
The raw shell/command modules, which report "changed" every run regardless of actual effect.
8. What's a security advantage of Ansible's agentless architecture?
No persistent daemon on managed servers needing its own patching/securing — smaller standing attack surface.
9. Does Ansible maintain a persistent state file like Terraform?
No — it checks actual current condition on each run rather than tracking explicit state.
10. What common pattern combines Terraform and Ansible in one pipeline?
Terraform provisions infrastructure first, then Ansible configures/deploys onto the newly-created resources.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. A playbook reports "changed" on every single run despite nothing actually being different.
Likely using shell/command modules instead of proper idempotent modules for that task — replace with the appropriate purpose-built module (e.g. apt, template, systemd).
2. A config file change was deployed but the service never picked up the new config.
Check the task actually notified a handler (and the handler name matches exactly), and confirm the handler ran (it only fires when the notifying task reports "changed" — if the file appeared identical for some reason, the handler wouldn't trigger).
3. Ansible can't find newly-provisioned EC2 instances from a recent Terraform apply.
Check the dynamic inventory's tag filters match the actual tags Terraform applied to the new instances, and confirm the instances are in "running" state (not still provisioning).
4. A playbook run against a large fleet is very slow.
Check if tasks are running serially when they could safely run in parallel (Ansible's default `forks` setting limits concurrent host execution) — increasing forks (with appropriate care for target load) can speed up large fleet runs.
5. An Ansible playbook works fine against 5 test servers but fails unpredictably at scale against 200 servers.
Check for non-idempotent tasks (shell/command based) that might behave differently depending on existing state variance across a larger, less uniform fleet, and verify SSH connection limits/concurrency settings aren't being exceeded under higher parallel host counts.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Write a playbook installing and configuring Nginx idempotently, and verify a second run reports no changes.
  2. Set up AWS EC2 dynamic inventory filtered by a specific tag, and verify it correctly discovers Terraform-provisioned instances.
  3. Extract a reusable role from a monolithic playbook, parameterize it with variables, and reuse it for two different server groups with different variable values.
  4. Deliberately use a shell command for a task that has a proper idempotent module available, observe it reporting "changed" every run, then fix it with the correct module.
  5. Chain a Terraform apply (provisioning an EC2 instance) directly into an Ansible playbook run (configuring it) within a single automation script, end to end.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What's the difference between Terraform and Ansible, given they're both "Infrastructure as Code" tools?
Terraform provisions infrastructure itself — deciding whether a VM, network, or database exists and with what properties. Ansible configures what happens on ALREADY-EXISTING infrastructure — installing packages, managing configuration files, deploying applications. They operate at genuinely different layers and are commonly used together, sequentially, rather than being interchangeable alternatives.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why is Ansible described as "agentless," and what's the practical benefit?
It requires no persistent software running on managed servers — it connects over standard SSH and executes transient Python-based modules, cleaning up afterward. The practical benefit is a smaller standing attack surface (no additional persistent service to secure/patch on every managed node) and simpler onboarding of new servers (just needs SSH access and Python, no agent installation/registration step).
Intermediate: Explain idempotency in Ansible with a concrete example of how a poorly-written task could violate it.
Idempotency means repeated playbook runs produce the same end state without unintended side effects. A task like `shell: "echo 'export PATH=$PATH:/opt/myapp/bin' >> ~/.bashrc"` would violate this — every single run appends ANOTHER duplicate line to .bashrc, since the shell module has no awareness of "is this line already there." A properly idempotent approach uses the `lineinfile` module instead, which checks whether that exact line already exists before deciding to add it, guaranteeing the same end result regardless of how many times it runs.
Intermediate-Advanced: Design a CI/CD pipeline stage that provisions new infrastructure with Terraform and immediately configures it with Ansible, ensuring the dynamic inventory correctly picks up the newly-created resources.
The pipeline runs terraform apply first, tagging newly-created EC2 instances with identifiable tags (e.g. Role=webserver, Environment=prod) as part of the Terraform resource definitions. After terraform apply completes, the pipeline waits/polls for the instances to reach a "running" state and pass any initial health checks (avoiding a race where Ansible tries to SSH before the instance/SSH daemon is actually ready — echoing Module 1's boot-sequence timing concerns). The pipeline then invokes ansible-playbook using a dynamic AWS EC2 inventory plugin filtered by those exact tags, ensuring it automatically targets precisely the instances just created by that specific Terraform run, without any manual IP/hostname bookkeeping between the two tool invocations.
Advanced: A security team requires that no server in the fleet ever has a persistent configuration-management agent installed, due to a strict minimal-attack-surface policy, while still needing consistent, auditable configuration across hundreds of servers. How does Ansible's architecture specifically satisfy this requirement, and what are the trade-offs?
Ansible's agentless model satisfies this directly — there's no persistent daemon to install or maintain on any managed server at all, only transient SSH-based execution during actual playbook runs, meeting the strict no-persistent-agent policy natively without any special configuration. The trade-off is that Ansible provides no CONTINUOUS, self-correcting enforcement between runs the way an agent-based tool (which checks in on its own periodic schedule) would — configuration drift between playbook runs isn't automatically detected or corrected until someone explicitly re-runs the playbook. For a security-conscious team, this trade-off is typically addressed by scheduling frequent, regular Ansible runs (e.g. via a CI/CD pipeline on a cron trigger, or triggered by relevant infrastructure change events) combined with drift-detection tooling and thorough audit logging of every playbook execution, achieving effectively continuous compliance without ever introducing a persistent agent process onto the managed fleet.
Module 28 / 30 — Ansible. Next up: ArgoCD — GitOps, closing the loop by having Kubernetes deployments themselves driven declaratively from git, building directly on Module 12's Helm and Module 11's reconciliation philosophy.
DevOps / SDE-1 Interview Track — Module 29 / 30

ArgoCD, from first principles to production

GitOps — closing the loop by making git the single source of truth for what's actually RUNNING in Kubernetes, with continuous reconciliation instead of CI/CD pipelines pushing changes imperatively.

01 Why GitOps / Push vs Pull Deployment

What is it / why needed

ArgoCD is a GitOps continuous delivery tool for Kubernetes — it continuously watches a git repository (holding Kubernetes manifests or Helm charts, Module 12) and automatically reconciles the cluster's actual state to match what's declared in git, using the SAME controller/reconciliation-loop philosophy as Module 11's Kubernetes controllers themselves, just one level up (reconciling the cluster's applications, not just individual pods).

TRADITIONAL CI/CD PUSH MODEL (Module 23/24): CI pipeline ──has credentials, actively pushes──► kubectl apply / helm upgrade ──► Cluster (the CI system needs cluster-admin-ish credentials to push changes IN) GITOPS PULL MODEL (ArgoCD): Git repo (source of truth) ◄──ArgoCD continuously watches/pulls──── ArgoCD (running INSIDE the cluster) │ reconciles cluster state to match git, automatically (the cluster pulls changes; no external system ever needs direct push credentials to the cluster)
Push (CI/CD directly applies)Pull (GitOps/ArgoCD)
Who initiates deploymentThe CI/CD pipelineArgoCD itself, running inside the cluster, pulling from git
Cluster credentials needed externallyYes — CI/CD needs cluster access from OUTSIDENo — ArgoCD already runs inside the cluster; no external system needs cluster-admin credentials
Drift correctionNone automatic — drift persists until the next explicit CI runContinuous — ArgoCD constantly compares and can auto-correct drift
Source of truth for "what's deployed"Whatever the last CI run happened to pushGit, unambiguously — the cluster's state is a reflection of a specific git commit

Common mistakes

  • Giving a CI/CD pipeline (Module 23/24) broad, standing cluster-admin credentials for direct push-based deployment — a real security exposure GitOps specifically avoids by flipping the model to pull-based.

Interview Q

Q: Why does the GitOps pull model reduce security risk compared to a traditional CI/CD pipeline pushing deployments directly?
A: In the push model, the CI/CD system (running outside the cluster, e.g. GitHub Actions or Jenkins from Modules 23/24) needs standing credentials with enough privilege to modify cluster resources — a genuinely sensitive credential living in external CI infrastructure. In the pull model, ArgoCD runs INSIDE the cluster and pulls changes from git itself; no external system ever needs direct cluster-modifying credentials at all, since the cluster is reconciling itself toward git rather than being pushed into by something external.

02 Application CRD & Sync

ArgoCD's core object is the Application custom resource (a Kubernetes CRD, extending Module 11's Kubernetes API model) — declaring which git repo/path to watch and which cluster/namespace to deploy it into.

apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Application
metadata:
  name: myapp
  namespace: argocd
spec:
  source:
    repoURL: https://github.com/myorg/myapp-config.git
    path: helm/myapp                      # points at a Helm chart (Module 12) in the config repo
    targetRevision: main
    helm:
      valueFiles: [values-prod.yaml]
  destination:
    server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
    namespace: production
  syncPolicy:
    automated:
      prune: true                          # remove resources deleted from git
      selfHeal: true                         # revert manual cluster changes back to match git (see next section)
Notice ArgoCD deploys a Helm chart (Module 12) directly — it commonly sits ON TOP of Helm rather than replacing it: Helm handles templating/packaging, ArgoCD handles the continuous git-to-cluster reconciliation loop that Helm itself (a push-based CLI tool, per Module 12's closing point) doesn't natively provide.

Common mistakes

  • Confusing ArgoCD and Helm as competing tools — they're commonly complementary, with ArgoCD orchestrating WHEN/whether a Helm chart gets applied based on git state, rather than replacing Helm's templating role.

Interview Q

Q: Is ArgoCD a replacement for Helm?
A: Not typically — they solve different problems and are commonly used together. Helm (Module 12) handles templating and packaging Kubernetes manifests into versioned, parameterized charts. ArgoCD handles continuously watching a git repository (which may contain a Helm chart) and reconciling the cluster to match it — providing the GitOps automation/drift-correction layer that plain Helm CLI usage (a manual, push-based tool) doesn't natively provide on its own.

03 Drift Detection & Self-Healing

What is it / why needed

ArgoCD continuously compares the LIVE state of cluster resources against what's declared in git — directly the same drift concept from Module 27's Terraform, but continuous rather than only detected at the next manual `plan`, and (with selfHeal: true) automatically CORRECTED rather than requiring an explicit apply.

Engineer manually runs: kubectl scale deployment/myapp --replicas=10 │ ▼ ArgoCD's continuous reconciliation loop notices: live state (10 replicas) ≠ git-declared state (3 replicas) │ ▼ With selfHeal: true → ArgoCD automatically reverts it back to 3 replicas within seconds, without any human needing to run terraform apply or kubectl apply manually
The same lesson as Module 27's Terraform drift section, but faster and more surprising: a manual "emergency" kubectl change against an ArgoCD-managed Application gets silently and near-immediately reverted if selfHeal is enabled — far faster than Terraform's drift correction (which only happens on the next explicit apply). Any genuine emergency change MUST go through git (even if that means an expedited, reviewed commit) rather than a direct kubectl command, or it simply won't stick.

Common mistakes

  • Manually kubectl-editing an ArgoCD-managed resource during an incident, not realizing selfHeal will revert it within moments — wasted effort and confusion during exactly the highest-pressure moments.
  • Disabling selfHeal broadly "to allow manual tweaks" instead of establishing a fast, reviewed git-commit process for genuine emergencies — reintroduces the exact untracked-drift risk GitOps is meant to eliminate.

Interview Q

Q: An engineer manually scales a Deployment via kubectl during an incident, but the change reverts within seconds. Why, and what should they have done instead?
A: The Deployment is managed by an ArgoCD Application with selfHeal enabled — ArgoCD's continuous reconciliation loop detected the live state now differs from git's declared replica count and automatically corrected it back. The engineer should instead have made the change by committing an updated replica count (or scaling config) to the git repository ArgoCD watches — even under time pressure, an expedited, reviewed git commit is the correct path to a change that will actually persist, rather than a direct kubectl command that GitOps is specifically designed to override.

04 Sync Strategies & Waves

ConceptPurpose
Manual syncChanges are detected but NOT automatically applied — a human explicitly clicks/triggers "Sync" in the ArgoCD UI/CLI, useful for production environments wanting an extra manual gate
Automated syncChanges in git are applied automatically, continuously — used for lower-risk environments or teams with high confidence in their CI validation (Module 23/24's pipeline gates)
Sync wavesOrder resources within an Application to deploy sequentially (e.g. a ConfigMap before the Deployment that references it) using annotation-based ordering, similar in spirit to Terraform's dependency graph (Module 27) but explicitly declared rather than inferred
# Sync wave ordering via annotation
metadata:
  annotations:
    argocd.argoproj.io/sync-wave: "0"    # ConfigMap — deployed first
---
metadata:
  annotations:
    argocd.argoproj.io/sync-wave: "1"    # Deployment referencing it — deployed after

Common mistakes

  • Using fully automated sync in production without sufficiently rigorous CI validation gates (Module 23's scan-gating discussion) upstream — automated sync means whatever passes CI and lands in the watched git branch deploys automatically, so the safety net needs to be genuinely solid before that branch.
  • Not using sync waves for genuinely order-dependent resources — race conditions where a Deployment starts before a ConfigMap/Secret it needs actually exists.

Interview Q

Q: Why might a production ArgoCD Application use manual sync instead of automated sync, even though automated sync is more "hands-off"?
A: Manual sync adds a deliberate human checkpoint before changes actually apply to production, even after they've merged to the watched git branch — useful when the team wants an extra layer of oversight for production specifically (e.g. reviewing exactly what ArgoCD's diff shows before confirming), rather than fully trusting automated sync to apply anything that lands in that branch immediately, however solid the upstream CI validation is believed to be.

05 Security Model

Since ArgoCD runs INSIDE the cluster with (often quite broad) permissions to modify resources, its own security posture matters enormously — directly extending Module 21's Kubernetes RBAC and Module 13's IAM least-privilege principles to the GitOps controller itself.

ConcernMitigation
ArgoCD's own cluster permissionsScope its service account's RBAC as narrowly as genuinely needed per-Application/namespace, rather than blanket cluster-admin
Who can modify Application definitions / git repo accessGit repo branch protection (Module 3) + ArgoCD's own RBAC for who can create/edit Applications
Secrets in gitNever commit plaintext secrets to the watched git repo — use a secrets-management integration (e.g. External Secrets Operator pulling from AWS Secrets Manager, referenced but not stored in git) instead
Real risk to internalize: ArgoCD is effectively a privileged, automated actor with the power to modify production Kubernetes resources based purely on what lands in a git branch — this makes that git repo's branch protection (Module 3) and access control just as security-critical as the cluster's own RBAC. A compromised commit access to the watched repo is functionally equivalent to compromised cluster-admin access, given automated sync.

Common mistakes

  • Weak branch protection on the git repo ArgoCD watches for automated sync — effectively as dangerous as weak cluster RBAC, since anything merged there deploys automatically.
  • Committing plaintext secrets directly into the ArgoCD-watched repo instead of referencing an external secrets manager.

Interview Q

Q: Why should the git repository ArgoCD watches for automated sync be treated with the same security rigor as the Kubernetes cluster's own RBAC?
A: With automated sync enabled, anything merged into the watched branch deploys to the cluster automatically and continuously, without further human gatekeeping. This makes commit/merge access to that repository functionally equivalent to having deployment power over the cluster — weak branch protection (Module 3) or lax repo access control there is just as dangerous as an overly permissive Kubernetes RBAC role, since either path leads to the same outcome: unauthorized changes actually running in production.

★ Mini Project

# Install ArgoCD and set up a GitOps-managed deployment for the app from earlier modules

kubectl create namespace argocd
kubectl apply -n argocd -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/argoproj/argo-cd/stable/manifests/install.yaml

kubectl port-forward svc/argocd-server -n argocd 8080:443
# login: admin / (initial password from argocd-initial-admin-secret)

# Create an Application pointing at a git repo containing the Helm chart from Module 12
argocd app create myapp \
  --repo https://github.com/myorg/myapp-config.git \
  --path helm/myapp \
  --dest-server https://kubernetes.default.svc \
  --dest-namespace production \
  --sync-policy automated \
  --self-heal \
  --auto-prune

argocd app sync myapp
argocd app get myapp                     # confirm status: Synced, Healthy

# Prove GitOps drift-correction:
kubectl scale deployment/myapp -n production --replicas=10
sleep 5
kubectl get deployment/myapp -n production   # observe ArgoCD already reverted it back to git's declared count

# Prove the correct way to make a change: commit an update to the git repo (e.g. new image tag)
# and watch ArgoCD automatically detect and sync it — no kubectl/helm command needed from you at all

✓ Revision Sheet

  • GitOps/ArgoCD = pull-based deployment, ArgoCD runs INSIDE the cluster, watching git and reconciling — no external CI system needs standing cluster-admin credentials (unlike push-based CI/CD).
  • Application CRD declares which git repo/path/Helm chart (Module 12) to watch and which cluster/namespace to deploy into. ArgoCD commonly sits ON TOP of Helm, not replacing it.
  • Continuous drift detection/correction (with selfHeal) — far faster than Terraform's plan-triggered drift detection (Module 27); manual kubectl changes get reverted within seconds.
  • Any genuine change MUST go through git — manual cluster edits against a selfHeal-enabled Application simply don't persist.
  • Manual sync = extra human gate before applying; Automated sync = continuous, hands-off (needs solid upstream CI validation, Module 23/24).
  • Sync waves order resource deployment explicitly (e.g. ConfigMap before Deployment) via annotations.
  • The watched git repo's access control/branch protection is as security-critical as cluster RBAC itself — automated sync means repo write access ≈ deployment power.
  • Never commit plaintext secrets to the watched repo — use an external secrets manager integration instead.

Q 10 MCQs

1. Does GitOps use a push or pull deployment model?
Pull — the cluster (via ArgoCD, running inside it) pulls changes from git.
2. Does a CI/CD pipeline need standing cluster-admin credentials in a GitOps model?
No — ArgoCD, running inside the cluster, handles applying changes; no external system needs direct push credentials.
3. What Kubernetes object type is ArgoCD's core "Application"?
A Custom Resource Definition (CRD).
4. Does ArgoCD replace Helm?
No — they're commonly complementary; ArgoCD often deploys a Helm chart as its source.
5. What happens to a manual kubectl change against an ArgoCD Application with selfHeal enabled?
It gets automatically reverted back to match git within seconds.
6. What's the difference between manual sync and automated sync?
Manual sync requires an explicit human trigger to apply detected changes; automated sync applies them continuously and automatically.
7. What ArgoCD feature explicitly orders resource deployment within an Application?
Sync waves.
8. Should plaintext secrets be committed to a git repo ArgoCD watches?
No — use an external secrets manager integration instead.
9. Why is branch protection on an ArgoCD-watched repo security-critical?
With automated sync, merged changes deploy automatically — weak repo access control is functionally equivalent to weak cluster RBAC.
10. What's the correct way to make an urgent production change to an ArgoCD-managed, selfHeal-enabled Application?
Commit the change to the watched git repository (even expedited/reviewed quickly) rather than a direct kubectl/manual change, which would simply be reverted.

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. A manual scaling change during an incident keeps getting reverted within seconds.
Expected — selfHeal is enabled on that ArgoCD Application; make the change via a git commit instead, not a direct kubectl command.
2. An ArgoCD Application shows "OutOfSync" but automated sync isn't applying the change.
Check whether automated sync is actually enabled on that Application (it may be manual-sync-only by design), and whether there's a sync error/health issue blocking automatic reconciliation (check argocd app get for details).
3. A Deployment fails to start because a ConfigMap it depends on doesn't exist yet.
Missing sync-wave ordering — add explicit sync-wave annotations to ensure the ConfigMap deploys before the Deployment that references it.
4. A compromised contributor's merge to the watched repo resulted in unauthorized production changes.
Branch protection/access control on the ArgoCD-watched repo was insufficient — tighten required reviews/approvals on that branch, treating it with the same rigor as cluster RBAC given automated sync's direct deployment power.
5. An Application's sync fails with an error referencing a secret that doesn't exist in the cluster.
Likely expected if secrets are intentionally excluded from git (correct practice) — verify the external secrets manager integration (e.g. External Secrets Operator) is properly configured and has actually synced that specific secret into the cluster before the Application's sync attempt.

▶ 5 Coding/Lab Exercises

  1. Install ArgoCD in a test cluster and create an Application pointing at a Helm chart in a git repo, with automated sync and selfHeal enabled.
  2. Manually kubectl-edit a resource managed by that Application and time how quickly ArgoCD reverts it.
  3. Add sync-wave annotations to a ConfigMap and its dependent Deployment, and verify the deployment order in the ArgoCD UI/logs.
  4. Switch an Application from automated to manual sync, make a git change, and confirm it's detected but not applied until you explicitly sync.
  5. Integrate an external secrets manager (e.g. a mock/test setup) so a Deployment can reference a secret without it ever being committed to the watched git repo.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What is GitOps and how does ArgoCD implement it?
GitOps is the practice of using a git repository as the single source of truth for what should be running in a system, with automated tooling continuously reconciling actual state to match it. ArgoCD implements this for Kubernetes by running inside the cluster, continuously watching a specified git repo/path, and automatically applying/correcting the cluster's resources to match what's declared there.
Beginner-Intermediate: Why is the pull-based GitOps model considered more secure than a traditional push-based CI/CD deployment?
In a push model, an external CI/CD system needs standing credentials capable of modifying cluster resources from OUTSIDE the cluster — a sensitive credential living in external infrastructure. In GitOps's pull model, the reconciling agent (ArgoCD) already runs INSIDE the cluster and pulls changes from git itself, so no external system needs direct cluster-modifying access at all — reducing the attack surface for credential compromise.
Intermediate: An engineer argues that automated sync with selfHeal makes emergency incident response harder, since manual fixes get reverted. How would you respond to this concern?
The concern is valid but addressable — the correct emergency response under GitOps isn't a direct kubectl change (which selfHeal will revert), but an expedited, reviewed git commit to the watched repository, which ArgoCD will then apply just as quickly through its normal automated sync path. Teams can establish a fast-track review process for genuine emergencies (e.g. a lightweight, pre-approved emergency-change process) so the git-first workflow doesn't meaningfully slow down urgent fixes, while still preserving git as the single, auditable source of truth rather than allowing untracked manual drift during high-pressure moments.
Intermediate-Advanced: Design a GitOps repository structure and sync strategy for an organization running dev, staging, and production environments on the same EKS cluster (or separate clusters), balancing deployment velocity against production safety.
Separate ArgoCD Applications per environment, each pointing at a distinct path or branch/tag in the config repo (or entirely separate repos for stricter isolation) — e.g. dev tracks the main branch with automated sync and selfHeal for fast iteration, staging tracks a release branch with automated sync for pre-production validation, and production either tracks a specifically-tagged release commit with MANUAL sync (requiring explicit human confirmation after reviewing ArgoCD's diff) or automated sync gated behind a mandatory CI pipeline (Module 23/24) with thorough test/scan gates on the promotion path from staging to production tags. This balances fast dev iteration against a deliberate, reviewable gate before anything reaches production, while still keeping git as the unambiguous source of truth for every environment's actual deployed state.
Advanced: Design the complete security and operational model for ArgoCD managing a multi-tenant EKS cluster where different teams should only be able to deploy to their own namespaces, tying together concepts from this entire course.
ArgoCD's own Kubernetes RBAC (via its service account, per Module 21's RBAC principles) is scoped so its cluster-wide reconciliation privilege is deliberately constrained where possible (e.g. using ArgoCD's own project/RBAC features to restrict which Applications can target which namespaces/clusters, rather than one monolithic all-powerful ArgoCD identity). Each team has its own ArgoCD "Project" (a further scoping construct within ArgoCD itself) restricting which git repositories and destination namespaces their Applications may reference — team A's Applications can never accidentally (or maliciously) target team B's namespace. Each team's config repository has its own branch protection (Module 3) and review requirements matching its risk profile, since — per this module's core security point — repo write access there is functionally equivalent to deployment power within that team's scope. Secrets are never committed to any watched repo, instead sourced via IRSA-authenticated (Module 21) External Secrets Operator integrations pulling from AWS Secrets Manager, scoped per-namespace via appropriately narrow IAM roles (Module 13). CI pipelines (Module 23/24) building and pushing images for each team use their own scoped OIDC-federated deploy roles, and the resulting image tags are what actually get committed to each team's config repo — meaning the full chain from code commit to running pod is entirely git-driven, IAM-scoped per tenant, and auditable end to end without any team ever holding standing, cluster-wide deployment credentials.
