01 Vertical vs Horizontal Scaling

What is it / why needed

Every system eventually outgrows its current capacity — the fundamental question is HOW to add more. This choice ripples through nearly every module in this course: it's why Module 17's Auto Scaling Groups exist, why Module 11's Kubernetes has an HPA, and why Module 19's RDS distinguishes Read Replicas from simply resizing an instance.

Vertical ScalingHorizontal Scaling
ApproachMake ONE machine bigger (more CPU/RAM)Add MORE machines
CeilingHard physical/cost limit — eventually there's no bigger instance to buyEffectively unbounded (add more nodes)
ComplexitySimple — no architectural change neededRequires the app to be stateless/shareable (load balancing, Module 16; session handling, Module 5)
DowntimeOften requires a restart/resize operationCan scale with zero downtime (new nodes join without touching existing ones)
This is precisely why Module 5 flagged the session-vs-JWT trade-off, and why Module 19 distinguishes Multi-AZ from Read Replicas: horizontal scaling FORCES you to confront statefulness. A stateless web tier (Module 16's ALB in front of interchangeable instances) scales horizontally trivially; a stateful component (a database, an in-memory session store) needs deliberate architecture (replication, sharding, externalized state) to scale horizontally at all.

Interview Q

Q: Why is horizontal scaling generally preferred over vertical scaling for a production web tier, despite vertical scaling being architecturally simpler?
A: Vertical scaling has a hard ceiling (eventually no bigger instance exists) and often a single point of failure (one big machine, however powerful, is still one machine). Horizontal scaling has no such ceiling and, combined with a load balancer (Module 16) across multiple Availability Zones (Module 15), provides genuine fault tolerance — losing one of many nodes barely dents capacity, versus losing your one vertically-scaled machine taking down everything.

02 CAP Theorem & Consistency Models

What is it / why needed

The CAP theorem states that a distributed system can only guarantee TWO of three properties simultaneously during a network partition: Consistency (every read gets the most recent write), Availability (every request gets a response, even if not the latest data), Partition tolerance (the system keeps working despite network failures between nodes).

The nuance interviewers actually want: partition tolerance isn't really optional in any real distributed system (networks WILL partition eventually) — so CAP in practice is really a choice between C and A specifically DURING a partition. This directly explains Module 19's RDS Read Replica trade-off: replicas are asynchronous (favoring availability — they keep serving reads even if slightly behind) rather than synchronous (which would favor consistency but could block/fail reads during replication lag or a network hiccup to the primary).
System exampleChoice during partitionWhy
RDS Multi-AZ (Module 19) synchronous replicationFavors ConsistencyStandby only takes over via controlled failover; writes aren't acknowledged until synchronously replicated
RDS Read Replicas (Module 19)Favors AvailabilityKeep serving reads even with replication lag, rather than blocking
DNS (Module 6)Favors AvailabilityCached/stale answers are served rather than failing outright during resolver/authoritative server issues

Common mistakes

  • Treating CAP as "pick any 2 of 3, always" — in practice, partition tolerance is a given for any real distributed system, so the meaningful design choice is C vs A specifically under partition conditions.

Interview Q

Q: Explain why Read Replicas (Module 19) represent a specific CAP theorem trade-off.
A: Read Replicas use asynchronous replication — they continue serving read requests (favoring Availability) even when there's replication lag relative to the primary (a form of temporary inconsistency), rather than blocking/refusing reads until they're guaranteed perfectly consistent with the primary. This is a deliberate choice favoring availability over strict consistency specifically for the read path, which is why applications needing absolutely fresh data for certain reads should route those specific queries to the primary instead.

03 Caching Strategies

What is it / why needed

Caching stores a copy of expensive-to-compute or slow-to-fetch data somewhere faster to access — directly the same principle behind Module 6's DNS TTL caching, Module 9's Docker layer caching, and Module 23's CI dependency caching, now applied at the application data layer.

PatternHow it worksTrade-off
Cache-aside (lazy loading)App checks cache first; on a miss, reads from DB and populates the cacheSimple, but first request after any cache miss is always slow ("cold" cache)
Write-throughEvery write goes to the cache AND the database simultaneouslyCache always consistent with DB, but adds write latency
Write-behind (write-back)Write to cache immediately, asynchronously persist to DB laterFastest writes, but risks data loss if the cache fails before the async DB write completes
Cache invalidation — famously one of the hardest problems in computer science, for real production reasons: when underlying data changes, a stale cache entry can silently serve wrong data indefinitely if invalidation isn't handled correctly. Common strategies: TTL-based expiry (simple, but stale data can be served for up to the TTL duration — the exact same trade-off flagged in Module 6's DNS TTL discussion), or explicit invalidation on write (more complex but more accurate).

Common mistakes

  • Caching data that changes frequently with a long TTL — serves meaningfully stale data for extended periods, the same lesson as Module 6's "lower TTL before a migration" point, now applied to application caches.
  • No cache invalidation strategy at all on write-heavy data — the cache-aside pattern alone doesn't automatically know when underlying data changes unless writes also explicitly invalidate the relevant cache key.

Interview Q

Q: Why is cache invalidation considered one of the hardest problems in distributed systems?
A: A cache is, by definition, a COPY of data that lives elsewhere — the moment the source of truth changes, that copy risks becoming silently stale, and there's no universal, simple way to guarantee every cache holding that data is immediately and correctly updated or removed, especially across multiple cache layers/instances. Strategies like TTL expiry accept some staleness window as a trade-off for simplicity; explicit invalidation-on-write is more accurate but requires careful, comprehensive coverage of every code path that modifies the underlying data.

04 Database Scaling: Sharding & Replication

Directly extends Module 19's RDS discussion to the general system design vocabulary.

TechniqueWhat it doesTrade-off
Read Replicas (Module 19)Scale READ capacity by distributing reads across copiesDoesn't help write scaling at all; all writes still go to one primary
Sharding (horizontal partitioning)Split data across multiple independent databases by some key (e.g. user_id range or hash) — each shard holds a SUBSET of total dataScales both reads AND writes, but cross-shard queries/joins become genuinely hard, and re-sharding later (as data grows unevenly) is a major undertaking
Vertical partitioningSplit different TABLES/features onto different databases (e.g. user data on one DB, orders on another)Simpler than sharding, but doesn't help if a SINGLE table/feature itself outgrows one database
Why sharding is a genuinely hard, often-avoided-until-necessary decision: once data is sharded by a key (say, user_id), a query needing to join across shards (e.g. "find all orders across all users in a date range" for an admin report) can no longer be a simple database query — it requires application-level fan-out and aggregation across multiple shards, real added complexity. Sharding is usually a last resort after read replicas and vertical partitioning are exhausted, not a first move.

Common mistakes

  • Sharding prematurely, before genuinely exhausting simpler options (read replicas, vertical partitioning, or simply a bigger instance) — takes on substantial complexity (cross-shard query difficulty, re-sharding pain) before it's actually necessary.
  • Choosing a poor shard key that leads to uneven distribution ("hot shards") — some shards end up far busier than others, undermining the whole point of distributing load.

Interview Q

Q: Why don't Read Replicas (Module 19) solve a write-scaling bottleneck, and what's the next step if writes themselves are the bottleneck?
A: Read Replicas only distribute READ traffic — every write still goes to the single primary database, so a write-throughput bottleneck is entirely untouched by adding more replicas. If writes are genuinely the bottleneck (after confirming a bigger instance/vertical scaling isn't sufficient), the next step is typically sharding — horizontally partitioning data across multiple independent databases so writes can be distributed too, accepting the real complexity cost of harder cross-shard queries and difficult future re-sharding.

05 Load Balancing, Queues & Async Processing

Load balancing recap

Module 16's ALB concepts (path-based routing, health checks, connection draining) generalize to the broader system design vocabulary: any system distributing requests across multiple backend instances needs a load-balancing layer, health awareness, and graceful handling of instance churn — whether that's an AWS-managed ALB or a conceptual "load balancer" in a system design interview answer.

Message queues — decoupling and async processing

A queue (SQS, Kafka, RabbitMQ) lets a producer hand off work WITHOUT waiting for it to be processed synchronously — the consumer processes it independently, at its own pace. This directly solves a real scaling problem: a slow downstream operation (sending an email, processing an image, generating a report) shouldn't make the user's original request wait, and a sudden burst of requests shouldn't overwhelm a downstream system that can only handle a steady rate.

Synchronous (tightly coupled, no buffering): User request ──► App ──► [slow email service] ──► Response (user waits for the ENTIRE chain, and a burst of requests floods the email service directly) Asynchronous via queue (decoupled, buffered): User request ──► App ──► enqueue message ──► Response (fast, doesn't wait) │ Consumer pulls messages at ITS OWN pace ──► [email service] (a burst of requests just makes the queue longer temporarily; downstream isn't overwhelmed)
Idempotency matters here too — directly echoing Module 5's HTTP idempotency discussion: most queue systems provide "at-least-once" delivery (a message might be processed more than once, e.g. if a consumer crashes after processing but before acknowledging). Consumers MUST be designed idempotently (the same message processed twice has the same end effect) or a queue-based architecture can cause duplicate side effects — the exact same idempotency principle from Module 5's POST/payment discussion, now at the message-queue layer.

Common mistakes

  • Building a queue consumer that isn't idempotent, then being surprised by duplicate processing (double emails, double charges) under at-least-once delivery semantics.
  • Using synchronous, tightly-coupled calls for genuinely slow/bursty downstream operations instead of decoupling with a queue — creates unnecessary latency and cascading failure risk (a slow downstream service directly slows/breaks the upstream caller).

Interview Q

Q: Why must consumers of a message queue typically be designed to be idempotent?
A: Most queue systems guarantee "at-least-once" delivery rather than "exactly-once" — a message can be redelivered and processed more than once (e.g. if a consumer crashes after processing the work but before acknowledging receipt of the message). If processing that message twice has a different or harmful cumulative effect (like sending a duplicate email or double-charging a customer), the system will produce incorrect behavior under this normal, expected delivery semantic — the same idempotency principle flagged for HTTP POST retries in Module 5.

06 Reliability Patterns

PatternPurposeWhere seen earlier in this course
Retry with backoffRetry a failed operation, waiting progressively longer between attempts, rather than hammering a struggling downstream immediatelyModule 2's health-check retry loop exercise
Circuit breakerAfter repeated failures to a downstream service, stop calling it entirely for a cooldown period — fail fast instead of piling up slow/failing requestsConceptually similar to Module 16's ALB marking a target unhealthy and routing around it
Graceful degradationIf a non-critical dependency fails, serve a reduced/partial experience rather than failing the entire request—
BulkheadingIsolate resources (thread pools, connections) per dependency so one slow/failing dependency can't exhaust resources needed by unrelated parts of the systemConceptually similar to Module 21's per-pod resource requests/limits isolating one workload from starving another
Circuit breakers directly parallel Module 16's ALB health checks, one layer up the stack: where an ALB stops routing to an unhealthy TARGET (a specific server instance), a circuit breaker (implemented in application code, e.g. via a library like Resilience4j) stops calling an unhealthy DEPENDENCY (an entire downstream service) — same underlying philosophy of "stop sending traffic to something that's clearly failing," applied at a different architectural layer.

Common mistakes

  • Retrying failed calls immediately and aggressively without backoff — can worsen an already-struggling downstream service's load, sometimes called a "retry storm."
  • No circuit breaker on calls to an unreliable downstream dependency — a struggling dependency's slowness cascades and can take down the CALLING service too, as its own resources (threads, connections) get tied up waiting on slow calls.

Interview Q

Q: How does a circuit breaker pattern conceptually relate to what an ALB's health check does (Module 16)?
A: Both share the same underlying philosophy — stop sending traffic to something that's demonstrably failing, rather than continuing to hit it and risk overwhelming it further or wasting resources waiting on responses that likely won't come. An ALB health check operates at the load-balancer layer, routing around unhealthy backend TARGETS. A circuit breaker operates within application code, "opening" (stopping calls) to an unhealthy downstream DEPENDENCY after repeated failures, then periodically testing (half-open state) whether it's recovered before fully resuming normal traffic.

07 A Framework for Interview System Design Questions

How to structure an answer when asked to "design X" in an interview — the actual meta-skill being tested.

1. CLARIFY REQUIREMENTS (don't skip this — shows you don't just jump to a solution) - Functional: what must the system actually do? - Non-functional: expected scale (users, requests/sec), read vs write ratio, consistency requirements, latency expectations 2. ESTIMATE (back-of-envelope numbers ground the design in reality) - Rough QPS, storage growth per day/year, bandwidth 3. HIGH-LEVEL DESIGN (the big boxes first, details later) - Client → Load Balancer (Mod 16) → App tier (Mod 14/17/21) → Cache (this module) → Database (Mod 19, + sharding/replication if scale demands) → Queue (this module) for async work 4. DEEP DIVE on the 1-2 genuinely hard/interesting parts - Whatever the interviewer signals interest in, or the most technically challenging piece of YOUR specific design (e.g. "how does the cache stay consistent," "how do we handle a hot shard") 5. ADDRESS FAILURE MODES & TRADE-OFFS explicitly - What happens if the cache goes down? The database? A whole AZ (Mod 15)? - State the CAP trade-off you're making and WHY, rather than pretending you can have perfect consistency AND availability AND partition tolerance
The most common interview mistake, worth stating explicitly: jumping straight to a detailed technical solution before clarifying scale/requirements. A system serving 100 users and one serving 100 million users warrant genuinely different architectures — designing the second system's complexity for the first's actual needs is itself a red flag, not a sign of expertise.

Interview Q

Q: An interviewer asks you to "design a URL shortener." What's your very first move, before drawing any boxes?
A: Clarify requirements and scale FIRST — expected reads-per-second vs writes-per-second (URL shorteners are typically extremely read-heavy), whether custom aliases are needed, whether analytics/click-tracking matters, and rough total URL volume expected. Jumping straight into a detailed architecture (sharding strategy, caching layers) before establishing these basics risks over- or under-engineering the solution relative to what's actually being asked, and signals to the interviewer that requirements-gathering isn't part of your process.

★ Mini Project

Apply the full framework to a concrete design, using components from across this entire course.

DESIGN PROMPT: "Design a scalable image-sharing service (like a simplified Instagram)."

1. CLARIFY:
   - Read-heavy (viewing images) vs write-heavy (uploads)? → Assume heavily read-skewed.
   - Scale: assume 10M daily active users, 1M image uploads/day, 100M image views/day.
   - Consistency: eventual consistency acceptable for view counts/likes; uploads must not be lost.

2. ESTIMATE:
   - 100M views/day ≈ 1,150 reads/sec average (with peak multiples for real traffic patterns)
   - 1M uploads/day ≈ 12 writes/sec average
   - Image storage: assume 2MB average × 1M/day ≈ 2TB/day of new image data

3. HIGH-LEVEL DESIGN:
   Client ──► ALB (Mod 16, multi-AZ, Mod 15) ──► App tier (EKS, Mod 21, horizontally scaled, Mod 17-style HPA/ASG)
                                                      │
                              ┌───────────────────────┼────────────────────────┐
                              ▼                       ▼                        ▼
                     Cache (Redis, for hot/     Metadata DB (RDS,       Object storage (S3, Mod 18,
                     recently-viewed image      Mod 19, with Read      for the actual image BYTES —
                     metadata & feed data)      Replicas given the     never store binary blobs in
                                                 read-heavy load)       the relational DB itself)
                              │
                              ▼
                     Async queue (Mod 30's queue pattern) for:
                     - image processing (thumbnail generation, content moderation)
                     - fanning out to followers' feeds (doesn't need to block the upload response)

4. DEEP DIVE — feed generation (a genuinely hard, interesting part):
   - Fan-out-on-write: precompute each follower's feed at upload time (fast reads, expensive/complex writes
     for users with huge follower counts — a "hot write" problem)
   - Fan-out-on-read: compute the feed at view time by querying followed users' recent posts (simple writes,
     more expensive reads, but avoids the hot-write problem entirely)
   - A hybrid is common in practice: fan-out-on-write for typical users, fan-out-on-read for
     accounts with enormous follower counts (celebrities) to avoid the hot-write bottleneck.

5. FAILURE MODES:
   - Cache down → fall back to DB directly (Mod 19's Read Replicas absorb the extra load,
     accepting higher latency temporarily rather than total failure — CAP: favoring Availability)
   - One AZ down → ALB + multi-AZ ASG/EKS routes around it entirely (Mod 15/16/17)
   - Upload succeeds but async processing (thumbnail gen) fails → retry with backoff
     (this module's reliability pattern), idempotent processing given at-least-once
     queue delivery semantics

✓ Revision Sheet

  • Vertical scaling: simple, hard ceiling. Horizontal scaling: unbounded, but forces confronting statefulness (sessions, DB writes).
  • CAP theorem: partition tolerance is a given in practice; the real trade-off is Consistency vs Availability during a partition.
  • Caching: cache-aside (simple, cold-start miss), write-through (consistent, slower writes), write-behind (fast, riskier). Invalidation is the hard part — TTL (simple, stale window) vs explicit invalidation (accurate, more complex).
  • DB scaling: Read Replicas (reads only) → vertical partitioning (split by table/feature) → sharding (splits both reads/writes, but hard cross-shard queries and re-sharding pain) — usually in that order of adoption.
  • Queues decouple producer/consumer, absorb bursts — but at-least-once delivery REQUIRES idempotent consumers (same principle as Module 5's HTTP idempotency).
  • Reliability patterns: retry-with-backoff (avoid retry storms), circuit breaker (stop calling failing dependencies, same philosophy as ALB health checks), graceful degradation, bulkheading (resource isolation per dependency).
  • Interview framework: Clarify requirements/scale → Estimate → High-level design → Deep dive on hard parts → Explicitly address failure modes and trade-offs.

Q 10 MCQs

1. What's the main limitation of vertical scaling?
A hard physical/cost ceiling — eventually no bigger machine exists to scale to.
2. In practice, what's the real trade-off in the CAP theorem?
Consistency vs Availability specifically during a network partition, since partition tolerance is effectively unavoidable in real distributed systems.
3. What caching pattern populates the cache only after a miss?
Cache-aside (lazy loading).
4. Why is cache invalidation considered hard?
A cache is a copy of data elsewhere; ensuring every cached copy is correctly updated/removed when the source changes has no universally simple solution.
5. Do Read Replicas help scale database writes?
No — only reads; all writes still go to the primary.
6. What database scaling technique splits data across multiple independent databases by a key?
Sharding (horizontal partitioning).
7. Why must message queue consumers typically be idempotent?
Most queues provide at-least-once delivery — a message can be processed more than once.
8. What reliability pattern stops calling a repeatedly-failing downstream dependency for a cooldown period?
Circuit breaker.
9. What's the first step recommended when answering a system design interview question?
Clarify functional and non-functional requirements/scale before designing anything.
10. What feed-generation strategy avoids a "hot write" problem for users with huge follower counts?
Fan-out-on-read (or a hybrid approach using it selectively for high-follower accounts).

! 5 Debugging Scenarios

1. A dashboard shows the database's write throughput is the clear bottleneck, but the team already has several Read Replicas.
Read Replicas don't help writes at all — need to first confirm vertical scaling is genuinely exhausted, then consider sharding or vertical partitioning depending on the actual write pattern.
2. Users occasionally see clearly outdated data despite a caching layer being in place.
Check the cache's TTL/invalidation strategy — likely either a too-long TTL or a missing explicit invalidation-on-write path for that specific data.
3. A payment processing queue consumer occasionally double-charges customers after a consumer restart.
The consumer isn't idempotent under the queue's at-least-once delivery semantics — needs an idempotency key/dedup mechanism (Module 5's pattern) to safely handle redelivered messages.
4. A single slow downstream dependency is causing cascading slowness/failures across an otherwise healthy system.
Missing a circuit breaker (and/or bulkheading) — the calling service's own resources are getting tied up waiting on the slow dependency instead of failing fast and isolating the impact.
5. A celebrity user's post takes an unusually long time to appear in followers' feeds compared to regular users.
Likely using pure fan-out-on-write for everyone — a hybrid approach (fan-out-on-read for very high-follower-count accounts) avoids the hot-write bottleneck this specific case is hitting.

▶ 5 System Design Exercises

  1. Design a URL shortener, explicitly applying the 5-step interview framework, including estimating storage/QPS at 100M shortened URLs.
  2. Design a rate limiter for a public API, discussing where state (request counts) should live for it to work correctly across multiple horizontally-scaled app instances.
  3. Design a notification system (push/email/SMS) that must never send a duplicate notification, considering queue delivery semantics.
  4. Design the caching strategy for a product catalog page that changes prices frequently but product descriptions rarely — propose different TTLs/invalidation approaches for each.
  5. Design a system for handling a sudden 100x traffic spike (a "flash sale") on an e-commerce checkout flow, addressing queueing, circuit breakers, and graceful degradation explicitly.

? 5 Interview Questions (beginner → advanced)

Beginner: What's the difference between vertical and horizontal scaling?
Vertical scaling makes a single machine more powerful (more CPU/RAM). Horizontal scaling adds more machines to share the load. Horizontal scaling generally has no hard ceiling and provides better fault tolerance, but requires the system to handle distributing work/state across multiple instances.
Beginner-Intermediate: Explain the CAP theorem trade-off in the context of a Read Replica setup.
Read Replicas favor Availability over strict Consistency — they continue serving read requests even when there's some replication lag (temporary inconsistency) relative to the primary, rather than blocking reads until guaranteed fully consistent. This is a deliberate, sensible trade-off for most read traffic, with the caveat that reads genuinely requiring the absolute latest data should be routed to the primary instead.
Intermediate: Design a caching strategy for a news website's homepage, considering that content updates frequently but doesn't need to be instantaneous.
Use cache-aside with a short-to-moderate TTL (e.g. 30-60 seconds) appropriate for how "fresh" the homepage genuinely needs to feel — accepting that a brief staleness window is fine for most news content. For genuinely breaking news requiring near-immediate visibility, either use a much shorter TTL for that specific content type or add explicit cache invalidation triggered when breaking news is published, rather than applying one uniform TTL to fundamentally different freshness requirements across the page.
Intermediate-Advanced: A system experiences cascading failures whenever a single downstream payment provider becomes slow (not fully down, just slow). Diagnose the likely architectural gap and propose a fix.
Likely missing both a circuit breaker (to stop calling the slow provider after repeated timeouts/failures, failing fast instead of piling up slow requests) and bulkheading (isolating the thread pool/connection pool used for payment-provider calls from the rest of the application's resources, so a slow payment provider can't exhaust resources needed by unrelated request paths). Implementing both means a slow payment provider degrades ONLY payment-related functionality (ideally with graceful degradation — e.g. queueing the payment for later retry rather than failing the whole checkout) rather than cascading into an application-wide outage.
Advanced: Design a globally-distributed system serving users across multiple continents with low latency, strong-enough consistency for user-facing data, and resilience to a full regional outage — tying together concepts from across this course.
Deploy the application across multiple AWS regions, each with its own multi-AZ VPC (Module 15), EKS cluster (Module 21) behind a regional ALB (Module 16), auto-scaled (Module 17), with Route53 (Module 6) using latency-based or geolocation routing to direct users to their nearest healthy region. Data layer: a regional primary database (Module 19) per region for low-latency local writes on region-specific data, with asynchronous cross-region replication for data that needs global visibility (accepting eventual consistency — a deliberate CAP trade-off favoring availability/partition-tolerance for the cross-region case) alongside synchronous Multi-AZ replication WITHIN each region for local high availability (favoring consistency at that smaller blast-radius scale). A CDN/edge cache layer serves static assets and cacheable content close to users globally, reducing cross-region latency for read-heavy content. For a full regional outage, Route53 health checks (Module 6/16) detect the region's unhealthy status and failover routes traffic to the nearest healthy alternate region — accepting that in-flight writes to the failed region's primary during the outage window may need reconciliation once it recovers, an explicit, documented trade-off rather than an unaddressed edge case, following this module's core framework principle of stating trade-offs explicitly rather than pretending a design achieves perfect consistency, availability, and partition tolerance simultaneously.
